# Dependent prepositions: adjective patterns

> The prepositions you do not get to choose — the ones an adjective drags along behind it, so that *good* takes *at*, *afraid* takes *of*, and *married* takes *to*, for no reason anyone can give.

[← The grammar of prepositions](../README.md) &middot; [All categories](README.md) &middot; [Verb patterns](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md)

Everywhere else in this dictionary a preposition means something, and picking the right one is a matter of seeing the relationship clearly: *in* encloses, *after* follows, *despite* concedes. This file is the exception, because here the preposition is **fixed by the word in front of it** — the only reason *good* takes *at* while *ready* takes *for* and *aware* takes *of* is that four hundred years of use settled it that way, and the pairs have to be learned as units, the way irregular verbs are learned. What *can* be learned systematically is what may follow the preposition: since these are real prepositions, a verb after one has to be a **gerund** and never an infinitive (*accustomed to driving*, not ✗ *accustomed to drive*), and that single rule is the highest-value thing in the file. The **Contrast** line in each entry does the other half of the work, because a great many of these adjectives take two or three prepositions with genuinely different meanings — *good at* / *good for* / *good with*, *concerned about* / *concerned with*, *familiar with* / *familiar to*.

**What lives here, and what lives next door.** This file holds the **adjective** patterns, plus the handful of verb phrases caught in the same fixed *to* — [look forward to](#look-forward-to) above all. The **verb** patterns, including the sets where swapping the preposition changes the meaning outright, are catalogued in [Dependent prepositions: verb patterns](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md) rather than repeated here: [hear about / from / of](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#hear-about-hear-from-hear-of), [agree with / to / on](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#agree-with-agree-to-agree-on), [look at / for / after / into](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#look-at-look-for-look-after-look-into), [think about / of](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#think-about-think-of), [talk to / with / about](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#talk-to-talk-with-talk-about), [wait for / on](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#wait-for-wait-on), and [care about / for](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#care-about-care-for). The plain prepositions themselves — [at](01-core-in-on-at.md#at), [of](02-core-relational.md#of), [to](02-core-relational.md#to), [for](02-core-relational.md#for), [with](02-core-relational.md#with), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about) — are explained in the core files and are only linked to from here, never re-explained.

## The two traps this file is built around

### The gerund rule, and the *to* that is not an infinitive

The word *to* has two jobs in English, and they look identical on the page. In *I want to leave*, *to* is the marker of an infinitive and a bare verb has to follow it. In *I object to leaving*, *to* is a preposition and a **noun-like thing** has to follow it — a noun, a pronoun, or a gerund. Nothing in the spelling tells you which one you have; you have to know it about the word in front.

The test is simple and it never fails: **put a noun after the *to*.** If the result is grammatical, the *to* is a preposition and a verb in that slot must be *-ing*.

| Phrase | Noun test | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| *accustomed to* | *accustomed to the noise* ✓ | preposition → *accustomed to working nights* |
| *used to* (adjective) | *used to the noise* ✓ | preposition → *used to working nights* |
| *look forward to* | *look forward to the weekend* ✓ | preposition → *look forward to hearing from you* |
| *opposed to* | *opposed to the plan* ✓ | preposition → *opposed to raising rates* |
| *committed to* | *committed to the project* ✓ | preposition → *committed to finishing it* |
| *dedicated to* | *dedicated to her students* ✓ | preposition → *dedicated to teaching* |
| *close to* | *close to the end* ✓ | preposition → *close to quitting* |
| *subject to* | *subject to review* ✓ | preposition → *subject to being audited* |
| *want to* | ✗ *want to the noise* | infinitive marker → *want to work* |
| *able to* | ✗ *able to the noise* | infinitive marker → *able to work* |
| *ready to* | ✗ *ready to the noise* | infinitive marker → *ready to work* |

The errors this prevents are the standard ones in advanced English, and they survive in the writing of people who are otherwise fluent:

- ✗ *I look forward to hear from you.* → ✓ *I look forward to **hearing** from you.*
- ✗ *She isn't accustomed to drive on the left.* → ✓ *accustomed to **driving** on the left.*
- ✗ *We're committed to improve service.* → ✓ *committed to **improving** service.*
- ✗ *He's opposed to change the schedule.* → ✓ *opposed to **changing** the schedule.*
- ✗ *I'm interested to buy your car.* → ✓ *interested in **buying** your car.*

The same rule governs every other preposition in the file, but *to* is the only one that can be mistaken for something else, which is why nearly all the damage happens there. After *of*, *at*, *for*, *in*, *on*, *with*, and *about*, no one is tempted: nobody writes ✗ *capable of do it* or ✗ *good at cook*.

### The *used to* ambiguity

*Used to* is two unrelated constructions that are spelled the same and, in most American speech, pronounced the same — /ˈjustə/, with the *s* of *loose*, not the *z* of *lose*.

**1. The habitual past — a semi-modal, followed by a bare infinitive.** *I used to smoke.* This is about a discontinued habit, it has no present tense, and its subject cannot be *be*: ✗ *I am used to smoke.* Its negative and question forms drop the *d* in American writing — *didn't use to*, *did she use to* — and *never used to* is the commoner negative in speech.

**2. The adjective *used*, followed by the preposition *to*.** *I'm used to the noise. I'm used to working nights.* This is about familiarity, it appears with *be*, *get*, and *become*, and a verb after it must be a gerund. It is a plain synonym of *accustomed to*.

The two are told apart by what sits in front and what sits behind:

| | Habitual past | Adjective (= accustomed) |
|---|---|---|
| Front | subject alone: *I used to* | *be* / *get* / *become*: *I'm used to*, *I got used to* |
| Behind | bare verb: *used to smoke* | noun or *-ing*: *used to the smoke*, *used to smoking* |
| Tense | past only | any tense |
| Negative | *didn't use to*, *never used to* | *isn't used to*, *wasn't used to* |

So *I used to drive to work* means the speaker no longer does; *I'm used to driving to work* means the speaker finds it normal; and *I got used to driving to work* means the speaker came, over time, to find it normal. Both entries are in the A–Z below, at [used to](#used-to) and [accustomed to](#accustomed-to).

### *Different from*, *different than*, *different to*

The one place in this file where a usage question is live rather than settled, and where the honest American answer has three parts.

- ***Different from*** is the standard American choice and is never wrong. Before a noun or pronoun it is what careful writing uses: *Her account is different from his.*
- ***Different than*** is common, respectable American English, and it is the better choice **before a clause**, where *from* forces a clumsy *what*: *The job turned out different than I expected* beats *different from what I expected* in speech and is defensible in print. Before a bare noun (*different than his*) some editors still object, so *from* is the safer default there.
- ***Different to*** is British. It is not an error in London and it is not used in the United States; American readers hear it as foreign.

*Differ* takes *from* only — ✗ *differs than*. And the comparative-conjunction *than* is treated as a word in its own right at [than](09-comparison-opposition.md#than). See the full entry at [different from](#different-from).

## A–Z: adjective + preposition

### absent from

**Pronunciation:** /ˈæbsənt frʌm/ &middot; *AB-sunt from* &middot; weak /ˈæbsənt frəm/
**Русский:** отсутствующий где-л. — не быть там, где ожидалось; не встречаться в тексте или списке
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral, tilting formal; the everyday word is *missing*
**Complement:** noun phrase or pronoun naming a place, an occasion, or a body of work (*absent from school*, *absent from the record*) — **never a gerund** and **never a clause**. There is no ✗ *absent from being there*.
**Pattern:** *be absent from* [place/event] &middot; [noun] *absent from* [noun] &middot; *conspicuously* / *notably* / *entirely absent from* [noun]
**Variants:** *missing from* is the neutral everyday equivalent and takes the same complement. *Away from* covers only the physical sense.
**Contrast:** ✗ *absent of* and ✗ *absent in* are errors here — the fixed preposition is *from*. Do not confuse the adjective with the marginal preposition *absent* meaning "in the absence of" (*absent a court order, the file stays sealed*), which takes a noun with no *from* at all and is a different word; see [absent](08-accompaniment-exception.md#absent). Against *away from*, which reports a body somewhere else, *absent from* reports a gap where something was expected.

Not present where it was supposed to be — and, in the extended use, not to be found in a text, a list, or an argument.

1. Two jurors were *absent from* the courtroom when the verdict came in.
2. Any mention of the earlier settlement is *absent from* the report.
3. She has been *absent from* work since Thursday.
4. Warmth was conspicuously *absent from* his reply.
5. That name is *absent from* every version of the guest list I've seen.

**See also:** [free of](#free-of), [short of](#short-of), [lacking in](#lacking-in), [from](02-core-relational.md#from)

### accountable to, accountable for

**Pronunciation:** /əˈkaʊntəbəl tu, əˈkaʊntəbəl fɔr/ &middot; *uh-KOWN-tuh-bul too, uh-KOWN-tuh-bul for* &middot; weak /əˈkaʊntəbəl tə/ and /əˈkaʊntəbəl fər/ — in running speech the whole tail flattens to "uh-KOWN-tuh-bul-tuh" and "uh-KOWN-tuh-bul-fur"
**Русский:** подотчётный кому-л. (to), ответственный за что-л. (for)
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; the standard word in management, government, and journalism about both
**Complement:** after *to*, a **person or body** that can demand the explanation — a noun or pronoun only, **never a gerund** (✗ *accountable to hiring*); after *for*, the **thing you must explain** — a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*accountable for keeping the receipts*), and **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *accountable for to keep the receipts*). No *that*-clause attaches to either.
**Pattern:** *be accountable to* [person / body] &middot; *be accountable for* [thing / verb-ing] &middot; *hold* [someone] *accountable for* [thing] &middot; *accountable to* [person] *for* [thing] — both slots at once &middot; *directly* / *ultimately* / *personally accountable*
**Variants:** *answerable to* and *answerable for* split exactly the same way and are the slightly more formal, British-leaning twins. *Responsible to* / *responsible for* is the everyday pair and divides identically.
**Contrast:** ***To* names the one who can demand an accounting; *for* names the thing accounted for.*** *She is accountable to the board* says who can call her in; *She is accountable for the shortfall* says what she will be asked about. The two slots can be filled at once — *accountable to Congress for every dollar* — which is the clearest proof they are not alternatives. Against [responsible for](#responsible-for), which can mean merely "caused it," *accountable for* always implies somebody is entitled to an explanation. ✗ *accountable of* and ✗ *accountable with* are errors.

Obliged to explain and justify — *to* whoever may demand it, and *for* whatever falls inside your charge.

1. The commissioner is *accountable to* a board of five elected members.
2. Every site manager is *accountable for* the safety of the crew on shift.
3. Somebody is going to be held *accountable for* losing the original file.
4. In a co-op, the staff are *accountable to* the residents, not to a landlord.
5. She is *accountable to* the state *for* every dollar of the grant.

**See also:** [answerable to](#answerable-to), [responsible for](#responsible-for), [liable for, liable to](#liable-for-liable-to), [subject to](#subject-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### accustomed to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈkʌstəmd tu/ &middot; *uh-KUSS-tumd too* &middot; weak /əˈkʌstəmd tə/
**Русский:** привыкший к — после to герундий, не инфинитив
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; *used to* is the everyday version
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*accustomed to working late*) — **never a to-infinitive.** ✗ *accustomed to work late* is the classic error; the *to* here is a preposition, not an infinitive marker, which the noun test proves: *accustomed to the work* ✓. A *that*-clause is impossible.
**Pattern:** *be accustomed to* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *become* / *grow* / *get accustomed to* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *accustom* [someone] *to* [noun / verb-ing]
**Variants:** *used to* — identical in meaning, far commoner, informal to neutral. *Habituated to* and *inured to* are the clinical and the grim versions; *inured to* implies the thing endured is unpleasant.
**Contrast:** ***Accustomed to* + *-ing* versus the habitual past *used to* + bare verb** is the pair to keep straight: *I'm accustomed to driving* (I find it normal) is not *I used to drive* (I no longer do). An older *accustomed to do* survives in nineteenth-century prose and in a few frozen legal phrases, but in modern American English it reads as a mistake — write the gerund. Note also that the adjective describes a settled state while *get accustomed to* describes the process of arriving at it.

Used to something by repetition, so that it no longer registers as strange or difficult.

1. She's *accustomed to* the noise from the freeway and sleeps right through it.
2. Crews here are *accustomed to* working in twenty below.
3. It took a month to *get accustomed to* the new software.
4. He is not *accustomed to* being interrupted.
5. Passengers *accustomed to* the old schedule kept showing up an hour early.

**See also:** [used to](#used-to), [familiar with](#familiar-with), [comfortable with](#comfortable-with), [look forward to](#look-forward-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### acquainted with

**Pronunciation:** /əˈkweɪntɪd wɪθ/ &middot; *uh-KWAYN-tid with* &middot; weak /əˈkweɪntəd wəθ/, the final consonant often voiced to /wəð/ before a vowel
**Русский:** знакомый с — с человеком или с предметом, темой
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal-leaning; in speech Americans say *know* or *familiar with* and save *acquainted with* for introductions and legal statements
**Complement:** a noun phrase or pronoun naming a person, a subject, a document, or a place. A **gerund** is grammatical but rare and stiff (*acquainted with running a floor crew*), and a **to-infinitive is impossible** — ✗ *acquainted with to run the crew*. The noun test settles it: *acquainted with the crew* ✓, so *with* is doing ordinary prepositional work.
**Pattern:** *be acquainted with* [person / subject] &middot; *get* / *become acquainted with* &middot; *acquaint* [someone] *with* [noun] &middot; *be well* / *slightly* / *only vaguely acquainted with* &middot; *make the acquaintance of* — the noun frame, with *of*
**Variants:** *familiar with* is the neutral everyday synonym; *conversant with* is the formal one for a body of knowledge; *know* is the plain verb and takes a direct object with no preposition at all.
**Contrast:** Against [familiar with](#familiar-with), which reports how much you know, *acquainted with* keeps a trace of the introduction — you *become acquainted with* a person by meeting them, and the phrase is faintly cool about how well you know them. ✗ *acquainted to* is an error in every frame, including the causative: you *acquaint someone with* the rules, never ✗ *to* the rules. Note that the related noun changes the preposition twice over: *an acquaintance with* the material, but *an acquaintance of* mine.

Introduced to someone, or informed enough about a subject to find your way around it.

1. I'm only slightly *acquainted with* her husband.
2. Anyone *acquainted with* the case knows the timeline doesn't work.
3. It took the new hire a week to get *acquainted with* the filing system.
4. The judge is thoroughly *acquainted with* the record and won't need the summary.
5. We became *acquainted with* the neighbors over a fence repair neither of us wanted.

**See also:** [familiar with](#familiar-with), [conversant with](#conversant-with), [cognizant of](#cognizant-of), [aware of](#aware-of), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### addicted to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈdɪktɪd tu/ &middot; *uh-DIK-tid too* &middot; weak /əˈdɪktɪd tə/
**Русский:** зависимый от, подсевший на — о наркотике или, шутливо, о привычке
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral in the clinical sense; casually hyperbolic in the other (*addicted to that show*)
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*addicted to checking her phone*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *addicted to check*) and never a clause.
**Pattern:** *be* / *become* / *get addicted to* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *addict* [someone] *to* [noun] &middot; *addicted to* [substance]
**Variants:** *hooked on* is the informal equivalent and takes *on*, not *to*. *Dependent on* is the medical term for the physical half of addiction.
**Contrast:** ✗ *addicted with* and ✗ *addicted of* are errors. Against *hooked on*, which is slang and almost always light-hearted, *addicted to* carries the clinical sense unless the context is plainly joking. Note that the noun keeps the preposition — *an addiction to painkillers* — while the everyday alternative changes it: *a dependence on painkillers*.

Unable to stop taking or doing something, whether that is meant literally or as a joke about a habit.

1. He was *addicted to* prescription painkillers for two years.
2. Half the office is *addicted to* that cold brew from downstairs.
3. Kids who get *addicted to* scrolling sleep about an hour less.
4. She's *addicted to* buying supplies she already owns.
5. The clinic treats patients *addicted to* opioids and alcohol alike.

**See also:** [hooked on](#hooked-on), [dependent on](#dependent-on), [obsessed with](#obsessed-with), [crazy about](#crazy-about), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### adjacent to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈdʒeɪsənt tu/ &middot; *uh-JAY-sunt too* &middot; weak /əˈdʒeɪsənt tə/
**Русский:** прилегающий к, соседний с — граничащий вплотную
**Type:** adjective + preposition, working as a complex preposition of place &middot; **Register:** formal to technical — zoning ordinances, real-estate listings, radiology reports, geometry; conversation says *next to*
**Complement:** a noun phrase or pronoun naming a place or a physical thing — **never a gerund** (✗ *adjacent to parking*) and never a clause. Used attributively with no complement at all (*the adjacent room*), it is a plain adjective and not a preposition; that use is outside this entry.
**Pattern:** *be adjacent to* [place] &middot; [noun] *adjacent to* [noun] &middot; *immediately* / *directly* / *physically adjacent to* &middot; *the adjacent* [noun] — attributive, no preposition
**Variants:** *next to* is the everyday equivalent; *abutting* and *contiguous with* are the surveyor's and the statute's words; *bordering on* adds a shared edge and carries a figurative use (*bordering on rude*) that *adjacent to* does not have.
**Contrast:** ***Adjacent to* claims a shared boundary; [close to](#close-to) claims only nearness.*** Two lots adjacent to each other touch; two lots close to each other need not. In American zoning and title work that difference decides cases, which is why the formal word survives. ✗ *adjacent with* and ✗ *adjacent of* are errors. The purely spatial prepositions this competes with — *next to*, *beside*, *by* — are catalogued in [place and position](03-place-position.md).

Sharing a border or a wall with something — touching it, or as near as makes no difference.

1. The lot *adjacent to* the school was rezoned for townhouses.
2. Park in the garage *adjacent to* the courthouse, not the lot across the street.
3. The mass is *adjacent to* the artery, which is why they won't operate.
4. Two rooms *adjacent to* the kitchen were knocked together into an office.
5. In the figure, the shaded square is *adjacent to* the right angle.

**See also:** [close to](#close-to), [parallel to](#parallel-to), [perpendicular to](#perpendicular-to), [proximal to, distal to](#proximal-to-distal-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### adverse to

**Pronunciation:** /ædˈvɜrs tu/ &middot; *ad-VURSS too*; also *AD-vurss* attributively &middot; weak /ædˈvɜrs tə/
**Русский:** противоречащий интересам, неблагоприятный для — юр./книжн.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; an American meets it in legal filings (*a position adverse to the trustee*), in medical reporting (*adverse event*), and in finance, essentially never in conversation
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming an interest, a party, or a position — **never a gerund** (✗ *adverse to signing*) and never a to-infinitive. The word is also common attributively with no complement (*adverse weather*, *an adverse ruling*, *adverse possession*), which is the adjective alone.
**Pattern:** [noun] *adverse to* [interest / party] &middot; *take a position adverse to* [noun] &middot; *adverse* [noun] — attributive &middot; *adversely affect* [noun] — the adverb, with no preposition
**Variants:** *contrary to* and *opposed to* cover the general sense in plain English; *hostile to* covers the personal one; *unfavorable to* is the neutral choice for rulings and conditions.
**Contrast:** ***Adverse* is not *averse*, and this is the single commonest educated mix-up in American English.*** *Averse to* means personally disinclined and is what you want after *I'm not*: ✓ *I'm not averse to a second cup*, ✗ *I'm not adverse to a second cup*. *Adverse to* means working against someone's interest, and its subject is a condition, a ruling, or a party — not a feeling. Note also the frozen legal compounds, where no *to* follows at all: *adverse party*, *adverse witness*, *adverse possession*.

Running against someone's interest — of a ruling, a fact, a condition, or an opposing party.

1. Counsel disclosed a client whose interests are *adverse to* the estate's.
2. The ruling was *adverse to* every party except the insurer.
3. Testimony *adverse to* the defense came from its own expert.
4. A finding *adverse to* the agency would cost it the contract.
5. Anything *adverse to* the bondholders has to appear in the filing.

**See also:** [opposed to](#opposed-to), [prejudicial to](#prejudicial-to), [detrimental to](#detrimental-to), [repugnant to](#repugnant-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### afraid of

**Pronunciation:** /əˈfreɪd ʌv/ &middot; *uh-FRAYD uv* &middot; weak /əˈfreɪd əv/, and before a consonant often /əˈfreɪd ə/
**Русский:** боящийся чего-л. — в русском «бояться» + родительный падеж
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*afraid of falling*) — **never a to-infinitive after *of***. The adjective also takes a bare to-infinitive with **no preposition** (*afraid to ask*) and a *that*-clause (*afraid that we're too late*), but those are different frames, not alternatives inside this one.
**Pattern:** *be afraid of* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be afraid to* [verb] &middot; *be afraid* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *be afraid for* [someone] &middot; *afraid of* [someone] *-ing* (*afraid of the roof collapsing*)
**Variants:** *scared of* is the everyday synonym, *frightened of* the slightly more formal one, *terrified of* the intensified one — all three take *of*.
**Contrast:** ***Afraid of doing* versus *afraid to do* is a real difference.** *Afraid of falling* names a fear of something that might happen to you; *afraid to jump* names a reluctance to act. *I was afraid of waking her* is about the risk; *I was afraid to wake her* is about hesitating. And *afraid for* changes the target entirely — *afraid for the children* means you fear on their behalf, not that you fear them. ✗ *afraid from* is an error.

Frightened of something, or unwilling to do something because of what might follow.

1. She's been *afraid of* dogs since she was bitten at six.
2. He was *afraid of* saying the wrong thing in front of the whole department.
3. I'm not *afraid of* hard work — I'm afraid of pointless work.
4. They were *afraid for* their parents, who lived two blocks from the fire.
5. Don't be *afraid of* asking; the worst they can say is no.

**See also:** [scared of](#scared-of), [frightened of](#frightened-of), [nervous about](#nervous-about), [worried about](#worried-about), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### amenable to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈminəbəl tu/ &middot; *uh-MEE-nuh-bul too*; the older *uh-MEN-uh-bul* survives in law &middot; weak /əˈminəbəl tə/
**Русский:** готовый согласиться на; о вещи — поддающийся (лечению, анализу); книжн.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to neutral; the "willing" sense is ordinary business English, the "yields to" sense belongs to science, medicine, and law
**Complement:** a noun phrase or a **gerund** — *amenable to moving the date* ✓ — and **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *amenable to move the date*. The noun test proves the point: *amenable to the change* ✓, so the *to* here is a preposition and the *-ing* form is obligatory.
**Pattern:** *be amenable to* [proposal / verb-ing] &middot; *be amenable to* [treatment / analysis / suit] &middot; *find* [someone] *amenable to* [noun] &middot; *perfectly* / *entirely* / *not especially amenable to*
**Variants:** *open to* and *receptive to* are the everyday equivalents in the willingness sense; *susceptible to* and *susceptible of* cover the "yields to" sense; *tractable* covers it with no preposition at all.
**Contrast:** **The subject decides the sense.** With a person, *amenable to* means willing to go along: *she's amenable to a later start*. With a thing, it means it yields to some treatment or procedure: *amenable to surgery*, *amenable to statistical analysis*, and in law *amenable to suit* — subject to a court's power. Against [opposed to](#opposed-to), which is a settled position, *amenable to* reports a door left open. ✗ *amenable with* is an error.

Willing to go along with something, when the subject is a person; open to being treated, analyzed, or sued, when it is a thing.

1. The seller turned out to be *amenable to* closing two weeks early.
2. Nobody on the committee is *amenable to* another round of edits.
3. The infection proved *amenable to* a two-week course of antibiotics.
4. That question isn't *amenable to* a yes-or-no answer.
5. A foreign parent may still be *amenable to* suit in the state where it sells.

**See also:** [susceptible to, susceptible of](#susceptible-to-susceptible-of), [conducive to](#conducive-to), [opposed to](#opposed-to), [subject to](#subject-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### analogous to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈnæləɡəs tu/ &middot; *uh-NAL-uh-gus too* &middot; weak /əˈnæləɡəs tə/. The *g* is **hard** /ɡ/, unlike the /dʒ/ of the noun *analogy* — ✗ "uh-NAL-uh-jus" is a common misreading
**Русский:** аналогичный чему-л. — сходный по функции или по структуре отношений
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal and scholarly — scientific writing, legal reasoning from precedent, academic prose; conversation says *like*
**Complement:** a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*analogous to running the trial twice*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *analogous to run the trial twice*) and never a *that*-clause. A *wh*-clause is fine: *analogous to what happens in the second reactor*.
**Pattern:** *be analogous to* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *a case analogous to* [noun] &middot; *closely* / *roughly* / *only loosely analogous to* &middot; *by analogy to* [noun] — the noun frame
**Variants:** *analogous with* turns up but is not the standard American choice; *comparable to* and *parallel to* are the neutral alternatives; the noun goes both ways — *an analogy to* or *an analogy with*, catalogued in [noun patterns](16-dependent-nouns.md).
**Contrast:** ***Analogous to* claims that two things stand in the same relation, not that they look alike*** — a heart is analogous to a pump because of what it does, not what it resembles, while [similar to](#similar-to) claims plain likeness. In biology the word is a term of art opposed to *homologous*: a bird's wing and an insect's wing are analogous (same function, separate origins) and not homologous (same origin). ✗ *analogous of* and ✗ *analogous from* are errors.

Corresponding in function or in structure of relations, so that reasoning about the one carries over to the other.

1. The heart is *analogous to* a pump, though the metaphor gives out quickly.
2. Her situation is closely *analogous to* the one the court decided in 2019.
3. A bird's wing is *analogous to* an insect's wing but not homologous with it.
4. Billing by the hour here is *analogous to* metering electricity.
5. The claim is roughly *analogous to* arguing that umbrellas cause rain.

**See also:** [similar to](#similar-to), [equivalent to](#equivalent-to), [parallel to](#parallel-to), [isomorphic to](#isomorphic-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### ancillary to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈænsəˌlɛri tu/ &middot; *AN-suh-lair-ee too* &middot; weak /ˈænsəˌlɛri tə/. British English stresses the second syllable, *an-SIL-uh-ree*; American English does not
**Русский:** вспомогательный по отношению к, служебный при чём-л. — книжн./юр.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — hospital billing (*ancillary services*), contracts (*ancillary agreement*), and federal courts (*ancillary jurisdiction*); it is an office and a filing word, not a spoken one
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the main thing supported. A **gerund** is possible in careful writing (*ancillary to running the main plant*) but rare, and a **to-infinitive is impossible**: ✗ *ancillary to run the plant*.
**Pattern:** *be ancillary to* [main activity] &middot; [service / agreement] *ancillary to* [noun] &middot; *ancillary* [noun] — attributive &middot; *merely* / *purely ancillary to*
**Variants:** *incidental to* and *collateral to* are the near twins in legal drafting; *subordinate to* and *secondary to* are the plainer versions; *auxiliary to* is the older and slightly more mechanical one.
**Contrast:** ***Ancillary to* says a thing exists to support the main thing; [incidental to](#incidental-to) says it merely comes along with it.*** A hospital's lab is ancillary to patient care because it serves it; the parking fee is incidental to the visit because it just happens alongside. Against [subordinate to](#subordinate-to), which ranks two things, *ancillary to* describes a supporting function and implies no chain of command. ✗ *ancillary of* and ✗ *ancillary with* are errors.

Supporting the main thing rather than being it — a service, a document, or a proceeding that exists to make the principal one work.

1. Imaging and lab work are billed as services *ancillary to* the procedure.
2. The confidentiality letter is *ancillary to* the purchase agreement and expires with it.
3. Storage is *ancillary to* the shipping business, not a line of its own.
4. The court kept the counterclaim as a matter *ancillary to* the main action.
5. Everything else in the budget is *ancillary to* payroll.

**See also:** [incidental to](#incidental-to), [incident to](#incident-to), [subordinate to](#subordinate-to), [appurtenant to](#appurtenant-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### angry with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈæŋɡri wɪθ/ &middot; *ANG-gree with* &middot; weak /ˈæŋɡri wəθ/
**Русский:** сердитый на — with на человека, at на вещь, about из-за положения дел
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; *mad at* is the American conversational default
**Complement:** noun phrase or pronoun after *with* and *at*; noun phrase or **gerund** after *about* (*angry about being passed over*) — **never a to-infinitive after any of the three.** A *that*-clause attaches to the bare adjective: *angry that no one told her*.
**Pattern:** *be angry with* [person] &middot; *be angry at* [person or thing] &middot; *be angry about* [situation] &middot; *be angry* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *get angry with* [person] *about* [thing]
**Variants:** *angry at* and *angry with* are both standard American English before a person; older handbooks insist on *with* for people and *at* for things, and that distribution still reads as slightly more careful, but *angry at her* is unremarkable in American print.
**Contrast:** **The three prepositions split by target.** *With* (and, in American usage, *at*) takes the **person**; *about* takes the **situation**; *at* alone is what you use for an inanimate cause (*angry at the delay*). You can be *angry with your brother about the money* — both slots filled at once — but ✗ *angry with the money* is wrong. Compare *annoyed with*, which follows the same split at lower intensity, and *furious with*, at higher.

Feeling strong displeasure — with a person, at a thing, or about a state of affairs.

1. She was *angry with* her brother for weeks after the funeral.
2. Don't get *angry at* the referee; get better at defense.
3. Everyone in the room was *angry about* how the vote had been scheduled.
4. He's still *angry with* himself for not asking more questions.
5. I was *angry that* nobody had bothered to call.

**See also:** [mad at](#mad-at), [annoyed with](#annoyed-with), [furious with](#furious-with), [upset about](#upset-about), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### annoyed with

**Pronunciation:** /əˈnɔɪd wɪθ/ &middot; *uh-NOYD with* &middot; weak /əˈnɔɪd wəθ/
**Русский:** раздражённый, недовольный кем-л. — слабее, чем angry
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or pronoun after *with* and *at*; noun phrase or **gerund** after *about* and *by* (*annoyed by having to ask twice*) — **never a to-infinitive.** A *that*-clause attaches to the bare adjective: *annoyed that it took three calls*.
**Pattern:** *be annoyed with* [person] &middot; *be annoyed at* / *about* [thing or situation] &middot; *be annoyed by* [the thing doing the annoying] &middot; *be annoyed* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *annoyed with* [person] *for* [verb-ing]
**Variants:** *irritated with/by*, *irked by*, and *put out with* all sit in the same range; *bugged by* is the casual American version.
**Contrast:** **Three prepositions, three roles.** *With* takes the **person** you are annoyed at; *about* or *at* takes the **situation**; *by* takes the **cause acting on you** and is the passive-flavored one — *annoyed by the noise* treats the noise as the agent, while *annoyed about the noise* treats it as the topic of your complaint. Use *for* + *-ing* to name what the person did: *annoyed with him for forgetting*. ✗ *annoyed of* is an error.

Mildly angry — the low end of the same scale that runs up through *angry* to *furious*.

1. I'm *annoyed with* my landlord for ignoring three emails.
2. She looked *annoyed at* the interruption but waved him in.
3. Passengers were *annoyed by* the constant announcements.
4. He's *annoyed about* having to redo the entire form.
5. Try not to get *annoyed with* her; she's had a week of this.

**See also:** [angry with](#angry-with), [upset about](#upset-about), [fed up with](#fed-up-with), [patient with](#patient-with), [by](02-core-relational.md#by)

### answerable to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈænsərəbəl tu/ &middot; *AN-sur-uh-bul too* &middot; weak /ˈænsərəbəl tə/ — and note the silent *w*, as in the verb
**Русский:** подотчётный кому-л., обязанный отчитываться за что-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal, with a British tilt; American English usually reaches for *accountable to* or the plain *reports to*, and keeps *answerable* for rhetorical weight (*answerable to no one*)
**Complement:** after *to*, a noun or pronoun naming the person or body owed the explanation — **never a gerund**; after *for*, a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*answerable for having signed it*), never a to-infinitive.
**Pattern:** *be answerable to* [person / body] &middot; *be answerable for* [thing / verb-ing] &middot; *answerable to* [person] *for* [thing] &middot; *answerable to no one* — the fixed rhetorical frame
**Variants:** *accountable to* / *accountable for* is the ordinary American equivalent and splits identically. *Responsible to* / *responsible for* is the everyday pair. The verb *answer* itself takes the same two prepositions in the same two senses, with no adjective in between.
**Contrast:** The split matches [accountable to, accountable for](#accountable-to-accountable-for) exactly: *to* takes the one who may demand an answer, *for* takes the matter. What separates the two adjectives is tone — *answerable* is the older and more moralized word, at home in *answerable to history* and *answerable to the voters*, while *accountable* is the neutral organizational term. Note the trap of the other *answerable*, meaning "able to be answered" (*an answerable charge*), which takes no preposition at all. See also the zero-preposition verb entry at [answer](#answer): you *answer to* a supervisor but *answer* a question, with nothing in between.

Obliged to give an account of yourself to someone, or for something.

1. The inspector general is *answerable to* Congress, not to the agency she audits.
2. Elected officials are *answerable to* the people who put them there.
3. He behaved as if he were *answerable to* nobody at all.
4. The contractor is *answerable for* every subcontractor on the site.
5. She is *answerable to* the trustees *for* how the endowment is invested.

**See also:** [accountable to, accountable for](#accountable-to-accountable-for), [responsible for](#responsible-for), [liable for, liable to](#liable-for-liable-to), [answer](#answer), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### antecedent to

**Pronunciation:** /ˌæntəˈsidənt tu/ &middot; *an-tuh-SEE-dunt too* &middot; weak /ˌæntəˈsidənt tə/
**Русский:** предшествующий чему-л. — во времени или в порядке рассуждения; книжн.
**Type:** adjective + preposition, often working as a complex preposition &middot; **Register:** formal and dated — nineteenth-century prose, older judicial opinions, philosophy; an American meets it in quoted authority, not in speech, where the words are *before* and *prior to*
**Complement:** a noun phrase, or a **gerund** in careful writing (*antecedent to filing suit*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *antecedent to file suit*) and never a clause.
**Pattern:** [noun] *antecedent to* [event] &middot; *be antecedent to* [noun] &middot; *logically* / *temporally antecedent to* &middot; *the antecedent* — the noun, in grammar the word a pronoun points back to
**Variants:** *prior to* is the living formal equivalent, *previous to* the slightly stiffer one, *anterior to* the exact synonym in the same dated register, and *before* the plain word that beats all of them.
**Contrast:** ***Antecedent to* covers both coming earlier in time and coming first in logic***, and the second is what keeps it alive in philosophical writing: *a duty antecedent to any promise* is not merely earlier, it is more basic. Against [previous to](#previous-to) and *prior to*, which are purely temporal, that logical use is the only reason to prefer it. Do not confuse the adjective with the grammatical noun *antecedent*, which takes *of* (*the antecedent of "which"*). For the plain time prepositions see [time](05-time.md).

Coming before something else, either in time or in the order of reasoning.

1. Every condition *antecedent to* closing had been satisfied by Friday.
2. The right of way was *antecedent to* the subdivision and survived it.
3. Hume's question is whether any idea is *antecedent to* experience.
4. Facts *antecedent to* the merger are outside the scope of the audit.
5. A duty of care *antecedent to* the contract may still be owed.

**See also:** [anterior to, posterior to](#anterior-to-posterior-to), [previous to](#previous-to), [preparatory to](#preparatory-to), [subject to](#subject-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### anterior to, posterior to

**Pronunciation:** /ænˈtɪriər tu, pɑˈstɪriər tu/ &middot; *an-TEER-ee-ur too, pah-STEER-ee-ur too* &middot; weak /ænˈtɪriər tə/ and /pɑˈstɪriər tə/
**Русский:** расположенный спереди / сзади от — анат., относительно другой структуры
**Type:** paired adjectives + preposition &middot; **Register:** technical — anatomy, radiology, and dentistry, where they are the everyday professional words; plus an archaic formal use for time, found in old legal and scholarly prose and nowhere else
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming a body structure, or, in the dated temporal use, a date or an event — **never a gerund** and never a clause. Both words also stand attributively with no complement (*the anterior wall*, *the posterior fossa*), which is the plain adjective.
**Pattern:** [structure] *anterior to* [structure] &middot; [structure] *posterior to* [structure] &middot; *just* / *immediately anterior to* &middot; *anteroposterior* — the compound, as in an AP film
**Variants:** *in front of* and *behind* are the plain equivalents; *ventral to* and *dorsal to* are the comparative-anatomy pair, which for a four-legged animal is not the same axis; *prior to* and *subsequent to* replace the dated temporal use.
**Contrast:** **In American clinical use these are strictly spatial, and the temporal sense is dead outside quotation.** Write *the incision is anterior to the ear*, but not ✗ *anterior to the surgery* for "before it" unless you are imitating older prose. The pair is also relative, not absolute: a structure is anterior *to* something, so the object is obligatory — *the anterior surface* alone is the attributive adjective, not this pattern. In dentistry the plural nouns *anteriors* and *posteriors* name the front and back teeth and take no preposition.

Situated toward the front of the body, or toward the back of it, relative to some other structure.

1. The sternum is *anterior to* the heart.
2. The kidneys lie *posterior to* the intestines, against the back wall.
3. The lesion sits just *anterior to* the ear canal.
4. The esophagus runs *posterior to* the trachea.
5. In the older reports, any conveyance *anterior to* the mortgage took priority — the archaic temporal use.

**See also:** [antecedent to](#antecedent-to), [proximal to, distal to](#proximal-to-distal-to), [adjacent to](#adjacent-to), [previous to](#previous-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### anxious about

**Pronunciation:** /ˈæŋkʃəs əˈbaʊt/ &middot; *ANK-shuss uh-BOWT* &middot; weak /ˈæŋkʃəs əˈbaʊt/, casually /ˈæŋkʃəs ˈbaʊt/
**Русский:** тревожащийся о чём-л.; с инфинитивом (anxious to) — страстно желающий
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; slightly more formal than *nervous about*
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *about* (*anxious about flying*) — **never a to-infinitive after *about***. The adjective separately takes a bare to-infinitive (*anxious to help*), *for* + noun (*anxious for news*), and a *that*-clause (*anxious that nothing goes wrong*, with an optional subjunctive in formal writing).
**Pattern:** *be anxious about* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be anxious to* [verb] &middot; *be anxious for* [noun] &middot; *be anxious* (*that*) [clause]
**Variants:** *nervous about* and *worried about* are the everyday equivalents; *apprehensive about* is the formal one.
**Contrast:** ***Anxious about* is worry; *anxious to* is eagerness.** *Anxious about starting* means dreading it; *anxious to start* means impatient to begin — the same adjective, opposite emotional loads, decided entirely by the preposition versus the infinitive. Purists once objected to the eager sense altogether, but it is standard American English now. *Anxious for* splits the difference: *anxious for the results* is wanting them badly, usually with an edge of worry.

Uneasy about something ahead — and, in the infinitive frame, keen for it to happen.

1. He's *anxious about* the biopsy results.
2. She was *anxious about* moving so far from her sister.
3. Parents are *anxious about* the new attendance policy.
4. I'm *anxious to* get started before the weather turns.
5. Everyone in the waiting room was *anxious for* news.

**See also:** [nervous about](#nervous-about), [worried about](#worried-about), [eager for](#eager-for), [afraid of](#afraid-of), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about)

### apprised of

**Pronunciation:** /əˈpraɪzd ʌv/ &middot; *uh-PRIZED uv* &middot; weak /əˈpraɪzd əv/, and before a consonant often /əˈpraɪzd ə/
**Русский:** поставленный в известность о — офиц.
**Type:** adjective (past participle) + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — police reports, corporate memoranda, and court filings, where *keep the court apprised* is a fixed phrase; conversation says *told* or *kept posted*
**Complement:** a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*apprised of being under investigation*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *apprised of to change the date*). A *that*-clause attaches to the verb, not to this frame: *the clerk apprised him that the hearing had moved*, but ✗ *apprised of that the hearing had moved*.
**Pattern:** *be apprised of* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *keep* [someone] *apprised of* [noun] &middot; *apprise* [someone] *of* [noun] — the active verb &middot; *fully* / *duly* / *promptly apprised of*
**Variants:** *informed of* and *notified of* are the plain equivalents and take the same *of*; *advised of* is the police and banking version; *made aware of* is the everyday paraphrase.
**Contrast:** ***Apprised* is not *appraised*.*** To apprise is to inform; to appraise is to value or assess, and it takes a direct object with no preposition: ✓ *the jeweler appraised the ring*, ✗ *I was appraised of the situation*. This misspelling is common enough in American business writing to be worth a deliberate check. Against [aware of](#aware-of), which reports a state of knowledge however acquired, *apprised of* reports that somebody actively told you.

Informed of something by someone whose job it was to tell you.

1. Keep me *apprised of* any change in her condition.
2. The board was never *apprised of* the second offer.
3. Counsel will keep the court *apprised of* the settlement talks.
4. Residents were *apprised of* the shutoff two days in advance.
5. He claims he was not *apprised of* being named in the complaint.

**See also:** [aware of](#aware-of), [cognizant of](#cognizant-of), [conscious of](#conscious-of), [mindful of](#mindful-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### appurtenant to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈpɜrtənənt tu/ &middot; *uh-PUR-tuh-nunt too* &middot; weak /əˈpɜrtənənt tə/
**Русский:** принадлежащий земельному участку — юр., переходит вместе с землёй
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal, and narrowly so — real-property law, deeds, title reports, and condominium declarations; outside a closing binder an American will never meet it
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming land, an estate, or a right attached to land — **never a gerund**, never a clause. The word is also used attributively in the frozen term *an easement appurtenant*, where the noun comes first and no complement follows.
**Pattern:** *an easement appurtenant to* [parcel] &middot; [right] *appurtenant to* [estate] &middot; *rights and privileges appurtenant to* [noun] &middot; *the appurtenances* — the noun, in the deed formula *together with all appurtenances*
**Variants:** *incident to* is the broader legal synonym; *attached to* and *belonging to* are the plain-English versions; *pertaining to* covers the looser sense of "having to do with."
**Contrast:** **The point of the word is that the right travels with the land.** An easement *appurtenant to* a parcel passes automatically to the next owner; an *easement in gross* belongs to a person and does not. Against [incident to](#incident-to), which is general, *appurtenant to* is specific to real property. Do not confuse it with *pertinent to*, which means relevant: ✗ *the question is appurtenant to the issue* is not English. ✗ *appurtenant of* and ✗ *appurtenant with* are errors.

Attached by law to a piece of land, so that whoever takes the land takes the right along with it.

1. The driveway easement is *appurtenant to* the back parcel and passes with the deed.
2. Parking space 14 is *appurtenant to* the unit, not separately salable.
3. Water rights *appurtenant to* the ranch were valued separately in the estate.
4. The declaration lists every right *appurtenant to* ownership of a unit.
5. A dock permit *appurtenant to* the lot cannot be transferred to a neighbor.

**See also:** [incident to](#incident-to), [ancillary to](#ancillary-to), [incidental to](#incidental-to), [pertinent to](#pertinent-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### ashamed of

**Pronunciation:** /əˈʃeɪmd ʌv/ &middot; *uh-SHAYMD uv* &middot; weak /əˈʃeɪmd əv/
**Русский:** стыдящийся чего-л. — «мне стыдно за»
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*ashamed of lying*) — **never a to-infinitive after *of***. The adjective also takes a bare to-infinitive (*ashamed to admit it*) and a *that*-clause (*ashamed that he'd said nothing*).
**Pattern:** *be ashamed of* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be ashamed of* [someone] *for* [verb-ing] &middot; *be ashamed to* [verb] &middot; *be ashamed* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *ashamed of oneself*
**Variants:** *embarrassed about* / *by* covers the mild social version; *ashamed* is the moral one. *Mortified by* is the intensified form.
**Contrast:** ***Ashamed of doing* versus *ashamed to do*** — the first looks back at something already done, the second hesitates before doing it. *I'm ashamed of asking* means the asking happened and I regret it; *I'm ashamed to ask* means I have not asked and would rather not. ✗ *ashamed about* is not idiomatic for the moral sense — that is *embarrassed about*'s territory — and ✗ *ashamed for* works only when someone else's conduct is meant (*I'm ashamed for you*, feeling shame on your behalf).

Feeling guilt or dishonor about something you have done, or about someone connected to you.

1. She was *ashamed of* the way she'd spoken to the clerk.
2. Nobody should be *ashamed of* needing help.
3. He was *ashamed of* himself before the front door closed.
4. I'm *ashamed to* say I never opened the envelope.
5. They were *ashamed of* their father for the first time in their lives.

**See also:** [guilty of](#guilty-of), [sorry for](#sorry-for), [proud of](#proud-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### astonished at

**Pronunciation:** /əˈstɑnɪʃt æt/ &middot; *uh-STON-isht at* &middot; weak /əˈstɑnɪʃt ət/
**Русский:** поражённый, изумлённый чем-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to literary; stronger and rarer than *surprised*
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *at* and *by* (*astonished at finding the door open*) — **never a to-infinitive after either.** A bare to-infinitive attaches to the adjective directly (*astonished to learn*), as does a *that*-clause (*astonished that anyone showed up*).
**Pattern:** *be astonished at* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be astonished by* [noun] &middot; *be astonished to* [verb] &middot; *be astonished* (*that*) [clause]
**Variants:** *astonished at* and *astonished by* are both standard; *at* leans toward the reaction, *by* toward the cause. *Amazed at/by*, *astounded by*, and *stunned by* are the near-neighbors.
**Contrast:** Same split as *surprised* and *shocked*: ***at* frames the thing as what you are reacting to; *by* frames it as what acted on you** — *astonished at the price* is your reaction to a number, *astonished by the news* is the news doing something to you. With a verb, the two available frames mean different things: *astonished at hearing it* reports the moment of hearing, *astonished to hear it* reports the reaction on hearing. ✗ *astonished of* and ✗ *astonished from* are errors.

Struck by something so unexpected that it briefly stops you.

1. I was *astonished at* how quickly the line moved.
2. She was *astonished at* the size of the bill.
3. Visitors are always *astonished by* the acoustics in that room.
4. He was *astonished to* find his name on the list.
5. They were *astonished at* finding the cabin exactly as they'd left it.

**See also:** [surprised at](#surprised-at), [shocked at](#shocked-at), [impressed with](#impressed-with), [at](01-core-in-on-at.md#at)

### available to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈveɪləbəl tu/ &middot; *uh-VAY-luh-bull too* &middot; weak /əˈveɪləbəl tə/
**Русский:** доступный кому-л. — который можно получить, использовать или к кому можно обратиться
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or pronoun naming a **person or group** after *to*; noun phrase or **gerund** after *for* (*available for hire*, *available for booking*) — **never a gerund after *to***. The adjective also takes a bare to-infinitive meaning "free to" (*available to talk at four*), which is a different frame from *available to* + person.
**Pattern:** *be available to* [person] &middot; *be available for* [purpose / verb-ing] &middot; *be available to* [verb] &middot; *make* [something] *available to* [person] &middot; *available from* [source] &middot; *available at* [place]
**Variants:** *open to* covers the "not restricted" sense; *accessible to* the "reachable" one.
**Contrast:** ***Available to* names who can get it; *available for* names what it is for.** *The report is available to members* (who may have it) versus *The room is available for meetings* (what it may be used for). The infinitive frame is a third thing entirely: *She's available to answer questions* is about her free time, not about who has access to her. ✗ *available with* is an error, though *available in* is correct for formats and sizes (*available in blue*).

Able to be had, used, reached, or spoken to.

1. Transcripts are *available to* any student who requests one.
2. The gym is *available for* private events on Sundays.
3. Dr. Alvarez is *available to* meet on Thursday morning.
4. That grant is only *available to* first-time applicants.
5. Two of the three units are still *available for* rent.

**See also:** [useful for](#useful-for), [prepared for](#prepared-for), [suitable for](#suitable-for), [to](02-core-relational.md#to), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### aware of

**Pronunciation:** /əˈwɛr ʌv/ &middot; *uh-WAIR uv* &middot; weak /əˈwɛr əv/
**Русский:** осознающий что-л., знающий о — «в курсе»
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, **gerund** (*aware of being watched*), or a *wh*-clause (*aware of what it would cost*) — **never a to-infinitive.** A *that*-clause attaches to the bare adjective with no *of*: *aware that the deadline had moved*, and ✗ *aware of that the deadline had moved* is an error.
**Pattern:** *be aware of* [noun / verb-ing / wh-clause] &middot; *be aware* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *become* / *make* [someone] *aware of* [noun] &middot; *acutely* / *dimly* / *painfully aware of* [noun]
**Variants:** *conscious of* is the close synonym and behaves identically; *cognizant of* is the bureaucratic version.
**Contrast:** **The *of* drops before a *that*-clause.** This is the single error worth watching: ✓ *aware of the problem*, ✓ *aware that there's a problem*, ✗ *aware of that there's a problem*. Against *conscious of*, which leans toward felt, bodily awareness (*conscious of a draft*), *aware of* leans toward knowledge. And note *aware* has no comparative with *more aware of* as a fixed pattern — it takes degree adverbs instead: *fully*, *keenly*, *vaguely aware of*.

Knowing that something exists or is the case.

1. Were you *aware of* the change in the return policy?
2. She's *aware of* how it looks and doesn't care.
3. He became *aware of* someone standing behind him.
4. The board was fully *aware* that the numbers wouldn't hold.
5. I'm painfully *aware of* having missed the obvious answer.

**See also:** [conscious of](#conscious-of), [familiar with](#familiar-with), [sensitive to](#sensitive-to), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### bad at

**Pronunciation:** /bæd æt/ &middot; *BAD at* &middot; weak /bæd ət/
**Русский:** неумелый в чём-л. — bad at неумелый, bad for вредный, bad with не умеющий обращаться с
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *at* (*bad at remembering names*) — **never a to-infinitive.** ✗ *bad at remember names* and ✗ *bad at to remember* are both wrong. After *for* the complement is a person or thing affected, and a gerund is possible (*bad for keeping food fresh* is odd; *bad for your teeth* is the norm).
**Pattern:** *be bad at* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be bad for* [person / thing] &middot; *be bad with* [category of thing one handles badly] &middot; *be bad about* [verb-ing] (habitually negligent)
**Variants:** *poor at* is the formal version, *terrible at* and *awful at* the emphatic ones, *hopeless at* and *useless at* the self-deprecating ones, *slow at* the specific one. All take *at*.
**Contrast:** **Three prepositions, three meanings, and they are not interchangeable.** *Bad at math* = unskilled. *Bad for you* = harmful. *Bad with money* = unable to handle a whole category well — *with* is the one used for people, animals, money, and tools (*bad with kids*, *bad with numbers*). A fourth frame, *bad about* + *-ing*, means chronically failing to do something: *I'm bad about returning calls*. ✗ *bad in math* is an error in American English, though *bad in bed* and *bad in a crisis* are fixed idioms where *in* names a situation.

Not good at something, or harmful to something — the split depends entirely on the preposition.

1. I'm hopelessly *bad at* spelling anyone's name right the first time.
2. Standing all day is *bad for* your back.
3. He's *bad with* directions and always has been.
4. She's *bad about* answering email between semesters.
5. Our whole family is *bad at* small talk.

**See also:** [good at](#good-at), [poor at](#poor-at), [terrible at](#terrible-at), [hopeless at](#hopeless-at), [harmful to](#harmful-to), [at](01-core-in-on-at.md#at)

### based on

**Pronunciation:** /beɪst ɑn/ &middot; *BAYST on* &middot; weak /beɪst ən/
**Русский:** основанный на — на источнике, фактах или принципе
**Type:** participial adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*based on assuming the worst*) — **never a to-infinitive** and **never a *that*-clause.** ✗ *based on that the market recovers* must be *based on the assumption that the market recovers*.
**Pattern:** *be based on* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; [noun] *based on* [noun] &middot; *base* [something] *on* [something] &middot; *based on* [noun], [clause] — the dangling sentence-adverbial use
**Variants:** *based upon* is the formal equivalent, common in contracts and older prose. *Based in* is a **different word combination** meaning headquartered, not derived: *a firm based in Denver*.
**Contrast:** ***Based on* versus *based in*** is the trap: *The movie is based on a true story* (derived from) versus *The company is based in Seattle* (located at). ✗ *based off of* is widespread in American speech and still nonstandard in writing; editors change it to *based on*. The floating sentence-opener *Based on the survey, we cut the budget* is criticized as a dangling participle — the budget is not based on the survey, the decision is — and careful writing prefers *On the basis of* or *Judging by*. See [on the basis of](11-complex-multiword.md#on-the-basis-of).

Built or derived from a source, a set of facts, or a principle.

1. The screenplay is *based on* a magazine article from 1974.
2. Rates are *based on* your driving record, not your zip code.
3. She made the call *based on* twenty years of doing this.
4. The whole argument is *based on* assuming that nobody moves.
5. Their pricing is *based upon* volume, tier by tier.

**See also:** [dependent on](#dependent-on), [related to](#related-to), [subject to](#subject-to), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### beholden to

**Pronunciation:** /bɪˈhoʊldən tu/ &middot; *bih-HOHL-dun too* &middot; weak /bəˈhoʊldən tə/ — the *d* is usually swallowed into the *n*, giving "bih-HOHL-un-tuh"
**Русский:** обязанный кому-л. за услугу и связанный этим — книжн.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to literary, and a standing word in American political journalism (*beholden to donors*); it survives almost nowhere else
**Complement:** a noun or pronoun naming the person, group, or interest you owe — **never a gerund** (✗ *beholden to giving*) and never an infinitive. A second slot with *for* names the debt: *beholden to her for the introduction*.
**Pattern:** *be beholden to* [person / group] &middot; *beholden to* [person] *for* [favor] &middot; *beholden to no one* — by far the commonest frame &middot; *feel* / *remain beholden to*
**Variants:** *indebted to* is the neutral equivalent and takes *for* the same way; *obligated to* covers the duty; *in someone's debt* is the everyday paraphrase; *under obligation to* is the formal noun frame.
**Contrast:** ***Beholden to* is about owing a favor that constrains you***, which is why it is nearly always negative in American use: to say a senator is *beholden to* an industry is to say the industry owns the vote. Against [obligated to, obliged to](#obligated-to-obliged-to), which names a duty that may be perfectly honorable, *beholden to* names a dependence somebody would rather not admit. Note that the word is archaic outside this frame — *beholden* is not a participle of *behold* in modern English, whatever it looks like. ✗ *beholden of* and ✗ *beholden by* are errors.

Under an obligation to someone who did you a favor, and constrained by it.

1. He ran without taking corporate money so he'd be *beholden to* nobody.
2. The board is *beholden to* two donors who between them fund the building.
3. I don't want to be *beholden to* my brother-in-law for a job.
4. Editors who are *beholden to* advertisers stop printing certain stories.
5. She remained *beholden to* the professor who had vouched for her.

**See also:** [obligated to, obliged to](#obligated-to-obliged-to), [grateful to](#grateful-to), [answerable to](#answerable-to), [dependent on](#dependent-on), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### binding on, binding upon

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbaɪndɪŋ ɑn, ˈbaɪndɪŋ əˈpɑn/ &middot; *BYNE-ding on, BYNE-ding uh-PON* &middot; weak /ˈbaɪndɪŋ ən/ and /ˈbaɪndɪŋ əˈpɒn/
**Русский:** обязательный для кого-л. — юр., имеющий обязательную силу
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal and formal; the natural home is a contract, an arbitration clause, or an appellate opinion, and *binding upon* is the drafter's variant of the same phrase
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the **person or entity bound** — a party, a successor, an agency, a court — and **never a gerund** (✗ *binding on signing*) and never a clause. What the obligation *is* does not go in this slot; that goes in the sentence around it.
**Pattern:** *be binding on* [party] &middot; *binding upon* [party] *and* [party's successors] — the standard drafting formula &middot; *legally* / *contractually binding on* &middot; *a binding agreement* — attributive, no complement
**Variants:** *binding upon* is stylistically identical and commoner in drafted documents; *enforceable against* is the sharper term for the same idea; *bind* as a verb takes a direct object with no preposition (*the agreement binds the parties*).
**Contrast:** ***Binding on* takes the person bound, while [bound by](#bound-by) takes the thing that binds*** — the same fact from the two ends: *the arbitration clause is binding on the parties* = *the parties are bound by the arbitration clause*. Do not confuse either with the temporal *upon*: *binding upon execution* means "binding as soon as it is signed," and there the *upon* is a time preposition with a different job. ✗ *binding to* and ✗ *binding for* are errors in this sense.

Having legal force against someone, so that they cannot walk away from it.

1. The settlement is *binding on* both parties and their successors.
2. A ruling from the Ninth Circuit is *binding on* district courts in that circuit.
3. The letter of intent is *binding upon* the seller only as to confidentiality.
4. Arbitration under this section is final and *binding on* the employer.
5. Nothing said in mediation is *binding on* anyone unless it is written down.

**See also:** [bound by](#bound-by), [enforceable against](#enforceable-against), [subject to](#subject-to), [incumbent on, incumbent upon](#incumbent-on-incumbent-upon), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### bored with

**Pronunciation:** /bɔrd wɪθ/ &middot; *BORD with* &middot; weak /bɔrd wəθ/
**Русский:** которому скучно от чего-л., наскучивший
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*bored with waiting*) — **never a to-infinitive.** *Bored to* exists only in the fixed hyperboles *bored to death* and *bored to tears*, which are not this pattern.
**Pattern:** *be bored with* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be bored by* [the thing that bores you] &middot; *get bored with* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *bored out of one's mind*
**Variants:** *bored with* and *bored by* are both standard American English. ***Bored of*** is now extremely common in speech on both sides of the Atlantic and still marked in careful American writing — it began as a British colloquialism, modeled on *tired of*, and editors change it to *with*. If you want a safe choice, use *with*.
**Contrast:** ***With* takes the thing you have had enough of; *by* takes the thing actively boring you** — *bored with the job* is your state, *bored by his lecture* points at the cause, exactly as *annoyed with* / *annoyed by* divides. Against *tired of*, which can mean either weary or fed up, *bored with* is specifically about lack of interest, and against *fed up with*, which carries irritation, *bored with* carries only flatness.

Tired of something because it holds no interest.

1. The kids got *bored with* the museum after twenty minutes.
2. She's *bored with* explaining the same policy every August.
3. I was *bored by* the first hour and asleep by the second.
4. He gets *bored with* any project once the hard part is over.
5. Nobody is *bored with* the paycheck, just with the work.

**See also:** [tired of](#tired-of), [fed up with](#fed-up-with), [sick of](#sick-of), [interested in](#interested-in), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### bound by

**Pronunciation:** /baʊnd baɪ/ &middot; *BOWND by* &middot; weak /baʊnd bə/, and the *d* usually drops before a consonant: "BOWN-buh"
**Русский:** связанный чем-л. — правилом, обещанием, документом
**Type:** adjective (past participle) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; ordinary in contracts, oaths, and journalism, and common in everyday speech about rules
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the rule, promise, or document that constrains — **never a gerund** (✗ *bound by keeping quiet*) and never a to-infinitive. The other *bound* patterns take entirely different complements and are listed under Contrast.
**Pattern:** *be bound by* [contract / rule / oath] &middot; *feel bound by* [noun] &middot; *legally* / *morally* / *contractually bound by* &middot; *bound by law to* [verb] — the two frames stacked
**Variants:** *subject to* covers the same ground more loosely; *governed by* is the neutral drafting word; *tied to* is the informal one; *bound to* + noun survives in *bound to secrecy* and *bound to an apprenticeship*.
**Contrast:** **Three different *bounds* live behind this word, and only one of them is this entry.** *Bound by* + noun means constrained by a rule (*bound by the NDA*). *Bound to* + **bare verb** means certain to happen and has nothing to do with obligation (*it's bound to rain*) — the *to* there is an infinitive marker, not a preposition. *Bound for* + place names a destination (*bound for Denver*), a use catalogued in [participial prepositions](12-marginal-participial.md). Keeping the three apart is worth the trouble, because ✗ *bound by to rain* and ✗ *bound to the NDA* are both wrong.

Legally or morally tied to a rule, a promise, or a document, and unable to disregard it.

1. Everyone who signs is *bound by* the confidentiality clause for five years.
2. The court is *bound by* precedent it may privately dislike.
3. I'm *bound by* what I told them last week, whether or not it was smart.
4. Employees are *bound by* the handbook only where it tracks the contract.
5. She didn't feel *bound by* an agreement she'd never seen.

**See also:** [binding on, binding upon](#binding-on-binding-upon), [subject to](#subject-to), [obligated to, obliged to](#obligated-to-obliged-to), [committed to](#committed-to), [by](02-core-relational.md#by)

### busy with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbɪzi wɪθ/ &middot; *BIZ-ee with* &middot; weak /ˈbɪzi wəθ/ — note the spelling trap: *busy* is pronounced with /ɪ/, not the /u/ the *u* suggests
**Русский:** занятый чем-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral and everyday
**Complement:** a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*busy with getting the truck loaded*) — **never a to-infinitive** after *with*: ✗ *busy with to load the truck*. The adjective's other frame takes a bare *-ing* with **no preposition at all** (*busy loading the truck*), which is the commoner construction in speech.
**Pattern:** *be busy with* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be busy* [verb-ing] — no preposition &middot; *keep* [someone] *busy with* [noun] &middot; *too busy for* [noun] &middot; *too busy to* [verb] — infinitive after *too*
**Variants:** *occupied with* and *tied up with* are the near equivalents; *swamped with* and *buried in* are the informal intensifiers; *engaged in* is the formal one.
**Contrast:** ***Busy with* + noun, *busy* + *-ing* with nothing between, and *too busy to* + infinitive*** are three separate frames, and mixing them produces the standard errors: ✗ *busy with cook dinner*, ✗ *busy to cook dinner*. Note also the difference between *busy with the kids* (they are what occupies you) and *busy for the kids* (which is not English) — the "on behalf of" idea needs a rewrite. ✗ *busy in cooking* is an Indian-English pattern that American readers hear as foreign, though it is centuries old.

Occupied by something that is taking up your time.

1. She's *busy with* the year-end close until the fifteenth.
2. He was *busy with* getting the storm windows up before the rain.
3. The whole crew is *busy with* a rush order and nobody can take the call.
4. Don't bother him — he's *busy* fixing the dishwasher.
5. I'm too *busy* this week *to* take on anything else.

**See also:** [involved in](#involved-in), [engaged to](#engaged-to), [committed to](#committed-to), [content with](#content-with), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### capable of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkeɪpəbəl ʌv/ &middot; *KAY-puh-bull uv* &middot; weak /ˈkeɪpəbəl əv/
**Русский:** способный на что-л., умеющий — после of герундий
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*capable of running the whole shift alone*) — **never a to-infinitive.** ✗ *capable to do it* is one of the most common errors in this file; the correct forms are *capable of doing it* or *able to do it*, and the two adjectives are not interchangeable in structure.
**Pattern:** *be capable of* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *more than capable of* [verb-ing] &middot; *capable of* [noun] (*capable of great cruelty*)
**Variants:** *able to* + infinitive is the everyday equivalent with the opposite structure. *Up to* + *-ing* is the informal one (*up to running it alone*).
**Contrast:** ***Capable of* + *-ing* versus *able to* + verb** is the pair to memorize, because they mean nearly the same thing and take opposite complements: ✓ *capable of finishing*, ✓ *able to finish*, ✗ *capable to finish*, ✗ *able of finishing*. Note also the flavor difference: *capable of* often implies latent capacity — including the capacity to do harm (*capable of murder*) — while *able to* reports plain ability in a situation. *Incapable of* is the negative and behaves identically.

Having the power, skill, or disposition to do something.

1. She's *capable of* running the department without any help.
2. The engine is *capable of* two hundred thousand miles with care.
3. I didn't think he was *capable of* that kind of patience.
4. Anyone is *capable of* making that mistake at four in the morning.
5. The new scanner is *capable of* reading damaged plates.

**See also:** [incapable of](#incapable-of), [expert at](#expert-at), [skilled at](#skilled-at), [good at](#good-at), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### careful with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɛrfəl wɪθ/ &middot; *KAIR-full with* &middot; weak /ˈkɛrfəl wəθ/
**Русский:** осторожный с — with с вещью, about в решении, of остерегаться, to не забыть сделать
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase after *with* and *of*; noun phrase or **gerund** after *about* (*careful about choosing a contractor*) — **never a to-infinitive after any preposition**, though the adjective takes a bare infinitive directly (*careful to lock up*), which is a separate frame and a very common one.
**Pattern:** *be careful with* [thing handled] &middot; *be careful about* [matter / verb-ing] &middot; *be careful of* [hazard] &middot; *be careful to* [verb] &middot; *be careful* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *Careful!* (imperative alone)
**Variants:** *cautious about*, *wary of*, and *particular about* cover neighboring ground; *watch out for* is the informal equivalent of *careful of*.
**Contrast:** **Four frames, four jobs.** *Careful with the glasses* = handle the thing gently. *Careful about what you promise* = exercise judgment in a matter. *Careful of the step* = watch out for a hazard, slightly formal in American English, where *watch out for* or *mind* is commoner. *Careful to sign both copies* = don't forget to do it. The infinitive frame is the one that means "make sure you do"; ✗ *careful of signing* would not carry that meaning at all.

Taking pains — with a thing, about a decision, of a danger, or to do something right.

1. Be *careful with* that box; the bottom is soft.
2. She's *careful about* who she lends money to.
3. *Careful of* the loose railing on the second landing.
4. He was *careful to* copy the whole team on the reply.
5. You have to be *careful with* dogs you don't know.

**See also:** [suspicious of](#suspicious-of), [sensitive to](#sensitive-to), [serious about](#serious-about), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### certain of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsɜrtn ʌv/ &middot; *SUR-tun uv* &middot; weak /ˈsɜrtn əv/
**Русский:** уверенный в чём-л.; с инфинитивом — непременно случится
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; *sure* is the everyday word
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, **gerund** (*certain of winning*), or a *wh*-clause (*certain about what she saw*) — **never a to-infinitive after the preposition.** A *that*-clause attaches to the bare adjective (*certain that he'd locked it*), and a bare to-infinitive attaches directly with a **different meaning** (*certain to happen* = bound to happen).
**Pattern:** *be certain of* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be certain about* [matter] &middot; *be certain* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *be certain to* [verb] &middot; *make certain of* [noun] &middot; *for certain*
**Variants:** *sure of* / *sure about* is the everyday twin and follows every one of these frames. *Positive about* is the emphatic conversational version.
**Contrast:** ***Certain of* versus *certain to*** is a genuine ambiguity trap: *He's certain of promotion* means he feels sure; *He's certain to be promoted* means it is bound to happen and says nothing about his feelings. *Of* leans toward a fact you are confident in (*certain of the date*); *about* leans toward a matter you have considered (*certain about the decision*), and *about* is the one that takes a *wh*-clause comfortably. ✗ *certain for* is an error.

Free of doubt — or, in the infinitive frame, guaranteed to happen.

1. I'm *certain of* the date because it was my mother's birthday.
2. She wasn't *certain about* signing a two-year lease.
3. Make *certain of* the spelling before it goes to print.
4. Rain is *certain to* ruin the Saturday game.
5. Nobody was *certain* that the vote would even be held.

**See also:** [sure of](#sure-of), [confident of](#confident-of), [confused about](#confused-about), [aware of](#aware-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### characteristic of

**Pronunciation:** /ˌkɛrəktəˈrɪstɪk ʌv/ &middot; *kair-uk-tuh-RISS-tik uv* &middot; weak /ˌkɛrəktəˈrɪstɪk əv/
**Русский:** характерный для, свойственный кому-л. или чему-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to neutral; common in academic and critical writing
**Complement:** noun phrase or pronoun, and a **gerund** where the thing described is an action (*characteristic of dismissing critics out of hand* is possible but heavy) — **never a to-infinitive** and **never a clause.**
**Pattern:** *be characteristic of* [person / group / period] &middot; [noun] *characteristic of* [noun] &middot; *it is characteristic of* [someone] *to* [verb]
**Variants:** *typical of* is the everyday equivalent and can be mildly disapproving; *distinctive of* stresses what sets something apart; *peculiar to* means found in that one place only.
**Contrast:** ***Characteristic of* is neutral; *typical of* often is not.** *That's typical of him* usually means he has disappointed you again, while *That's characteristic of him* is a description. Note the frame *It's characteristic of her to volunteer first*, where the infinitive belongs to the *it*-construction and not to the preposition. ✗ *characteristic for* and ✗ *characteristic to* are errors, though *peculiar to* and *unique to* correctly take *to*.

Belonging to the recognizable pattern of a person, group, style, or period.

1. The long opening sentence is *characteristic of* her later essays.
2. Fog like this is *characteristic of* the coast in June.
3. Restraint was *characteristic of* the whole administration.
4. That kind of joinery is *characteristic of* Shaker furniture.
5. It's *characteristic of* him to send a note the next morning.

**See also:** [typical of](#typical-of), [similar to](#similar-to), [known for](#known-for), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### close to
