**Pronunciation:** /kloʊs tu/ &middot; *KLOHSS too* &middot; weak /kloʊs tə/ — note the adjective is /kloʊs/ with an *s*, not the verb /kloʊz/
**Русский:** близкий к — в пространстве, в отношениях, по количеству или во времени
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*close to giving up*) — **never a to-infinitive.** ✗ *close to give up* is the error the noun test catches: *close to the end* ✓, so the *to* is a preposition.
**Pattern:** *be close to* [place / person / amount / verb-ing] &middot; *come close to* [verb-ing] &middot; *close to* [number] &middot; *nowhere close to* [noun / verb-ing]
**Variants:** *near to* is possible but *near* alone is the American default before a place (*near the airport*). *On the verge of* + *-ing* is the formal equivalent of the "about to" sense.
**Contrast:** **Three senses, one preposition.** Physical (*close to the station*), emotional (*close to her grandmother*), and quantitative or imminent (*close to sixty*, *close to quitting*). The last of these competes with *about to*, which takes a bare infinitive: ✓ *close to quitting*, ✓ *about to quit*, ✗ *close to quit*. Note that *close* in the "nearly" sense also works adverbially with no *to* at all (*close on a hundred people*), and that the neighboring spatial preposition [near](03-place-position.md#near) is treated in its own right.

Near — in space, in relationship, in quantity, or in time.

1. Their new place is *close to* the water treatment plant, unfortunately.
2. She's very *close to* her older sister.
3. Repairs came *close to* four thousand dollars.
4. He was *close to* walking out in the middle of the meeting.
5. We're *close to* having enough signatures.

**See also:** [related to](#related-to), [similar to](#similar-to), [ready for](#ready-for), [near](03-place-position.md#near), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### coextensive with

**Pronunciation:** /ˌkoʊɪkˈstɛnsɪv wɪθ/ &middot; *koh-ik-STEN-siv with* &middot; weak /ˌkoʊɪkˈstɛnsɪv wəθ/
**Русский:** совпадающий по объёму с — книжн./юр., покрывающий ровно ту же область
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal and technical — legal opinions, philosophy, and logic, where it is a precise word for "covering exactly the same ground"; never conversational
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the other extent, scope, or set — **never a gerund** and never a clause. The two things compared must be of the same kind: a duty with a duty, a set with a set, a term with a term.
**Pattern:** *be coextensive with* [scope / term / set] &middot; *exactly* / *not quite* / *roughly coextensive with* &middot; *coextensive in scope with* [noun]
**Variants:** *coterminous with* is the near twin and is commoner for periods of time and for boundaries; *congruent with* and *identical to* are the plainer words; *conterminous with* is the geographer's spelling for shared borders.
**Contrast:** ***Coextensive with* says two things cover the same range; [coterminous with](#coterminous-with) says they begin and end together.*** A duty coextensive with a risk is exactly as broad as the risk; a lease coterminous with the loan expires the day the loan does. Against [equivalent to](#equivalent-to), which claims equal value, *coextensive with* claims equal extent and nothing more. ✗ *coextensive to* is an error.

Covering exactly the same range or scope as something else, with no part of either falling outside the other.

1. The insurer's duty to defend is not *coextensive with* its duty to indemnify.
2. The statute's reach is *coextensive with* the commerce power, and no wider.
3. The city's water district is roughly *coextensive with* the county line.
4. The class of people harmed is not *coextensive with* the class who filed.
5. He argued that the moral rule is *coextensive with* the legal one here.

**See also:** [coterminous with](#coterminous-with), [concurrent with](#concurrent-with), [congruent to, congruent with](#congruent-to-congruent-with), [equivalent to](#equivalent-to), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### cognizant of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑɡnəzənt ʌv/ &middot; *KOG-nuh-zunt uv* &middot; weak /ˈkɑɡnəzənt əv/; the older *KON-uh-zunt*, with a silent *g*, still turns up in law
**Русский:** осведомлённый о — книжн./юр.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — memos, opinions, and official statements; in speech it sounds like a person reaching for a bigger word than *aware*
**Complement:** a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*cognizant of being the only one there*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *cognizant of to be there*). A *that*-clause attaches to the bare adjective with no preposition: *cognizant that the deadline had passed* ✓, but ✗ *cognizant of that the deadline had passed*.
**Pattern:** *be cognizant of* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be cognizant* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *fully* / *well* / *painfully cognizant of* &middot; *take cognizance of* [noun] — the noun frame, standard in law
**Variants:** *aware of* is the neutral everyday word; *conscious of* stresses the noticing; *mindful of* adds that you are letting the fact guide you; *apprised of* means somebody told you.
**Contrast:** Against [aware of](#aware-of), which is the plain word, *cognizant of* is a register choice and nothing more — it means the same thing at a higher altitude, and American editors often cut it back. The legal noun phrase *take cognizance of* is different in kind: a court that takes cognizance of a matter assumes jurisdiction over it. ✗ *cognizant about*, ✗ *cognizant to*, and ✗ *cognizant with* are errors.

Aware of something, and aware of it in a way you are prepared to be held to.

1. The committee was fully *cognizant of* the risk when it approved the loan.
2. Officers must be *cognizant of* the policy before they use the device.
3. She was *cognizant of* being the only person in the room who had read it.
4. The opinion is *cognizant of* the burden this puts on small filers.
5. We are *cognizant of* the deadline and are working through the weekend.

**See also:** [aware of](#aware-of), [conscious of](#conscious-of), [mindful of](#mindful-of), [apprised of](#apprised-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### comfortable with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkʌmftərbəl wɪθ/ &middot; *KUMF-tur-bull with* &middot; weak /ˈkʌmftərbəl wəθ/; also four syllables /ˈkʌmfərtəbəl/
**Русский:** спокойно относящийся к чему-л., которого что-л. устраивает
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; heavily used in American workplace English
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*comfortable with saying no*) — **never a to-infinitive after *with***. The adjective separately takes a bare to-infinitive (*comfortable to sit in*, describing the chair, not the sitter), which is a different construction.
**Pattern:** *be comfortable with* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be comfortable* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *feel comfortable with* [noun] &middot; *comfortable in* [a role or setting]
**Variants:** *at ease with* is the formal version; *okay with* and *fine with* are the conversational ones and take the same complements.
**Contrast:** ***Comfortable with* is about the person's ease; *comfortable to* + infinitive is about the object's comfort.** *I'm comfortable with the chair* means I accept it; *The chair is comfortable to sit in* means it feels good. Against *used to*, which reports habituation, *comfortable with* reports acceptance — you can be used to something without being comfortable with it, which is exactly why both phrases exist. ✗ *comfortable of* is an error.

At ease with a thing, an arrangement, or a course of action — willing to accept it.

1. Are you *comfortable with* the numbers as they stand?
2. She's not *comfortable with* speaking in front of the board.
3. I'd be more *comfortable with* a written agreement.
4. He's *comfortable with* silence in a way most people aren't.
5. Nobody was *comfortable with* how fast the vote was scheduled.

**See also:** [used to](#used-to), [accustomed to](#accustomed-to), [content with](#content-with), [familiar with](#familiar-with), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### committed to

**Pronunciation:** /kəˈmɪtɪd tu/ &middot; *kuh-MIT-id too* &middot; weak /kəˈmɪtɪd tə/
**Русский:** приверженный чему-л., связавший себя обязательством — после to герундий
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; institutional in tone when used of organizations
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*committed to reducing emissions*) — **never a to-infinitive.** ✗ *committed to reduce emissions* is among the most frequent errors in corporate and academic English. The noun test settles it: *committed to the reduction* ✓, so *to* is a preposition.
**Pattern:** *be committed to* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *commit* [oneself] *to* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *deeply* / *firmly* / *fully committed to* [noun]
**Variants:** *dedicated to* and *devoted to* are the close synonyms, both with the same gerund requirement. *Pledged to* takes an infinitive instead (*pledged to cut rates*), which is why the two get confused.
**Contrast:** ***Committed to* + *-ing* versus *pledged to* / *vowed to* + infinitive.** Same meaning, opposite structures: ✓ *committed to cutting rates*, ✓ *pledged to cut rates*, ✗ *committed to cut rates*. Note the unrelated sense of the verb *commit* with no preposition (*commit a crime*) and the passive *committed to* meaning institutionalized (*committed to a state hospital*), which uses the same string for something else entirely.

Bound by conviction or promise to a cause, a course of action, or a relationship.

1. The district is *committed to* keeping class sizes under twenty-five.
2. She's *committed to* the neighborhood in a way newcomers rarely are.
3. We are fully *committed to* finishing before the frost.
4. He wasn't ready to be *committed to* anyone.
5. Both parties say they're *committed to* reopening the talks.

**See also:** [devoted to](#devoted-to), [dedicated to](#dedicated-to), [opposed to](#opposed-to), [intent on](#intent-on), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### common to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑmən tu/ &middot; *KOM-un too* &middot; weak /ˈkɑmən tə/
**Русский:** общий для — присущий каждому из названных
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; ordinary in scientific and analytical writing (*a feature common to both groups*)
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming **two or more** things, or a plural or collective noun — *common to both drafts*, *common to mammals*, *common to the whole family*. A **gerund is impossible** here (✗ *common to running*), and so is a clause. The complement must be plural or collective in sense, because the word means "shared by."
**Pattern:** [feature] *common to* [group] &middot; *common to both* / *all* / *every* [noun] &middot; *have* [something] *in common with* [noun] — the noun frame, which switches to *with*
**Variants:** *shared by* is the plain equivalent; *universal to* and *characteristic of* cover neighboring ideas; *in common with* is the phrase you need when the subject is one of the sharers.
**Contrast:** ***Common to* takes the sharers; *in common with* takes the other party.*** A trait *common to* both species is shared by them; *in common with most birds, it molts in fall* puts the subject alongside the group. Against [characteristic of](#characteristic-of), which says a trait is typical of one thing, *common to* says a trait is present in several. Watch the everyday sense of *common* meaning "frequent," which takes no preposition (*a common mistake*) and, in the phrase *common in*, takes a place or population: *the fungus is common in the Southeast* — frequent there, not shared by it.

Shared by two or more things, so that each of them has it.

1. A dislike of paperwork is *common to* every trade I've worked in.
2. The bug is *common to* both the desktop and the phone builds.
3. Sickle cell trait is *common to* populations where malaria was endemic.
4. What's *common to* all four accounts is the missing half hour.
5. Roof access is *common to* the units and maintained by the association.

**See also:** [characteristic of](#characteristic-of), [peculiar to](#peculiar-to), [typical of](#typical-of), [specific to](#specific-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### compatible with

**Pronunciation:** /kəmˈpætəbəl wɪθ/ &middot; *kum-PAT-uh-bull with* &middot; weak /kəmˈpætəbəl wəθ/
**Русский:** совместимый с
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; technical in computing, formal in argument
**Complement:** noun phrase or pronoun, and a **gerund** where an activity is meant (*compatible with running two shifts*) — **never a to-infinitive** and **never a clause.**
**Pattern:** *be compatible with* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; [noun] *compatible with* [noun] &middot; *not compatible with* [noun] &middot; *backward compatible with* [noun]
**Variants:** *consistent with* is the argument-and-evidence version; *suited to* the person-and-role version. *Incompatible with* is the direct negative.
**Contrast:** ***Compatible with* asks whether two things can coexist; *consistent with* asks whether they contradict each other.** A finding is *consistent with* a theory (it does not disprove it); a printer is *compatible with* a laptop (they work together). ✗ *compatible to* is an error, which is worth flagging because *similar to*, *identical to*, and *equal to* all take *to* and pull *compatible* toward it. See [consistent with](09-comparison-opposition.md#consistent-with).

Able to exist or work together without conflict.

1. The new cable isn't *compatible with* anything we already own.
2. That schedule isn't *compatible with* having a family.
3. Their goals turned out to be *compatible with* ours after all.
4. The format is *compatible with* every reader we tested.
5. His account is not *compatible with* the timeline in the report.

**See also:** [consistent with](09-comparison-opposition.md#consistent-with), [suitable for](#suitable-for), [similar to](#similar-to), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### concerned about

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈsɜrnd əˈbaʊt/ &middot; *kun-SURND uh-BOWT* &middot; weak /kənˈsɜrnd əˈbaʊt/, casually /kənˈsɜrnd ˈbaʊt/
**Русский:** обеспокоенный чем-л.; concerned with — занимающийся чем-л., касающийся чего-л.
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *about*, *with*, and *for* (*concerned about losing the contract*) — **never a to-infinitive after any of them.** A *that*-clause attaches to the bare adjective: *concerned that the numbers were revised twice*.
**Pattern:** *be concerned about* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be concerned with* [subject matter] &middot; *be concerned for* [someone's welfare] &middot; *be concerned* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *as far as* [X] *is concerned*
**Variants:** *worried about* is the everyday equivalent of the *about* sense only. *Deal with* and *be about* replace the *with* sense in plainer prose.
**Contrast:** **This is one of the sharpest three-way splits in the file.** ***Concerned about*** = worried. ***Concerned with*** = having to do with, dealing with — no worry at all: *Chapter 4 is concerned with tax policy* means it is about tax policy. ***Concerned for*** = anxious on someone's behalf: *concerned for her safety*. Substituting one for another changes the sentence completely: *a report concerned with air quality* studies it, while *a report concerned about air quality* is alarmed by it. The frozen *as far as I'm concerned* uses yet another sense — "in my judgment."

Worried about something, dealing with something, or anxious on someone's behalf.

1. Residents are *concerned about* the noise from the new terminal.
2. The second half of the book is *concerned with* labor law.
3. She was *concerned for* her father, alone in the house all winter.
4. I'm *concerned about* signing anything before the audit closes.
5. *As far as* the bank *is concerned*, the loan is already closed.

**See also:** [worried about](#worried-about), [anxious about](#anxious-about), [involved in](#involved-in), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about)

### concomitant with

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈkɑmɪtənt wɪθ/ &middot; *kun-KOM-uh-tunt with* &middot; weak /kənˈkɑmətənt wəθ/
**Русский:** сопутствующий чему-л. — книжн.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal and technical — clinical writing above all (*concomitant medications* on any American intake form), plus economics and academic prose
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming what accompanies — **never a gerund** in normal use and never a clause. The word is also common attributively with no complement at all: *concomitant therapy*, *the concomitant rise in costs*.
**Pattern:** [thing] *concomitant with* [thing] &middot; *concomitant* [noun] — attributive &middot; *with the concomitant* [noun] — the fixed prepositional phrase &middot; *concomitantly with* — the adverb
**Variants:** *accompanying* and *attendant on* are the near synonyms; *coincident with* stresses the timing; *along with* is the plain preposition; *simultaneous with* is the neutral technical word.
**Contrast:** ***Concomitant with* claims that two things go together, not that one causes the other*** — which is exactly why medical and economic writing likes it: a concomitant rise in costs accompanies the change without being blamed on it. Against [concurrent with](#concurrent-with), which is strictly about running at the same time, *concomitant with* implies the two belong to one picture. In clinical use the attributive form has nearly swallowed the prepositional one: a chart lists *concomitant medications*, not drugs *concomitant with* the trial drug. ✗ *concomitant to* is occasionally seen and is not the standard choice.

Occurring alongside something else, as part of the same situation.

1. Fatigue is *concomitant with* nearly every drug in this class.
2. The layoffs were *concomitant with* a slump nobody had forecast.
3. Rising rents, *concomitant with* flat wages, pushed the vacancy rate down.
4. List any medication *concomitant with* the study drug on page two.
5. The risks *concomitant with* early release were argued at length.

**See also:** [concurrent with](#concurrent-with), [contemporaneous with](#contemporaneous-with), [coextensive with](#coextensive-with), [incidental to](#incidental-to), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### concurrent with

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈkɜrənt wɪθ/ &middot; *kun-KUR-unt with* &middot; weak /kənˈkɜrənt wəθ/
**Русский:** одновременный с, идущий параллельно по времени
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — sentencing orders, project schedules, and academic calendars; the adverb *concurrently* is the commoner form in speech
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the other event, term, or period — **never a gerund** (✗ *concurrent with filing*, where careful writing puts a noun: *concurrent with the filing*) and never a clause.
**Pattern:** [term / event] *concurrent with* [term / event] &middot; *run concurrent with* [noun] &middot; *concurrently with* — the adverb, which is what most writers actually use &middot; *concurrent jurisdiction* / *a concurring opinion* — attributive relatives
**Variants:** *simultaneous with* is the neutral equivalent; *contemporaneous with* is the historian's and the lawyer's word for documents made at the same time; *coincident with* stresses that the overlap may be accidental; *at the same time as* is the plain phrase.
**Contrast:** ***Concurrent with* means running at the same time; [contemporaneous with](#contemporaneous-with) means made or existing at the same time.*** Sentences run concurrent with one another; a memo is contemporaneous with the meeting it records. Against [coterminous with](#coterminous-with), which requires the same beginning and end, *concurrent with* only requires overlap. In American sentencing the pair to know is *concurrent* versus *consecutive*: concurrent sentences are served together, consecutive ones back to back. ✗ *concurrent to* is an error.

Running at the same time as something else.

1. The two sentences run *concurrent with* each other, so he serves eight years.
2. Demolition is *concurrent with* the utility work, which is why the street is closed.
3. Her fellowship was *concurrent with* the last year of the grant.
4. State and federal courts have jurisdiction *concurrent with* one another here.
5. The audit was *concurrent with* the transition to the new system.

**See also:** [contemporaneous with](#contemporaneous-with), [coterminous with](#coterminous-with), [concomitant with](#concomitant-with), [coextensive with](#coextensive-with), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### conditional on, conditional upon

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈdɪʃənəl ɑn, kənˈdɪʃənəl əˈpɑn/ &middot; *kun-DISH-un-ul on, kun-DISH-un-ul uh-PON* &middot; weak /kənˈdɪʃənəl ən/
**Русский:** обусловленный чем-л. — действует, только если
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to neutral; ordinary in offers, admissions letters, statistics, and contracts, with *upon* the drafter's variant
**Complement:** a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*conditional on passing the physical*) — **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *conditional on pass the physical*, ✗ *conditional on to pass*. A *that*-clause is possible only through a dummy noun: *conditional on the understanding that you resign*.
**Pattern:** *be conditional on* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *conditional upon* [noun] — same phrase, formal spelling &middot; *make* [something] *conditional on* [noun] &middot; *a conditional offer* — attributive &middot; *conditional on* [noun] in statistics: *the probability conditional on X*
**Variants:** *contingent on* and *contingent upon* are the exact equivalents and are commoner in American contracts; *dependent on* covers the same ground in plain English; *subject to* is the loosest of the family.
**Contrast:** *Conditional on* and [contingent on, contingent upon](#contingent-on-contingent-upon) are interchangeable in ordinary use; where they part is flavor — *conditional* points at a stated condition somebody wrote down, *contingent* at an uncertain future event. In statistics *conditional on* is a term of art with no substitute: *the distribution of Y conditional on X* is the distribution once X is known. Against [dependent on](#dependent-on), which can describe an ongoing reliance, *conditional on* describes a gate that has to be passed. ✗ *conditional to* is an error.

Holding only if something else happens or turns out to be true.

1. The offer is *conditional on* a clean background check.
2. Admission is *conditional upon* finishing the two prerequisites this summer.
3. Funding for the second year is *conditional on* hitting the enrollment target.
4. Everything in the estimate is *conditional on* the permit coming through.
5. The forecast is *conditional on* the reservoir staying above sixty percent.

**See also:** [contingent on, contingent upon](#contingent-on-contingent-upon), [dependent on](#dependent-on), [subject to](#subject-to), [reliant on](#reliant-on), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### conducive to

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈdusɪv tu/ &middot; *kun-DOO-siv too* &middot; weak /kənˈdusəv tə/; the British *kun-DYOO-siv* is not used in America
**Русский:** способствующий чему-л. — создающий подходящие условия
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to neutral; common in writing about conditions and environments (*conducive to learning*, *conducive to sleep*)
**Complement:** a noun phrase or a **gerund** — *conducive to learning*, *conducive to getting anything done* — and **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *conducive to learn*, ✗ *conducive to get anything done*. The noun test settles it instantly: *conducive to sleep* ✓, so the *to* is a preposition. Because so many of its complements are *-ing* nouns already, the error hides easily.
**Pattern:** *be conducive to* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *hardly* / *not exactly* / *far from conducive to* &middot; *conditions conducive to* [noun] &middot; *conduce to* [noun] — the rare formal verb
**Variants:** *favorable to* is the neutral equivalent; *good for* is the plain one; *helpful to* and *supportive of* cover parts of the range; *propitious for* is the literary version.
**Contrast:** ***Conducive to* names conditions that make something more likely, not something that causes it*** — a quiet room is conducive to studying, it does not produce studying. The subject is nearly always a setting, a policy, or a state of affairs rather than a person: ✗ *she is conducive to the project* is not English. Against [amenable to](#amenable-to), which is about willingness, *conducive to* is about circumstances. ✗ *conducive for* is a frequent error in American student writing, and ✗ *conducive with* is worse.

Tending to bring something about, by making the conditions right for it.

1. Fluorescent lighting and a broken chair are not *conducive to* concentration.
2. The new schedule is more *conducive to* getting the trucks out on time.
3. Warm, wet weather is *conducive to* mold in these crawlspaces.
4. Nothing about that meeting was *conducive to* an honest answer.
5. A short walk after dinner is *conducive to* sleeping through the night.

**See also:** [amenable to](#amenable-to), [detrimental to](#detrimental-to), [harmful to](#harmful-to), [useful for](#useful-for), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### confident of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑnfɪdənt ʌv/ &middot; *KON-fih-dunt uv* &middot; weak /ˈkɑnfɪdənt əv/
**Русский:** уверенный в исходе; about в вопросе, in в чьих-л. способностях
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *of* and *about* (*confident of winning*, *confident about starting over*) — **never a to-infinitive.** A *that*-clause attaches to the bare adjective (*confident that the funding will come through*), and *confident in* takes the **thing or person trusted**, not the outcome.
**Pattern:** *be confident of* [outcome / verb-ing] &middot; *be confident about* [matter] &middot; *be confident in* [person / method / oneself] &middot; *be confident* (*that*) [clause]
**Variants:** *sure of* and *certain of* cover the same ground more plainly; *optimistic about* is the hedged version.
**Contrast:** ***Confident of the result* is about the outcome; *confident in the team* is about the thing you trust.** You can be confident in a surgeon without being confident of a cure. *About* is the loose everyday option and takes a general matter (*confident about the move*). ✗ *confident on* is an error, and ✗ *confident to do it* should be *confident enough to do it* or *confident about doing it*.

Feeling sure — of an outcome, about a matter, or in someone's ability.

1. The coach is *confident of* a win on Saturday.
2. She's *confident about* going back to school at forty.
3. I'm *confident in* the crew; I'm less *confident in* the forecast.
4. He's *confident of* finishing the draft before the holidays.
5. Investors were *confident that* rates had peaked.

**See also:** [certain of](#certain-of), [sure of](#sure-of), [proud of](#proud-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### confused about

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈfjuzd əˈbaʊt/ &middot; *kun-FYOOZD uh-BOWT* &middot; weak /kənˈfjuzd əˈbaʊt/
**Русский:** не понимающий чего-л., запутавшийся в
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, **gerund** (*confused about being billed twice*), or a *wh*-clause (*confused about what to do next*) after *about*; noun phrase or gerund after *by* — **never a to-infinitive after either.**
**Pattern:** *be confused about* [matter / wh-clause] &middot; *be confused by* [the thing causing confusion] &middot; *get confused about* [noun] &middot; *confuse* [X] *with* [Y]
**Variants:** *unclear about* and *fuzzy on* are the neighbors; *puzzled by* and *baffled by* are the intensified versions and prefer *by*.
**Contrast:** ***About* names the topic of your confusion; *by* names its cause*** — *confused about the schedule* (I don't understand it) versus *confused by the schedule* (it confuses me). Keep both apart from *confuse X with Y*, the verb pattern meaning to mistake one for the other: *I always confuse him with his brother*, where ✗ *confuse him to his brother* is wrong. ✗ *confused of* and ✗ *confused on* are errors, though *fuzzy on the details* is idiomatic.

Unable to understand something, or mixed up about it.

1. Half the class was *confused about* which chapters were on the exam.
2. I'm *confused about* what the second charge is for.
3. Voters were *confused by* the new ballot layout.
4. She got *confused about* the time zones and called at four a.m.
5. Don't *confuse* the deadline *with* the postmark date.

**See also:** [aware of](#aware-of), [certain of](#certain-of), [familiar with](#familiar-with), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about)

### congruent to, congruent with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑŋɡruənt tu, ˈkɑŋɡruənt wɪθ/ &middot; *KONG-groo-unt too, KONG-groo-unt with* &middot; weak /ˈkɑŋɡruənt tə/ and /ˈkɑŋɡruənt wəθ/; the stress-shifted *kun-GROO-unt* is also heard
**Русский:** конгруэнтный (мат.); согласующийся с чем-л. (перен.)
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** technical in the *to* frame (geometry, number theory), formal-neutral in the *with* frame (psychology, management, and ordinary argument)
**Complement:** a noun phrase — a figure, a number, a set of values, a description. A **gerund is possible but rare** in the *with* frame (*congruent with saying one thing and doing another*), and a to-infinitive is **impossible** in either.
**Pattern:** [figure] *is congruent to* [figure] — geometry &middot; *a ≡ b congruent to* [modulus] — number theory, read "congruent to b modulo n" &middot; *be congruent with* [values / account] &middot; *not entirely congruent with* [noun]
**Variants:** *congruent with* is the everyday choice outside mathematics and is what most American writers use for beliefs, values, and accounts. *Consistent with* is the plain equivalent; *consonant with* the formal one; *in keeping with* the idiomatic one.
**Contrast:** **The preposition tracks the field.** Geometry says *triangle ABC is congruent to triangle DEF*, and a math teacher will mark *congruent with* wrong there; outside mathematics, *congruent with* is standard and *congruent to* sounds like a misapplied classroom habit. Against [equivalent to](#equivalent-to), which claims equal value, congruence claims that two things match point for point. Against [consonant with](#consonant-with), which is nearly a synonym in the figurative use, *congruent with* is the commoner word in American management and therapy talk.

Matching another thing exactly — in shape and size, in the mathematical use; in substance, in the figurative one.

1. The two triangles are *congruent to* each other by side-angle-side.
2. Seventeen is *congruent to* two modulo five.
3. Her testimony is not *congruent with* what she wrote in the log.
4. We hire people whose habits are *congruent with* how the shop actually runs.
5. The budget has to be *congruent with* the plan the board approved in March.

**See also:** [equivalent to](#equivalent-to), [consonant with](#consonant-with), [isomorphic to](#isomorphic-to), [compatible with](#compatible-with), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### conscious of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑnʃəs ʌv/ &middot; *KON-shuss uv* &middot; weak /ˈkɑnʃəs əv/
**Русский:** осознающий, замечающий что-л. и держащий это в уме
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*conscious of being stared at*) — **never a to-infinitive.** A *that*-clause attaches to the bare adjective (*conscious that the room had gone quiet*), and ✗ *conscious of that* is an error.
**Pattern:** *be conscious of* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be conscious* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *become conscious of* [noun] &middot; *acutely conscious of* [noun] &middot; [noun]*-conscious* as a suffix: *health-conscious*, *cost-conscious*
**Variants:** *aware of* is the everyday twin and follows the same frames. *Mindful of* adds a note of deliberate attention.
**Contrast:** ***Conscious of* leans toward felt, immediate awareness; *aware of* leans toward knowledge.** You are *conscious of* a draft on your neck and *aware of* a change in the law; either word will pass in most sentences, but the drift is real. The suffixed compounds are a separate and very productive pattern — *safety-conscious*, *brand-conscious* — where no preposition appears at all. ✗ *conscious about* is an error in this sense, though *self-conscious about* is correct and means embarrassed.

Noticing something, or keeping it in mind.

1. He was *conscious of* every squeak the floor made.
2. She's *conscious of* how much the delay is costing them.
3. I became *conscious of* someone watching from the porch.
4. The design is *conscious of* the building's original proportions.
5. She's self-*conscious about* her accent on the phone.

**See also:** [aware of](#aware-of), [sensitive to](#sensitive-to), [careful with](#careful-with), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### consonant with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑnsənənt wɪθ/ &middot; *KON-suh-nunt with* &middot; weak /ˈkɑnsənənt wəθ/
**Русский:** согласующийся с принципом, ценностями — книжн.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal and somewhat literary — judicial opinions, policy writing, and criticism; in speech an American says *in line with* or *consistent with*
**Complement:** a noun phrase, or a **gerund** in careful writing (*consonant with treating everyone alike*) — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *consonant with treat everyone alike*) and never a *that*-clause.
**Pattern:** *be consonant with* [principle / practice] &middot; *wholly* / *entirely* / *not consonant with* &middot; *consonance with* — the noun frame, also with *with*
**Variants:** *consistent with* is the neutral American equivalent and is what most editors will substitute; *in keeping with* is the idiomatic one; *congruent with* and *in harmony with* cover the same ground; *compatible with* is the everyday word.
**Contrast:** The word carries its musical metaphor: things *consonant with* each other sound well together, so it fits principles, traditions, and values better than facts. For a factual match, [compatible with](#compatible-with) or *consistent with* is the better word — *the injuries are consistent with a fall*, not ✗ *consonant with a fall*, which a doctor would never write. Do not confuse the adjective with the noun *consonant* (the speech sound), which takes no preposition. ✗ *consonant to* is an error.

In agreement with a principle, a practice, or a body of values.

1. The ruling is *consonant with* forty years of the court's own precedent.
2. Their treatment of the tenants was hardly *consonant with* the mission statement.
3. Nothing in the proposal is *consonant with* what the charter says about notice.
4. The restoration is *consonant with* the building's original 1911 plans.
5. He wanted a career *consonant with* the way he'd been raised.

**See also:** [compatible with](#compatible-with), [congruent to, congruent with](#congruent-to-congruent-with), [germane to](#germane-to), [repugnant to](#repugnant-to), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### contemporaneous with

**Pronunciation:** /kənˌtɛmpəˈreɪniəs wɪθ/ &middot; *kun-tem-puh-RAY-nee-us with* &middot; weak /kənˌtɛmpəˈreɪniəs wəθ/
**Русский:** одновременный с, относящийся к тому же времени — книжн./юр.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal and technical — evidence law (*contemporaneous notes*), history, and geology; the everyday word is *at the same time as*
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the other event, document, or period — **never a gerund** (careful writing nominalizes: *contemporaneous with the signing*, not ✗ *contemporaneous with signing*) and never a clause.
**Pattern:** [document / event] *contemporaneous with* [event] &middot; *roughly* / *nearly* / *exactly contemporaneous with* &middot; *a contemporaneous record* — attributive &middot; *contemporaneously with* — the adverb
**Variants:** *concurrent with* is the word for things running at the same time; *simultaneous with* is the neutral technical choice; *coeval with* is the literary and geological one; *of the same period as* is the plain paraphrase.
**Contrast:** ***Contemporaneous* is not *contemporary*.*** *Contemporary* means either "of the present day" (*contemporary art*) or "of the same era as," and takes *with* only in the second sense; *contemporaneous with* always means "made or occurring at the same time as" and is the word American lawyers use for notes written while events happened — the whole evidentiary point being that they were not written later. Against [concurrent with](#concurrent-with), which is about duration overlapping, *contemporaneous with* is about the moment of origin. ✗ *contemporaneous to* is an error.

Made or happening at the same time as something else, and valuable precisely for that.

1. Her notes are *contemporaneous with* the call and were never edited.
2. The eruption was *contemporaneous with* the collapse of the settlement.
3. The email is *contemporaneous with* the meeting, so it carries weight.
4. Those deposits are roughly *contemporaneous with* the lower layer at the dig.
5. A memo written a year later is not *contemporaneous with* anything.

**See also:** [concurrent with](#concurrent-with), [concomitant with](#concomitant-with), [coterminous with](#coterminous-with), [antecedent to](#antecedent-to), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### content with

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈtɛnt wɪθ/ &middot; *kun-TENT with* &middot; weak /kənˈtɛnt wəθ/ — the adjective stresses the second syllable, unlike the noun *content* /ˈkɑntɛnt/
**Русский:** довольный тем, что есть, не требующий большего
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to slightly literary
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *with* (*content with staying home*) — **never a to-infinitive after *with***. The adjective also takes a bare to-infinitive directly (*content to wait*), which is the commoner frame of the two.
**Pattern:** *be content with* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be content to* [verb] &middot; *content oneself with* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *perfectly* / *quite content with* [noun]
**Variants:** *satisfied with* is the plainer synonym; *happy with* is the everyday one. *Contented with* is the same word in its fuller adjectival form and sounds more settled.
**Contrast:** ***Content with* + *-ing* and *content to* + verb both exist and mean nearly the same thing** — *content with waiting* and *content to wait* — which makes this one of the few adjectives here where the infinitive is not an error. The difference is slight: *with* frames the situation as something accepted, *to* frames it as something willingly done. *Content oneself with* is the reflexive frame meaning to settle for less than you wanted. ✗ *content of* is an error.

Satisfied with what there is, and not pushing for more.

1. She's *content with* a small garden and no lawn at all.
2. He was *content to* let his brother do the talking.
3. They had to *content themselves with* second place.
4. I'm perfectly *content with* the arrangement as written.
5. Nobody here is *content with* running the same numbers twice.

**See also:** [satisfied with](#satisfied-with), [happy about](#happy-about), [pleased with](#pleased-with), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### contingent on, contingent upon

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈtɪndʒənt ɑn, kənˈtɪndʒənt əˈpɑn/ &middot; *kun-TIN-junt on, kun-TIN-junt uh-PON* &middot; weak /kənˈtɪndʒənt ən/
**Русский:** зависящий от — состоится, только если случится нечто неопределённое
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to neutral; the standard word in American real-estate contracts (*contingent on financing*), employment offers, and grant agreements, with *upon* the drafter's variant
**Complement:** a noun phrase or a **gerund** — *contingent on selling the house* ✓ — and **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *contingent on sell the house*, ✗ *contingent to sell the house*. A clause needs a dummy noun: *contingent on the condition that the appraisal clears*.
**Pattern:** *be contingent on* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *contingent upon* [noun] &middot; *make* [something] *contingent on* [noun] &middot; *a contingency* / *contingent fee* / *contingent liability* — attributive relatives, no preposition
**Variants:** *conditional on* and *conditional upon* are the exact equivalents; *dependent on* is the plain-English version; *subject to* is looser and covers a wider range; *hinges on* is the informal verb.
**Contrast:** ***Contingent on* points at an uncertain future event; [conditional on](#conditional-on-conditional-upon) points at a stated condition.*** In practice American contracts use them interchangeably, and *contingent on* dominates in real estate: an offer contingent on inspection dies if the inspection fails. Against [dependent on](#dependent-on), which describes standing reliance (*dependent on the grant for salary*), *contingent on* describes a single gate. Note the noun *contingency*, which takes no preposition in its ordinary use, and the fee arrangement *on contingency*, where no *of* or *to* is possible.

Happening only if some uncertain thing happens first.

1. The offer is *contingent on* financing and on a satisfactory inspection.
2. Her bonus is *contingent upon* the division clearing its target.
3. Everything after phase two is *contingent on* the grant being renewed.
4. The permit is *contingent on* the traffic study, which nobody has scheduled.
5. Whether we drive is *contingent on* what the pass looks like Saturday.

**See also:** [conditional on, conditional upon](#conditional-on-conditional-upon), [dependent on](#dependent-on), [subject to](#subject-to), [reliant on](#reliant-on), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### conversant with

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈvɜrsənt wɪθ/ &middot; *kun-VUR-sunt with* &middot; weak /kənˈvɜrsənt wəθ/; the older *KON-vur-sunt* is chiefly British
**Русский:** хорошо разбирающийся в — книжн.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — résumés, expert declarations, and job descriptions; a spoken American says *knows his way around*
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming a subject, a body of rules, or a tool — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *conversant with to use the software*). A **gerund** is grammatical but stiff (*conversant with running a mill*); careful writers use a noun instead.
**Pattern:** *be conversant with* [subject / system] &middot; *be conversant in* [language / field] &middot; *thoroughly* / *fully* / *not especially conversant with* &middot; *make oneself conversant with* [noun]
**Variants:** *conversant in* is standard for languages and disciplines — *conversant in Spanish*, *conversant in tax law* — while *conversant with* suits documents, systems, and practices. *Familiar with* is the neutral everyday equivalent; *versed in* and *well versed in* are the formal ones; *fluent in* belongs to languages only.
**Contrast:** ***With* for a thing you have worked with, *in* for a field you have studied.*** Both are correct English, and the choice is idiomatic rather than logical: ✓ *conversant with the filing system*, ✓ *conversant in French*. Against [acquainted with](#acquainted-with), which reports an introduction, *conversant with* claims working knowledge — enough to hold up your end of a conversation, which is where the word comes from. Note that *conversant* has nothing to do with the verb *converse* in modern use. ✗ *conversant about* and ✗ *conversant to* are errors.

Knowing a subject or a system well enough to work in it without help.

1. Applicants must be *conversant with* federal procurement rules.
2. She's *conversant with* every payroll system this company has ever run.
3. The witness was not *conversant with* the actual conditions on the floor.
4. He's *conversant in* Portuguese and can handle the Lisbon calls.
5. Get *conversant with* the new codes before the inspection.

**See also:** [familiar with](#familiar-with), [acquainted with](#acquainted-with), [expert at](#expert-at), [skilled at](#skilled-at), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### coterminous with

**Pronunciation:** /koʊˈtɜrmɪnəs wɪθ/ &middot; *koh-TUR-muh-nus with* &middot; weak /koʊˈtɜrmənəs wəθ/
**Русский:** совпадающий по границам или по срокам с — книжн./юр.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — leases and loan documents (*a term coterminous with the note*), municipal law, and political geography; never conversational
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the other term, boundary, or period — **never a gerund** and never a clause.
**Pattern:** [term / district] *be coterminous with* [term / district] &middot; *make the lease coterminous with* [noun] &middot; *exactly* / *not quite coterminous with*
**Variants:** *conterminous with* is the same word in the spelling geographers prefer for shared borders — and *the conterminous United States* is the fixed term for the lower forty-eight. *Coextensive with* is the near twin for scope rather than time; *concurrent with* for events running together.
**Contrast:** ***Coterminous with* means beginning and ending together, or sharing a boundary; [coextensive with](#coextensive-with) means covering the same ground.*** A sublease coterminous with the master lease expires on the same day; a duty coextensive with a risk is the same size as the risk. Against [concurrent with](#concurrent-with), which needs only overlap, *coterminous with* demands the same endpoints. ✗ *coterminous to* is an error.

Ending at the same point as something else — in time, or along a shared border.

1. We made the sublease *coterminous with* the master lease so both end in June.
2. The school district is *coterminous with* the township, which simplifies the tax roll.
3. His term as trustee is *coterminous with* the mayor's.
4. The park's north edge is *coterminous with* the county line.
5. The warranty is *coterminous with* the financing, not with the roof's life.

**See also:** [coextensive with](#coextensive-with), [concurrent with](#concurrent-with), [contemporaneous with](#contemporaneous-with), [equivalent to](#equivalent-to), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### crazy about

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkreɪzi əˈbaʊt/ &middot; *KRAY-zee uh-BOWT* &middot; weak /ˈkreɪzi əˈbaʊt/, casually /ˈkreɪzi ˈbaʊt/
**Русский:** без ума от — разг.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** informal; conversational American English
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*crazy about camping*) — **never a to-infinitive.** ✗ *crazy about to camp* is not English.
**Pattern:** *be crazy about* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *not crazy about* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *go crazy over* [noun] &middot; *drive* [someone] *crazy* (no preposition on the object)
**Variants:** *wild about* and *nuts about* are the same register; *mad about* is the British equivalent and is not the American default. *Crazy for* survives mostly in song titles and in *crazy for you*.
**Contrast:** **The negative is the commonest use and it is understatement, not neutrality:** *I'm not crazy about the color* means you dislike it. Keep *crazy about* (fond of) apart from *crazy with* (overwhelmed by an emotion — *crazy with worry*) and from *drive someone crazy*, which takes a direct object and no preposition at all. In American English *mad about* means angry about, so *mad about her* and *crazy about her* are not synonyms here even though they are in London.

Enthusiastic about something or someone, to a degree the speaker admits is unreasonable.

1. He's been *crazy about* trains since he was four.
2. I'm not *crazy about* driving that stretch after dark.
3. She's *crazy about* the new place, cracked driveway and all.
4. The kids are *crazy about* their swim coach.
5. Everyone's *crazy about* that bakery on Third.

**See also:** [mad at](#mad-at), [keen on](#keen-on), [fond of](#fond-of), [obsessed with](#obsessed-with), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about)

### critical of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkrɪtɪkəl ʌv/ &middot; *KRIT-ih-kull uv* &middot; weak /ˈkrɪtɪkəl əv/
**Русский:** критикующий что-л., недовольный чем-л.; critical to/for — жизненно важный для
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*critical of spending that much*) — **never a to-infinitive** and **never a clause.**
**Pattern:** *be critical of* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *highly* / *sharply* / *openly critical of* [noun] &middot; *critical to* [something] — a different sense entirely &middot; *critical for* [purpose]
**Variants:** *disapproving of* and *disparaging about* are the near-synonyms; *hard on* is the informal one.
**Contrast:** ***Critical of* means finding fault; *critical to* and *critical for* mean essential.*** This is one word doing two unrelated jobs, and the preposition is the only signal: *critical of the plan* opposes it, *critical to the plan* is indispensable to it. A third sense, *in critical condition*, takes no preposition at all. ✗ *critical about* is an error for the fault-finding sense; use *of*.

Finding fault with something — or, with *to* and *for*, indispensable to it.

1. The auditor was *critical of* how the invoices were logged.
2. She's *critical of* everything except her own department.
3. Several members were openly *critical of* holding the vote in August.
4. Timing is *critical to* the whole operation.
5. Reviewers were *critical of* the ending but praised everything before it.

**See also:** [suspicious of](#suspicious-of), [essential to](#essential-to), [impressed with](#impressed-with), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### crowded with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkraʊdɪd wɪθ/ &middot; *KROW-did with* &middot; weak /ˈkraʊdɪd wəθ/
**Русский:** переполненный чем-л., набитый
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, usually plural or mass (*crowded with tourists*, *crowded with furniture*) — **never a gerund**, **never a to-infinitive**, and **never a clause.**
**Pattern:** *be crowded with* [plural / mass noun] &middot; [place] *crowded with* [noun] &middot; *crowd into* [place] — the verb, with a different preposition
**Variants:** *packed with*, *jammed with*, *thick with*, and *filled with* all take *with* in the same frame; *full of* takes *of* and is the commonest of the family.
**Contrast:** ***Crowded with* takes *with*; *full of* takes *of*.*** They mean nearly the same thing and their prepositions do not swap: ✗ *crowded of people*, ✗ *full with people*. Note that the verb goes elsewhere again — *people crowded into the lobby*, *crowd around the stage* — and that *crowded* alone, with no preposition, is the ordinary way to say a place has too many people in it.

Containing more people or things than there is comfortable room for.

1. The platform was *crowded with* commuters by six-fifteen.
2. Her desk is *crowded with* photographs and unopened mail.
3. The calendar is *crowded with* meetings that could have been emails.
4. Every bar downtown was *crowded with* people watching the game.
5. The little museum is *crowded with* objects nobody has cataloged.

**See also:** [full of](#full-of), [rich in](#rich-in), [short of](#short-of), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### cruel to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkruəl tu/ &middot; *KROO-ull too* &middot; weak /ˈkruəl tə/
**Русский:** жестокий к кому-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or pronoun naming the **person or animal treated badly** — **never a gerund after *to*** and **never a clause.** The adjective also takes the *it*-frame with an infinitive: *It was cruel to say that*, where the infinitive belongs to *it*, not to *cruel*.
**Pattern:** *be cruel to* [person / animal] &middot; *it is cruel to* [verb] &middot; *it was cruel of* [someone] *to* [verb] &middot; *cruel to be kind*
**Variants:** *mean to*, *harsh with*, *hard on*, and *unkind to* cover the range from playground to courtroom; all the "behavior toward a person" adjectives in this family take *to*.
**Contrast:** **Every adjective of how you treat someone takes *to*:** *cruel to*, *kind to*, *nice to*, *polite to*, *rude to*, *mean to*, *good to*. The pattern is reliable enough to guess with. What breaks it is *gentle with*, *patient with*, and *honest with*, which take *with* because they describe the manner of the handling rather than the direction of the treatment. The double frame *It was cruel of him to say that* uses *of* for the doer and *to* for the deed — ✗ *It was cruel from him* is an error.

Deliberately causing pain or suffering to someone.

1. He was *cruel to* the dog when he thought nobody was watching.
2. Middle school is *cruel to* anyone who stands out.
3. It was *cruel of* her *to* bring it up at the table.
4. The schedule is *cruel to* anyone with a long commute.
5. Time has been *cruel to* that building.

**See also:** [kind to](#kind-to), [rude to](#rude-to), [nice to](#nice-to), [gentle with](#gentle-with), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### curious about

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkjʊriəs əˈbaʊt/ &middot; *KYOOR-ee-uss uh-BOWT* &middot; weak /ˈkjʊriəs əˈbaʊt/
**Русский:** любопытствующий, желающий узнать о
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, **gerund** (*curious about trying it*), or a *wh*-clause (*curious about how they did it*) after *about* — **never a to-infinitive after *about***. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (*curious to see what happens*), which is a separate and very common frame.
**Pattern:** *be curious about* [noun / verb-ing / wh-clause] &middot; *be curious to* [verb] &middot; *curious as to* [wh-clause] &middot; *make* [someone] *curious about* [noun]
**Variants:** *interested in* is the neutral equivalent; *nosy about* is the disapproving one. *Curious as to whether* is the formal register.
**Contrast:** ***Curious about* names the topic; *curious to* names what you want to do about it.** *Curious about the results* is wondering; *curious to see the results* is wanting to look. This adjective is one of the small set — with *anxious*, *eager*, *interested*, and *keen* — where the bare infinitive is standard alongside the preposition. ✗ *curious of* and ✗ *curious for* are errors.

Wanting to know about something.

1. She's *curious about* how the machine actually works.
2. I was *curious about* the cost before I mentioned it to anyone.
3. Neighbors were *curious about* the trucks parked all week.
4. He was *curious to* hear what she'd say next.
5. Nobody seemed *curious about* where the money had gone.

**See also:** [interested in](#interested-in), [eager for](#eager-for), [enthusiastic about](#enthusiastic-about), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about)

### dedicated to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɛdɪkeɪtɪd tu/ &middot; *DED-ih-kay-tid too* &middot; weak /ˈdɛdɪkeɪtɪd tə/
**Русский:** посвятивший себя чему-л.; о вещи — предназначенный только для этого
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; warm about a person, institutional about an organization
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*dedicated to keeping the archive open*) — **never a to-infinitive.** ✗ *dedicated to keep the archive open* is the error, and the noun test settles it: *dedicated to the archive* ✓, so the *to* is a preposition. A *that*-clause is impossible.
**Pattern:** *be dedicated to* [cause / person / verb-ing] &middot; *dedicate* [a book / a bench / one's life] *to* [someone] &middot; [portion] *dedicated to* [noun] &middot; *a dedicated* [noun] — attributive, meaning reserved for one use: *a dedicated line*
**Variants:** *devoted to* and *committed to* are the near-twins and take the same gerund. *Dedicated to* sits between them — warmer than *committed*, more disciplined than *devoted*. *Pledged to* and *vowed to* mean the same thing and take an infinitive instead.
**Contrast:** ***Dedicated to* + *-ing* versus *pledged to* / *vowed to* + verb.** Same meaning, opposite structures: ✓ *dedicated to reducing waste*, ✓ *pledged to reduce waste*, ✗ *dedicated to reduce waste* — an error that turns up constantly in mission statements. Two other senses share the string and are not this pattern: the commemorative one (*a bench dedicated to her mother*) and the attributive one meaning single-purpose (*a dedicated server*), which takes no preposition at all. ✗ *dedicated for* and ✗ *dedicated with* are errors.

Giving one's whole effort to something — or, of a thing, set aside for a single purpose.

1. The foundation is *dedicated to* keeping small-town newspapers alive.
2. She's *dedicated to* her patients in a way the schedule never rewards.
3. Two rooms upstairs are *dedicated to* files nobody has opened since 2009.
4. He *dedicated* the book *to* the teacher who failed him twice.
5. The office is *dedicated to* answering every complaint within a week.

**See also:** [devoted to](#devoted-to), [committed to](#committed-to), [opposed to](#opposed-to), [accustomed to](#accustomed-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### delighted with

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈlaɪtɪd wɪθ/ &middot; *dih-LY-tid with* &middot; weak /dɪˈlaɪtɪd wəθ/
**Русский:** очень довольный, в восторге от
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral, warm; commoner in writing than in casual American speech
**Complement:** noun phrase after *with*, *by*, and *at*; a **gerund** is possible after *at* (*delighted at finding it still open*) — **never a to-infinitive after a preposition.** The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (*delighted to help*), which is by far its commonest frame, and a *that*-clause (*delighted that you could come*).
**Pattern:** *be delighted with* [thing received or achieved] &middot; *be delighted by* [the cause] &middot; *be delighted at* [news / turn of events] &middot; *be delighted to* [verb] &middot; *be delighted* (*that*) [clause]
**Variants:** *pleased with* is the same shape at lower temperature; *thrilled with* / *by* is the same shape at higher.
**Contrast:** ***With* takes the thing you got; *by* takes the thing that acted on you; *at* takes the news or the turn of events.*** *Delighted with the results* is your verdict on them; *delighted by her reply* points at what caused the delight; *delighted at the news* reports a reaction to information. The infinitive frame is the polite formula of American correspondence — *I'd be delighted to* — and it never takes a preposition. ✗ *delighted for* is an error except in *delighted for you*, meaning happy on your behalf.

Very pleased — a step warmer than *pleased*, a step cooler than *thrilled*.

1. The client was *delighted with* the first round of drawings.
2. She was *delighted by* how many people showed up.
3. He was *delighted at* the news that the school had reopened.
4. We'd be *delighted to* have you both for dinner.
5. Everyone was *delighted with* the way the porch turned out.

**See also:** [pleased with](#pleased-with), [happy about](#happy-about), [satisfied with](#satisfied-with), [impressed with](#impressed-with), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### dependent on

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈpɛndənt ɑn/ &middot; *dih-PEN-dunt on* &middot; weak /dɪˈpɛndənt ən/
**Русский:** зависящий от — нуждающийся в чём-л. или определяемый чем-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*dependent on getting the permit*) — **never a to-infinitive** and **never a *that*-clause.** A *wh*-clause is possible in the "determined by" sense: *dependent on how the vote goes*.
**Pattern:** *be dependent on* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *heavily* / *entirely* / *largely dependent on* [noun] &middot; *dependent on* [someone] *for* [something] &middot; *make* [X] *dependent on* [Y]
**Variants:** *dependent upon* is the formal equivalent, standard in contracts and academic writing. *Reliant on* is the close synonym; *contingent on* is the conditional one.
**Contrast:** ✗ *dependent of* and ✗ *dependent from* are errors — the preposition is fixed. Two spelling notes matter in American English: the **adjective is *dependent*** with *-ent* in every case, and the **noun for a person you support is also *dependent*** in American usage (*claim two dependents*), where British English spells the noun *dependant*. Note the two senses that share the pattern: *dependent on her mother* (needing support) versus *dependent on the weather* (determined by), which is why *The picnic is dependent on the weather* has nothing to do with trust. The opposite is *independent of*, with *of*, not *from*.

Needing someone or something in order to survive or continue — or determined by it.

1. The town is almost entirely *dependent on* the mill.
2. Whether we open Monday is *dependent on* the inspection.
3. She has two children *dependent on* her.
4. Funding is *dependent upon* a match from the county.
5. The whole schedule is *dependent on* getting the parts by Friday.

**See also:** [reliant on](#reliant-on), [independent of](#independent-of), [based on](#based-on), [subject to](#subject-to), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### derived from

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈraɪvd frʌm/ &middot; *dih-RYVED from* &middot; weak /dəˈraɪvd frəm/
**Русский:** происходящий от, выведенный из — из источника, текста или расчёта
**Type:** adjective (past participle) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; standard in science, etymology, finance, and cooking labels
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the source, or a **gerund** naming the activity a thing comes out of (*income derived from renting the top floor*) — **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *derived from rent the top floor*. A clause is impossible.
**Pattern:** *be derived from* [source] &middot; [noun] *derived from* [noun] — the reduced relative, which is where the phrase does most of its work &middot; *derive* [something] *from* [source] — the active verb &middot; *derived from* [language] — in etymology
**Variants:** *comes from* is the everyday equivalent; *obtained from* and *extracted from* are the physical ones; *based on* covers a looser relationship; *stems from* and *springs from* are the metaphorical verbs.
**Contrast:** ***Derived from* names an origin the thing was actually made out of or worked out from***, which is more than [based on](#based-on) claims: a screenplay is based on a novel, but morphine is derived from opium. In finance the word is exact — a derivative *derived from* an underlying asset takes its value from it. Note that the source, not the product, follows *from*: ✓ *aspirin is derived from willow bark*, ✗ *willow bark is derived from aspirin*. ✗ *derived of* is an error, and *derived by* names the person or method, not the source: *derived by Gauss*, *derived by regression*.

Having its origin or its substance in something earlier — a source material, a text, or a calculation.

1. The dye is *derived from* a root that only grows in two counties.
2. Nearly half the town's revenue is *derived from* the port.
3. The word is *derived from* an Old Norse verb meaning to scrape.
4. These figures are *derived from* the same survey the state uses.
5. Most of her income is *derived from* renting out the two back units.

**See also:** [based on](#based-on), [grounded in](#grounded-in), [founded on, founded upon](#founded-on-founded-upon), [independent of](#independent-of), [from](02-core-relational.md#from)

### deserving of

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈzɜrvɪŋ ʌv/ &middot; *dih-ZUR-ving uv* &middot; weak /dəˈzɜrvɪŋ əv/, and before a consonant often /dəˈzɜrvɪŋ ə/
**Русский:** заслуживающий чего-л. по делам или по достоинствам
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; in speech Americans use the plain verb (*she deserves a raise*) and keep *deserving of* for writing and for emphasis
**Complement:** a noun phrase, usually abstract — *deserving of praise*, *deserving of a second look* — or a **gerund** (*deserving of being taken seriously*). **Never a to-infinitive after *of***: ✗ *deserving of to win*. The bare adjective takes no preposition at all in *the deserving poor*.
**Pattern:** *be deserving of* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *hardly* / *richly* / *wholly deserving of* &middot; *deserve* [noun] — the verb, with a direct object and no preposition &middot; *deserve to* [verb] — the verb with an infinitive
**Variants:** *worthy of* is the close twin and is commoner; *meriting* is the participial version; the plain verb *deserve* replaces the whole phrase in ordinary speech.
**Contrast:** **The adjective takes *of*; the verb takes nothing.** ✓ *She deserves an answer.* ✓ *She is deserving of an answer.* ✗ *She deserves of an answer.* That is the whole trap, and it is the same trap as [worthy of](#worthy-of). Against *worthy of*, which can weigh a thing's quality (*a film worthy of the original*), *deserving of* is about what someone has earned by conduct. Note the fixed phrase *the deserving poor*, an old piece of charity vocabulary that now reads as loaded.

Having earned something by conduct or merit, and so entitled to it.

1. The proposal is *deserving of* a serious hearing, whatever its flaws.
2. Nobody on that crew was less *deserving of* the blame.
3. Her thirty years there are *deserving of* more than a sheet cake.
4. The complaint is *deserving of* being investigated, not filed away.
5. He behaved as if he were *deserving of* an exception to every rule.

**See also:** [worthy of](#worthy-of), [entitled to](#entitled-to), [eligible for](#eligible-for), [guilty of](#guilty-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### desirous of

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈzaɪrəs ʌv/ &middot; *dih-ZYE-rus uv* &middot; weak /dəˈzaɪrəs əv/
**Русский:** желающий чего-л. — устар./офиц., торжественный регистр
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal and dated — contract recitals (*the parties being desirous of settling all claims*), diplomatic notes, and nineteenth-century prose; in any other setting it sounds pompous, and modern American drafting guides tell you to write *want*
**Complement:** a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*desirous of obtaining a license*) — the gerund is by far the commoner of the two in the surviving legal formula. An older **to-infinitive** (*desirous to please*) is genuine but archaic and should not be imitated. No *that*-clause.
**Pattern:** *be desirous of* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *the parties, being desirous of* [verb-ing], *agree as follows* — the recital formula &middot; *desirous of* [noun] in official correspondence
**Variants:** *desiring to* + infinitive and *wishing to* + infinitive are the living formal equivalents; *anxious to* and *eager for* cover the everyday range; *want* is the word to use in any modern document.
**Contrast:** **This is a survival, not a live pattern.** Where *desirous of* still appears, it is nearly always inside a *whereas* clause in a contract, and the modern replacement is a plain verb: *The parties want to settle all claims.* Against [eager for](#eager-for) and [anxious about](#anxious-about), which describe feelings, *desirous of* in legal use describes an intention formally recorded. Do not confuse *desirous* (wanting something) with *desirable* (wanted by others) — ✗ *a desirous location* is a common slip for *desirable*.

Wanting something — in a formal, and now largely ceremonial, register.

1. The parties, being *desirous of* settling all claims, agree as follows.
2. The company is *desirous of* acquiring the adjoining parcel.
3. Any member *desirous of* speaking must be recognized by the chair.
4. He was *desirous of* an appointment but too proud to ask for one.
5. Both governments are *desirous of* avoiding further escalation.

**See also:** [eager for](#eager-for), [intent on](#intent-on), [content with](#content-with), [worthy of](#worthy-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### detrimental to

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdɛtrəˈmɛntəl tu/ &middot; *det-ruh-MEN-tul too* &middot; weak /ˌdɛtrəˈmɛntəl tə/, with the medial *t* flapped: "det-ruh-MEN-nul-tuh"
**Русский:** вредный для, наносящий ущерб — книжн.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to neutral; standard in public-health writing, HR documents, and legal filings, where the plain word would be *bad for*
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming what suffers, or a **gerund** with the same role — **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *detrimental to sleep well*. Note that the complement is the **victim**, not the cause: *detrimental to your health*, never ✗ *detrimental to smoking*.
**Pattern:** *be detrimental to* [person / interest / health] &middot; *seriously* / *materially detrimental to* &middot; *to the detriment of* [noun] — the noun frame &middot; *detrimental reliance* — the legal term, no preposition
**Variants:** *harmful to* and *damaging to* are the neutral equivalents; *bad for* is the plain one; *prejudicial to* is the legal one and adds unfairness; *deleterious to* is the clinical one.
**Contrast:** Against [harmful to](#harmful-to), which is the ordinary word for injury, *detrimental to* is the institutional register and is often used of interests, reputations, and finances rather than bodies. Against [prejudicial to](#prejudicial-to), which in law means unfairly damaging to a party's case, *detrimental to* is neutral about fairness. Watch the direction of the preposition: the thing after *to* is the one being hurt. ✗ *detrimental for* and ✗ *detrimental of* are errors.

Causing harm or loss to somebody or something.

1. Long shifts without a break are *detrimental to* accuracy on the line.
2. Any delay now would be *detrimental to* the whole schedule.
3. The report says the runoff is *detrimental to* the fishery downstream.
4. Posting about the case was *detrimental to* her own claim.
5. Nothing here is *detrimental to* the tenants; the change is administrative.

**See also:** [harmful to](#harmful-to), [prejudicial to](#prejudicial-to), [conducive to](#conducive-to), [adverse to](#adverse-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### devoted to

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈvoʊtɪd tu/ &middot; *dih-VOH-tid too* &middot; weak /dɪˈvoʊtɪd tə/
**Русский:** преданный кому-л., целиком отдающий себя чему-л.
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to warm
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*devoted to restoring the building*) — **never a to-infinitive.** ✗ *devoted to restore the building* is the error; the noun test settles it (*devoted to the restoration* ✓).
**Pattern:** *be devoted to* [person / cause / verb-ing] &middot; *devote* [time / a chapter] *to* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; [portion] *devoted to* [noun]
**Variants:** *dedicated to* and *committed to* are near-identical and take the same gerund. *Given over to* is the version used of space and time.
**Contrast:** ***Devoted to* is warmer than *dedicated to* and warmer still than *committed to*** — devotion suggests love, dedication suggests discipline, commitment suggests obligation. All three take the gerund, and this is the single most useful thing to know about the trio. A second, purely spatial sense assigns a portion of something: *two pages devoted to footnotes*, where no feeling is involved. ✗ *devoted for* and ✗ *devoted with* are errors.

Giving loyalty, love, or resources entirely to someone or something.

1. She was *devoted to* her students for thirty-one years.
2. Half the budget is *devoted to* maintaining the trails.
3. He's *devoted to* getting the archive digitized before he retires.
4. The last chapter is *devoted to* the 1919 strike.
5. They're a *devoted* pair, *devoted to* each other and to nobody else.

**See also:** [committed to](#committed-to), [dedicated to](#dedicated-to), [intent on](#intent-on), [married to](#married-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### different from

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɪfrənt frʌm/ &middot; *DIF-runt from* &middot; weak /ˈdɪfrənt frəm/; the careful three-syllable /ˈdɪfərənt/ is also standard
**Русский:** отличающийся от, не такой, как — from стандартная американская форма
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; *different from* is the standard American form in every register
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, **gerund** (*different from working alone*), or a free relative (*different from what I expected*) after *from* — **never a to-infinitive.** ***Different than*** additionally takes a **finite clause** (*different than I expected*), which is exactly the environment that makes it useful.
**Pattern:** [X] *is different from* [Y] &middot; *no different from* [noun] &middot; *very* / *quite* / *entirely different from* [noun] &middot; *different than* [clause] &middot; *differ from* [noun] — the verb, *from* only
**Variants:** ***different than*** is common, respectable American English, strongest before a clause; ***different to*** is British and is not used in the United States. *Differing from* and *distinct from* are the formal equivalents.
**Contrast:** **The honest American position has three parts.** *From* is standard and never wrong: use it before a noun or pronoun. *Than* is the better choice **before a clause**, where *from* forces the clumsy *from what*: *The job turned out different than I expected* reads better than *different from what I expected*, and no American editor will stop you. Before a bare noun (*different than his*) some editors still object, so *from* is safer there. *To* is British — correct in London, foreign in Chicago. Note that the verb *differ* takes only *from*: ✗ *differs than*, ✗ *differs to*. See also [different from](09-comparison-opposition.md#different-from), where the word is treated as a comparison preposition in its own right.

Not the same as something else — the central comparison adjective of English, and the one usage question in this file that is still live.

1. Her second album is completely *different from* the first.
2. City driving is *different from* anything he'd done upstate.
3. The final bill was *different than* we'd been told it would be.
4. This is no *different from* what happened in 2011.
5. Winters here are *different from* winters anywhere else I've lived.

**See also:** [similar to](#similar-to), [identical to](#identical-to), [equal to](#equal-to), [different from](09-comparison-opposition.md#different-from), [than](09-comparison-opposition.md#than), [from](02-core-relational.md#from)

### disappointed with

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdɪsəˈpɔɪntɪd wɪθ/ &middot; *diss-uh-POYN-tid with* &middot; weak /ˌdɪsəˈpɔɪntɪd wəθ/
**Русский:** разочарованный в ком-л. или чём-л., обманувшийся в ожиданиях
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase after *with*, *in*, and *by*; a **gerund** after *about* (*disappointed about missing it*) — **never a to-infinitive after a preposition.** The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (*disappointed to learn*) and a *that*-clause (*disappointed that no one called*).
**Pattern:** *be disappointed with* [thing] &middot; *be disappointed in* [person] &middot; *be disappointed by* [the cause] &middot; *be disappointed about* [situation / verb-ing] &middot; *be disappointed to* [verb] &middot; *be disappointed* (*that*) [clause]
**Variants:** *let down by* is the informal equivalent; *dissatisfied with* is the formal one and takes only *with*.
**Contrast:** ***Disappointed in* is personal and moral; *disappointed with* is an assessment.** *I'm disappointed in you* is a rebuke about character; *I'm disappointed with the room* is a review. Use *in* for people whose conduct fell short, *with* for things and outcomes, *by* when you want to point at the cause as an agent, and *about* for a situation. American speech leans on *in* for people far more than British English does. ✗ *disappointed of* and ✗ *disappointed from* are errors.

Let down because something or someone did not measure up.

1. Fans were *disappointed with* the ending.
2. Her father said he was *disappointed in* her, which was worse than shouting.
3. We were *disappointed by* how little the report actually said.
4. He's *disappointed about* missing the trip a second year running.
5. I was *disappointed to* find the office already closed.

**See also:** [satisfied with](#satisfied-with), [pleased with](#pleased-with), [upset about](#upset-about), [sad about](#sad-about), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### dispositive of

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈspɑzɪtɪv ʌv/ &middot; *dih-SPOZ-uh-tiv uv* &middot; weak /dəˈspɑzətəv əv/
**Русский:** разрешающий вопрос окончательно — юр.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal, narrowly — briefs, opinions, and motions; an American meets it in a court document and essentially nowhere else
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the question, issue, or case that the thing settles — **never a gerund**, never a clause. What follows *of* is the matter decided, not the deciding fact.
**Pattern:** *be dispositive of* [issue / case] &middot; *not dispositive of* [noun] — the commonest use, since the word usually appears in a denial &middot; *a dispositive motion* — attributive, meaning one that can end the case
**Variants:** *decisive of* is the near equivalent; *determinative of* is the drier one; *conclusive as to* takes a different preposition; in plain English, *settles the question*.
**Contrast:** ***Dispositive of* means it ends the inquiry, not merely that it matters*** — which is why the word so often appears negated: *the affidavit is relevant but not dispositive of the issue*. Against [material to](#material-to), which means capable of affecting the outcome, and [probative of](#probative-of), which means tending to prove a fact, *dispositive of* is the strongest of the three: material evidence bears on the question, probative evidence supports a finding, dispositive evidence closes it. Note the separate legal sense of *dispositive* meaning "relating to the disposition of property" (*the dispositive provisions of the will*), where no *of* follows. ✗ *dispositive to* is an error.

Settling a question outright, so that nothing further needs deciding.

1. The waiver, if valid, is *dispositive of* the entire claim.
2. That one email is not *dispositive of* intent, whatever the plaintiff says.
3. The court found the timing *dispositive of* the jurisdictional question.
4. Neither affidavit is *dispositive of* who was driving.
5. Counsel conceded the point was *dispositive of* the appeal.

**See also:** [material to](#material-to), [probative of](#probative-of), [indicative of](#indicative-of), [pertinent to](#pertinent-to), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### eager for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈiɡər fɔr/ &middot; *EE-gur for* &middot; weak /ˈiɡər fər/
**Русский:** жаждущий чего-л., нетерпеливо ожидающий
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase after *for* (*eager for news*) — **a gerund after *for* is possible but stilted**, and the natural way to put a verb after this adjective is the **bare to-infinitive** (*eager to start*), which is the commonest frame by a wide margin.
