A *that*-clause is possible in formal writing (*eager that the record show*).
**Pattern:** *be eager for* [noun] &middot; *be eager to* [verb] &middot; *be eager for* [someone] *to* [verb] &middot; *eager beaver* (fixed compound)
**Variants:** *keen on* + *-ing* and *keen to* + verb are the British-flavored equivalents; *anxious to* and *impatient for* / *to* are the everyday American ones.
**Contrast:** ***Eager for* takes a thing; *eager to* takes an action.*** *Eager for the results* versus *eager to see the results* — and the second is what to write when a verb is involved, because *eager for seeing the results* is not idiomatic. Note the sharp difference from *anxious about*, which is dread: *eager for Monday* and *anxious about Monday* are opposite emotions with the same referent. ✗ *eager of* and ✗ *eager at* are errors.

Impatiently wanting something to arrive or to happen.

1. The whole town was *eager for* a decision either way.
2. She's *eager to* get back on the water.
3. Investors are *eager for* any sign that hiring has picked up.
4. He was *eager for* his daughter *to* see the place before it sold.
5. New hires are always *eager for* feedback in the first month.

**See also:** [anxious about](#anxious-about), [keen on](#keen-on), [enthusiastic about](#enthusiastic-about), [ready for](#ready-for), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### eligible for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛlɪdʒəbəl fɔr/ &middot; *EL-uh-juh-bul for* &middot; weak /ˈɛlədʒəbəl fər/
**Русский:** имеющий право на, подходящий по условиям для
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; the standard word of American benefits, insurance, financial aid, and sports rosters
**Complement:** after *for*, a noun phrase naming the benefit, program, or status (*eligible for parole*, *eligible for the tax credit*) — and a **gerund** where the benefit is an activity (*eligible for reinstatement*, more naturally a noun). After **bare *to***, a **to-infinitive**: *eligible to vote*, *eligible to apply*. The two frames do not mix: ✗ *eligible for apply*, ✗ *eligible to a refund*.
**Pattern:** *be eligible for* [benefit / program] &middot; *be eligible to* [verb] &middot; *eligible for* [noun] *after* [period] &middot; *deem* / *declare* [someone] *eligible for* [noun] &middot; *eligibility for* [noun] — the noun frame keeps *for*
**Variants:** *entitled to* is the stronger word — eligibility lets you apply, entitlement means it is owed to you. *Qualified for* stresses ability rather than status; *in line for* is informal and predictive.
**Contrast:** ***Eligible for* + noun, *eligible to* + verb.*** This is the single thing to get right, and the mixed forms are the standard errors on American applications: ✓ *eligible for benefits*, ✓ *eligible to receive benefits*, ✗ *eligible for receive benefits*. Against [entitled to](#entitled-to), the difference is legal and real: an eligible applicant may still be denied, an entitled one may not. ✗ *eligible of* and ✗ *eligible in* are errors.

Meeting the conditions that let you apply for something or take part in it.

1. You're *eligible for* the credit if your income was under the cap last year.
2. He becomes *eligible for* parole in November.
3. Part-time staff are *eligible for* dental coverage after ninety days.
4. She's *eligible to* vote in the primary but not in the special election.
5. The building is *eligible for* listing on the state register.

**See also:** [entitled to](#entitled-to), [suitable for](#suitable-for), [ready for](#ready-for), [exempt from](#exempt-from), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### encumbered by, encumbered with

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈkʌmbərd baɪ, ɪnˈkʌmbərd wɪθ/ &middot; *in-KUM-burd by, in-KUM-burd with* &middot; weak /ənˈkʌmbərd bə/ and /ənˈkʌmbərd wəθ/
**Русский:** обременённый чем-л. — юр. залогом на имуществе; вообще — нагруженный ношей
**Type:** adjective (past participle) + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; a legal term of art about property, and a literary word about burdens generally
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the burden — a lien, a mortgage, a debt, a load, a set of duties. A **gerund is not used** here (✗ *encumbered with paying*), and no clause follows.
**Pattern:** *be encumbered by* [burden] &middot; *be encumbered with* [burden] &middot; *encumbered by debt* / *by regulation* / *with baggage* &middot; *unencumbered by* [noun] — the negative, which is commoner in journalism &middot; *an encumbrance on* [property] — the noun frame, with *on*
**Variants:** *burdened with* and *saddled with* are the plainer versions; *weighed down by* is the physical one; *subject to* is the neutral legal term for property carrying a lien.
**Contrast:** **The two prepositions are close, and the split is one of feel rather than rule:** *encumbered by* points at the agent doing the burdening (*encumbered by debt*, *encumbered by the rules*), while *encumbered with* points at the load itself (*encumbered with three suitcases*, *encumbered with obligations*). In property law both appear, and *encumbered by a mortgage* is the commoner phrasing. Note the frequent literary use of the negative, *unencumbered by*, which usually carries a sneer: *unencumbered by any knowledge of the subject*. The related noun takes *on*: *an encumbrance on the title*.

Weighed down by something you have to carry — a debt or lien on property, or a load of any kind.

1. The parcel is *encumbered by* a mortgage and two utility easements.
2. She arrived *encumbered with* a cooler, a folding chair, and a dog.
3. The agency is *encumbered by* rules written for a different century.
4. A title *encumbered by* an old judgment can't be insured until it clears.
5. He offers opinions *unencumbered by* any acquaintance with the file.

**See also:** [fraught with](#fraught-with), [replete with](#replete-with), [subject to](#subject-to), [appurtenant to](#appurtenant-to), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### endemic to

**Pronunciation:** /ɛnˈdɛmɪk tu/ &middot; *en-DEM-ik too* &middot; weak /ɛnˈdɛmɪk tə/
**Русский:** эндемичный для — встречающийся только здесь и постоянно
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** technical — ecology, epidemiology, and public health; used loosely in journalism for a problem that will not go away
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming a **place or a population** — *endemic to New Zealand*, *endemic to the region*, *endemic to that industry*. **Never a gerund** (✗ *endemic to logging trees*) and never a clause. If what follows is not a place or a group, the word is being misused.
**Pattern:** [species / disease] *be endemic to* [place] &middot; *endemic in* [population] — the epidemiologist's variant &middot; *endemic* [noun] — attributive (*endemic corruption*, *endemic species*)
**Variants:** *native to* is the neutral word for where a species originated; *indigenous to* is its formal twin for peoples and species; *confined to* and *restricted to* say the same thing about distribution without the biology.
**Contrast:** ***Endemic to* means found only there; *epidemic* and *pandemic* are about rate, not place.*** A disease endemic to a region is permanently present at a baseline level; an epidemic is a surge above it. Against [native to](#native-to), which says a species originated somewhere, *endemic to* says it is found nowhere else — every endemic species is native, but not every native one is endemic. The loose journalistic use (*corruption endemic to the department*) is now standard but keeps the "built into this place" idea. ✗ *endemic of* is an error; *endemic in* is fine before a population.

Found only in a particular place or population, and permanently present there.

1. The tortoise is *endemic to* a single island in the chain.
2. Cholera remains *endemic to* several delta regions.
3. Delays of this kind seem *endemic to* the permitting office.
4. Half the plants in the preserve are *endemic to* this valley.
5. The parasite is *endemic to* herds in the northern counties.

**See also:** [native to](#native-to), [peculiar to](#peculiar-to), [specific to](#specific-to), [common to](#common-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### endowed with

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈdaʊd wɪθ/ &middot; *in-DOWD with* &middot; weak /ənˈdaʊd wəθ/
**Русский:** наделённый чем-л. — качеством, полномочием или средствами
**Type:** adjective (past participle) + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to literary; an American knows it from the Declaration of Independence — *endowed by their Creator with certain unalienable Rights* — and from institutional writing about gifts
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the quality, power, or funds bestowed — **never a gerund** (✗ *endowed with running fast*) and never a clause. The giver, when named, goes in a *by*-phrase before the *with*-phrase.
**Pattern:** *be endowed with* [quality / power] &middot; *endowed by* [giver] *with* [gift] &middot; *richly* / *generously* / *amply endowed with* &middot; *endow* [an institution] *with* [funds] &middot; *an endowed chair* — attributive
**Variants:** *blessed with* is the warm everyday version; *gifted with* the slightly precious one; *possessed of* the formal one for qualities already held; *furnished with* and *equipped with* the practical ones.
**Contrast:** Against [possessed of](#possessed-of), which reports what somebody has, *endowed with* implies the quality was **given** — by nature, by a creator, or by a donor — which is why it fits rights, talents, and funds and not, say, debts. Note the two live senses: the abstract one (*endowed with patience*) and the financial one (*an endowed professorship*), where the verb takes money as its second object. A jocular use about physique exists and is worth knowing so as not to trip over it. ✗ *endowed by* + quality reverses the roles: *endowed by nature* names the giver, not the gift.

Given some quality, power, or fund of money by someone or something else.

1. The charter leaves the commission *endowed with* real subpoena power.
2. She was *endowed with* a memory for names that made the job easy.
3. The chair is *endowed with* a gift from a class that graduated in 1948.
4. The soil here is *endowed with* nothing but rocks and good drainage.
5. Human beings, the text says, are *endowed with* reason and conscience.

**See also:** [possessed of](#possessed-of), [vested with](#vested-with), [replete with](#replete-with), [full of](#full-of), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### enforceable against

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈfɔrsəbəl əˈɡɛnst/ &middot; *in-FOR-suh-bul uh-GENST* &middot; weak /ənˈfɔrsəbəl əˈɡɛnst/, the final *t* usually dropped before a consonant: "uh-GENS"
**Русский:** принудительно исполнимый в отношении кого-л. — юр.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal; the natural home is the enforceability opinion in a loan closing (*a valid obligation enforceable against the borrower in accordance with its terms*)
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the **party against whom** the obligation can be enforced — **never a gerund** and never a clause. Related slots take other prepositions: *enforceable by* [the party who may enforce], *enforceable in* [a court], *enforceable under* [a body of law].
**Pattern:** [obligation] *be enforceable against* [party] &middot; *valid, binding, and enforceable against* [party] — the closing-opinion formula &middot; *enforceable by* [party] *against* [party] &middot; *not enforceable against* [third party]
**Variants:** *binding on* is the everyday equivalent from the other direction; *actionable against* is narrower and concerns suits; *good against* is the older property phrase (*good against the world*).
**Contrast:** **Three prepositions divide the roles cleanly:** *enforceable **against*** the one who must perform, *enforceable **by*** the one who may sue, *enforceable **in*** the forum. A clause may be enforceable by the employer against the employee in state court — all three at once. Against [binding on, binding upon](#binding-on-binding-upon), which states the same fact in the ordinary drafting idiom, *enforceable against* is the term used when the question is whether a court would actually make somebody perform. ✗ *enforceable to* and ✗ *enforceable on* are errors.

Capable of being made binding on a particular person by a court.

1. The guaranty is *enforceable against* the parent company, not just the subsidiary.
2. An oral promise like that is rarely *enforceable against* anyone.
3. Counsel opined that the note is valid and *enforceable against* the borrower.
4. The covenant runs with the land and is *enforceable against* later owners.
5. A settlement signed by one heir is not *enforceable against* the others.

**See also:** [binding on, binding upon](#binding-on-binding-upon), [bound by](#bound-by), [liable for, liable to](#liable-for-liable-to), [immune from, immune to](#immune-from-immune-to), [against](09-comparison-opposition.md#against)

### engaged to

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈɡeɪdʒd tu/ &middot; *in-GAYJD too* &middot; weak /ɪnˈɡeɪdʒd tə/
**Русский:** помолвленный с; engaged in — занятый чем-л., engaged with — вовлечённый во что-л.
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a **person** after *to* — **never a gerund** (✗ *engaged to marrying*). After *in*, a noun phrase or **gerund** (*engaged in negotiating the lease*) — **never a to-infinitive after either.** The single exception is the frozen *engaged to be married*, where *to* is an infinitive marker inside a set phrase and no person follows it; that phrase is not a model for anything else the adjective does.
**Pattern:** *be engaged to* [person] &middot; *get engaged to* [person] &middot; *be engaged in* [activity / verb-ing] &middot; *be engaged with* [an issue or a community] &middot; *engaged to be married*
**Variants:** *betrothed to* is the archaic form; *promised to* survives in period fiction. For the activity sense, *involved in* and *occupied with* are the everyday equivalents.
**Contrast:** ***Engaged to* is about marriage; *engaged in* is about activity; *engaged with* is about sustained attention.*** *She's engaged to a lawyer* (they will marry), *She's engaged in litigation* (that is what she is doing), *She's engaged with the community* (she participates seriously). Swapping them produces nonsense or comedy. ✗ *engaged with a lawyer* does not mean betrothed. Note also *engaged* meaning occupied — *the line is engaged* — which is British; American English says *busy*.

Promised in marriage, or occupied in an activity, depending on the preposition.

1. She got *engaged to* her college roommate's brother.
2. The two firms are *engaged in* a dispute over the patent.
3. He's been *engaged to* the same woman for six years.
4. The department is *engaged in* rewriting the entire handbook.
5. Students who are *engaged with* the material don't need to be told twice.

**See also:** [married to](#married-to), [involved in](#involved-in), [committed to](#committed-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### enthusiastic about

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˌθuziˈæstɪk əˈbaʊt/ &middot; *in-thoo-zee-ASS-tik uh-BOWT* &middot; weak /ɪnˌθuziˈæstɪk əˈbaʊt/
**Русский:** восторженно относящийся к, горячо поддерживающий
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; the everyday words are *excited about* and *into*
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*enthusiastic about trying again*) — **never a to-infinitive.** ✗ *enthusiastic to start* is not idiomatic; write *eager to start*.
**Pattern:** *be enthusiastic about* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *less than enthusiastic about* [noun] &middot; *enthusiastic supporter of* [noun] — the noun form takes *of*
**Variants:** *excited about* is the everyday twin; *keen on* the British one; *into* the casual American one (*she's really into pottery*).
**Contrast:** ***Enthusiastic about* takes a gerund; *eager to* takes an infinitive*** — this pair is worth memorizing together, because they mean nearly the same thing and cannot borrow each other's structure: ✓ *enthusiastic about starting*, ✓ *eager to start*, ✗ *enthusiastic to start*. The litotes *less than enthusiastic about* is the standard American way of saying someone hated it. ✗ *enthusiastic for* and ✗ *enthusiastic of* are errors, though the noun *enthusiasm* takes *for*: *enthusiasm for the project*.

Showing eager approval of something.

1. The staff was *enthusiastic about* moving to a four-day week.
2. He's *enthusiastic about* anything with a two-stroke engine.
3. The board was less than *enthusiastic about* the price tag.
4. She's *enthusiastic about* teaching the intro section again.
5. Reviewers were *enthusiastic about* the first season and cooler about the second.

**See also:** [excited about](#excited-about), [eager for](#eager-for), [keen on](#keen-on), [interested in](#interested-in), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about)

### entitled to

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈtaɪtəld tu/ &middot; *in-TY-tuld too* &middot; weak /ənˈtaɪtəld tə/, the *t* flapped: "in-TY-dul-duh"
**Русский:** имеющий право на — по закону, договору или по заслугам
**Type:** adjective (past participle) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; the standard word in American benefits law, contracts, and everyday argument about rights
**Complement:** **two frames, and both are correct.** With a **noun phrase**, the *to* is a preposition: *entitled to a refund*, *entitled to back pay*. With a **bare verb**, the *to* is an infinitive marker: *entitled to know*, *entitled to vote*. What is **not** available is the gerund: ✗ *entitled to knowing*, ✗ *entitled to receiving a refund*. This is the one entry in the file where the noun test comes out positive (*entitled to a refund* ✓) and the infinitive is still legal, so the test has to be used in the other direction — the *-ing* form is the form to avoid.
**Pattern:** *be entitled to* [noun] &middot; *be entitled to* [verb] &middot; *entitle* [someone] *to* [noun] &middot; *an entitlement to* [noun] — the noun frame &middot; *entitled* [title] — the unrelated sense, "given the title," as in *a book entitled ...*
**Variants:** *eligible for* is the weaker word (you may apply); *have a right to* is the plain paraphrase and behaves the same way; *due* takes no preposition (*the refund due her*).
**Contrast:** ***Entitled to* means it is owed you; [eligible for](#eligible-for) means you may apply for it.*** An eligible applicant can be turned down; an entitled claimant can sue. Note the second, unrelated *entitled* meaning "titled" — *a memo entitled "Weekend Coverage"* — which takes no preposition and no complement of this kind; some American editors prefer plain *titled* there to keep the two apart. And note the modern pejorative *entitled* used alone (*an entitled kid*), which takes no complement at all. ✗ *entitled for* and ✗ *entitled of* are errors.

Having a right to something, whether by law, by contract, or by plain desert.

1. Every employee is *entitled to* an itemized pay stub.
2. You're *entitled to* know what the charge was before you sign.
3. She's *entitled to* three weeks off and hasn't taken a day.
4. Riders are *entitled to* a refund if the delay runs over an hour.
5. He isn't *entitled to* a hearing until the appeal is docketed.

**See also:** [eligible for](#eligible-for), [deserving of](#deserving-of), [worthy of](#worthy-of), [liable for, liable to](#liable-for-liable-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### envious of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛnviəs ʌv/ &middot; *EN-vee-uss uv* &middot; weak /ˈɛnviəs əv/
**Русский:** завидующий кому-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; *jealous of* is the everyday word
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*envious of having the whole summer free*) — **never a to-infinitive** and **never a clause.**
**Pattern:** *be envious of* [person / thing] &middot; *envious of* [someone's] [possession] &middot; *make* [someone] *envious*
**Variants:** *jealous of* covers the same ground in speech; *covetous of* is the biblical register.
**Contrast:** ***Envious of* wants what someone else has; *jealous of* also guards what it already has.*** *Envious of her promotion* is straightforward; *jealous of her promotion* means the same thing in ordinary speech, but *jealous of his time* means protective of it, a sense *envious* cannot carry. Careful writers keep *envy* for wanting and *jealousy* for guarding, though the distinction is widely ignored. ✗ *envious about* and ✗ *envious at* are errors.

Wanting what someone else has.

1. I'm *envious of* anyone who can sleep on a plane.
2. She was *envious of* the extra week they got in August.
3. He's *envious of* his brother's easy way with strangers.
4. Colleagues were quietly *envious of* the corner office.
5. Nobody is *envious of* the job once they've seen the hours.

**See also:** [jealous of](#jealous-of), [proud of](#proud-of), [suspicious of](#suspicious-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### equal to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈikwəl tu/ &middot; *EE-kwull too* &middot; weak /ˈikwəl tə/
**Русский:** равный чему-л.; идиом. — способный справиться с задачей
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*equal to running the whole thing alone*) — **never a to-infinitive.** ✗ *equal to run it* is an error, though the idiom *equal to the task* is a fixed noun frame that hides the same structure.
**Pattern:** [X] *is equal to* [Y] &middot; *equal to the task* / *equal to the occasion* &middot; *equal in* [respect] *to* [noun] &middot; *equal parts* [X] *and* [Y] &middot; *all things being equal*
**Variants:** *equivalent to* is the technical version; *the same as* is the everyday one; *on a par with* is the idiom of comparison.
**Contrast:** ***Equal to* takes *to*, never *than*.*** ✗ *equal than* is a persistent error made by speakers whose first language uses a comparative there; the same warning applies to *superior to*, *inferior to*, *senior to*, and *preferable to*, which all reject *than*. The idiomatic sense — *equal to the task*, meaning up to it — is not about quantity at all, and *equal to* + *-ing* belongs with it. See [equal to](09-comparison-opposition.md#equal-to) for the word as a comparison preposition.

The same in amount, value, or capability — or, idiomatically, up to what a job demands.

1. One tablespoon is *equal to* three teaspoons.
2. The settlement was *equal to* four months' pay.
3. She proved *equal to* the task within a week.
4. Nothing on the menu is *equal to* what they served here in 2003.
5. Their offer is *equal to* ours in everything but timing.

**See also:** [identical to](#identical-to), [similar to](#similar-to), [superior to](#superior-to), [inferior to](#inferior-to), [equal to](09-comparison-opposition.md#equal-to)

### equivalent to

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈkwɪvələnt tu/ &middot; *ih-KWIV-uh-lunt too* &middot; weak /əˈkwɪvələnt tə/
**Русский:** равнозначный, эквивалентный чему-л. — равный по действию, но не тождественный
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to technical; standard in science, finance, and translation, and common in ordinary argument
**Complement:** a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*equivalent to admitting the whole thing*) — **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *equivalent to admit the whole thing*. A *wh*-clause is fine: *equivalent to what the state pays*.
**Pattern:** *be equivalent to* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *roughly* / *functionally* / *morally equivalent to* &middot; *the equivalent of* [noun] — the noun frame, which switches to *of* &middot; *equivalent in* [respect] *to* [noun]
**Variants:** *tantamount to* is the near twin, restricted to the "amounts to the same thing" sense and nearly always about something bad; *identical to* claims sameness rather than equal value; *the same as* is the plain phrase; *comparable to* claims only that a comparison is fair.
**Contrast:** ***Equivalent to* claims equal value or effect; [identical to](#identical-to) claims the things are the same.*** A metric wrench may be equivalent to an inch wrench for the job without being identical to it. Note the switch of preposition between adjective and noun: *equivalent to a month's rent*, but *the equivalent of a month's rent*. Against [tantamount to](#tantamount-to), which is judgmental, *equivalent to* is neutral and quantitative. ✗ *equivalent with* is an error, and ✗ *equivalent as* is worse.

Equal in value, effect, or function to something else, without necessarily being the same thing.

1. The stipend is *equivalent to* about nineteen dollars an hour.
2. A year of the certificate program is *equivalent to* two semesters of credit.
3. Saying nothing here is *equivalent to* agreeing with them.
4. The generic is chemically *equivalent to* the brand and costs a fifth as much.
5. That degree is *equivalent to* an American master's for licensing purposes.

**See also:** [identical to](#identical-to), [tantamount to](#tantamount-to), [equal to](#equal-to), [synonymous with](#synonymous-with), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### essential to

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈsɛnʃəl tu/ &middot; *ih-SEN-shull too* &middot; weak /ɪˈsɛnʃəl tə/
**Русский:** необходимый для — то, без чего всё рушится
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *to* and *for* (*essential to keeping the doors open*) — **never a to-infinitive after the preposition.** The *it*-frame takes an infinitive with no preposition: *It is essential to arrive early*, and formal writing uses a subjunctive *that*-clause: *essential that he be present*.
**Pattern:** [X] *is essential to* [Y] &middot; [X] *is essential for* [purpose / verb-ing] &middot; *it is essential to* [verb] &middot; *it is essential that* [subjunctive clause] &middot; *absolutely essential*
**Variants:** *vital to*, *crucial to*, *critical to*, *indispensable to*, and *integral to* all pattern the same way and all take *to* by default and *for* before a purpose.
**Contrast:** ***To* names what the thing is essential to; *for* names what it is essential for.*** *Essential to the plan* (the plan needs it) versus *essential for germination* (it makes germination happen) — the drift is real but the two overlap widely and neither is wrong in most sentences. Watch the subjunctive: formal American writing keeps *It is essential that she be notified*, not *is notified*. ✗ *essential of* is an error.

Absolutely necessary — the thing without which the rest fails.

1. Trust is *essential to* any working partnership.
2. Water at the right temperature is *essential for* getting the dough to rise.
3. Timing was *essential to* the whole rescue.
4. It is *essential to* read the second page before signing.
5. Redundancy is *essential to* a system that can't be allowed to fail.

**See also:** [critical of](#critical-of), [relevant to](#relevant-to), [useful for](#useful-for), [suitable for](#suitable-for), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### excited about

**Pronunciation:** /ɪkˈsaɪtɪd əˈbaʊt/ &middot; *ik-SY-tid uh-BOWT* &middot; weak /ɪkˈsaɪtɪd əˈbaʊt/, casually /ɪkˈsaɪtɪd ˈbaʊt/
**Русский:** радостно предвкушающий что-л., воодушевлённый
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; extremely common in American speech and business writing
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *about* and *by* (*excited about starting*) — **never a to-infinitive after a preposition.** The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (*excited to announce*), and that frame is standard American English, not an error.
**Pattern:** *be excited about* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be excited by* [the cause] &middot; *be excited for* [an event, or a person on their behalf] &middot; *be excited to* [verb] &middot; *get excited about* [noun]
**Variants:** *thrilled about* / *to*, *psyched about* (informal), and *pumped about* (informal) all follow the same frames.
**Contrast:** **This adjective is unusual in taking both the gerund and the infinitive naturally, with a small difference:** *excited about starting* is about the whole prospect, *excited to start* is about the act itself and is the standard frame in announcements (*we're excited to share*). *Excited by* points at the cause as agent (*excited by the possibility*). ***Excited for*** has two uses that are worth separating: *excited for the game* (looking forward to an event, once informal and now general American) and *excited for you* (happy on your behalf). ✗ *excited of* is an error.

Eagerly looking forward to something, or stirred up by it.

1. The kids are *excited about* the trip to the coast.
2. We're *excited to* announce the new fall schedule.
3. She's *excited about* moving closer to her sister.
4. He was genuinely *excited for* her when the offer came through.
5. Everybody got *excited about* the renovation until they saw the estimate.

**See also:** [enthusiastic about](#enthusiastic-about), [eager for](#eager-for), [happy about](#happy-about), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about)

### exempt from

**Pronunciation:** /ɪɡˈzɛmpt frʌm/ &middot; *ig-ZEMPT from* &middot; weak /əɡˈzɛmp frəm/ — the *pt* cluster simplifies to /p/ before a consonant, so *exempt from* is normally "ig-ZEM-frum"
**Русский:** освобождённый от — от правила, налога, обязанности
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; the standard word of American tax, wage-and-hour law, and school and military rules
**Complement:** a noun phrase or a **gerund** — *exempt from paying sales tax* ✓ — and **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *exempt from pay sales tax*. The noun test is decisive: *exempt from the tax* ✓. No clause follows.
**Pattern:** *be exempt from* [rule / tax / requirement] &middot; *exempt* [someone] *from* [obligation] — the verb &middot; *exempt from* [noun] *under* [statute] &middot; *tax-exempt* / *an exempt employee* — attributive, no preposition
**Variants:** *excused from* is the everyday version for duties and classes; *immune from* is the legal one for liability and process; *free of* and *free from* cover the general sense; *not subject to* is the drafter's paraphrase.
**Contrast:** ***Exempt from* releases you from a rule that would otherwise apply to you*** — which is the exact opposite of [subject to](#subject-to), and the two often appear in the same sentence. Against [immune from, immune to](#immune-from-immune-to), the difference is what is escaped: an exemption is granted by a rule, an immunity is a shield against liability or process. In American employment law the word is a noun as well: an *exempt employee* is one exempt from overtime, and *nonexempt* is the pay-by-the-hour category. ✗ *exempt of* and ✗ *exempt to* are errors.

Released by a rule from something that would otherwise apply to you.

1. Nonprofits are *exempt from* the local business tax.
2. She's *exempt from* the language requirement because of the test score.
3. Nobody in this building is *exempt from* the fire drill.
4. Farm vehicles are *exempt from* the weight limit on that stretch.
5. Salaried supervisors are *exempt from* overtime under the state rule.

**See also:** [immune from, immune to](#immune-from-immune-to), [free of](#free-of), [subject to](#subject-to), [eligible for](#eligible-for), [from](02-core-relational.md#from)

### expert at

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛkspɜrt æt/ &middot; *EK-spurt at* &middot; weak /ˈɛkspɜrt ət/
**Русский:** искусный в чём-л. — прилагательное берёт at/in, а существительное an expert on
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *at* (*expert at getting people to talk*) and after *in* (*expert in tax law*) — **never a to-infinitive.** ✗ *expert to do it* is not English.
**Pattern:** *be expert at* [skill / verb-ing] &middot; *be expert in* [field] &middot; *an expert on* [subject] — the **noun** takes *on* &middot; *an expert in* [field] &middot; *expert witness* (no preposition)
**Variants:** *skilled at* / *in*, *proficient in*, *adept at*, and *a whiz at* cover the range from formal to slangy.
**Contrast:** **The adjective and the noun take different prepositions, and this is the trap.** ✓ *She is expert at reading X-rays* (adjective, *at* + skill) and ✓ *She is an expert on medieval coins* (noun, *on* + subject). *In* works for both when a **field** rather than a skill or a topic is meant: *expert in immunology*. ✗ *expert of* is an error in every frame. Note that American English uses the adjective *expert* far less than the noun; *she's an expert at* is commoner than *she's expert at*.

Highly skilled — at a practice, in a field, or, as a noun, on a subject.

1. He's *expert at* talking people down from a bad decision.
2. She's *expert in* the kind of soil chemistry nobody else here understands.
3. The witness is an *expert on* structural failures in older bridges.
4. They're *expert at* finding parking downtown, which is its own talent.
5. We need someone *expert in* export controls before we bid.

**See also:** [skilled at](#skilled-at), [good at](#good-at), [capable of](#capable-of), [at](01-core-in-on-at.md#at)

### familiar with

**Pronunciation:** /fəˈmɪljər wɪθ/ &middot; *fuh-MIL-yur with* &middot; weak /fəˈmɪljər wəθ/
**Русский:** хорошо знакомый с чем-л.; familiar to — знакомый кому-л., с другой стороны
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*familiar with running a crew*) — **never a to-infinitive.** A *wh*-clause is possible in the loose sense (*familiar with how it works*), and a *that*-clause is not: ✗ *familiar that*.
**Pattern:** *be familiar with* [thing known] &middot; *be familiar to* [person who knows it] &middot; *become* / *get familiar with* [noun] &middot; *be too familiar with* [person] — the disapproving sense
**Variants:** *acquainted with* is the formal equivalent; *up on* is the casual one (*up on the new rules*).
**Contrast:** ***The subject flips between the two prepositions, and this is the classic error.*** *I'm familiar with the software* — the knower is the subject. *The software is familiar to me* — the thing known is the subject. ✗ *The software is familiar with me* and ✗ *I'm familiar to the software* are both wrong. A third sense of the bare adjective means presumptuous intimacy (*don't be familiar*), which is why *too familiar with the customers* can be a complaint rather than a compliment.

Knowing something well enough to work with it — or, from the other side, being recognizable to someone.

1. Are you *familiar with* the county's permitting process?
2. Her face was *familiar to* everyone in the courtroom.
3. It takes a week to get *familiar with* the layout.
4. He's *familiar with* running a kitchen twice this size.
5. The tune was *familiar to* me but I couldn't place it.

**See also:** [accustomed to](#accustomed-to), [aware of](#aware-of), [used to](#used-to), [known for](#known-for), [with](02-core-relational.md#with), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### famous for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈfeɪməs fɔr/ &middot; *FAY-muss for* &middot; weak /ˈfeɪməs fər/
**Русский:** знаменитый чем-л.; famous as — знаменитый в качестве кого-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*famous for turning down the part*) after *for* — **never a to-infinitive.** After *as*, a noun phrase naming a role (*famous as a trial lawyer*).
**Pattern:** *be famous for* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be famous as* [role] &middot; *famous for being* [adjective] &middot; *world-famous for* [noun] &middot; *famous throughout* [place]
**Variants:** *known for* is the neutral everyday version; *renowned for* and *celebrated for* are the formal ones; *notorious for* is the negative one.
**Contrast:** ***For* names what you did; *as* names what you are.*** *Famous for her Fourth Symphony* versus *famous as a conductor*. Against *notorious for*, which is always negative, *famous for* is neutral to positive — so *famous for cutting corners* is a joke that works because it borrows the wrong word on purpose. ✗ *famous of* and ✗ *famous by* are errors.

Widely known — for something done, or as something one is.

1. The town is *famous for* a pie nobody there actually eats.
2. She's *famous for* never missing a deadline.
3. He was *famous as* a defense attorney long before the trial.
4. That stretch of coast is *famous for* fog in July.
5. The diner is *famous for* staying open through three hurricanes.

**See also:** [known for](#known-for), [notorious for](#notorious-for), [popular with](#popular-with), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### fed up with

**Pronunciation:** /ˌfɛd ˈʌp wɪθ/ &middot; *fed-UP with* &middot; weak /ˌfɛd ˈʌp wəθ/
**Русский:** сытый по горло чем-л. — разг.
**Type:** adjective phrase + preposition &middot; **Register:** informal to neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*fed up with waiting*) — **never a to-infinitive** and **never a clause.**
**Pattern:** *be fed up with* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *get fed up with* [noun] &middot; *fed up* (alone, with no complement) &middot; *thoroughly* / *completely fed up with* [noun]
**Variants:** *sick of*, *tired of*, *sick and tired of*, and *done with* are the American equivalents. ***Fed up of*** is British colloquial and is an error in American English.
**Contrast:** ***Fed up* takes *with*; *sick* and *tired* take *of*.*** ✗ *fed up of the noise* and ✗ *sick with the noise* both go wrong by borrowing the other pattern. Against *bored with*, which reports flatness, *fed up with* reports irritation that has been building — it is the end of a person's patience, not the end of their interest. The three-word intensifier *sick and tired of* is fixed and cannot be rebuilt as ✗ *sick and fed up of*.

Out of patience with something that has gone on too long.

1. She's *fed up with* being the only one who answers the phone.
2. Residents are *fed up with* the potholes on Elm.
3. I got *fed up with* the whole process and hired someone.
4. He's *fed up with* excuses about the delivery date.
5. By March everyone is *fed up with* winter.

**See also:** [sick of](#sick-of), [tired of](#tired-of), [bored with](#bored-with), [annoyed with](#annoyed-with), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### fond of

**Pronunciation:** /fɑnd ʌv/ &middot; *FOND uv* &middot; weak /fɑnd əv/
**Русский:** любящий что-л., питающий давнюю склонность к
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral, slightly old-fashioned and warm
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*fond of telling that story*) — **never a to-infinitive.** ✗ *fond to tell* is not English.
**Pattern:** *be fond of* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *grow* / *become fond of* [noun] &middot; *fond of saying* [quotation] &middot; *not overly fond of* [noun]
**Variants:** *keen on* is British; *partial to* is the same shade with a hint of indulgence; *like* is the plain verb.
**Contrast:** ***Fond of* + *-ing* often carries a note of habit rather than pleasure**: *He's fond of reminding us that he warned them* is a criticism, not a compliment. The bare adjective before a noun means affectionate (*a fond farewell*, *fond memories*) and is a different use with no preposition. ✗ *fond about* and ✗ *fond for* are errors, though the noun takes *for*: *a fondness for old maps*.

Having affection or a liking for something — often an established, habitual one.

1. She's *fond of* her students in a way that shows up in the details.
2. He's *fond of* saying the roof will last another winter.
3. I've grown *fond of* the noise from the rail yard.
4. They're not overly *fond of* surprises at that office.
5. My grandmother was *fond of* anything with cardamom in it.

**See also:** [crazy about](#crazy-about), [keen on](#keen-on), [proud of](#proud-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### founded on, founded upon

**Pronunciation:** /ˈfaʊndɪd ɑn, ˈfaʊndɪd əˈpɑn/ &middot; *FOWN-did on, FOWN-did uh-PON* &middot; weak /ˈfaʊndəd ən/
**Русский:** основанный на — на принципе, доказательствах или допущении
**Type:** adjective (past participle) + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; the natural home is writing about principles, claims, and institutions, and *upon* is the rhetorical variant
**Complement:** a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*founded on keeping everyone in the dark*) — **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *founded on keep everyone in the dark*. Also *founded in* before an abstract quality (*founded in fact*, *founded in law*), where *in* is idiomatic.
**Pattern:** *be founded on* [principle / evidence] &middot; *founded upon* [noun] — the formal variant &middot; *well founded* / *ill founded* — as one word in *unfounded* &middot; *found* [an institution] *on* [principle] — the active verb
**Variants:** *based on* is the ordinary everyday equivalent; *grounded in* is the near twin and prefers *in*; *predicated on* is the drier formal one; *resting on* is the metaphor made visible.
**Contrast:** Against [based on](#based-on), which is neutral and common, *founded on* carries the architectural metaphor and suits institutions, doctrines, and claims — a republic founded on a written constitution, a suspicion founded on nothing. Against [grounded in](#grounded-in), note the preposition split: things are *founded on* a foundation but *grounded in* a medium. The negative has fused into a single word: *an unfounded rumor*, never ✗ *a not founded rumor*. ✗ *founded in* is fine before *fact*, *law*, and *truth*, but ✗ *founded of* and ✗ *founded to* are errors.

Built on something as its base — a principle, a body of evidence, or an assumption.

1. The whole complaint is *founded on* a misreading of one paragraph.
2. Their objection is *founded upon* forty years of practice in the district.
3. A partnership *founded on* a handshake is hard to unwind.
4. The estimate was *founded on* prices from before the tariffs.
5. Her confidence is *founded on* having done the job twice before.

**See also:** [based on](#based-on), [grounded in](#grounded-in), [derived from](#derived-from), [dependent on](#dependent-on), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### fraught with

**Pronunciation:** /frɔt wɪθ/ &middot; *FRAWT with* &middot; weak /frɔt wəθ/, the *t* often lost before the *w*: "FRAW-with"
**Русский:** чреватый чем-л. — риском, трудностями, напряжением; книжн.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to journalistic; the phrase *fraught with peril* and *fraught with risk* are near-clichés of American editorial writing
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming something **bad and abundant** — danger, difficulty, risk, error, tension. **Never a gerund** (✗ *fraught with delaying*) and never a clause. The complement is essentially always negative: ✗ *fraught with opportunity* reads as a mistake, though the word once meant simply "laden."
**Pattern:** *be fraught with* [danger / difficulty] &middot; *a process fraught with* [noun] &middot; *fraught* — used alone as a modern adjective meaning tense (*a fraught conversation*), with no complement
**Variants:** *riddled with* is the near twin for flaws and errors; *rife with* is the journalist's word for something bad and widespread; *replete with* is the neutral-to-positive counterpart; *laden with* keeps the original cargo metaphor.
**Contrast:** Against [replete with](#replete-with), which is neutral or admiring and means "abundantly supplied," *fraught with* is always negative — the two words are the same idea with opposite polarity. Note the recent and now fully standard **bare** use of *fraught*: *the whole meeting was fraught* takes no complement at all and means tense rather than dangerous, and it is an adjective, not this preposition pattern. ✗ *fraught of* and ✗ *fraught by* are errors.

Loaded with something bad — risk, difficulty, tension, or error.

1. The handoff between the two systems is *fraught with* opportunities for error.
2. Any conversation about the will is *fraught with* forty years of grievance.
3. The route is *fraught with* switchbacks and no place to turn around.
4. Early estimates were *fraught with* assumptions nobody wrote down.
5. Their first season back was *fraught with* injuries.

**See also:** [replete with](#replete-with), [encumbered by, encumbered with](#encumbered-by-encumbered-with), [full of](#full-of), [crowded with](#crowded-with), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### free of

**Pronunciation:** /fri ʌv/ &middot; *FREE uv* &middot; weak /fri əv/
**Русский:** свободный от, лишённый чего-л. нежелательного
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase after *of* and *from* (*free of debt*, *free from interference*) — **a gerund is possible but rare** (*free from having to explain herself*). The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (*free to leave*), which is a different frame meaning permitted, not lacking.
**Pattern:** *be free of* [unwanted thing] &middot; *be free from* [an imposed condition] &middot; *be free to* [verb] &middot; [noun]*-free* as a suffix: *tax-free*, *gluten-free*, *scot-free*
**Variants:** *free of* and *free from* overlap almost completely; *from* is slightly more formal and is preferred where the thing removed was imposed by someone (*free from persecution*). *Rid of* and *devoid of* are the neighbors, both with *of*.
**Contrast:** ***Free of* / *from* means lacking; *free to* means allowed.*** *Free of charge* costs nothing; *free to go* may leave. The suffix *-free* has become the productive American way to say the same thing in a single word (*fee-free*, *fragrance-free*) and takes no preposition at all. ✗ *free with* means something different again — generous, and often too generous: *free with other people's money*.

Without something unwanted, or released from it.

1. The house came back from inspection *free of* any structural issues.
2. She's been *free of* the debt for two years.
3. The area is now *free from* the flooding that plagued it for a decade.
4. You're *free to* leave whenever you like.
5. Their new formula is *free of* dyes and preservatives.

**See also:** [safe from](#safe-from), [absent from](#absent-from), [independent of](#independent-of), [short of](#short-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### friendly with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈfrɛndli wɪθ/ &middot; *FREND-lee with* &middot; weak /ˈfrɛndli wəθ/
**Русский:** в дружеских отношениях с; friendly to — приветливый, доброжелательный к
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a **person or group** after *with* and *to*; a noun after *toward* — **never a gerund** and **never a to-infinitive** after any of them.
**Pattern:** *be friendly with* [person] &middot; *be friendly to* / *toward* [person] &middot; *get friendly with* [person] &middot; [noun]*-friendly* as a suffix: *kid-friendly*, *pet-friendly*, *user-friendly*
**Variants:** *toward* is the American spelling; *towards* is the British one, and both are read as ordinary in the United States. *On good terms with* is the formal equivalent of *friendly with*.
**Contrast:** ***Friendly with* means you have a relationship; *friendly to* means you behave pleasantly.*** *She's friendly with the mayor* implies they know each other; *She's friendly to everyone who comes in* describes her manner and implies nothing about acquaintance. That difference matters — the first can insinuate favoritism, which is exactly why reporters choose it. The productive suffix *-friendly* means accommodating to and takes no preposition: *a dog-friendly patio*. ✗ *friendly of* is an error, though *That was friendly of you* is a different, correct frame.

On good terms with someone, or pleasant in manner toward them.

1. He's been *friendly with* the family since before the kids were born.
2. The staff is *friendly to* anyone who walks in, regulars or not.
3. She got *friendly with* the contractor and now gets the good slots.
4. Their policy is *friendly toward* small vendors.
5. It's a *friendly* town, *friendly to* newcomers in a way that surprised us.

**See also:** [kind to](#kind-to), [nice to](#nice-to), [polite to](#polite-to), [popular with](#popular-with), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### frightened of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈfraɪtnd ʌv/ &middot; *FRY-tund uv* &middot; weak /ˈfraɪtnd əv/
**Русский:** напуганный чем-л., боящийся
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; slightly more formal than *scared of*
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*frightened of falling*) after *of*; noun phrase after *by* — **never a to-infinitive after a preposition.** The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (*frightened to move*), a separate frame.
**Pattern:** *be frightened of* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be frightened by* [the event that caused it] &middot; *be frightened to* [verb] &middot; *be frightened for* [someone] &middot; *frighten* [someone] (no preposition on the object)
**Variants:** *afraid of* and *scared of* are the everyday twins; *terrified of* and *petrified of* are the intensified ones.
**Contrast:** ***Frightened of* is a standing fear; *frightened by* is a single event.*** *She's frightened of dogs* describes a phobia; *She was frightened by the dog* describes one afternoon. This *of* / *by* split is the same one that runs through *annoyed*, *bored*, *confused*, and *shocked*, and it is the most transferable pattern in this file: ***of*** or ***with*** for the standing state, ***by*** for the agent that produced it. And as with *afraid*, *frightened to move* means too scared to act, not scared of the movement.

Feeling fear — of something in general, or by something in particular.

1. He's been *frightened of* elevators since the outage.
2. The horse was *frightened by* the truck backfiring.
3. She was too *frightened to* say anything at the time.
4. Small children are often *frightened of* men with beards.
5. They were *frightened for* their neighbors, whose lights had been off for days.

**See also:** [afraid of](#afraid-of), [scared of](#scared-of), [nervous about](#nervous-about), [shocked at](#shocked-at), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### full of

**Pronunciation:** /fʊl ʌv/ &middot; *FULL uv* &middot; weak /fʊl əv/, and in fast speech /ˈfʊlə/ — the *fulla* of *fulla nonsense*
**Русский:** полный чего-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, usually without an article for mass and plural nouns (*full of sand*, *full of tourists*) — **a gerund is not used**, and **never a to-infinitive.**
**Pattern:** *be full of* [noun] &middot; *full of* [abstract quality]: *full of ideas*, *full of himself* &middot; *fill* [something] *with* [something] — the verb takes *with* &middot; *filled with* [noun]
**Variants:** *filled with*, *packed with*, and *loaded with* take *with*; *replete with* is the formal one; *chock-full of* is the emphatic American one.
**Contrast:** ***Full takes *of*; *fill* and *filled* take *with*.*** ✗ *full with water* and ✗ *filled of water* are the two errors, and they are mirror images. The idiomatic *full of himself* means conceited; *full of it* is a polite American way to say someone is lying. Note that *full* also works with no complement (*I'm full*), where it means having eaten enough.

Containing as much as it can hold, or containing a great deal of something.

1. The trunk was *full of* road salt and jumper cables.
2. Her first draft is *full of* good sentences in the wrong order.
3. The lot was *full of* cars with out-of-state plates.
4. He's *full of* plans and short on follow-through.
5. The room was *full of* people who had never met the deceased.

**See also:** [crowded with](#crowded-with), [rich in](#rich-in), [short of](#short-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### furious with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈfjʊriəs wɪθ/ &middot; *FYOOR-ee-uss with* &middot; weak /ˈfjʊriəs wəθ/
**Русский:** в ярости на кого-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; the strongest ordinary word for anger
**Complement:** a **person** after *with*; a noun phrase or **gerund** after *about* and *at* (*furious about being kept waiting*) — **never a to-infinitive.** A *that*-clause attaches to the bare adjective: *furious that no one had called*.
**Pattern:** *be furious with* [person] &middot; *be furious about* / *at* / *over* [situation] &middot; *be furious* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *furious with* [person] *for* [verb-ing]
**Variants:** *livid*, *irate*, and *incensed* are the same intensity; *incensed* prefers *at* or *by*, *irate* prefers *about* or *at*.
**Contrast:** **Same split as *angry*: *with* for the person, *about* or *over* for the situation, *at* for either.** *Furious with her lawyer about the delay* fills both slots at once. Note that *furious* also means very fast (*a furious pace*), a sense with no preposition at all and no anger in it. ✗ *furious on* is an error.

Extremely angry.

1. She was *furious with* the contractor for starting without a permit.
2. Parents were *furious about* the way the closure was announced.
3. He's *furious at* himself for not reading the fine print.
4. The whole shop was *furious with* management by Thursday.
5. They were *furious that* nobody had thought to call the family first.

**See also:** [angry with](#angry-with), [mad at](#mad-at), [annoyed with](#annoyed-with), [upset about](#upset-about), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### generous with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdʒɛnərəs wɪθ/ &middot; *JEN-uh-russ with* &middot; weak /ˈdʒɛnərəs wəθ/
**Русский:** щедрый на что-л.; generous to — щедрый к кому-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a noun naming **what is given** after *with* (*generous with her time*); a **person** after *to* and *toward* — **never a gerund** after either, and never a to-infinitive, though the *it*-frame takes one: *It was generous of you to offer.*
**Pattern:** *be generous with* [resource] &middot; *be generous to* / *toward* [person] &middot; *it is generous of* [someone] *to* [verb] &middot; *generous in* [respect]
**Variants:** *liberal with* is the older, less loaded version; *free with* is the version that implies excess (*free with his opinions*).
**Contrast:** ***With* takes what you give; *to* takes who you give it to.*** *Generous with his time* and *generous to his employees* both work, and *generous to his time* does not. The *of*-frame is a third pattern common to all judgment adjectives — *kind of you*, *rude of him*, *generous of them* — where *of* names the doer and an infinitive names the deed. ✗ *generous for* is an error.

Giving freely — of a resource, or to a person.

1. She's *generous with* her time in a way that costs her weekends.
2. The company has been *generous to* families of the crew.
3. He's *generous with* praise and stingy with raises.
4. It was *generous of* you *to* cover the difference.
5. Be *generous with* the butter or the whole thing sticks.

**See also:** [kind to](#kind-to), [gentle with](#gentle-with), [patient with](#patient-with), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### gentle with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdʒɛntəl wɪθ/ &middot; *JEN-tull with* &middot; weak /ˈdʒɛntəl wəθ/
**Русский:** бережно, мягко обращающийся с кем-л. или чем-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a noun naming **what is handled** after *with* — a person, an animal, or an object. **Never a gerund**, **never a to-infinitive**, and never a clause. The *it*-frame takes an infinitive: *It was gentle of him to say it that way* is possible but rare.
**Pattern:** *be gentle with* [person / animal / object] &middot; *go gentle on* [someone] — informal &middot; *gentle on* [something], of products: *gentle on skin*
**Variants:** *easy on* is the informal equivalent (*go easy on him*); *tender with* is the warmer one; *careful with* overlaps almost entirely.
**Contrast:** ***Gentle takes *with*, not *to*,*** which breaks the pattern that governs most of the behavior adjectives — *kind to*, *cruel to*, *rude to*, but *gentle with* and *patient with*. The reason is that *gentle* and *patient* describe the **manner of handling** rather than the direction of treatment, and *with* is the preposition of manner. Product English uses a fourth frame, *gentle on*: *gentle on sensitive skin*. ✗ *gentle to* is not idiomatic in American English.

Handling someone or something carefully, without force.

1. Be *gentle with* the latch; it's original to the house.
2. She's remarkably *gentle with* frightened animals.
3. The nurse was *gentle with* him during the whole exam.
4. Go easy — be *gentle with* yourself about the first draft.
5. The formula is *gentle on* skin and still gets the grease off.

**See also:** [careful with](#careful-with), [patient with](#patient-with), [kind to](#kind-to), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### germane to

**Pronunciation:** /dʒərˈmeɪn tu/ &middot; *jur-MAYN too* &middot; weak /dʒərˈmeɪn tə/
**Русский:** имеющий прямое отношение к обсуждаемому предмету — книжн.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; an American meets it in parliamentary procedure (*the amendment is not germane to the bill*), in court, and in academic argument
**Complement:** a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*germane to deciding the motion*) — **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *germane to decide the motion*. A *wh*-clause is fine: *germane to what the committee is considering*.
**Pattern:** *be germane to* [question / matter] &middot; *not germane to* [noun] — the commonest frame, since the word is usually used to rule something out &middot; *strictly* / *directly germane to*
**Variants:** *relevant to* is the neutral everyday equivalent; *pertinent to* is its formal twin; *material to* is the legal one and is stronger; *apposite to* is the literary one; *on point* is the American lawyer's idiom.
**Contrast:** ***Germane to* is the word for topical fit, and its home is the rules of debate*** — in the House and in most American legislatures an amendment must be germane to the bill, meaning it must address the same subject. Against [relevant to](#relevant-to), which is the general word, *germane to* claims a closer relation: relevant evidence bears on the case, a germane amendment belongs to the very question at hand. Against [material to](#material-to), which asks whether something could change the outcome, *germane to* asks only whether it belongs to the subject. ✗ *germane with* and ✗ *germane for* are errors.

Belonging to the very subject under discussion, not merely touching on it.

1. The chair ruled that the amendment was not *germane to* the bill.
2. Whatever happened in 2011 is not *germane to* this year's audit.
3. Only questions *germane to* the witness's direct testimony are allowed.
4. Keep the comments *germane to* the item on the agenda.
5. That objection is *germane to* the schedule but not to the design.

**See also:** [relevant to](#relevant-to), [pertinent to](#pertinent-to), [material to](#material-to), [specific to](#specific-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### glad about

**Pronunciation:** /ɡlæd əˈbaʊt/ &middot; *GLAD uh-BOWT* &middot; weak /ɡlæd əˈbaʊt/
**Русский:** радующийся тому, что что-л. так
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral, slightly old-fashioned in the *about* frame
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *about* (*glad about not having to drive*) — **never a to-infinitive after *about***. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (*glad to help*) and a *that*-clause (*glad that you called*), and those two frames are far commoner than the prepositional one.
**Pattern:** *be glad about* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be glad of* [something one is grateful for] &middot; *be glad to* [verb] &middot; *be glad* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *be glad for* [someone]
**Variants:** *happy about* is the everyday equivalent and covers all the same frames; *pleased about* is the slightly more formal one.
**Contrast:** ***Glad to* + verb is the workhorse and means willing (*glad to help*); *glad about* + noun reports a feeling about a state of affairs.*** *Glad of* is an older frame that survives in *glad of the company* and *glad of the chance* and reads as literary in American English. *Glad for you* means pleased on your behalf, exactly parallel to *happy for you* and *excited for you*. ✗ *glad with* is an error.

Pleased that something is the case.

1. I'm *glad about* the way the whole thing worked out.
2. She's *glad about* not having to make the drive twice.
3. He was *glad of* the company on such a long stretch.
4. We'd be *glad to* look at the drawings tomorrow.
5. I'm just *glad* that somebody said it out loud.

**See also:** [happy about](#happy-about), [pleased with](#pleased-with), [grateful to](#grateful-to), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about)

### good at

**Pronunciation:** /ɡʊd æt/ &middot; *GOOD at* &middot; weak /ɡʊd ət/
**Русский:** умелый в чём-л. — at умение, for полезный, with умеющий обращаться с, to добрый к
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *at* (*good at listening*) — **never a to-infinitive.** ✗ *good at listen* and ✗ *good to listening* are the two ways this goes wrong. After *for*, a person or purpose; after *with*, a category of thing handled.
**Pattern:** *be good at* [skill / verb-ing] &middot; *be good for* [person / purpose] &middot; *be good with* [people / money / one's hands] &middot; *be good to* [person] &middot; *be good about* [verb-ing] &middot; *no good at* [noun]
**Variants:** *skilled at*, *strong at*, and *a natural at* are the formal, the sporting, and the admiring versions.
**Contrast:** **This is the four-way split that every learner of English eventually has to memorize.** ***Good at*** = skilled: *good at chemistry*. ***Good for*** = beneficial: *good for your knees*; and, in a second sense, *good for one more season* = lasting. ***Good with*** = able to handle a category: *good with kids*, *good with numbers*, *good with her hands*. ***Good to*** = kind: *She was good to me*. A fifth, *good about* + *-ing*, means reliably doing something: *good about calling ahead*. ✗ *good in math* is an error in American English, though *good in a crisis* is fixed and correct.

Skilled, beneficial, capable with something, or kind — the preposition decides which.

1. She's *good at* explaining the same thing four different ways.
2. Walking is *good for* your back and your temper.
3. He's *good with* horses and hopeless with paperwork.
4. That neighbor was *good to* us when my father was sick.
5. They're *good about* letting you know before the water goes off.

**See also:** [bad at](#bad-at), [skilled at](#skilled-at), [expert at](#expert-at), [useful for](#useful-for), [kind to](#kind-to), [at](01-core-in-on-at.md#at)

### grateful to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɡreɪtfəl tu/ &middot; *GRAYT-full too* &middot; weak /ˈɡreɪtfəl tə/
**Русский:** благодарный кому-л. (to) за что-л. (for)
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** a **person or institution** after *to*; a noun phrase or **gerund** after *for* (*grateful for being given a second chance*) — **never a to-infinitive after a preposition.** The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (*grateful to have it*) and a *that*-clause (*grateful that no one was hurt*).
**Pattern:** *be grateful to* [person] &middot; *be grateful for* [thing] &middot; *be grateful to* [person] *for* [thing / verb-ing] &middot; *be grateful to* [verb] &middot; *be grateful* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *I'd be grateful if you would* [verb]
**Variants:** *thankful for* covers the *for* half only; *indebted to* and *obliged to* cover the *to* half and are formal.
**Contrast:** ***To* takes the person, *for* takes the thing,*** and both slots can be filled at once: *grateful to the neighbors for the ride*. The trap is the double *to*: *I'm grateful to have it* uses an infinitive, not the person-slot, so ✗ *I'm grateful to the chance* is an error where *for* was needed. *Thankful* cannot take a person at all: ✗ *thankful to the neighbors* is odd, though *thankful to God* is a fixed exception.

Feeling thanks — to whoever gave it, for whatever was given.

1. We're *grateful to* the crew that stayed through the night.
2. She's *grateful for* every extra week the treatment bought.
3. I'm *grateful to* my sister *for* not saying I told you so.
4. He'd be *grateful to* have the report by Friday.
5. They were *grateful that* the storm turned before it reached the harbor.

**See also:** [thankful for](#thankful-for), [proud of](#proud-of), [sorry for](#sorry-for), [to](02-core-relational.md#to), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### grounded in

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɡraʊndɪd ɪn/ &middot; *GROWN-did in* &middot; weak /ˈɡraʊndəd ən/
**Русский:** опирающийся на — на доказательства, традицию или практический опыт
**Type:** adjective (past participle) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; common in academic writing, policy prose, and criticism
**Complement:** a noun phrase — a body of evidence, a tradition, a discipline — or a **gerund** (*grounded in doing the work yourself*). **Never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *grounded in do the work*. No clause follows.
**Pattern:** *be grounded in* [evidence / tradition] &middot; *firmly* / *solidly* / *deeply grounded in* &middot; *ground* [a claim] *in* [evidence] — the verb &middot; *well grounded in* [subject] — meaning well trained in it
**Variants:** *rooted in* is the organic metaphor and the commonest alternative; *based on* is the plain word; *founded on* takes *on* rather than *in*; *anchored in* is the nautical version.
**Contrast:** **The preposition is *in*, not *on*, and the metaphor explains why:** you are *grounded in* a medium that surrounds and holds you, but *founded on* a base you sit atop. ✗ *grounded on* turns up and reads as a slip. Note the two other *grounded*s an American hears daily: the punished teenager (*grounded for a week*), and the aircraft that cannot fly (*the fleet was grounded*) — neither takes *in*. And note *well-grounded in*, which is about a person's training rather than an argument's support.

Resting on something solid — evidence, tradition, or practical experience.

1. The recommendation is *grounded in* three years of intake data.
2. Her teaching is *grounded in* the years she spent on the floor of a plant.
3. An argument *grounded in* nothing but anecdote won't survive review.
4. The program is *grounded in* the same theory the state manual uses.
5. He wanted a plan *grounded in* what the crews could actually do.

**See also:** [based on](#based-on), [founded on, founded upon](#founded-on-founded-upon), [derived from](#derived-from), [implicit in](#implicit-in), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### guilty of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɡɪlti ʌv/ &middot; *GIL-tee uv* &middot; weak /ˈɡɪlti əv/
**Русский:** виновный в чём-л.; guilty about — испытывающий чувство вины из-за мелочи
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; legal in the *of* frame
**Complement:** a noun naming an **offense**, or a **gerund** naming the act (*guilty of falsifying the records*) after *of* — **never a to-infinitive.** After *about*, a noun phrase or gerund naming what you feel bad over (*guilty about leaving early*).
**Pattern:** *be* / *be found guilty of* [offense / verb-ing] &middot; *plead guilty to* [charge] — the one frame with *to* &middot; *feel guilty about* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *guilty as charged* &middot; *guilty conscience* (no preposition)
**Variants:** *culpable of* is the formal legal version; *responsible for* covers the non-criminal sense of having caused something.
**Contrast:** ***Guilty of* is about what you did; *guilty about* is about how you feel.*** *He's guilty of tax fraud* is a verdict; *He feels guilty about the tax return* is a mood. The two do not swap: ✗ *guilty about tax fraud* would report only remorse, and ✗ *feel guilty of leaving early* is wrong. A third preposition belongs to one fixed frame: you **plead guilty *to* a charge** — ✓ *pleaded guilty to two counts*, ✗ *pleaded guilty of two counts* — while the verdict itself keeps *of*: *found guilty of two counts*. The opposite of the legal sense is *innocent of*, also with *of*. ✗ *guilty for* is an error, though *guilty for having done it* appears in speech.

Having committed an offense — or, with *about*, feeling bad over something minor.

1. He was found *guilty of* two counts of mail fraud.
2. She pleaded *guilty to* the lesser charge, which is the one frame that uses *to*.
3. I'm *guilty of* forgetting names about four seconds after hearing them.
4. He feels *guilty about* not visiting more often.
5. The report found the agency *guilty of* nothing worse than sloppiness.

**See also:** [innocent of](#innocent-of), [ashamed of](#ashamed-of), [responsible for](#responsible-for), [sorry for](#sorry-for), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### happy about

**Pronunciation:** /ˈhæpi əˈbaʊt/ &middot; *HAP-ee uh-BOWT* &middot; weak /ˈhæpi əˈbaʊt/, casually /ˈhæpi ˈbaʊt/
**Русский:** довольный чем-л.; happy with результатом, happy for за кого-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *about* and *with* (*happy about being asked*) — **never a to-infinitive after a preposition.** The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (*happy to help*) and a *that*-clause (*happy that it's settled*).
**Pattern:** *be happy about* [situation / verb-ing] &middot; *be happy with* [thing or result being judged] &middot; *be happy for* [person] &middot; *be happy to* [verb] &middot; *be happy* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *not happy about* [noun]
**Variants:** *pleased about* / *with* is the more formal twin; *glad about* the slightly old-fashioned one; *thrilled about* the intensified one.
**Contrast:** **Three prepositions, three targets.** ***About*** = the situation: *happy about the move*. ***With*** = your verdict on a thing you are assessing: *happy with the contractor's work* — this is the one used in reviews and evaluations. ***For*** = on someone else's behalf: *happy for you*, which says nothing about your own situation. The infinitive frame *happy to* is a politeness formula in American business writing and means willing. The litotes *not happy about* is standard understatement for angry. ✗ *happy of* is an error.

Pleased — about a state of affairs, with a result, or for someone else.

1. Nobody was *happy about* the schedule change, but nobody fought it.
2. Are you *happy with* how the floors turned out?
3. She's genuinely *happy for* him, which is harder than it sounds.
4. I'd be *happy to* send over what we have.
5. Management wasn't *happy about* finding out from the newspaper.

**See also:** [pleased with](#pleased-with), [glad about](#glad-about), [satisfied with](#satisfied-with), [excited about](#excited-about), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about)

### harmful to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈhɑrmfəl tu/ &middot; *HARM-full too* &middot; weak /ˈhɑrmfəl tə/
**Русский:** вредный для, причиняющий вред
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; the everyday equivalent is *bad for*
**Complement:** noun phrase naming the **thing damaged** — a person, a body part, an environment, an interest. A **gerund** is possible where a practice is meant (*harmful to letting the market clear* is awkward; prefer a noun). **Never a to-infinitive**, never a clause.
**Pattern:** [X] *is harmful to* [Y] &middot; *potentially* / *directly harmful to* [noun] &middot; *harmful effects on* [noun] — the **noun** *effect* takes *on*, not *to*
**Variants:** *damaging to*, *injurious to*, and *detrimental to* are the formal equivalents, all with *to*. *Bad for* is the everyday one, with *for*.
**Contrast:** ***Harmful to* takes *to*; *bad for* takes *for*.*** They mean the same thing and their prepositions do not trade: ✗ *harmful for your lungs* is a common slip. Watch the related nouns, which take a third preposition: *harm to the reef* but *an effect on the reef* and *an impact on the reef*. ✗ *harmful with* is an error.

Causing damage or injury to something.

1. Sustained noise at that level is *harmful to* hearing.
2. The runoff is *harmful to* everything downstream of the plant.
3. Rumors like that are *harmful to* morale long after they're disproved.
4. The additive was found to be *harmful to* pollinators.
5. Delay is *harmful to* the case in ways the client doesn't see yet.

**See also:** [bad at](#bad-at), [safe from](#safe-from), [vulnerable to](#vulnerable-to), [sensitive to](#sensitive-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### hooked on

**Pronunciation:** /hʊkt ɑn/ &middot; *HOOKT on* &middot; weak /hʊkt ən/
**Русский:** подсевший на что-л., не могущий оторваться — разг., чаще шутливо
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** informal
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*hooked on watching the whole series at once*) — **never a to-infinitive** and **never a clause.**
**Pattern:** *be* / *get hooked on* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *get* [someone] *hooked on* [noun] &middot; *hooked* (alone): *one episode and I was hooked*
**Variants:** *addicted to* is the neutral-to-clinical equivalent with a different preposition; *into* is the mild informal one (*she's into birding*).
**Contrast:** ***Hooked takes *on*; *addicted* takes *to*.*** ✗ *hooked to* and ✗ *addicted on* are the two mirror errors, and they are common. Register also separates them: *hooked on* is almost always light, so using it for a real dependency reads as either euphemism or bravado. Note the unrelated literal *hooked to*, meaning physically attached (*hooked to a monitor*), which is correct and is a different word combination entirely.

Unable to leave something alone — usually said lightly.

1. She got *hooked on* crossword puzzles during the shutdown.
2. One taste and the kids were *hooked on* the stuff.
3. He's been *hooked on* those late-night repair videos for a year.
4. Don't get *hooked on* checking the numbers every hour.
5. The patient was *hooked to* a monitor — a different *hooked* entirely.

**See also:** [addicted to](#addicted-to), [obsessed with](#obsessed-with), [crazy about](#crazy-about), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### hopeless at

**Pronunciation:** /ˈhoʊpləs æt/ &middot; *HOHP-luss at* &middot; weak /ˈhoʊpləs ət/
**Русский:** безнадёжно неспособный к чему-л. — разг., чаще о себе
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** informal; usually self-deprecating
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *at* (*hopeless at remembering birthdays*) — **never a to-infinitive.** After *with*, a category handled badly. There is no clause frame.
**Pattern:** *be hopeless at* [skill / verb-ing] &middot; *be hopeless with* [category] &middot; *a hopeless case* &middot; *hopeless* alone, of a situation: *the traffic is hopeless*
**Variants:** *terrible at*, *useless at*, *no good at*, and *bad at* are the same idea at different volumes; *hopeless* is the most theatrical and the least literal.
**Contrast:** **All the "not good at" adjectives take *at* for skills and *with* for categories** — *hopeless at chess*, *hopeless with computers* — exactly parallel to *good at* / *good with*. ✗ *hopeless in math* is an error in American English. Do not confuse the skill sense with the situational one: *the situation is hopeless* means beyond saving and takes no preposition at all.

Very bad at something — said about oneself far more often than about anyone else.

1. I'm *hopeless at* keeping plants alive.
2. He's *hopeless at* saying no to a second helping.
3. She's *hopeless with* anything that requires a manual.
4. We're both *hopeless at* directions and we married each other anyway.
5. They're *hopeless at* scheduling and excellent at everything after that.

**See also:** [bad at](#bad-at), [terrible at](#terrible-at), [poor at](#poor-at), [good at](#good-at), [at](01-core-in-on-at.md#at)

### identical to

**Pronunciation:** /aɪˈdɛntɪkəl tu/ &middot; *eye-DEN-tih-kull too* &middot; weak /aɪˈdɛntɪkəl tə/
**Русский:** идентичный, в точности такой же, как
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase or pronoun — **never a gerund**, **never a to-infinitive**, and never a clause. A free relative works: *identical to what we ordered*.
**Pattern:** [X] *is identical to* [Y] &middot; [X] *is identical with* [Y] &middot; *virtually* / *nearly* / *all but identical to* [noun] &middot; *identical twins* (no preposition)
**Variants:** ***identical with*** is the older form, still standard and preferred in logic and mathematics (*A is identical with B*), while ***identical to*** is the ordinary modern choice in American English and the safer default.
**Contrast:** ***Identical takes *to* or *with*, never *than* and never *as*.*** ✗ *identical than the first* and ✗ *identical as the first* are both wrong; the *as* error comes from *the same as*, which is the one member of this family that does take *as*. Note the strength ladder: *similar to* (alike), *identical to* (indistinguishable), *the same as* (one and the same, or indistinguishable). Purists once objected to *nearly identical* on the grounds that identity admits no degrees; it is universal and unremarkable now.

Exactly the same as something else in every detail.

1. The second invoice was *identical to* the first, down to the typo.
2. Her handwriting is nearly *identical to* her mother's.
3. The replacement part is *identical to* the one that failed.
4. Their proposal is *identical with* ours in every material respect.
5. The two buildings are *identical to* each other from the street.

**See also:** [similar to](#similar-to), [equal to](#equal-to), [different from](#different-from), [the same as](09-comparison-opposition.md#the-same-as), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### illustrative of

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈlʌstrətɪv ʌv/ &middot; *ih-LUSS-truh-tiv uv* &middot; weak /əˈlʌstrətəv əv/; the older *IL-uh-stray-tiv* is chiefly British
**Русский:** наглядно показывающий что-л., служащий тому примером
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — academic and analytical writing, exhibits in briefs, museum labels; conversation says *a good example of*
**Complement:** a noun phrase, often abstract — a tendency, a problem, a style — or a **gerund** (*illustrative of how the office handles complaints* is the commoner *wh*-form). **Never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *illustrative of to handle complaints*.
**Pattern:** *be illustrative of* [tendency / problem] &middot; *merely* / *particularly illustrative of* &middot; *by way of illustration* — the noun frame &middot; *illustrative purposes only* — attributive, in the standard American disclaimer
**Variants:** *indicative of* points at evidence for a conclusion; *representative of* claims the example is typical; *emblematic of* and *symptomatic of* are the loaded versions; *an example of* is the plain noun phrase.
**Contrast:** ***Illustrative of* says a case shows what something is like; [indicative of](#indicative-of) says it is evidence that something is so.*** A photograph is illustrative of the damage; a rising fever is indicative of infection. Against [representative of](#representative-of), which claims the example is typical of a whole, *illustrative of* claims only that it makes the point clear.
