Note the fixed American legal and marketing phrase *for illustrative purposes only*, which disclaims that the sample binds anyone. ✗ *illustrative for* and ✗ *illustrative to* are errors.

Serving as an example that makes something clear.

1. The 2019 filing is *illustrative of* the problem, though it isn't the worst one.
2. His answer was *illustrative of* how little the training had covered.
3. These two cases are *illustrative of* a pattern the auditors flagged.
4. The exhibit is *illustrative of* the technique, not a finished piece.
5. The numbers are *illustrative of* a trend that started well before the merger.

**See also:** [indicative of](#indicative-of), [representative of](#representative-of), [symptomatic of](#symptomatic-of), [suggestive of](#suggestive-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### immune from, immune to

**Pronunciation:** /ɪˈmjun frʌm, ɪˈmjun tu/ &middot; *ih-MYOON from, ih-MYOON too* &middot; weak /əˈmjun frəm/ and /əˈmjun tə/
**Русский:** невосприимчивый к (to, биол. или по привычке); юр. обладающий иммунитетом от (from)
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral in the medical sense, legal in the *from* sense (*immune from suit*, *immune from prosecution*)
**Complement:** after either preposition, a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*immune to being embarrassed*, *immune from having to testify*) — **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *immune to be embarrassed*. No clause follows either form.
**Pattern:** *be immune to* [disease / influence] &middot; *be immune from* [suit / prosecution / liability] &middot; *grant* [someone] *immunity from* [noun] — the noun frame keeps *from* &middot; *immune system* — attributive, no complement
**Variants:** *resistant to* is the weaker medical word (it slows the thing down rather than stopping it); *exempt from* is the administrative twin of *immune from*; *impervious to* is the figurative twin of *immune to*; *insulated from* is the neutral paraphrase.
**Contrast:** ***To* for what cannot affect you; *from* for what cannot reach you.*** You are *immune to* measles and *immune to* flattery because they no longer work on you; you are *immune from* prosecution and *immune from* liability because the law puts them out of reach. The figurative uses divide the same way: *immune to criticism* means it bounces off, *immune from criticism* means nobody may deliver it. Both are standard American English, and the noun almost always takes *from*: *immunity from prosecution*. ✗ *immune of* and ✗ *immune against* are errors, though *vaccinated against* is correct with the other adjective.

Protected against something — by biology or by habit in the *to* frame, and by law in the *from* frame.

1. Most of the crew is *immune to* the strain that went through in the spring.
2. The witness testified only after being made *immune from* prosecution.
3. He seems entirely *immune to* embarrassment.
4. State agencies are *immune from* suit in these circumstances.
5. Nobody in that market was *immune to* the rate increase.

**See also:** [impervious to](#impervious-to), [exempt from](#exempt-from), [vulnerable to](#vulnerable-to), [susceptible to, susceptible of](#susceptible-to-susceptible-of), [from](02-core-relational.md#from)

### impervious to

**Pronunciation:** /ɪmˈpɜrviəs tu/ &middot; *im-PUR-vee-us too* &middot; weak /əmˈpɜrviəs tə/
**Русский:** непроницаемый для (воды); перен. — глухой к доводам, критике, стыду
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; literal in building and materials writing (*impervious to water*), figurative and slightly literary about people
**Complement:** a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*impervious to being told twice*) — **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *impervious to be told*. No clause follows.
**Pattern:** *be impervious to* [substance / influence] &middot; *utterly* / *seemingly* / *cheerfully impervious to* &middot; *an impervious surface* — attributive, and a term of art in stormwater ordinances
**Variants:** *immune to* is the near twin in the figurative use; *resistant to* is the weaker one; *proof against* is the older phrase and lives on in compounds (*waterproof*, *bulletproof*, *fireproof*); *insensible to* is the literary version.
**Contrast:** Against [immune to](#immune-from-immune-to), which suggests a defense that was acquired, *impervious to* suggests a material that never let anything through in the first place — which is why it is the crueler word about people: *impervious to argument* says nothing gets in. Note the American municipal use, where *impervious surface* means pavement and roof area that sheds rainwater, and the stormwater fee is charged by the square foot of it. ✗ *impervious of* and ✗ *impervious from* are errors.

Letting nothing through — water, in the literal sense; argument, criticism, or shame in the figurative one.

1. The liner is *impervious to* everything the plant runs through it.
2. He is *impervious to* hints and always has been.
3. The finish is *impervious to* road salt for about four winters.
4. The committee proved *impervious to* every argument the neighbors made.
5. She's *impervious to* the cold in a way the rest of us envy.

**See also:** [immune from, immune to](#immune-from-immune-to), [oblivious to](#oblivious-to), [indifferent to](#indifferent-to), [vulnerable to](#vulnerable-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### implicit in

**Pronunciation:** /ɪmˈplɪsɪt ɪn/ &middot; *im-PLISS-it in* &middot; weak /əmˈplɪsət ən/
**Русский:** подразумеваемый в чём-л. — присутствующий, но прямо не сказанный
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; the natural home is analysis — of texts, contracts, arguments, and policies
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the thing that contains the unstated element — a text, a claim, a bargain, a design. A **gerund** works where the container is an action (*implicit in asking for a raise*), and a **to-infinitive is impossible**: ✗ *implicit in to ask*.
**Pattern:** *be implicit in* [text / claim / arrangement] &middot; *implicit in* [noun] *is* [the thing implied] — the inverted frame, which is how the phrase usually appears &middot; *implicit bias* / *implicit consent* — attributive, no preposition
**Variants:** *inherent in* is the near neighbor and means built into the nature of the thing rather than left unsaid; *tacit* covers the same ground as an attributive adjective (*a tacit agreement*); *understood* and *unspoken* are the plain words.
**Contrast:** ***Implicit in* means present but unstated; [inherent in](#inherent-in) means part of the thing's nature.*** A promise of good faith is implicit in the contract because nobody wrote it down; risk is inherent in the business because you cannot have the one without the other. The direct opposite is *explicit*, which takes no preposition in this sense (*the contract is explicit*), and *implicit* also has an unrelated older sense of "unquestioning," as in *implicit trust*, where again no *in* follows. ✗ *implicit to* is an error, and ✗ *implicit of* is not English.

Contained in something without being said outright.

1. *Implicit in* the offer is an assumption that the roof gets replaced.
2. A duty of good faith is *implicit in* every contract in this state.
3. The criticism was *implicit in* the way she read the numbers aloud.
4. There's a threat *implicit in* the last paragraph, whatever they claim.
5. Consent is *implicit in* using the parking lot, according to the sign.

**See also:** [inherent in](#inherent-in), [intrinsic to](#intrinsic-to), [integral to](#integral-to), [indicative of](#indicative-of), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### impressed with

**Pronunciation:** /ɪmˈprɛst wɪθ/ &middot; *im-PREST with* &middot; weak /ɪmˈprɛst wəθ/
**Русский:** впечатлённый чем-л., приятно удивлённый
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *with* and *by* (*impressed by how quickly they turned it around*) — **never a to-infinitive.** A *wh*-clause is common (*impressed with how she handled it*), and a *that*-clause attaches to the bare adjective (*impressed that anyone noticed*).
**Pattern:** *be impressed with* [person / thing] &middot; *be impressed by* [the thing that impressed you] &middot; *be impressed* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *impress* [something] *on* [someone] — a different verb frame &middot; *not impressed*
**Variants:** *impressed with* and *impressed by* are interchangeable in almost every sentence; *by* is a shade more formal and points harder at the cause. *Taken with* is the warmer version; *struck by* the more sudden one.
**Contrast:** **This is the one *with* / *by* pair in the file where the difference is nearly nil** — unlike *annoyed with* / *annoyed by* or *bored with* / *bored by*, where the split is real, *impressed with the design* and *impressed by the design* mean the same thing. Keep both apart from *impress on*, the verb frame meaning to make someone understand: *impress on them that the deadline is real*. ✗ *impressed of* and ✗ *impressed about* are errors, and the British *not impressed with* is identical to the American use.

Admiring something because it was better than expected.

1. The committee was *impressed with* the second candidate.
2. I was *impressed by* how fast the crew cleared the lot.
3. She wasn't *impressed with* the excuse and said so.
4. Everyone was *impressed that* he'd fixed it with a pocketknife.
5. We came away *impressed with* the whole operation.

**See also:** [delighted with](#delighted-with), [satisfied with](#satisfied-with), [astonished at](#astonished-at), [critical of](#critical-of), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### incapable of

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈkeɪpəbəl ʌv/ &middot; *in-KAY-puh-bull uv* &middot; weak /ɪnˈkeɪpəbəl əv/
**Русский:** неспособный на что-л., не имеющий сил или характера сделать
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*incapable of sitting still*) — **never a to-infinitive.** ✗ *incapable to do it* is the error, exactly parallel to ✗ *capable to do it*; the negatives of *able to* are *unable to* and *not able to*, both with the infinitive.
**Pattern:** *be incapable of* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *seemingly* / *apparently incapable of* [verb-ing] &middot; *incapable of* [abstract noun]: *incapable of malice*
**Variants:** *unable to* + infinitive is the everyday negative with the opposite structure; *not up to* + *-ing* is the informal one.
**Contrast:** ***Incapable of* + *-ing* versus *unable to* + verb.*** The two negatives of ability take opposite complements and cannot borrow: ✓ *incapable of finishing*, ✓ *unable to finish*, ✗ *incapable to finish*, ✗ *unable of finishing*. There is also a difference of scope: *unable to finish* usually means blocked in this instance, while *incapable of finishing* impugns the person's capacity in general — which is why the second one insults and the first does not.

Lacking the power, skill, or character to do something.

1. He seems *incapable of* leaving a message under two minutes.
2. The old boiler is *incapable of* heating the third floor.
3. She's *incapable of* passing a bookstore without going in.
4. The court found him *incapable of* managing his own affairs.
5. They were *incapable of* agreeing on even the meeting time.

**See also:** [capable of](#capable-of), [bad at](#bad-at), [hopeless at](#hopeless-at), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### incident to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɪnsɪdənt tu/ &middot; *IN-suh-dunt too* &middot; weak /ˈɪnsədənt tə/
**Русский:** вытекающий из чего-л. как правовое следствие — юр.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal; the American reader meets it in one place above all — *a search incident to arrest* — and otherwise in deeds, employment agreements, and tax rules
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the principal thing — an arrest, an office, an ownership interest, a transaction. A **gerund** appears in drafting (*expenses incident to closing the sale*), and a **to-infinitive is impossible**: ✗ *incident to close the sale*.
**Pattern:** [thing] *incident to* [principal thing] &middot; *a search incident to* [arrest] — the constitutional doctrine &middot; *rights and duties incident to* [office / ownership] &middot; *incident to employment* — the workers' compensation phrase
**Variants:** *incidental to* is the everyday twin and is the form to use for minor accompanying things; *appurtenant to* is the property-law word for rights attached to land; *attendant on* is the literary version.
**Contrast:** ***Incident to* says a thing belongs to another as one of its normal legal consequences; [incidental to](#incidental-to) says it merely happens alongside and is minor.*** Powers incident to the office of trustee come with the office as of right; the printing costs incidental to the filing are trivia. American lawyers keep the pair apart deliberately, and outside law the shorter form has almost entirely won. Do not read *incident* here as the noun meaning "an event" — different word, and it takes no preposition. ✗ *incident of* means something else again (*an incident of ownership*, in estate tax, is a right that comes with it).

Arising as a normal legal consequence of something else, and belonging to it as of right.

1. Officers may search the passenger compartment *incident to* a lawful arrest.
2. The powers *incident to* the office pass to whoever holds it next.
3. Costs *incident to* recording the deed are the buyer's.
4. Injuries *incident to* employment are covered without regard to fault.
5. Any right *incident to* the policy has to be listed on the schedule.

**See also:** [incidental to](#incidental-to), [appurtenant to](#appurtenant-to), [ancillary to](#ancillary-to), [concomitant with](#concomitant-with), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### incidental to

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɪnsɪˈdɛntəl tu/ &middot; *in-suh-DEN-tul too* &middot; weak /ˌɪnsəˈdɛntəl tə/, with a flapped *t*: "in-suh-DEN-nul-tuh"
**Русский:** побочный при чём-л. — сопутствующий главному и малозначительный рядом с ним
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; ordinary in expense reports (*incidental expenses*), contracts, and analytical prose
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the main thing, or a **gerund** (*incidental to running the shop*) — **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *incidental to run the shop*.
**Pattern:** *be incidental to* [main activity] &middot; [cost / effect] *incidental to* [noun] &middot; *merely* / *purely incidental to* &middot; *incidentals* — the noun, meaning small extra costs, with no preposition
**Variants:** *ancillary to* is the formal twin for supporting services; *incident to* is the legal form for consequences that come as of right; *secondary to* ranks the two things; *peripheral to* stresses the edge.
**Contrast:** ***Incidental to* means minor and merely accompanying, and it is the word to reach for outside law.*** Against [incident to](#incident-to), which claims a legal connection, *incidental to* claims no connection beyond happening alongside. Against [ancillary to](#ancillary-to), which supports the main thing on purpose, *incidental to* implies nobody planned it. In per-diem rules the frozen American phrase is *meals and incidental expenses* — the noun *incidentals* is what a hotel bill calls the minibar. ✗ *incidental of* and ✗ *incidental with* are errors.

Happening alongside the main thing and of small importance next to it.

1. The travel costs are *incidental to* the grant and capped at eight percent.
2. Whatever damage the tree did was *incidental to* the storm, not the crew.
3. Filing is *incidental to* her actual job, which is answering the phone.
4. The gift shop is *incidental to* the museum's mission, if lucrative.
5. Any benefit to the neighbors was *incidental to* a project built for the port.

**See also:** [incident to](#incident-to), [ancillary to](#ancillary-to), [concomitant with](#concomitant-with), [peculiar to](#peculiar-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### incumbent on, incumbent upon

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈkʌmbənt ɑn, ɪnˈkʌmbənt əˈpɑn/ &middot; *in-KUM-bunt on, in-KUM-bunt uh-PON* &middot; weak /ənˈkʌmbənt ən/
**Русский:** лежащий на ком-л. как обязанность — книжн., в обороте it is incumbent on X to
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — editorials, opinions, and official correspondence; the whole phrase is a set piece and *upon* is at least as common as *on*
**Complement:** a noun phrase or pronoun naming the **person under the duty** — never the duty itself. The duty goes in a **to-infinitive** at the end of the sentence, which makes this a two-part frame: *it is incumbent on the board to review the policy*. ✗ *incumbent on reviewing the policy* is an error, and so is ✗ *the board is incumbent on reviewing*.
**Pattern:** *it is incumbent on* [person] *to* [verb] — nearly the only frame in live use &middot; *it is incumbent upon us to* [verb] &middot; *the duty incumbent on* [person]
**Variants:** *the burden is on* [someone] *to* [verb] is the plain equivalent; *obligated to* and *required to* say the same thing about a person directly; *behooves* is the archaic verb that fills the same slot (*it behooves us to check*).
**Contrast:** **The frame is impersonal and the infinitive is obligatory**, which is what makes this entry unusual in a file about gerunds: the *on* takes the person, and the duty must be an infinitive. Note that the unrelated noun *incumbent* — the officeholder currently in the job — takes no preposition and is far commoner in American political writing; *the incumbent is running again* has nothing to do with this pattern. ✗ *incumbent to* and ✗ *incumbent for* are errors.

Resting on someone as a duty — in a fixed frame with *it* and an infinitive.

1. It is *incumbent on* the seller to disclose what she knows about the roof.
2. It is *incumbent upon* every member to read the minutes before the vote.
3. The duty *incumbent on* a trustee does not end when the money runs out.
4. It's *incumbent on* us to say plainly what the study did not find.
5. It was *incumbent upon* the agency to publish the rule before enforcing it.

**See also:** [obligated to, obliged to](#obligated-to-obliged-to), [binding on, binding upon](#binding-on-binding-upon), [responsible for](#responsible-for), [answerable to](#answerable-to), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### independent of

**Pronunciation:** /ˌɪndɪˈpɛndənt ʌv/ &middot; *in-dih-PEN-dunt uv* &middot; weak /ˌɪndɪˈpɛndənt əv/
**Русский:** независимый от — не подконтрольный, не содержащийся и не определяемый чем-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase or pronoun; a **gerund** is possible where an activity is meant (*independent of needing outside funding*) — **never a to-infinitive** and never a clause.
**Pattern:** *be independent of* [noun] &middot; *be independent from* [a former parent body] &middot; *independently of* [noun] — the adverb &middot; *financially independent*
**Variants:** ***independent from*** is used, and it is idiomatic where separation from a former controller is meant (*independent from Spain since 1821*). For the ordinary "not determined by" sense, ***of*** is the standard American choice: *independent of the outcome*.
**Contrast:** ***Independent of* is the default; *independent from* is for secession.*** A variable is *independent of* another; a colony becomes *independent from* an empire. The negative side of the same word takes *on*, not *of*: ✓ *dependent on*, ✗ *dependent of* — so the pair *dependent on* / *independent of* is one of the small number of places in English where a prefix changes the preposition. The adverb keeps the *of*: *arrived at independently of one another*.

Not controlled by, supported by, or determined by something else.

1. The audit is *independent of* both the board and the union.
2. Two labs reached the same figure *independently of* each other.
3. She's been financially *independent of* her parents since twenty.
4. The result is *independent of* how the sample was ordered.
5. The territory became *independent from* the mainland administration in 1976.

**See also:** [dependent on](#dependent-on), [free of](#free-of), [reliant on](#reliant-on), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### indicative of

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈdɪkətɪv ʌv/ &middot; *in-DIK-uh-tiv uv* &middot; weak /ənˈdɪkətəv əv/
**Русский:** свидетельствующий о, указывающий на
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; standard in clinical notes, audit findings, and analytical journalism, where the plain phrase would be *a sign of*
**Complement:** a noun phrase, or a **gerund** where the sign points to an activity (*indicative of having been moved*) — **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *indicative of to be moved*. A *wh*-clause is common and correct: *indicative of what the crew found*.
**Pattern:** *be indicative of* [condition / conclusion] &middot; *not necessarily indicative of* [noun] — the hedged frame, and a standing phrase in American investment disclaimers (*past performance is not indicative of future results*) &middot; *the indicative mood* — the grammar term, unrelated
**Variants:** *a sign of* and *evidence of* are the plain noun phrases; *suggestive of* is weaker; *symptomatic of* is the medical one; *diagnostic of* is the strongest — it settles the question.
**Contrast:** **A scale of strength runs through this family:** *suggestive of* hints, *indicative of* points, *symptomatic of* names a condition by its signs, and *diagnostic of* proves. Against [illustrative of](#illustrative-of), which offers an example, *indicative of* offers evidence. Do not confuse the adjective with the grammatical *indicative* (the mood of ordinary statements), which never takes *of*. ✗ *indicative to* and ✗ *indicative for* are errors.

Serving as a sign that something is the case.

1. The wear pattern is *indicative of* a wheel that was never aligned.
2. Two late filings are not *indicative of* fraud by themselves.
3. Her tone was *indicative of* how the rest of the meeting would go.
4. Past performance is not *indicative of* future results.
5. Soil staining *indicative of* an old leak turned up under the slab.

**See also:** [suggestive of](#suggestive-of), [symptomatic of](#symptomatic-of), [illustrative of](#illustrative-of), [predictive of](#predictive-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### indifferent to

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈdɪfərənt tu/ &middot; *in-DIF-runt too* &middot; weak /ɪnˈdɪfərənt tə/
**Русский:** равнодушный к, безразличный
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or pronoun; a **gerund** where an activity is meant (*indifferent to being noticed*) — **never a to-infinitive** and never a clause.
**Pattern:** *be indifferent to* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *seemingly* / *utterly indifferent to* [noun] &middot; *a matter of indifference to* [someone] — the noun keeps *to*
**Variants:** *unmoved by*, *unconcerned about*, and *apathetic about* / *toward* are the neighbors; *couldn't care less about* is the informal one.
**Contrast:** ***Indifferent to* takes *to*, not *about* or *by*.*** ✗ *indifferent about the outcome* is a common slip; the word that takes *about* is *unconcerned*. Note the second, older sense of *indifferent* meaning mediocre (*an indifferent meal*), where no preposition appears at all — this sense is now chiefly literary but still turns up in reviews.

Having no interest in something, and no feeling either way about it.

1. He is genuinely *indifferent to* what anyone thinks of the house.
2. The cat was *indifferent to* the entire commotion.
3. Voters were *indifferent to* both candidates and stayed home.
4. She's *indifferent to* prizes, which is why she keeps winning them.
5. The market has been *indifferent to* the announcement all week.

**See also:** [interested in](#interested-in), [bored with](#bored-with), [sensitive to](#sensitive-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### inferior to

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈfɪriər tu/ &middot; *in-FEER-ee-ur too* &middot; weak /ɪnˈfɪriər tə/
**Русский:** уступающий чему-л. — ниже по качеству, рангу или значимости
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or pronoun — **never a gerund**, **never a to-infinitive**, and never a clause.
**Pattern:** [X] *is inferior to* [Y] &middot; *inferior in* [respect] *to* [noun] &middot; *vastly* / *markedly inferior to* [noun] &middot; *an inferior* [noun] — attributive, no preposition
**Variants:** *worse than* is the everyday equivalent and takes *than*, which is exactly the confusion this entry exists to prevent. *Not a patch on* is the informal British one.
**Contrast:** ***Inferior takes *to*, never *than*.*** ✗ *inferior than* is one of the most persistent errors in academic English, because the adjective is comparative in meaning even though it is not comparative in form. The same rule governs *superior to*, *senior to*, *junior to*, *prior to*, *preferable to*, and *anterior to* — all Latin comparatives, all with *to*, all rejecting *than*. Note also that *inferior* cannot be intensified with *more*: ✗ *more inferior to* is redundant.

Lower in quality, rank, or importance than something else.

1. The replacement fabric is noticeably *inferior to* the original.
2. He never felt *inferior to* anyone in the room, which was half the problem.
3. The second edition is *inferior to* the first in every way but the index.
4. These parts are *inferior in* finish *to* what we ordered.
5. Nothing about the design is *inferior to* the competition; it's just late.

**See also:** [superior to](#superior-to), [equal to](#equal-to), [different from](#different-from), [than](09-comparison-opposition.md#than), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### inherent in

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈhɪrənt ɪn/ &middot; *in-HEER-unt in* &middot; weak /ənˈhɪrənt ən/; the variant *in-HAIR-unt* is equally American
**Русский:** присущий чему-л. по природе, неотделимый от
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to neutral; standard in law, risk writing, and philosophy
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the thing whose nature contains the quality — *inherent in the design*, *inherent in any partnership*. A **gerund** is available where the container is an activity (*inherent in running a plant this old*), and a **to-infinitive is impossible**: ✗ *inherent in to run a plant*.
**Pattern:** *be inherent in* [thing / activity] &middot; *risks inherent in* [noun] — the commonest collocation by a wide margin &middot; *inherent* [noun] — attributive (*an inherent conflict*, *inherent powers*) &middot; *inhere in* [noun] — the rare verb
**Variants:** *intrinsic to* is the near twin and takes *to*; *built into* is the plain modern phrase; *of the essence of* is the philosopher's formula; *native to* covers the biological case.
**Contrast:** ***Inherent* takes *in*; [intrinsic to](#intrinsic-to) takes *to*, and this is the pair most often crossed.*** ✗ *inherent to the design* is now common in American technical writing and is still marked as an error by careful editors; write *inherent in the design* or *intrinsic to the design*. Against [implicit in](#implicit-in), which is about what was left unsaid, *inherent in* is about what could not be removed: the risk inherent in the work would exist even if everyone spelled it out. ✗ *inherent of* is not English.

Belonging to the nature of a thing, so that it cannot be separated from it.

1. There's a conflict *inherent in* letting the vendor audit its own invoices.
2. The risks *inherent in* the procedure were explained twice.
3. Delay is *inherent in* any process that needs three signatures.
4. Courts have powers *inherent in* the judicial function itself.
5. The instability is *inherent in* the design, not a manufacturing defect.

**See also:** [intrinsic to](#intrinsic-to), [implicit in](#implicit-in), [integral to](#integral-to), [endemic to](#endemic-to), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### innocent of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɪnəsənt ʌv/ &middot; *IN-uh-sunt uv* &middot; weak /ˈɪnəsənt əv/
**Русский:** невиновный в чём-л.; шутл. — начисто лишённый какого-л. качества
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal in the legal sense; literary in the extended one
**Complement:** a noun naming an **offense**, or a **gerund** naming the act (*innocent of having any part in it*) — **never a to-infinitive** and never a clause.
**Pattern:** *be innocent of* [offense / verb-ing] &middot; *be found innocent of* [charge] &middot; *innocent of* [any knowledge / all irony] — the extended sense &middot; *presumed innocent* (no preposition)
**Variants:** *not guilty of* is the everyday and, strictly, the legally accurate phrase; *cleared of* is what happens after a verdict; *blameless in* covers the non-criminal sense.
**Contrast:** ***Innocent of* takes *of*, matching *guilty of*.*** The legal note is worth knowing: American juries return *not guilty*, not *innocent*, so *found innocent of* is journalism rather than law. The extended sense means entirely lacking something and is faintly ironic: *a paragraph innocent of punctuation* has none. ✗ *innocent from* and ✗ *innocent about* are errors.

Not guilty of an offense — or, playfully, entirely without some quality.

1. He maintained he was *innocent of* every charge on the indictment.
2. She was *innocent of* any involvement in the second transfer.
3. The jury found him not *guilty*; the paper said *innocent of* all counts.
4. His memo was *innocent of* any acquaintance with the actual budget.
5. They were *innocent of* the whole scheme until the subpoena arrived.

**See also:** [guilty of](#guilty-of), [free of](#free-of), [responsible for](#responsible-for), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### integral to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɪntɪɡrəl tu/ &middot; *IN-tuh-grul too*; the stress-shifted *in-TEG-rul* is also standard American &middot; weak /ˈɪntəɡrəl tə/
**Русский:** составляющий неотъемлемую часть чего-л., а не добавку к нему
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; ordinary in business and technical writing about how parts fit into wholes
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the whole, or a **gerund** naming the activity (*integral to keeping the line running*) — **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *integral to keep the line running*.
**Pattern:** *be integral to* [whole / process] &middot; *absolutely* / *utterly integral to* &middot; *an integral part of* [noun] — the noun frame, which switches to *of* &middot; *integral* [noun] — attributive, as in *integral memory*
**Variants:** *essential to* and *central to* are the everyday equivalents; *crucial to* and *vital to* raise the temperature; *part and parcel of* is the idiom; *intrinsic to* is about nature rather than function.
**Contrast:** **Adjective *to*, noun *of*.** ✓ *integral to the process*, ✓ *an integral part of the process*, ✗ *an integral part to the process*. Against [essential to](#essential-to), which says the whole fails without it, *integral to* says the thing is a built-in part of the whole — the difference is between necessity and membership, though in practice the two are used interchangeably. Against [intrinsic to](#intrinsic-to), note that *integral* is about structure (it is one of the parts) and *intrinsic* about nature (it is of the essence). ✗ *integral for* and ✗ *integral in* are errors.

Forming a necessary part of a larger whole rather than an addition to it.

1. The loading dock is *integral to* how the store gets restocked.
2. Her role became *integral to* the project about a month in.
3. Testing is *integral to* the release process, not a step you can skip.
4. The porch is *integral to* the structure and can't simply be removed.
5. Volunteers are *integral to* keeping the clinic open on Saturdays.

**See also:** [essential to](#essential-to), [intrinsic to](#intrinsic-to), [inherent in](#inherent-in), [ancillary to](#ancillary-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### intent on

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈtɛnt ɑn/ &middot; *in-TENT on* &middot; weak /ɪnˈtɛnt ən/
**Русский:** твёрдо намеренный сделать что-л., поглощённый этим — после on герундий
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*intent on finishing before dark*) — **never a to-infinitive.** ✗ *intent to finish* is wrong as an adjective phrase, though the **noun** *intent* does take an infinitive: *with intent to defraud*, which is exactly where the confusion comes from.
**Pattern:** *be intent on* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be intent upon* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *seemingly intent on* [verb-ing] &middot; *with intent to* [verb] — the noun frame
**Variants:** *intent upon* is the formal equivalent. *Bent on* is the near-identical informal one and always implies a bad outcome; *determined to* takes an infinitive instead.
**Contrast:** ***Intent on* + *-ing* versus *determined to* + verb.*** Same meaning, opposite structure: ✓ *intent on winning*, ✓ *determined to win*, ✗ *intent to win* as an adjective. Note the legal noun phrase *with intent to kill*, where *intent* is a noun and takes an infinitive correctly — the adjective and the noun are spelled the same and take different complements, which is why this pair produces so many mistakes. *Bent on* is the version reserved for destructive purposes: *bent on revenge*.

Fixed on doing something, with attention that excludes everything else.

1. She was *intent on* getting the whole roof done in one weekend.
2. He seemed *intent on* proving a point nobody had disputed.
3. The department is *intent on* keeping the case out of the papers.
4. They were *intent upon* the map and didn't hear the door.
5. He was charged with assault *with intent to* rob — the noun frame.

**See also:** [committed to](#committed-to), [serious about](#serious-about), [reluctant to](#reluctant-to), [obsessed with](#obsessed-with), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### interested in

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɪntrəstɪd ɪn/ &middot; *IN-truss-tid in* &middot; weak /ˈɪntrəstɪd ən/; the careful four-syllable /ˈɪntərəstɪd/ also occurs
**Русский:** интересующийся чем-л., заинтересованный в
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*interested in buying*) — **never a to-infinitive after *in***. ✗ *interested in to buy* and ✗ *interested to buy* for this meaning are both errors. The adjective does take a bare to-infinitive in a **different sense** — *interested to hear*, *interested to see* — meaning curious to learn, and that frame works only with verbs of perceiving and learning.
**Pattern:** *be interested in* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be interested in* [someone] *-ing* &middot; *be interested to* [hear / know / see / learn] &middot; *take an interest in* [noun] &middot; *an interest in* [noun] — the noun keeps *in*
**Variants:** *into* is the casual American version (*he's into woodworking*); *keen on* the British one; *curious about* the neighboring one.
**Contrast:** **The single highest-frequency error with this adjective is ✗ *I am interested to buy your car*.** Write *interested in buying*. The infinitive frame is real but narrow: it takes verbs of finding out — *I'd be interested to hear what she says* — and it means "curious", not "wanting to acquire". Compare *interested in* (wanting, engaged with) against *curious about* (wondering) and *concerned with* (dealing with). ✗ *interested about* is an error, though *interested in* and *interested by* differ the usual way, *by* pointing at the cause.

Wanting to know about something, or wanting to have or do it.

1. She's *interested in* nineteenth-century glass, the more chipped the better.
2. We'd be *interested in* buying the lot next door if it ever comes up.
3. He's not *interested in* arguing about it again.
4. I'd be *interested to* hear how the meeting went.
5. Anyone *interested in* volunteering should email the office.

**See also:** [curious about](#curious-about), [keen on](#keen-on), [enthusiastic about](#enthusiastic-about), [involved in](#involved-in), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### intrinsic to

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈtrɪnzɪk tu/ &middot; *in-TRIN-zik too*; also *in-TRIN-sik* &middot; weak /ənˈtrɪnzək tə/
**Русский:** внутренне присущий чему-л., идущий от самой его сути
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal — philosophy, biology, economics, and careful technical writing
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the thing whose nature includes the property, or a **gerund** (*intrinsic to being a small landlord*) — **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *intrinsic to be a landlord*.
**Pattern:** *be intrinsic to* [thing / role] &middot; *intrinsic* [noun] — attributive (*intrinsic value*, *intrinsic motivation*, *intrinsic muscles*) &middot; *intrinsically* [adjective] — the adverb
**Variants:** *inherent in* is the near twin with a different preposition; *essential to* is the plainer word; *built into* is the everyday phrase; *of its very nature* is the formal paraphrase. The antonym *extrinsic* takes *to* as well.
**Contrast:** ***Intrinsic to* versus [inherent in](#inherent-in) is a preposition split with no meaning behind it*** — the two adjectives cover nearly the same ground and simply take different prepositions, which is exactly why writers cross them. Against [integral to](#integral-to), which is about being one of the parts, *intrinsic to* is about belonging to the nature of the thing. In finance the attributive use is a term of art: *intrinsic value* is what the thing is worth apart from what the market says. ✗ *intrinsic in* is a slip, though it appears; ✗ *intrinsic of* is not English.

Belonging to the essential nature of something rather than coming from outside it.

1. Curiosity is *intrinsic to* the job; the paperwork is not.
2. Some risk is *intrinsic to* lending, however good the underwriting.
3. The delay is *intrinsic to* the process, not a sign anybody is slacking.
4. Rewards *intrinsic to* the work keep people longer than bonuses do.
5. That quality is *intrinsic to* the material and survives any finish.

**See also:** [inherent in](#inherent-in), [integral to](#integral-to), [essential to](#essential-to), [peculiar to](#peculiar-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### involved in

**Pronunciation:** /ɪnˈvɑlvd ɪn/ &middot; *in-VOLVD in* &middot; weak /ɪnˈvɑlvd ən/
**Русский:** участвующий в, замешанный в
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *in* and *with* (*involved in running the campaign*) — **never a to-infinitive.** ✗ *involved to run it* is an error.
**Pattern:** *be involved in* [activity / verb-ing] &middot; *be involved with* [person / organization] &middot; *get involved in* [situation] &middot; *involve* [someone] *in* [something] — the verb &middot; *deeply* / *heavily involved in* [noun]
**Variants:** *engaged in* is the formal equivalent for activities; *mixed up in* is the disapproving informal one; *caught up in* is the passive one.
**Contrast:** ***Involved in* an activity; *involved with* a person or an organization.*** *Involved in the merger* describes what you were doing; *involved with the company* describes an ongoing connection; and *involved with someone* is a standard American euphemism for a romantic relationship, which is why the two prepositions are worth keeping apart. Note the verb frame *involve someone in something* and the noun frame *involvement in* / *with*, which follow the same split. ✗ *involved on* is an error.

Taking part in something, or connected with someone or something.

1. Three departments were *involved in* drafting the policy.
2. She's been *involved with* the food bank since it opened.
3. He got *involved in* a dispute that had nothing to do with him.
4. They were *involved in* running the whole festival for eleven years.
5. Nobody wanted to be *involved with* that firm after the settlement.

**See also:** [engaged to](#engaged-to), [concerned about](#concerned-about), [interested in](#interested-in), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### isomorphic to

**Pronunciation:** /ˌaɪsəˈmɔrfɪk tu/ &middot; *eye-suh-MOR-fik too* &middot; weak /ˌaɪsəˈmɔrfək tə/
**Русский:** изоморфный чему-л. — мат., имеющий в точности ту же структуру
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** technical — mathematics above all (group theory, graph theory), then linguistics, crystallography, and, as a borrowed metaphor, organizational theory
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the other structure — a group, a graph, a system — **never a gerund** and never a clause. The two things compared must be structures of the same kind.
**Pattern:** [structure] *is isomorphic to* [structure] &middot; *isomorphic with* [noun] — the variant, commoner outside mathematics &middot; *an isomorphism between* [X] *and* [Y] — the noun frame, with *between* &middot; *isomorphic* [noun] — attributive
**Variants:** *isomorphic with* is standard in the looser social-science use; *homomorphic to* is the weaker mathematical relation; *structurally identical to* is the paraphrase; *congruent to* is geometry's version of the same instinct.
**Contrast:** **In mathematics *isomorphic to* is exact and technical:** two structures are isomorphic when a one-to-one mapping preserves all the relevant operations, which is a far stronger claim than similarity. The borrowed use in organizational theory (*the new agency is isomorphic to the one it replaced*) means only that the shapes match, and specialists find it loose. Against [analogous to](#analogous-to), which says reasoning carries over, *isomorphic to* says the structures match exactly. Note the noun's different preposition: *an isomorphism between* two groups, not ✗ *of* them. ✗ *isomorphic of* is an error.

Having exactly the same structure as something else, so that one can be mapped onto the other point for point.

1. That group is *isomorphic to* the integers under addition.
2. Any two cyclic groups of the same order are *isomorphic to* each other.
3. The two graphs look different but are *isomorphic to* one another.
4. The new org chart is *isomorphic to* the one they abandoned in 2016.
5. He showed the puzzle is *isomorphic to* a problem already solved in 1974.

**See also:** [congruent to, congruent with](#congruent-to-congruent-with), [analogous to](#analogous-to), [equivalent to](#equivalent-to), [identical to](#identical-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### jealous of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdʒɛləs ʌv/ &middot; *JEL-uss uv* &middot; weak /ˈdʒɛləs əv/
**Русский:** завидующий кому-л.; ревниво оберегающий своё
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*jealous of having to share the office*) — **never a to-infinitive** and never a clause.
**Pattern:** *be jealous of* [person / thing] &middot; *be jealous of* [someone's] [possession or advantage] &middot; *jealously guard* [something] &middot; *jealous of* [something one protects]: *jealous of her privacy*
**Variants:** *envious of* is the formal twin for the wanting sense only; *possessive of* and *protective of* cover the guarding sense.
**Contrast:** ***Jealous* does two jobs with one preposition.*** *Jealous of her sister's job* wants what someone else has — that is *envious of* territory. *Jealous of her own time* guards what it already has, and *envious* cannot mean that. A third use covers romantic insecurity: *jealous of the new coworker* can mean either wanting his position or fearing he is a rival, and only context separates them. ✗ *jealous about* and ✗ *jealous with* are errors; the noun keeps *of*: *jealousy of his success*.

Wanting what someone else has — or fiercely protective of something you have.

1. He's *jealous of* anyone who gets to work from home.
2. She's *jealous of* her Sundays and won't schedule anything.
3. The younger one is *jealous of* every minute the baby takes.
4. Small firms are *jealous of* their independence.
5. I'm *jealous of* the way he can sleep through anything.

**See also:** [envious of](#envious-of), [suspicious of](#suspicious-of), [proud of](#proud-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### keen on

**Pronunciation:** /kin ɑn/ &middot; *KEEN on* &middot; weak /kin ən/
**Русский:** увлечённый чем-л. — в амер. англ. чаще в отрицании: not keen on
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral in British English; in American English it reads as mildly British or bookish, and survives mainly in the negative
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *on* (*keen on hiking*) — **never a to-infinitive after *on***. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (*keen to start*), which is the frame American writers reach for when they use the word at all.
**Pattern:** *be keen on* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be keen to* [verb] &middot; *not too keen on* [noun] &middot; *keen* alone, meaning sharp or intense: *a keen eye*, *keen interest*
**Variants:** *into*, *crazy about*, and *enthusiastic about* are the American everyday equivalents; *eager to* replaces *keen to*.
**Contrast:** ***Keen on* + *-ing* versus *keen to* + verb*** is the same split as *eager for* / *eager to*: *on* takes the thing, *to* takes the action. The American usage note matters here — a sentence like *She's keen on the idea* is understood everywhere but sounds imported in the United States, while the negative *I'm not too keen on that* is thoroughly naturalized American understatement. ✗ *keen about* and ✗ *keen of* are errors; the intensifier sense (*a keen sense of smell*) takes no preposition.

Enthusiastic about something — most often heard, in America, in the negative.

1. I'm not too *keen on* driving all that way for one meeting.
2. He's *keen on* anything involving a boat.
3. The board wasn't *keen on* the timing.
4. She's *keen to* get started before the permits expire.
5. They were *keen on* the location and cool on the price.

**See also:** [enthusiastic about](#enthusiastic-about), [crazy about](#crazy-about), [eager for](#eager-for), [fond of](#fond-of), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### kind to

**Pronunciation:** /kaɪnd tu/ &middot; *KYND too* &middot; weak /kaɪnd tə/
**Русский:** добрый, внимательный к кому-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a **person or animal** after *to* — **never a gerund**, never a to-infinitive, never a clause. The judgment frame takes *of* plus a doer plus an infinitive: *It was kind of you to call.*
**Pattern:** *be kind to* [person / animal] &middot; *it is kind of* [someone] *to* [verb] &middot; *be kind enough to* [verb] &middot; *kind about* [something] — tolerant regarding &middot; *kind to* [a surface or a body part]: *kind to your knees*
**Variants:** *nice to*, *good to*, and *decent to* are the everyday equivalents; *considerate of* and *thoughtful toward* are the formal ones.
**Contrast:** ***To* names who receives the kindness; *of* names who supplied it.*** *Kind to me* and *kind of you* are both correct and mean different things, and the second is a fixed politeness frame in American English: *That's kind of you.* ✗ *kind with* is an error, which is worth flagging because *gentle with* and *patient with* sit right next door and take *with*. The extended sense — *a shampoo that's kind to color-treated hair* — keeps *to*.

Treating someone with warmth and consideration.

1. She was *kind to* every nervous first-year who came through the door.
2. Be *kind to* the guy at the counter; it's his second day.
3. It was *kind of* you *to* wait.
4. The years have been *kind to* that house.
5. The new surface is *kind to* runners' knees.

**See also:** [nice to](#nice-to), [polite to](#polite-to), [cruel to](#cruel-to), [generous with](#generous-with), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### known for

**Pronunciation:** /noʊn fɔr/ &middot; *NOHN for* &middot; weak /noʊn fər/
**Русский:** известный чем-л.; known as — как кто-л., known to — кому-л. известный
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *for* (*known for showing up early*) — **never a to-infinitive after *for***. After *as*, a noun naming a role or a name (*known as the Iron Range*). After *to*, a person who knows it. The passive *known to* + infinitive (*known to happen*) is a separate frame meaning reported to.
**Pattern:** *be known for* [quality / verb-ing] &middot; *be known as* [role / name] &middot; *be known to* [person] &middot; *be known to* [verb] &middot; *make* [something] *known to* [someone] &middot; *little known outside* [place]
**Variants:** *famous for* is the strong version; *notorious for* the negative one; *renowned for* the formal one; *recognized as* the credential version.
**Contrast:** **Four prepositions, four jobs, and this is one of the busiest words in the file.** ***For*** = what you do or have: *known for her sourdough*. ***As*** = what you are called or counted as: *known as Big Jim*, *known as a hard grader*. ***To*** + person = who knows it: *known to the police*. ***To*** + infinitive = reported to do it: *bears are known to open car doors*. Substituting one for another goes wrong immediately: ✗ *known as her sourdough*. ✗ *known of* is an error, though *unknown to* and *known by* (with an agent) are both correct.

Recognized by other people — for something, as something, or to someone.

1. The neighborhood is *known for* its stubborn holdout houses.
2. He's *known as* the only person who can fix that machine.
3. The car was already *known to* the county sheriff.
4. Storms like that are *known to* strand traffic for hours.
5. She's *known for* returning calls the same day, which is rarer than it should be.

**See also:** [famous for](#famous-for), [notorious for](#notorious-for), [familiar with](#familiar-with), [as](10-topic-reference-role.md#as), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### lacking in

**Pronunciation:** /ˈlækɪŋ ɪn/ &middot; *LAK-ing in* &middot; weak /ˈlækɪŋ ən/
**Русский:** лишённый качества, которого следовало бы ожидать
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** an **abstract quality** after *in* (*lacking in imagination*) — **never a gerund** and **never a to-infinitive.** The bare participle also takes a direct object with no preposition (*lacking any evidence*), and the plain verb *lack* takes a direct object and never a preposition.
**Pattern:** *be lacking in* [quality] &middot; *be lacking* [noun] &middot; *be sadly* / *conspicuously lacking in* [quality] &middot; *lack* [something] — the **verb, with no preposition** &middot; *a lack of* [noun] — the **noun, with *of***
**Variants:** *deficient in* is the technical version; *short on* and *low on* the informal ones; *devoid of* and *wanting in* the formal ones.
**Contrast:** **The three related forms take three different structures, and this is the trap.** ✓ *The plan lacks detail* (verb, no preposition — ✗ *lacks of detail*). ✓ *The plan is lacking in detail* (adjective, *in*). ✓ *There is a lack of detail* (noun, *of*). Learners who have met the noun first tend to write ✗ *lacks of detail*, which is the single commonest error involving this word; see the zero-preposition entry for [lack](#lack). *Lacking in* prefers abstract qualities, so *lacking in courage* is natural and *lacking in a hammer* is not.

Short of some quality that ought to be there.

1. The proposal is not *lacking in* ambition, only in numbers.
2. His apology was conspicuously *lacking in* any actual apology.
3. The building is *lacking in* the kind of light the studio needs.
4. She's never been *lacking in* nerve.
5. The report is *lacking* several attachments referenced in the index.

**See also:** [lack](#lack), [short of](#short-of), [absent from](#absent-from), [free of](#free-of), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### late for

**Pronunciation:** /leɪt fɔr/ &middot; *LAYT for* &middot; weak /leɪt fər/
**Русский:** опоздавший на что-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a noun naming an **appointment or event** after *for* (*late for the meeting*) — **never a gerund after *for*** and never a to-infinitive. After *in*, a **gerund** naming what was done late (*late in filing*), which is the formal frame. After *with*, the thing owed (*late with the rent*).
**Pattern:** *be late for* [event] &middot; *be late to* [event or place] &middot; *be late in* [verb-ing] &middot; *be late with* [something owed] &middot; *run late* &middot; *late* alone, of time: *it's getting late*
**Variants:** *late to* is increasingly common in American English before an event, and *late to the party* is a fixed idiom. *Behind on* covers the "late with" sense: *behind on the rent*.
**Contrast:** ***For* takes the event you failed to reach on time; *with* takes the thing you failed to hand over; *in* takes the action you were slow to perform.*** *Late for the hearing*, *late with the filing*, *late in filing* — three frames, three grammatical shapes. The opposite is *on time for* or *punctual for*, both with *for*; there is no ✗ *early for* / *late of* symmetry, since *early for* is correct and *late of* means something else entirely (*Ann Reilly, late of Cleveland* is the obituary formula for "formerly of").

Arriving or acting after the time you were supposed to.

1. She was *late for* the interview and got the job anyway.
2. He's *late with* the rent for the third month running.
3. The district was *late in* notifying families about the closure.
4. Don't be *late to* your own party.
5. I'm always *late for* things that start on the half hour.

**See also:** [punctual for](#punctual-for), [ready for](#ready-for), [prepared for](#prepared-for), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### liable for, liable to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈlaɪəbəl fɔr, ˈlaɪəbəl tu/ &middot; *LY-uh-bul for, LY-uh-bul too* &middot; weak /ˈlaɪəbəl fər/ and /ˈlaɪəbəl tə/; two syllables, *LY-bul*, is a common casual reduction
**Русский:** юр. отвечающий за (for) перед кем-л. (to); разг. — склонный, того и гляди сделает
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal in the *for* frame, neutral to informal in the "likely" frame — and the informal one is thoroughly American
**Complement:** after *for*, a noun phrase naming the **harm or debt** you must answer for (*liable for the damages*) — **never a gerund** in the legal sense. After *to*, either a noun naming the **person owed** or a **person harmed** (*liable to the buyer*), or a **bare infinitive** in the entirely separate "likely to" sense (*liable to break*). The infinitive frame is the informal one: ✗ *liable to breaking*.
**Pattern:** *be liable for* [damages / debt] &middot; *be liable to* [person] *for* [amount] &middot; *be liable to* [verb] — the "likely" sense &middot; *hold* [someone] *liable for* [noun] &middot; *jointly and severally liable for* — the standard American formula
**Variants:** *responsible for* is the everyday equivalent of the legal sense; *on the hook for* is the slang one; *apt to* and *likely to* replace the informal sense; *answerable for* is the formal one.
**Contrast:** **Three frames, three meanings.** *Liable for the repairs* names what you owe. *Liable to the landlord* names whom you owe it to. *Liable to freeze tonight* means likely, has nothing to do with law, and is only ever followed by a bare verb — a use that strikes British ears as folksy and is unremarkable in America. There is also the older *liable to* + noun meaning "exposed to" (*liable to prosecution*, *liable to a fine*), which survives in statutes. ✗ *liable of* is an error, and ✗ *liable to pay* in the legal sense should be *liable for payment* unless you mean it is likely to happen.

Legally answerable for something, answerable to somebody — or, in the everyday sense, likely to do something.

1. The contractor is *liable for* any damage to the sidewalk.
2. Partners are jointly and severally *liable for* the firm's debts.
3. She's *liable to* the tenants *for* the deposit, whatever the manager did.
4. That pipe is *liable to* freeze if the crawlspace door stays open.
5. He's *liable to* say something he can't take back.

**See also:** [responsible for](#responsible-for), [accountable to, accountable for](#accountable-to-accountable-for), [answerable to](#answerable-to), [subject to](#subject-to), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### look forward to

**Pronunciation:** /lʊk ˈfɔrwərd tu/ &middot; *look FOR-wurd too* &middot; weak /lʊk ˈfɔrwərd tə/
**Русский:** с нетерпением ждать — после to герундий, не инфинитив
**Type:** verb + preposition — included here because it is the classic case of the fixed *to* &middot; **Register:** neutral; the standard sign-off of American business correspondence
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*look forward to hearing from you*) — **never a to-infinitive.** ✗ *I look forward to hear from you* is the single most-corrected error in professional English, and the noun test proves the point: *look forward to the weekend* ✓, so *to* is a preposition.
**Pattern:** *look forward to* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *look forward to* [someone] *-ing* (*look forward to your joining us*) &middot; *be looking forward to* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *much* / *greatly looking forward to* [noun]
**Variants:** *look ahead to* is the neutral news-writing version; *anticipate* takes a direct object with no preposition at all (*anticipate the results*).
**Contrast:** **The whole difficulty is that *forward to* looks like an infinitive and is not.** Compare the three: ✓ *look forward to seeing you*, ✓ *hope to see you*, ✗ *look forward to see you*. The same fixed *to* governs [object to](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#object-to), *be opposed to*, *be accustomed to*, *be used to*, *be committed to*, *be dedicated to*, [confess to](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#confess-to), [admit to](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#admit-to), and [resort to](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#resort-to) — a list worth learning as a set, because every one of them takes *-ing*: ✓ *object to being asked*, ✓ *confessed to taking it*, ✓ *resorted to calling her at home*. In the progressive, *I'm looking forward to* is warmer and more personal than the simple *I look forward to*, which is the formal sign-off. This is the one verb phrase given a full entry here rather than next door; the rest of the *look* family — *look at*, *look for*, *look after*, *look into* — is treated with the verbs.

Anticipate something with pleasure.

1. I *look forward to* hearing from you at your convenience.
2. She's *looking forward to* three weeks with no email.
3. We *look forward to* working with your team on the next phase.
4. He *looks forward to* Thursdays the way other people look forward to Fridays.
5. Nobody is *looking forward to* explaining this to the board.

**See also:** [accustomed to](#accustomed-to), [used to](#used-to), [opposed to](#opposed-to), [committed to](#committed-to), [look at / for / after / into](../../03-verbs/catalog/22-verb-preposition.md#look-at-look-for-look-after-look-into), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### loyal to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈlɔɪəl tu/ &middot; *LOY-ul too* &middot; weak /ˈlɔɪəl tə/
**Русский:** верный, преданный кому-л. или чему-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a noun phrase or pronoun naming the person, group, or cause you stand by — **never a gerund** (✗ *loyal to showing up*) and never a clause. The abstract complement (*loyal to the idea*, *loyal to a fault* — the fixed phrase) is a noun in every case.
**Pattern:** *be loyal to* [person / institution / cause] &middot; *remain* / *stay loyal to* &middot; *fiercely* / *doggedly loyal to* &middot; *loyal to a fault* — the frozen phrase &middot; *loyalty to* [noun] — the noun keeps *to*
**Variants:** *faithful to* is the near twin and covers marriage and duty; *devoted to* stresses feeling; *true to* covers principles and one's word; *committed to* is the workplace version.
**Contrast:** Against [devoted to](#devoted-to), which is about attachment and effort, *loyal to* is about not defecting — it presupposes that leaving was an option. Against *faithful to*, the difference is mostly domain: faithfulness belongs to promises and marriages, loyalty to people, teams, and brands. Note that *loyal* rarely takes a complement at all in advertising (*loyal customers*), where the noun follows the adjective instead. ✗ *loyal with* and ✗ *loyal for* are errors.

Steadfast in supporting a person, a group, or a cause when it would be easy not to be.

1. She stayed *loyal to* the shop through two changes of ownership.
2. He's *loyal to* the people who hired him, not to the company that bought it.
3. Customers *loyal to* the old formula complained for a year.
4. They remained *loyal to* a candidate everyone else had written off.
5. Nobody in that district is *loyal to* a party for its own sake.

**See also:** [devoted to](#devoted-to), [committed to](#committed-to), [dedicated to](#dedicated-to), [married to](#married-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### mad at

**Pronunciation:** /mæd æt/ &middot; *MAD at* &middot; weak /mæd ət/
**Русский:** злящийся на кого-л. — разг., обычное американское слово
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** informal; the American conversational default for anger
**Complement:** a **person** after *at*; a noun phrase or **gerund** after *about* (*mad about being left out*) — **never a to-infinitive.** A *that*-clause attaches to the bare adjective: *mad that nobody told her*.
**Pattern:** *be mad at* [person] &middot; *be mad about* [thing] &middot; *get mad at* [person] &middot; *be mad at* [person] *for* [verb-ing] &middot; *be mad about* [someone] — the British "in love with" sense &middot; *mad* alone, meaning insane
**Variants:** *angry with* / *at* is the neutral version; *ticked off at* and *pissed at* are the escalating informal ones.
**Contrast:** **The American and British senses of *mad about* are opposites, and this is a genuine transatlantic trap.** In American English, *mad about the schedule* means **angry** about it; in British English, *mad about the boy* means **crazy about** him. Americans use *crazy about* for affection and reserve *mad* for anger, so a British sentence like *She's mad about him* will be misread in the United States. Within American usage the split is clean: ***at*** takes the person, ***about*** takes the situation. ✗ *mad with* is an error in American English except in the fixed *mad with grief*, meaning driven insane.

Angry — the everyday American word, informal but universal.

1. She's still *mad at* me for telling her mother.
2. He got *mad about* the parking situation and wrote three emails.
3. Don't be *mad at* the dog; you left the gate open.
4. They were *mad that* the meeting had been moved without notice.
5. I'm not *mad at* you, I'm *mad about* how it was handled.

**See also:** [angry with](#angry-with), [furious with](#furious-with), [annoyed with](#annoyed-with), [crazy about](#crazy-about), [at](01-core-in-on-at.md#at)

### married to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈmɛrid tu/ &middot; *MAIR-eed too* &middot; weak /ˈmɛrid tə/
**Русский:** женатый на, замужем за; перен. — намертво привязанный к
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a **person** after *to*; in the figurative sense, an idea or an institution (*married to the job*) — **never a gerund** and **never a to-infinitive.**
**Pattern:** *be married to* [person] &middot; *get married to* [person] &middot; *marry* [person] — the **verb, with no preposition** &middot; *be married* (alone) &middot; *married with children* — a fixed idiom about having them, not about whom you married &middot; *married into* [a family]
**Variants:** *wed to* is the headline and literary version and takes the same *to*.
**Contrast:** ***Married to*, not ✗ *married with*.*** This is one of the most common errors made by speakers of Romance and Slavic languages, whose equivalents use "with," and it is worth drilling: ✓ *She's married to a pilot*, ✗ *She's married with a pilot*. The one place *with* is correct is the fixed phrase *married with two kids*, where *with* introduces the children, not the spouse. Note also that the **verb takes no preposition at all** — ✓ *He married her*, ✗ *He married with her*, ✗ *He married to her* — so the adjective and the verb differ; see the zero-preposition entry for [marry](#marry). *Married into* means joining a family by marriage.

Joined in marriage to someone — or, figuratively, bound to something.

1. She's been *married to* the same man since 1994.
2. He got *married to* his high-school lab partner twenty years later.
3. They're *married with* three kids and a dog nobody planned on.
4. The department is *married to* a process that stopped working years ago.
5. She *married* a man from Duluth — the verb, with no preposition.

**See also:** [marry](#marry), [engaged to](#engaged-to), [committed to](#committed-to), [related to](#related-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### material to

**Pronunciation:** /məˈtɪriəl tu/ &middot; *muh-TEER-ee-ul too* &middot; weak /məˈtɪriəl tə/
**Русский:** существенный для решения или исхода — юр./книжн.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal and accounting; in those fields it is a precise term (*material to the decision*, *a material misstatement*), and it is nearly the only place the adjective takes a complement
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the question, decision, or outcome that the fact could affect, or a **gerund** (*material to deciding the motion*). **Never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *material to decide the motion*. A *wh*-clause is fine: *material to what the buyer agreed to pay*.
**Pattern:** *be material to* [issue / decision] &middot; *a fact material to* [noun] &middot; *not material to* [noun] — the common denial &middot; *a material breach* / *material weakness* — attributive terms of art, no preposition
**Variants:** *relevant to* is the broader everyday word; *germane to* is about topical fit; *pertinent to* is the formal synonym of *relevant*; *significant to* is the plain paraphrase.
**Contrast:** ***Relevant* is the low bar and *material* the high one.*** Evidence is relevant to a case if it bears on it at all; a fact is material to a decision only if it could have changed the outcome — which is why American securities law defines materiality by whether a reasonable investor would want to know. Against [dispositive of](#dispositive-of), which settles the question outright, *material to* only means the fact could matter. Watch the unrelated senses of *material*: the noun (*building material*), and the adjective meaning physical (*material evidence* in the sense of tangible), neither of which takes *to*. ✗ *material for* and ✗ *material in* are errors here.

Important enough to a decision or an outcome that it could change it.

1. The lawsuit is *material to* the buyer's decision and has to be disclosed.
2. Whether the roof leaked is *material to* the price, not just to the mood.
3. Auditors flagged an error *material to* the quarter's results.
4. Nothing in the correspondence is *material to* the jurisdictional question.
5. Any change *material to* the coverage must be reported within ten days.

**See also:** [relevant to](#relevant-to), [germane to](#germane-to), [pertinent to](#pertinent-to), [dispositive of](#dispositive-of), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### mindful of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈmaɪndfəl ʌv/ &middot; *MYND-ful uv* &middot; weak /ˈmaɪn(d)fəl əv/ — the *d* is usually dropped: "MYNE-ful-uv"
**Русский:** помнящий о чём-л. и сообразующий с этим свои действия
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to neutral; common in official statements and in careful speech, and quite separate from the wellness sense of *mindfulness*
**Complement:** a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*mindful of being the newest person in the room*) — **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *mindful of to be the newest*. A *that*-clause attaches to the bare adjective with no preposition: *mindful that the deadline is Friday* ✓.
**Pattern:** *be mindful of* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be mindful* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *ever* / *always* / *painfully mindful of* &middot; *bear* [something] *in mind* — the idiom, with a different shape
**Variants:** *conscious of* and *aware of* are the neutral equivalents; *cognizant of* the formal one; *alert to* points forward at a risk; *heedful of* is the literary version.
**Contrast:** ***Mindful of* means you are letting the fact govern how you act***, which is more than [aware of](#aware-of) claims: a driver aware of the ice knows about it, a driver mindful of the ice slows down. Against [conscious of](#conscious-of), which reports what is present in your mind, *mindful of* reports what is guiding your conduct — which is why it fits instructions and public statements so well. Note the separate modern noun *mindfulness*, a meditation practice, which takes *of* only in its older sense. ✗ *mindful about* and ✗ *mindful to* are errors, though the imperative *mind the gap* is a different construction entirely.

Keeping something in mind and letting it shape what you do.

1. Be *mindful of* the neighbors when you run the saw before eight.
2. The court was *mindful of* the burden this places on small towns.
3. Ever *mindful of* the budget, she priced three options before calling.
4. He wrote the memo *mindful of* how it would read in six months.
5. We're *mindful of* being guests in somebody else's building.

**See also:** [aware of](#aware-of), [conscious of](#conscious-of), [cognizant of](#cognizant-of), [careful with](#careful-with), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### native to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈneɪtɪv tu/ &middot; *NAY-tiv too* &middot; weak /ˈneɪtəv tə/
**Русский:** родом из, исконно обитающий или произрастающий в
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to technical — biology, gardening, and geography; also used figuratively of habits and instincts
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming a **place, a region, or a habitat** — *native to the Pacific Northwest*, *native to alkaline soils*. **Never a gerund** and never a clause. When the complement is a person rather than a place, the frame changes: *native speaker of Spanish*, *a native of Ohio* — both with *of*.
**Pattern:** [species] *be native to* [place] &middot; *plants native to* [region] &middot; *a native of* [place] — the noun frame, with *of* &middot; *native* [noun] — attributive (*native grasses*, *native speaker*)
**Variants:** *indigenous to* is the formal equivalent and is the preferred word for peoples; *endemic to* is stronger and means found nowhere else; *originating in* is the neutral paraphrase; the opposites are *introduced*, *nonnative*, and *invasive*.
**Contrast:** ***Native to* says it originated here; [endemic to](#endemic-to) says it is found only here.*** Douglas fir is native to the Northwest and also grows elsewhere; an island snail may be endemic to one valley. Note the switch between adjective and noun: a species is *native to* Ohio, but a person is *a native of* Ohio — ✗ *he is native to Ohio* is not how Americans say it about people. In computing, *native to* has a live technical sense (*an app native to the platform*), meaning built for it rather than ported. ✗ *native of* after the adjective and ✗ *native in* are errors.

Originating in a particular place and belonging there naturally.

1. The shrub is *native to* the dry side of the mountains.
2. These beetles aren't *native to* North America and have no predators here.
3. The nursery stocks only plants *native to* the watershed.
4. Cynicism about zoning is *native to* this county.
5. The format is *native to* the older software and has to be converted.

**See also:** [endemic to](#endemic-to), [peculiar to](#peculiar-to), [specific to](#specific-to), [common to](#common-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### nervous about

**Pronunciation:** /ˈnɜrvəs əˈbaʊt/ &middot; *NUR-vuss uh-BOWT* &middot; weak /ˈnɜrvəs əˈbaʊt/
**Русский:** нервничающий из-за предстоящего
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *about* (*nervous about flying*) — **never a to-infinitive after *about***. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (*nervous to ask*), less commonly. After *of*, a thing feared — a usage that is British-flavored and rarer in the United States.
**Pattern:** *be nervous about* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *get nervous about* [noun] &middot; *be nervous of* [thing] — chiefly British &middot; *make* [someone] *nervous* (no preposition on the object)
**Variants:** *anxious about* is the more formal twin; *worried about* the more consequential one; *jittery about* and *antsy about* the informal American ones.
**Contrast:** ***Nervous about* is about an upcoming event; *afraid of* is about a standing fear.*** *Nervous about the flight* is one flight; *afraid of flying* is every flight. In American English, ✗ *nervous of dogs* sounds foreign — the American choice is *afraid of dogs* or *nervous around dogs*. Note the useful fourth frame *nervous around*, which names the company that produces the feeling rather than the event.

Uneasy about something that is coming.

1. He's *nervous about* the presentation on Monday.
2. She gets *nervous about* driving in snow after what happened.
3. Everyone was *nervous about* how the vote would go.
4. I'm *nervous about* leaving the house empty for three weeks.
5. He's *nervous around* horses, which makes them nervous too.

**See also:** [anxious about](#anxious-about), [worried about](#worried-about), [afraid of](#afraid-of), [scared of](#scared-of), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about)

### new to

**Pronunciation:** /nu tu/ &middot; *NOO too* &middot; weak /nu tə/ — American English says *NOO*, not the British *NYOO*
**Русский:** новичок в чём-л., ещё не освоившийся; о вещи — впервые встреченный кем-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral and everyday
**Complement:** a noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*new to managing people*) — **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *new to manage people*. The noun test settles it: *new to the job* ✓.
**Pattern:** *be new to* [place / job / activity] &middot; *new to* [someone] — meaning unfamiliar **to** them: *that idea is new to me* &middot; *brand new to* [noun] &middot; *fairly* / *relatively new to*
**Variants:** *unfamiliar with* looks at the same fact from the other end; *a newcomer to* is the noun phrase; *green at* is the informal one for inexperience; *just starting out* is the plain paraphrase.
**Contrast:** **The subject decides which way the phrase points.** With a person as subject, *new to* means inexperienced at something: *she's new to the department*. With a thing as subject, it means unfamiliar to somebody: *this rule is new to me*. The two are the same construction pointed in opposite directions, and only context tells them apart. Against [unfamiliar with](#familiar-with), which reports what you do not know, *new to* reports how recently you arrived. ✗ *new in* is used of places in some varieties (*new in town* is the fixed American exception) and ✗ *new at* survives only in *new at this*.

Recently arrived at something and not yet used to it — or, of a thing, not previously encountered.

1. He's *new to* the route and still checking addresses twice.
2. I'm *new to* keeping bees and have already been stung twice.
3. That objection is *new to* me — nobody raised it in committee.
4. She's *new to* the city but not *new to* the work.
5. Anyone *new to* the building gets a badge on the first day.

**See also:** [familiar with](#familiar-with), [accustomed to](#accustomed-to), [used to](#used-to), [acquainted with](#acquainted-with), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### nice to

**Pronunciation:** /naɪs tu/ &middot; *NYSS too* &middot; weak /naɪs tə/
**Русский:** любезный, приветливый с кем-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** informal to neutral
**Complement:** a **person or animal** after *to* — **never a gerund**, never a to-infinitive, never a clause. The judgment frame takes *of* plus a doer and an infinitive (*nice of you to come*), and the evaluative frame takes an infinitive with no preposition (*nice to meet you*).
**Pattern:** *be nice to* [person] &middot; *it is nice of* [someone] *to* [verb] &middot; *it is nice to* [verb] &middot; *be nice about* [something] — gracious regarding &middot; *play nice with* [someone]
**Variants:** *kind to*, *good to*, *pleasant to*, and *decent to* are the same frame at different temperatures.
**Contrast:** **The three *nice* frames say three different things.** *Nice to meet you* evaluates the meeting. *Nice of you to come* praises the person for coming. *Be nice to your brother* instructs about treatment. The last is the one that takes the fixed preposition, and it patterns with *kind to*, *polite to*, *rude to*, and *cruel to*. ✗ *nice with* is an error except in the informal *play nice with*, and ✗ *nice for you to come* changes the meaning to convenience rather than kindness.

Behaving pleasantly toward someone.

1. Be *nice to* your sister; she's had a long week.
2. The staff there is *nice to* everybody, which is not nothing.
3. It was *nice of* him *to* drive all that way.
4. She was *nice about* the whole misunderstanding.
5. The weather has been *nice to* us for three straight weekends.

**See also:** [kind to](#kind-to), [polite to](#polite-to), [friendly with](#friendly-with), [rude to](#rude-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### notorious for

**Pronunciation:** /noʊˈtɔriəs fɔr/ &middot; *noh-TOR-ee-uss for* &middot; weak /noʊˈtɔriəs fər/
**Русский:** печально известный чем-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to journalistic
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *for* (*notorious for showing up late*) — **never a to-infinitive.** After *as*, a noun naming a role (*notorious as a bad tipper*).
**Pattern:** *be notorious for* [fault / verb-ing] &middot; *be notorious as* [role] &middot; *notoriously* [adjective] — the adverb frame &middot; *notorious among* [group]
**Variants:** *infamous for* is the same idea with a stronger moral charge; *known for* is the neutral one; *famous for* is the positive one.
**Contrast:** ***Notorious is always negative.*** *Famous for its pies* and *notorious for its pies* are opposite reviews. The word cannot be used approvingly without irony, which is why ✗ *notorious for her generosity* reads as a joke. The adverb frame is often the neater choice — *a notoriously slow intersection* beats *an intersection notorious for slowness*. ✗ *notorious of* and ✗ *notorious about* are errors.

Widely known for something bad.

1. That intersection is *notorious for* fender-benders.
2. He's *notorious for* rewriting the agenda ten minutes before the meeting.
3. The permit office is *notorious for* losing paperwork.
4. She was *notorious as* the toughest grader in the department.
5. August is *notorious for* thunderstorms that arrive exactly at rush hour.

**See also:** [famous for](#famous-for), [known for](#known-for), [popular with](#popular-with), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)
