### obligated to, obliged to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɑblɪɡeɪtɪd tu, əˈblaɪdʒd tu/ &middot; *OB-luh-gay-tid too, uh-BLYJED too* &middot; weak /ˈɑbləɡeɪdəd tə/ and /əˈblaɪdʒd tə/
**Русский:** обязанный сделать что-л. по долгу или закону; в старом смысле — признательный кому-л.
**Type:** adjective (past participle) + preposition or infinitive marker &middot; **Register:** *obligated to* is the standard American form; *obliged to* is commoner in Britain and survives in America in the courteous *much obliged*
**Complement:** **two frames, and the *to* is a different word in each.** Before a **person or body**, *to* is a preposition: *obligated to the bank*, *obliged to her for the introduction*. Before a **duty**, *to* is an infinitive marker and a bare verb follows: *obligated to notify the tenant*. What is impossible is the gerund: ✗ *obligated to notifying the tenant*. The noun test tells you which frame you are in — *obligated to the bank* ✓ is prepositional, *obligated to notify* is not.
**Pattern:** *be obligated to* [verb] &middot; *be obligated to* [person] *to* [verb] — both slots at once &middot; *feel obligated to* [verb] &middot; *be under an obligation to* [verb] &middot; *much obliged* — the fixed courtesy, no complement
**Variants:** *required to* and *bound to* fill the duty frame; *indebted to* and *beholden to* fill the person frame in the "owe a favor" sense; *have to* is the plain everyday verb.
**Contrast:** ***Obligated* for duties, *obliged* for favors*** is the working American split: *I'm obligated to file by the fifteenth* is a duty, *I'm obliged to you for the ride* is thanks. British English uses *obliged* for both and finds *obligated* heavy. Against [beholden to](#beholden-to), which always implies a constraining debt, *obligated to* is neutral about how you feel. Note that this entry is one of the few in the file where a bare infinitive is **required** — the fixed *to* here is not always a preposition, which is exactly why the gerund error does not occur with it.

Bound by duty or by law to do something — or, in the older sense, indebted to someone for a kindness.

1. The landlord is *obligated to* give twenty-four hours' notice.
2. Nobody is *obligated to* answer a question at the counter.
3. She felt *obligated to* say something before the vote.
4. We're *obligated to* the bondholders first and to everyone else after.
5. I'm much *obliged to* you for driving out here in this weather.

**See also:** [beholden to](#beholden-to), [bound by](#bound-by), [incumbent on, incumbent upon](#incumbent-on-incumbent-upon), [liable for, liable to](#liable-for-liable-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### oblivious to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈblɪviəs tu/ &middot; *uh-BLIV-ee-us too* &middot; weak /əˈblɪviəs tə/
**Русский:** не замечающий того, что прямо перед ним — обычно с укором
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral, with a critical edge — to call someone oblivious is not a compliment
**Complement:** a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*oblivious to being watched*) — **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *oblivious to be watched*. A *that*-clause is impossible; use *unaware that* instead.
**Pattern:** *be oblivious to* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *oblivious of* [noun] — the older variant &middot; *completely* / *blissfully* / *cheerfully oblivious to* &middot; *oblivious* — used alone, with the complement understood
**Variants:** *unaware of* is the neutral equivalent; *blind to* is the metaphorical one; *heedless of* is literary; *out of it* is the slang paraphrase.
**Contrast:** ***Oblivious to* and *oblivious of* are both correct***, with *to* now dominant in American English and *of* the older and more literary choice; usage guides once insisted the word meant only "forgetful of" (from Latin *oblivisci*), but that battle ended a century ago. Against [unaware of](#aware-of), which is neutral, *oblivious to* implies the person should have noticed — the word carries a judgment. Against [impervious to](#impervious-to), which means it does not get through, *oblivious to* means it never registered at all. ✗ *oblivious about* is an error.

Not noticing something that is right there, and usually blamed for it.

1. He walked into the meeting *oblivious to* what everyone else had heard.
2. She's *oblivious to* how loud that laugh is.
3. Drivers seem *oblivious to* the new signal at the crosswalk.
4. The kids played on, *oblivious to* the fact that it was pouring.
5. He was *oblivious to* being the only one still arguing.

**See also:** [aware of](#aware-of), [conscious of](#conscious-of), [indifferent to](#indifferent-to), [impervious to](#impervious-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### obsessed with

**Pronunciation:** /əbˈsɛst wɪθ/ &middot; *ub-SEST with* &middot; weak /əbˈsɛst wəθ/
**Русский:** одержимый чем-л., не могущий перестать думать о
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral in the clinical sense; heavily used as hyperbole in American speech
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*obsessed with getting it right*) — **never a to-infinitive** and never a clause.
**Pattern:** *be obsessed with* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *become obsessed with* [noun] &middot; *obsessed by* [an idea that pursues you] &middot; *an obsession with* [noun] — the noun keeps *with*
**Variants:** *fixated on* takes *on*; *preoccupied with* takes *with*; *consumed by* takes *by*. The casual American *can't stop thinking about* is the plain paraphrase.
**Contrast:** ***Obsessed with* is the ordinary choice; *obsessed by* casts the idea as the agent that pursues the person*** — *obsessed by the thought that he'd forgotten something* is more literary and slightly more sinister than *obsessed with*. ✗ *obsessed about* is an error, although the verb *obsess* takes *about* correctly (*stop obsessing about it*), which is the source of the mistake. ✗ *obsessed on* borrows from *fixated on*.

Unable to stop thinking about something.

1. She's *obsessed with* getting the drainage right before the spring rains.
2. He's been *obsessed with* that band since eighth grade.
3. The department is *obsessed with* metrics that measure nothing.
4. I'm *obsessed with* this bread and I'm not going to apologize.
5. He was *obsessed by* the idea that the file had been altered.

**See also:** [addicted to](#addicted-to), [hooked on](#hooked-on), [crazy about](#crazy-about), [intent on](#intent-on), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### opposed to

**Pronunciation:** /əˈpoʊzd tu/ &middot; *uh-POHZD too* &middot; weak /əˈpoʊzd tə/
**Русский:** выступающий против чего-л. — после to герундий
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*opposed to raising the fee*) — **never a to-infinitive.** ✗ *opposed to raise the fee* is the error, and it is common in policy writing; the noun test settles it (*opposed to the increase* ✓).
**Pattern:** *be opposed to* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be strongly* / *firmly* / *bitterly opposed to* [noun] &middot; *oppose* [something] — the **verb, with no preposition** &middot; *as opposed to* [noun] — the comparison idiom
**Variants:** *against* is the plain preposition that says the same thing and takes the same complement (*against raising the fee*). *Averse to* is the formal near-synonym and also takes a gerund.
**Contrast:** **The verb and the adjective take different structures.** ✓ *They oppose the plan* (no preposition), ✓ *They are opposed to the plan* — and ✗ *They oppose to the plan* is a frequent error. Note the separate idiom *as opposed to*, which is a comparison marker rather than a statement of opposition: *hourly as opposed to salaried* simply contrasts two categories; see [as opposed to](09-comparison-opposition.md#as-opposed-to). *Not opposed to* is standard American understatement for mildly in favor.

Against something — a plan, a policy, a course of action.

1. The council is *opposed to* rezoning the whole block at once.
2. She's not *opposed to* working weekends, within reason.
3. Most residents are *opposed to* the highway extension.
4. He's firmly *opposed to* anything that lengthens the meeting.
5. We're talking about a lease, *as opposed to* a purchase.

**See also:** [committed to](#committed-to), [reluctant to](#reluctant-to), [against](09-comparison-opposition.md#against), [as opposed to](09-comparison-opposition.md#as-opposed-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### orthogonal to

**Pronunciation:** /ɔrˈθɑɡənəl tu/ &middot; *or-THOG-uh-nul too* &middot; weak /ɔrˈθɑɡənəl tə/
**Русский:** перпендикулярный (мат.); перен. — лежащий в другой плоскости, не относящийся к делу
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** technical in mathematics and statistics; borrowed as jargon into engineering, economics, and American office talk, where *orthogonal to* means "beside the point"
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the other vector, factor, dimension, or question — **never a gerund** and never a clause.
**Pattern:** [vector] *be orthogonal to* [vector] &middot; *orthogonal to* [the question at hand] — the jargon use &middot; *mutually orthogonal* &middot; *orthogonality* — the noun
**Variants:** *perpendicular to* is the geometric equivalent in two and three dimensions; *at right angles to* is the plain phrase; *independent of* and *unrelated to* are what the jargon use actually means; *uncorrelated with* is the statistician's term.
**Contrast:** ***Perpendicular to* is about lines you can draw; *orthogonal to* generalizes the idea to any number of dimensions*** — and to statistics, where orthogonal factors vary independently. The office use (*that's orthogonal to what we're deciding*) is a borrowed metaphor meaning "unrelated, and let's not conflate them"; some readers find it affected, and *unrelated to* says the same thing. Against [independent of](#independent-of), which is the plain word for the same relation, *orthogonal to* claims the two things can be varied without touching each other. ✗ *orthogonal with* and ✗ *orthogonal from* are errors.

At right angles to something — literally, in geometry, and figuratively, of questions that do not bear on each other.

1. The two vectors are *orthogonal to* each other, so their dot product is zero.
2. Choose a basis in which each axis is *orthogonal to* the others.
3. The two treatment factors were made *orthogonal to* one another by design.
4. Cost is *orthogonal to* the question of whether the thing works at all.
5. His objection is *orthogonal to* the motion on the floor.

**See also:** [perpendicular to](#perpendicular-to), [parallel to](#parallel-to), [independent of](#independent-of), [tangent to](#tangent-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### parallel to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈpærəˌlɛl tu/ &middot; *PAIR-uh-lel too* &middot; weak /ˈpærəˌlɛl tə/ — note the spelling, one *r* and two *l*'s in the middle
**Русский:** параллельный чему-л. — в пространстве или в развитии
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to technical; geometry, road directions, and analytical writing about resemblances
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the line, path, course, or case that runs alongside — **never a gerund** and never a clause. The variant *parallel with* takes the same complement.
**Pattern:** [line / street] *be parallel to* [line] &middot; *run parallel to* [noun] &middot; *parallel with* [noun] — the equally standard variant &middot; *a parallel to* / *a parallel with* [noun] — the noun frame, both prepositions available &middot; *draw a parallel between* [X] *and* [Y]
**Variants:** *parallel with* is fully standard and slightly commoner when the two things move together in time; *alongside* is the plain preposition; *analogous to* covers the figurative sense more precisely.
**Contrast:** ***Parallel to* and *parallel with* are both correct***, and no American reader will notice which you used; if there is a tendency, *to* suits fixed geometric relations (*a line parallel to the base*) and *with* suits things developing side by side (*her career ran parallel with his*). Against [analogous to](#analogous-to), which claims corresponding relations, *parallel to* claims that two courses run alongside without meeting. The noun takes *between* in the *draw a parallel* frame, which is a third pattern worth keeping separate. ✗ *parallel of* is an error, except in the geographic noun (*the 49th parallel*).

Running alongside something at a constant distance, in space or in development.

1. The service road runs *parallel to* the highway for about four miles.
2. Draw a line *parallel to* the base through the midpoint.
3. Her research is *parallel to* what the Michigan group published last spring.
4. The bike path is *parallel to* the river the whole way.
5. The two investigations proceeded *parallel to* each other for months.

**See also:** [perpendicular to](#perpendicular-to), [adjacent to](#adjacent-to), [analogous to](#analogous-to), [orthogonal to](#orthogonal-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### passionate about

**Pronunciation:** /ˈpæʃənɪt əˈbaʊt/ &middot; *PASH-uh-nit uh-BOWT* &middot; weak /ˈpæʃənət əˈbaʊt/, and in fast speech the preposition reduces to "'BOWT"
**Русский:** страстно увлечённый чем-л. — не на минуту, а надолго
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; a fixture of American cover letters, mission statements, and dating profiles, to the point of being a cliché in all three
**Complement:** a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*passionate about getting kids outdoors*) — **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *passionate about to teach*, ✗ *passionate to teach* in this sense. The noun test is immediate: *passionate about the work* ✓.
**Pattern:** *be passionate about* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *deeply* / *genuinely passionate about* &middot; *a passion for* [noun] — the noun frame, which switches to *for* &middot; *passionate* [noun] — attributive (*a passionate defense*)
**Variants:** *enthusiastic about* is the neutral equivalent; *crazy about* and *nuts about* are the informal ones; *devoted to* and *committed to* take *to*; *keen on* is British-leaning.
**Contrast:** **Adjective *about*, noun *for*.** ✓ *passionate about food*, ✓ *a passion for food*, ✗ *a passion about food*, ✗ *passionate for food*. That switch is the only structural trap here. The register trap is larger: American hiring managers have read *passionate about* so many thousand times that the phrase now carries almost no information, and a concrete verb usually beats it. Against [enthusiastic about](#enthusiastic-about), which is about visible energy, *passionate about* claims a deeper and more durable commitment.

Caring intensely about something over a long stretch, not just at the moment.

1. She's *passionate about* getting the trail finished before the fall.
2. He's *passionate about* barbecue in a way that has cost him a garage.
3. The whole staff is *passionate about* the work and underpaid for it.
4. I'm not *passionate about* spreadsheets, but I'm good at them.
5. They're *passionate about* keeping the pool open year-round.

**See also:** [enthusiastic about](#enthusiastic-about), [crazy about](#crazy-about), [serious about](#serious-about), [committed to](#committed-to), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about)

### patient with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈpeɪʃənt wɪθ/ &middot; *PAY-shunt with* &middot; weak /ˈpeɪʃənt wəθ/
**Русский:** терпеливый с кем-л., не раздражающийся
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a **person, animal, or process** after *with* — **never a gerund**, never a to-infinitive. The adjective also appears bare (*be patient*) and with *about* naming the matter (*patient about the delay*).
**Pattern:** *be patient with* [person / process] &middot; *be patient about* [matter] &middot; *be patient* (alone) &middot; *lose patience with* [person] — the noun keeps *with*
**Variants:** *tolerant of* takes *of*; *forbearing toward* is the formal one; *easy on* is the informal one.
**Contrast:** ***Patient takes *with*, not *to*,*** which puts it with *gentle with* and against *kind to*, *nice to*, and *polite to*. The reason is the same: *with* marks the manner of dealing, *to* marks the direction of treatment. Note that the noun follows the adjective — *patience with the process*, *out of patience with him* — and that the medical noun *patient* is a different word entirely, taking *of* only in the possessive sense (*a patient of Dr. Reyes*). ✗ *patient at* is an error.

Willing to put up with delay, difficulty, or slowness without getting angry.

1. She's endlessly *patient with* students who ask the same question twice.
2. Be *patient with* the software; it takes a minute to index.
3. He was never *patient with* anyone who hadn't read the material.
4. The coach is *patient with* mistakes and hard on carelessness.
5. Everyone lost *patience with* the process about six months in.

**See also:** [gentle with](#gentle-with), [kind to](#kind-to), [annoyed with](#annoyed-with), [indifferent to](#indifferent-to), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### peculiar to

**Pronunciation:** /pɪˈkjuljər tu/ &middot; *pih-KYOOL-yur too* &middot; weak /pəˈkjuljər tə/
**Русский:** свойственный только кому-л. или чему-л. и никому больше
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to neutral; standard in scholarly and technical writing, where it means "belonging exclusively to" and carries none of the "odd" sense
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the group, place, or thing that alone has the feature — **never a gerund** and never a clause.
**Pattern:** [feature] *be peculiar to* [group / place] &middot; *not peculiar to* [noun] — a common frame for denying that a problem is local &middot; *a usage peculiar to* [dialect] &middot; *peculiar* — the everyday adjective meaning strange, with no complement
**Variants:** *specific to* is the neutral modern equivalent; *unique to* is the strongest; *characteristic of* stresses typicality rather than exclusivity; *endemic to* covers species and diseases.
**Contrast:** ***Peculiar to* means found only there; the bare adjective *peculiar* means odd***, and the presence of the complement is what tells the two apart: *a custom peculiar to the valley* is exclusive, *a peculiar custom* is strange. Against [characteristic of](#characteristic-of), which says the trait is typical of a thing but may appear elsewhere, *peculiar to* claims exclusivity. Against [common to](#common-to), the two are near opposites: common to several, peculiar to one. ✗ *peculiar for* and ✗ *peculiar of* are errors.

Belonging to one group, place, or thing and to no other.

1. The vowel is *peculiar to* speakers born east of the river.
2. That kind of feud is not *peculiar to* small towns, whatever people say.
3. The bookkeeping method is *peculiar to* this one branch of the business.
4. There's a stubbornness *peculiar to* people who have farmed dry land.
5. The defect is *peculiar to* units built in the first two months.

**See also:** [specific to](#specific-to), [characteristic of](#characteristic-of), [endemic to](#endemic-to), [common to](#common-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### perpendicular to

**Pronunciation:** /ˌpɜrpənˈdɪkjələr tu/ &middot; *pur-pun-DIK-yuh-lur too* &middot; weak /ˌpɜrpənˈdɪkjələr tə/
**Русский:** перпендикулярный чему-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to technical — geometry, construction, and directions
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the line, surface, or direction — **never a gerund** and never a clause.
**Pattern:** [line] *be perpendicular to* [line / plane] &middot; *run* / *sit* / *set* [something] *perpendicular to* [noun] &middot; *the perpendicular* — the noun, meaning a vertical line &middot; *at right angles to* — the plain equivalent
**Variants:** *at right angles to* is the everyday paraphrase; *orthogonal to* is the generalized mathematical term; *normal to* is the physicist's word for perpendicular to a surface; *square to* is the carpenter's.
**Contrast:** Against [orthogonal to](#orthogonal-to), which generalizes to any number of dimensions and to statistics, *perpendicular to* stays concrete and geometric. Note that in physics and engineering the word for the same relation to a *surface* is *normal to*, which is the term a reader will meet in optics and mechanics. In carpentry, *square to* does the same job. ✗ *perpendicular with* and ✗ *perpendicular from* are errors.

Meeting something at a right angle.

1. The new wing is *perpendicular to* the original building.
2. Set the fence *perpendicular to* the blade before you cut.
3. The cross street runs *perpendicular to* the avenue for two blocks.
4. Drop a line *perpendicular to* the base from the opposite vertex.
5. Park *perpendicular to* the curb here, not at an angle.

**See also:** [parallel to](#parallel-to), [orthogonal to](#orthogonal-to), [adjacent to](#adjacent-to), [tangent to](#tangent-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### pertinent to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈpɜrtənənt tu/ &middot; *PUR-tuh-nunt too* &middot; weak /ˈpɜrtənənt tə/
**Русский:** относящийся к делу, имеющий прямое касательство к
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; the natural home is a document request, a policy memo, or a records search — *all documents pertinent to the transaction*
**Complement:** a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*pertinent to deciding whether to appeal*) — **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *pertinent to decide*. A *wh*-clause is fine: *pertinent to what the committee has to vote on*.
**Pattern:** *be pertinent to* [matter] &middot; [documents / facts] *pertinent to* [noun] &middot; *directly* / *hardly pertinent to* &middot; *pertain to* [noun] — the verb, catalogued with the verb patterns
**Variants:** *relevant to* is the neutral everyday word and will replace it anywhere; *germane to* is the parliamentary one; *material to* is the legal one; *applicable to* covers rules rather than facts; *appertaining to* is the archaic legal form.
**Contrast:** *Pertinent to* and [relevant to](#relevant-to) are close to interchangeable, with *pertinent* the more formal and slightly narrower of the two — it suggests the thing bears directly on the matter rather than merely touching it. Against [material to](#material-to), which in law asks whether the fact could change the outcome, *pertinent to* asks only whether it belongs to the subject. Do not confuse the adjective with *impertinent*, whose everyday meaning is "rude," though its older sense — not pertinent — survives in the legal phrase *scandalous and impertinent matter*. ✗ *pertinent for* and ✗ *pertinent with* are errors.

Bearing directly on the matter at hand.

1. Please produce every email *pertinent to* the September order.
2. That fact is interesting but not *pertinent to* the claim she filed.
3. The manual's third chapter is the only part *pertinent to* our setup.
4. Keep the questions *pertinent to* the position, not to her family.
5. Anything *pertinent to* safety goes in the first section of the report.

**See also:** [relevant to](#relevant-to), [germane to](#germane-to), [material to](#material-to), [probative of](#probative-of), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### pleased with

**Pronunciation:** /plizd wɪθ/ &middot; *PLEEZD with* &middot; weak /plizd wəθ/
**Русский:** довольный чем-л.
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase after *with*, *about*, and *by*; a **gerund** after *about* (*pleased about being asked*) — **never a to-infinitive after a preposition.** The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (*pleased to announce*) and a *that*-clause (*pleased that it worked out*).
**Pattern:** *be pleased with* [thing assessed] &middot; *be pleased about* [situation] &middot; *be pleased by* [the cause] &middot; *be pleased to* [verb] &middot; *be pleased* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *pleased with oneself*
**Variants:** *happy with* is the everyday equivalent; *satisfied with* the more measured one; *delighted with* the warmer one.
**Contrast:** ***With* takes what you are judging; *about* takes the situation; *by* takes the cause.*** *Pleased with the results* is a verdict; *pleased about the outcome* is a mood; *pleased by her reaction* points at what produced it. The infinitive frame is a formality marker — *We are pleased to inform you* — and never takes a preposition. *Pleased with himself* is fixed and means smug, not merely satisfied. ✗ *pleased of* is an error.

Satisfied and glad about something.

1. The client was *pleased with* the revised layout.
2. She's *pleased about* getting the earlier slot.
3. Everyone was *pleased by* how smoothly the move went.
4. We are *pleased to* offer you the position.
5. He looked far too *pleased with* himself for someone who'd just been lucky.

**See also:** [happy about](#happy-about), [satisfied with](#satisfied-with), [delighted with](#delighted-with), [content with](#content-with), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### polite to

**Pronunciation:** /pəˈlaɪt tu/ &middot; *puh-LYT too* &middot; weak /pəˈlaɪt tə/
**Русский:** вежливый с кем-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a **person** after *to* — **never a gerund** and never a to-infinitive. The judgment frame takes *of* plus a doer and an infinitive: *It was polite of him to ask first.*
**Pattern:** *be polite to* [person] &middot; *it is polite to* [verb] &middot; *it is polite of* [someone] *to* [verb] &middot; *polite about* [something] — gracious regarding
**Variants:** *courteous to*, *civil to*, and *respectful toward* are the formal ladder; *nice to* is the everyday one.
**Contrast:** ***Polite to* joins *kind to*, *nice to*, *rude to*, and *cruel to* in taking *to* for the person treated.*** ✗ *polite with* is an error. The difference from *nice to* is one of temperature: politeness is a matter of form and can be cold — *He was perfectly polite to her* often implies he was nothing more — while *nice to* implies warmth. *Polite about* names the matter one is gracious concerning: *polite about the burned dinner*.

Showing good manners toward someone.

1. Be *polite to* the inspector even if he's wrong.
2. She was *polite to* everyone and warm to almost no one.
3. It's *polite to* let the host start eating first.
4. It was *polite of* him *to* pretend not to notice.
5. The clerk stayed *polite to* the very end of a long afternoon.

**See also:** [rude to](#rude-to), [nice to](#nice-to), [kind to](#kind-to), [friendly with](#friendly-with), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### poor at

**Pronunciation:** /pʊr æt/ &middot; *POOR at* &middot; weak /pʊr ət/; also /pɔr/, rhyming with *pore*
**Русский:** слабый в чём-л. (о навыке); poor in — бедный чем-л. (о ресурсе)
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to neutral; *bad at* is the everyday version
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *at* (*poor at estimating time*) — **never a to-infinitive.** After *in*, a resource lacked (*poor in mineral content*), which is a different sense and the direct opposite of *rich in*.
**Pattern:** *be poor at* [skill / verb-ing] &middot; *be poor in* [resource] &middot; *a poor* [noun] — attributive, no preposition &middot; *poor showing* / *poor judgment*
**Variants:** *bad at*, *weak at*, *not strong at*, and *terrible at* run from neutral to blunt; *deficient in* is the formal counterpart of *poor in*.
**Contrast:** ***Poor at* a skill; *poor in* a resource.*** *Poor at math* is unskilled; *soil poor in nitrogen* is lacking a substance, and the second sense is the exact mirror of *rich in*. ✗ *poor on* and ✗ *poor with* are errors for the skill sense, though *poor with money* does occur informally on the model of *good with money*. Note the register split: American writing prefers *bad at* in speech and *poor at* in evaluations and reports.

Not good at something — or, in the other sense, short of some substance.

1. He's *poor at* estimating how long anything will take.
2. The topsoil here is *poor in* organic matter.
3. She's *poor at* delegating and knows it.
4. The team is *poor at* closing out games it should win.
5. Most of the district's schools are *poor in* everything but enthusiasm.

**See also:** [bad at](#bad-at), [terrible at](#terrible-at), [hopeless at](#hopeless-at), [rich in](#rich-in), [at](01-core-in-on-at.md#at)

### popular with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈpɑpjələr wɪθ/ &middot; *POP-yuh-lur with* &middot; weak /ˈpɑpjələr wəθ/
**Русский:** популярный среди, пользующийся любовью у
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a **person or group** after *with* and *among* — **never a gerund** and never a to-infinitive. After *in*, a place: *popular in the Midwest*.
**Pattern:** *be popular with* [group] &middot; *be popular among* [group] &middot; *be popular in* [place] &middot; *prove popular with* [group] &middot; *not popular with* [group] — the standard understatement
**Variants:** *popular among* is the formal equivalent and is preferred when the group is large or statistical; *a hit with* is the informal one; *well liked by* is the passive one.
**Contrast:** ***With* takes the audience; *in* takes the territory; *among* takes a group considered as a set.*** *Popular with teachers* and *popular among teachers* differ only in formality, but *popular in Texas* means something else — geography, not audience. ✗ *popular by* is an error (that would be the passive *liked by*), and ✗ *popular to* is an error, though *popular with* and *appeal to* cover the same ground with different words.

Liked by a lot of people in some group or place.

1. The evening section is *popular with* students who work days.
2. That policy was never *popular with* the people who had to enforce it.
3. Sardines are *popular in* parts of the coast and nowhere else.
4. The trail is *popular among* trail runners on weekday mornings.
5. Telling them at the last minute will not be *popular with* anyone.

**See also:** [famous for](#famous-for), [friendly with](#friendly-with), [known for](#known-for), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### possessed of

**Pronunciation:** /pəˈzɛst ʌv/ &middot; *puh-ZEST uv* &middot; weak /pəˈzɛst əv/, and before a consonant often /pəˈzɛs ə/ — the *t* drops: "puh-ZESS-uh"
**Русский:** обладающий чем-л. — качеством, способностью, имуществом; книжн./юр.
**Type:** adjective (past participle) + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal and literary, with a legal survival; an American meets *possessed of* in wills, in older fiction (*a single man in possession of a good fortune* is the same idea), and in judicial prose
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming what is held — a quality, a fortune, an estate, a faculty. **Never a gerund** and never a clause. The related but opposite frames take other prepositions and are set out under Contrast.
**Pattern:** *be possessed of* [quality / property] &middot; *seized and possessed of* [land] — the old conveyancing pair &middot; *possessed of* [one's faculties] &middot; *in possession of* [noun] — the noun frame, with *of*
**Variants:** *in possession of* is the ordinary modern phrase and is what a police report uses; *endowed with* covers qualities given rather than held; *have* is the plain verb that replaces all of them.
**Contrast:** ***Possessed of* means holding; *possessed by* means owned or controlled by; *possessed with* means gripped by an idea.*** A landowner is possessed of a hundred acres, a character in a horror film is possessed by a demon, and an obsessive is possessed with the notion that the neighbors are listening. Only the first is this entry. Against [endowed with](#endowed-with), which implies a giver, *possessed of* says only that the thing is held. The whole pattern is formal: in ordinary American English, write *has*. ✗ *possessed to* is not English.

Having something — a quality, a faculty, or an estate — in the formal register.

1. The testator was *possessed of* three properties at the time of death.
2. She is *possessed of* a memory that makes note-taking look optional.
3. Anyone *possessed of* the least common sense would have called first.
4. He remained *possessed of* his faculties to the end.
5. The trust is *possessed of* assets that have never been appraised.

**See also:** [endowed with](#endowed-with), [vested with](#vested-with), [seized of, seised of](#seized-of-seised-of), [replete with](#replete-with), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### predictive of

**Pronunciation:** /prɪˈdɪktɪv ʌv/ &middot; *prih-DIK-tiv uv* &middot; weak /prəˈdɪktəv əv/
**Русский:** позволяющий предсказать что-л., прогностический в отношении
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** technical — statistics, medicine, and evaluation research; the everyday phrase is *predicts* or *is a good sign of*
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the outcome predicted, or a **gerund** (*predictive of dropping out*) — **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *predictive of to drop out*. A *wh*-clause is fine: *predictive of who will renew*.
**Pattern:** *be predictive of* [outcome] &middot; *highly* / *only weakly predictive of* &middot; *predict* [outcome] — the verb, direct object, no preposition &middot; *predictive value* / *predictive model* — attributive
**Variants:** *a predictor of* is the noun phrase and takes *of* as well; *associated with* is the weaker and more careful claim; *diagnostic of* concerns present conditions rather than future ones; *prognostic of* is the clinical word for outcome.
**Contrast:** ***Predictive of* points forward; [indicative of](#indicative-of) points at what is already the case.*** A test result indicative of infection says something about now; a score predictive of relapse says something about later. Statisticians are careful with the word because prediction is not causation — *predictive of* claims only that knowing one improves your guess about the other. Note the verb takes no preposition at all: ✓ *the model predicts turnover*, ✗ *the model predicts of turnover*. ✗ *predictive for* is an error, though *a predictor for* turns up in clinical writing.

Useful for forecasting something else, whether or not it causes it.

1. Attendance in the first two weeks is *predictive of* whether a student finishes.
2. The marker is only weakly *predictive of* recurrence.
3. Nothing in the interview turned out to be *predictive of* performance.
4. Rainfall in March is *predictive of* the fire season by August.
5. Late payments are *predictive of* default about a year out.

**See also:** [indicative of](#indicative-of), [symptomatic of](#symptomatic-of), [suggestive of](#suggestive-of), [proportional to](#proportional-to), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### prejudicial to

**Pronunciation:** /ˌprɛdʒəˈdɪʃəl tu/ &middot; *pred-juh-DISH-ul too* &middot; weak /ˌprɛdʒəˈdɪʃəl tə/
**Русский:** наносящий ущерб правам или позиции, притом несправедливо — юр.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal; an American meets it in a courtroom objection (*unfairly prejudicial to the defendant*) and in employment and military discipline (*conduct prejudicial to good order and discipline*)
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the person, party, or interest harmed — **never a gerund** and never a clause. The complement is the victim, never the cause.
**Pattern:** *be prejudicial to* [party / interest] &middot; *unfairly prejudicial to* [noun] — the evidentiary standard &middot; *conduct prejudicial to* [noun] &middot; *without prejudice to* [rights] — the fixed phrase, catalogued with the legal prepositions
**Variants:** *detrimental to* is the neutral equivalent; *damaging to* the plain one; *adverse to* points at interests; *harmful to* covers people and bodies.
**Contrast:** ***Prejudicial to* adds unfairness to harm.*** In American evidence law all damaging evidence hurts one side — that is the point of it — so the question is never whether evidence is prejudicial but whether it is *unfairly* prejudicial, meaning it invites the jury to decide on the wrong basis. Against [detrimental to](#detrimental-to), which is neutral about fairness, and [adverse to](#adverse-to), which describes a position rather than an effect, *prejudicial to* is the strongest of the three. Note the noun phrase *without prejudice to*, which means "without giving up," and the everyday sense of *prejudice* as bias, which takes *against*. ✗ *prejudicial for* and ✗ *prejudicial of* are errors.

Damaging to someone's rights, position, or case, and unfairly so.

1. The photographs were ruled unfairly *prejudicial to* the defendant.
2. Delay of this length is *prejudicial to* the tenants' ability to respond.
3. The transfer would be *prejudicial to* creditors who filed on time.
4. Conduct *prejudicial to* good order carries its own charge.
5. Nothing in the amendment is *prejudicial to* rights already vested.

**See also:** [detrimental to](#detrimental-to), [adverse to](#adverse-to), [harmful to](#harmful-to), [material to](#material-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### preparatory to

**Pronunciation:** /prɪˈpærəˌtɔri tu/ &middot; *prih-PAIR-uh-tor-ee too* &middot; weak /prəˈpærəˌtɔri tə/
**Русский:** предваряющий что-л., совершаемый в порядке подготовки к — книжн.
**Type:** adjective functioning as a complex preposition &middot; **Register:** formal and somewhat stiff — corporate minutes, engineering reports, and older prose; in speech an American says *before* or *to get ready for*
**Complement:** a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*preparatory to filing the petition*) — the gerund is the commoner of the two — and **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *preparatory to file the petition*.
**Pattern:** [action] *preparatory to* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *steps preparatory to* [noun] &middot; *preparatory* [noun] — attributive (*preparatory work*, *a preparatory school*)
**Variants:** *prior to* and *in advance of* are the neutral formal equivalents; *in preparation for* is the noun phrase and takes *for*; *before* is the plain word and beats all of them in most sentences.
**Contrast:** **The phrase implies purpose, not just sequence.** *Preparatory to* says the first action was done *in order to* make the second possible, where *prior to* says only that it came first. Note the switch of preposition in the noun frame: *preparatory to the audit* but *in preparation for the audit*. American style guides treat the whole phrase as a candidate for cutting — *before the audit* usually says it better. For the plain time prepositions, see [time](05-time.md). ✗ *preparatory for* is an error in this frame.

Done in order to get ready for something that follows.

1. The crew shut off the water *preparatory to* replacing the main.
2. Files were boxed and indexed *preparatory to* the move.
3. She resigned from the board *preparatory to* filing her own suit.
4. Soil samples were taken *preparatory to* any grading work.
5. The rehearsal is *preparatory to* Thursday's walkthrough with the inspector.

**See also:** [previous to](#previous-to), [antecedent to](#antecedent-to), [prepared for](#prepared-for), [ready for](#ready-for), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### prepared for

**Pronunciation:** /prɪˈpɛrd fɔr/ &middot; *prih-PAIRD for* &middot; weak /prɪˈpɛrd fər/
**Русский:** готовый к чему-л.; с инфинитивом — готовый сделать что-л.
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a noun naming **what is coming** after *for* (*prepared for the storm*) — a gerund after *for* is possible but heavy. The adjective takes a **bare to-infinitive** directly (*prepared to leave*), which is the frame for willingness and is very common. **Never a to-infinitive after *for*.**
**Pattern:** *be prepared for* [event / eventuality] &middot; *be prepared to* [verb] &middot; *prepare for* [event] — the verb &middot; *prepare* [someone] *for* [event] &middot; *be well prepared for* [noun]
**Variants:** *ready for* / *ready to* runs exactly parallel and is the everyday version; *braced for* is the version reserved for something bad.
**Contrast:** ***Prepared for* is readiness; *prepared to* is willingness.*** *I'm prepared for a long meeting* means I have the coffee; *I'm prepared to stay late* means I'm willing. This is the same split as *ready for* / *ready to*, and the pair is worth learning as one lesson. ✗ *prepared of* is an error, and note that the verb *prepare* takes *for* the same way (*prepare for the worst*) but *prepare* + object takes no preposition (*prepare dinner*).

Made ready in advance — for what is coming, or to do what is asked.

1. The crew was *prepared for* a two-day outage and got a five-day one.
2. She's *prepared to* testify if it comes to that.
3. Nobody was *prepared for* how quiet the building would be.
4. Are you *prepared for* the questions they're going to ask?
5. We came *prepared for* rain and got sun the whole week.

**See also:** [ready for](#ready-for), [late for](#late-for), [suitable for](#suitable-for), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### previous to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈpriviəs tu/ &middot; *PREE-vee-us too* &middot; weak /ˈpriviəs tə/
**Русский:** предшествующий чему-л., до — книжн. вместо before
**Type:** adjective functioning as a complex preposition &middot; **Register:** formal and slightly dated; *prior to* has largely displaced it in American writing, and *before* beats both
**Complement:** a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*previous to joining the firm*) — **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *previous to join the firm*. No clause follows; for a clause use *before*.
**Pattern:** [event] *previous to* [event] &middot; *previous to* [verb-ing] &middot; *the previous* [noun] — the ordinary attributive adjective, which is far commoner &middot; *previously* — the adverb
**Variants:** *prior to* is the living formal twin and is what American business writing uses; *before* is the plain word; *ahead of* covers sequence in queues and schedules; *antecedent to* and *anterior to* are the archaic-formal versions.
**Contrast:** **Everything *previous to* can do, *before* can do more simply**, which is why usage guides on both sides of the Atlantic single the phrase out: *before the hearing* is shorter than *previous to the hearing* and loses nothing. Where it survives is in formal narrative and in older legal prose. Against [preparatory to](#preparatory-to), which implies purpose, *previous to* claims nothing but sequence. Note that the attributive adjective (*the previous tenant*) is entirely ordinary modern English and is not this pattern. For the plain time prepositions, see [time](05-time.md).

Coming before something in time — a formal way of saying *before*.

1. *Previous to* the merger, the two firms shared a single supplier.
2. She had no experience with grant work *previous to* joining the office.
3. Nothing in the file is dated *previous to* March.
4. *Previous to* that season, the team had never sold out a weeknight game.
5. Any claim arising *previous to* closing stays with the seller.

**See also:** [antecedent to](#antecedent-to), [preparatory to](#preparatory-to), [anterior to, posterior to](#anterior-to-posterior-to), [subject to](#subject-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### privy to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈprɪvi tu/ &middot; *PRIV-ee too* &middot; weak /ˈprɪvi tə/
**Русский:** посвящённый во что-л. — допущенный к тайне, плану, договору; не просто знающий
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to neutral; common in journalism and in speech about secrets (*not privy to those conversations*), and a technical term in contract law
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the information, discussion, or agreement you are inside of — **never a to-infinitive** (✗ *privy to know*), and a **gerund** is awkward enough that careful writers avoid it: prefer *privy to the discussions* over ✗ *privy to their discussing it*.
**Pattern:** *be privy to* [information / discussion] &middot; *not privy to* [noun] — the commonest frame by far &middot; *privy to the contract* — the legal sense &middot; *privity of contract* / *privity with* [party] — the noun frame
**Variants:** *in on* is the informal equivalent (*in on the plan*); *party to* is the legal near-twin and is catalogued with the noun patterns; *aware of* is weaker; *in the loop* is the office idiom.
**Contrast:** ***Privy to* means admitted to something private, not merely aware of it*** — the word implies you were let in. Against [aware of](#aware-of), which reports knowledge from any source, *privy to* reports access. The legal sense is narrower still: parties in *privity* have a direct contractual relationship, which is why a subcontractor is generally not privy to the owner's contract. Note the unrelated noun *privy* meaning an outhouse, a homograph that gives the word a faint comic edge in speech. ✗ *privy of* and ✗ *privy with* are errors, though *in privity with* is the correct legal phrase.

Let in on something private — a conversation, a plan, or a contract.

1. I wasn't *privy to* whatever they decided in that meeting.
2. Only three people were *privy to* the offer before it was announced.
3. Staff *privy to* the numbers signed confidentiality agreements.
4. The subcontractor was never *privy to* the owner's contract.
5. She was *privy to* the discussions but had no vote.

**See also:** [aware of](#aware-of), [cognizant of](#cognizant-of), [apprised of](#apprised-of), [acquainted with](#acquainted-with), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### probative of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈproʊbətɪv ʌv/ &middot; *PROH-buh-tiv uv* &middot; weak /ˈproʊbədəv əv/, with the *t* flapped
**Русский:** доказывающий спорный факт — юр., доказательственный
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal, narrowly — evidence law; the word is essentially confined to briefs, rulings, and evidence classrooms
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the **fact to be proved** — *probative of intent*, *probative of knowledge*, *probative of identity*. **Never a gerund** and never a clause.
**Pattern:** *be probative of* [fact at issue] &middot; *highly* / *marginally probative of* &middot; *probative value* — the noun phrase weighed against unfair prejudice under the American rules of evidence &middot; *more probative than prejudicial*
**Variants:** *tending to prove* is the plain paraphrase; *evidence of* is the everyday noun phrase; *indicative of* is the general word; *dispositive of* is the strongest.
**Contrast:** **The whole American law of evidence turns on a scale this word sits on:** evidence must be *relevant*, its *probative value* is then weighed against the risk of unfair prejudice, and only rarely is anything *dispositive of* the question. So *probative of* claims that the item tends to prove a fact — no more. Against [indicative of](#indicative-of), which is the general-purpose word for a sign, *probative of* is a term of art with a legal test behind it. ✗ *probative to* and ✗ *probative for* are errors.

Tending to prove a fact that is actually in dispute.

1. The receipt is *probative of* when he was in the building, not of why.
2. Counsel argued the tattoo was not *probative of* membership in anything.
3. Prior repairs are *probative of* notice, which is the whole issue here.
4. The court found the recording highly *probative of* intent.
5. Testimony about her reputation is not *probative of* what happened that night.

**See also:** [dispositive of](#dispositive-of), [material to](#material-to), [indicative of](#indicative-of), [suggestive of](#suggestive-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### proportional to

**Pronunciation:** /prəˈpɔrʃənəl tu/ &middot; *pruh-POR-shuh-nul too* &middot; weak /prəˈpɔrʃənəl tə/
**Русский:** пропорциональный чему-л.; разг. — соразмерный
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** technical in the mathematical sense, neutral in the everyday one about fairness
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the other quantity — **never a gerund** and never a clause. Where the relation is inverse, the fixed phrase is *inversely proportional to*, with the same complement.
**Pattern:** [quantity] *be proportional to* [quantity] &middot; *directly proportional to* &middot; *inversely proportional to* — as one quantity rises the other falls &middot; *in proportion to* [noun] — the prepositional phrase &middot; *proportionate to* [noun] — the variant adjective, commoner in law and about fairness
**Variants:** *proportionate to* is the near-identical alternative and is the usual word in legal and ethical writing (*a punishment proportionate to the offense*); *in proportion to* is the phrase for informal comparisons; *scales with* is the working-scientist's verb; *commensurate with* is the formal near-synonym.
**Contrast:** ***Proportional to* is a claim about a constant ratio***: if y is proportional to x, doubling x doubles y, and *inversely proportional to* means doubling x halves y. The everyday sense is looser and means only "appropriately sized," which is where *proportionate to* usually sits. Against [equivalent to](#equivalent-to), which claims equal value, proportionality claims a fixed relationship between two changing quantities. The noun switches preposition: *in proportion to*, never ✗ *in proportional to*. ✗ *proportional with* is an error.

Standing in a constant ratio to something else — or, loosely, sized to fit it.

1. The force is *proportional to* the square of the current.
2. Brightness falls off *inversely proportional to* the square of the distance.
3. Each partner's share is *proportional to* what they put in.
4. Staffing should be *proportional to* the number of beds, not to the square footage.
5. The fine is *proportional to* the tonnage, so the big haulers pay most.

**See also:** [equivalent to](#equivalent-to), [equal to](#equal-to), [predictive of](#predictive-of), [related to](#related-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### proud of

**Pronunciation:** /praʊd ʌv/ &middot; *PROWD uv* &middot; weak /praʊd əv/
**Русский:** гордящийся чем-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*proud of having finished it alone*) — **never a to-infinitive after *of***. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (*proud to serve*) and a *that*-clause (*proud that she'd asked*).
**Pattern:** *be proud of* [person / thing / verb-ing] &middot; *be proud of* [someone] *for* [verb-ing] &middot; *be proud to* [verb] &middot; *be proud* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *take pride in* [noun] — the noun frame uses *in*
**Variants:** *pleased with* is the cooler version; *boastful about* the negative one; *take pride in* the formal noun frame.
**Contrast:** ***Proud of doing* looks back at something accomplished; *proud to do* looks forward or reports an honor.*** *I'm proud of serving* recalls the service; *I'm proud to serve* accepts it now. Note that the **noun changes preposition**: ✓ *proud of her work*, ✓ *pride in her work*, ✗ *pride of her work* — a good example of why these patterns cannot be reasoned out from meaning. ✗ *proud for* and ✗ *proud about* are errors.

Feeling satisfaction at something you or someone connected to you has done.

1. She's *proud of* every one of those students.
2. He's *proud of* having built the whole deck himself.
3. They were *proud of* their daughter *for* saying something.
4. I'm *proud to* be part of the project.
5. The town takes *pride in* the fact that nothing here has ever burned down.

**See also:** [ashamed of](#ashamed-of), [pleased with](#pleased-with), [grateful to](#grateful-to), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### proximal to, distal to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈprɑksəməl tu, ˈdɪstəl tu/ &middot; *PROK-suh-mul too, DISS-tul too* &middot; weak /ˈprɑksəməl tə/ and /ˈdɪstəl tə/
**Русский:** проксимальный / дистальный по отношению к — анат., ближе к месту прикрепления или дальше
**Type:** paired adjectives + preposition &middot; **Register:** technical — anatomy, surgery, dentistry, and gastroenterology; an American meets them in an operative report or a physical-therapy note and nowhere else
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the reference point on the body or along a structure — **never a gerund** and never a clause. The pair is relative: everything is proximal or distal *to* something, so the complement is obligatory unless the reference point is understood (*the distal end*).
**Pattern:** [structure] *be proximal to* [structure] &middot; [structure] *be distal to* [structure] &middot; *just proximal to* / *immediately distal to* &middot; *the proximal* / *distal end* — attributive, complement understood
**Variants:** *nearer to* and *farther from* are the plain equivalents; *upstream of* and *downstream of* do the same job for vessels, bowel, and pipelines; *central* and *peripheral* cover the same idea for the body as a whole.
**Contrast:** ***Proximal* means nearer the trunk or the point of origin; *distal* means farther from it***, so the elbow is proximal to the wrist and the wrist is distal to the elbow. The pair runs along a limb or a tube, unlike [anterior to, posterior to](#anterior-to-posterior-to), which runs front to back. Outside anatomy, *proximal* has a live use in psychology (*proximal cause*, *the zone of proximal development*), and in law the near-homophone is *proximate cause* — a different word with an *-ate* ending. ✗ *proximal from* and ✗ *distal from* are errors: both take *to*.

Nearer to the point of attachment, or farther from it, along a limb or a tube.

1. The fracture is just *proximal to* the wrist.
2. The stent sits *distal to* the narrowing.
3. Sensation is intact everywhere *distal to* the injury.
4. The elbow is *proximal to* the forearm and distal to the shoulder.
5. Bleeding was found *distal to* the anastomosis.

**See also:** [anterior to, posterior to](#anterior-to-posterior-to), [adjacent to](#adjacent-to), [close to](#close-to), [native to](#native-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### punctual for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈpʌŋktʃuəl fɔr/ &middot; *PUNK-choo-ull for* &middot; weak /ˈpʌŋktʃuəl fər/
**Русский:** приходящий точно к назначенному времени
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; American speech says *on time*
**Complement:** a noun naming an **appointment or event** after *for* (*punctual for appointments*) — **never a gerund**, **never a to-infinitive**, never a clause. After *in*, a **gerund** naming what is done on time (*punctual in paying*).
**Pattern:** *be punctual for* [event] &middot; *be punctual in* [verb-ing] &middot; *be punctual about* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *punctual* alone: *she is always punctual*
**Variants:** *on time for* is the ordinary American equivalent and is far commoner; *prompt about* / *in* covers the same ground in business writing.
**Contrast:** ***Punctual for* an event; *punctual in* or *about* an activity.*** The word is the positive counterpart of *late for* and takes the same *for*. In American English it is markedly formal — a manager writes *punctual and dependable* in a review but says *she's always on time* out loud — so choosing it in casual writing sounds stiff. ✗ *punctual to* is an error, and ✗ *punctual at* is an error except with a clock time: *punctual at nine* is possible but odd.

Arriving or acting exactly at the appointed time.

1. He is unfailingly *punctual for* every shift.
2. The trains here are *punctual for* about eight months of the year.
3. She's *punctual in* answering even the tedious emails.
4. Guests are asked to be *punctual for* the six o'clock seating.
5. Being *punctual for* the first meeting bought him a lot of goodwill.

**See also:** [late for](#late-for), [ready for](#ready-for), [prepared for](#prepared-for), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### ready for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɛdi fɔr/ &middot; *RED-ee for* &middot; weak /ˈrɛdi fər/
**Русский:** готовый к чему-л. — подготовленный, согласный или вот-вот приступающий
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a noun naming **what is coming** after *for* (*ready for the season*) — a gerund after *for* is uncommon and reads as heavy. The adjective takes a **bare to-infinitive** directly (*ready to go*), which is the frame for willingness or imminence. **Never a to-infinitive after *for*.**
**Pattern:** *be ready for* [event / thing] &middot; *be ready to* [verb] &middot; *get ready for* [event] &middot; *be ready with* [something prepared in hand] &middot; *ready when you are*
**Variants:** *prepared for* / *to* is the formal twin and splits the same way; *set for* and *all set to* are the informal ones.
**Contrast:** ***Ready for* takes a thing; *ready to* takes an action.*** ✓ *ready for the interview*, ✓ *ready to answer*, ✗ *ready for answering* in most contexts. Note that *ready to* has a second, non-volitional sense meaning on the point of — *ready to collapse* does not mean the building is willing. *Ready with* is a third frame naming something held in reserve: *ready with an excuse*. ✗ *ready of* is an error.

Prepared, willing, or on the point of something.

1. The field won't be *ready for* play until Thursday.
2. She's *ready to* sign as soon as the numbers come back.
3. Get the kids *ready for* school; the bus moved up ten minutes.
4. He was *ready with* an answer before anyone finished asking.
5. That transmission looks *ready to* give out any day now.

**See also:** [prepared for](#prepared-for), [eager for](#eager-for), [late for](#late-for), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### related to

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈleɪtɪd tu/ &middot; *rih-LAY-tid too* &middot; weak /rɪˈleɪtɪd tə/
**Русский:** связанный с — родством, причинно или по теме
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or pronoun; a **gerund** where an activity is meant (*costs related to running the shop*) — **never a to-infinitive** and never a clause.
**Pattern:** [X] *is related to* [Y] &middot; *closely* / *distantly related to* [noun] &middot; [noun]*-related*: *weather-related delays* &middot; *relate to* [something] — the verb, meaning to identify with &middot; *in relation to* [noun]
**Variants:** *connected to* / *with*, *linked to*, and *tied to* are the neighbors; *akin to* is the loose literary one.
**Contrast:** ***Related to* covers kinship, causal connection, and topical relevance with one preposition.*** ✗ *related with* is an error, and it is a common one because *connected with* is correct. Keep the adjective apart from the verb *relate to* meaning to sympathize (*I can relate to that*), which is informal American English and says something about the speaker rather than the two things. The compound suffix *-related* has become the compact newsroom form: *a heat-related death*.

Connected — by family, by cause, or by subject matter.

1. She's *related to* half the families on that road.
2. The two outages turned out not to be *related to* each other.
3. Anything *related to* payroll goes through the third floor.
4. Costs *related to* running the second location doubled in a year.
5. Sales are up, though not for any reason *related to* the campaign.

**See also:** [relevant to](#relevant-to), [similar to](#similar-to), [married to](#married-to), [based on](#based-on), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### relevant to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɛləvənt tu/ &middot; *REL-uh-vunt too* &middot; weak /ˈrɛləvənt tə/
**Русский:** имеющий значение для, относящийся к обсуждаемому
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase or pronoun; a **gerund** where an activity is meant (*relevant to deciding the appeal*) — **never a to-infinitive** and never a clause.
**Pattern:** [X] *is relevant to* [Y] &middot; *directly* / *hardly* / *no longer relevant to* [noun] &middot; *relevance to* [noun] — the noun keeps *to* &middot; *relevant for* [purpose] — less common
**Variants:** *pertinent to* and *germane to* are the formal equivalents, both with *to*; *bear on* is the verb version (*that bears on the question*).
**Contrast:** ***Relevant takes *to*, never *for* in the ordinary sense and never *with*.*** ✗ *relevant with the discussion* is an error; ✗ *relevant of* is an error. *Relevant to the case* means bearing on it; *relevant for the case* is used occasionally to mean useful for that purpose, but *to* is the safe default. Compare *related to*, which asserts a connection of any kind, against *relevant to*, which asserts that the connection matters for the question at hand.

Bearing on the matter under discussion, in a way that matters.

1. None of that is *relevant to* whether the permit was filed on time.
2. The judge ruled the testimony *relevant to* the second count.
3. Half the syllabus is no longer *relevant to* how the work is actually done.
4. Anything *relevant to* the audit should be flagged.
5. Her earlier experience is directly *relevant to* this job.

**See also:** [related to](#related-to), [essential to](#essential-to), [subject to](#subject-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### reliant on

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈlaɪənt ɑn/ &middot; *rih-LY-unt on* &middot; weak /rɪˈlaɪənt ən/
**Русский:** зависящий от чего-л., полагающийся на
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*reliant on getting the grant renewed*) — **never a to-infinitive** and never a clause.
**Pattern:** *be reliant on* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be reliant upon* [noun] &middot; *heavily* / *increasingly* / *overly reliant on* [noun] &middot; *self-reliant* (no complement) &middot; *rely on* [someone] *to* [verb] — the verb frame
**Variants:** *reliant upon* is the formal equivalent; *dependent on* is the everyday twin and is far commoner.
**Contrast:** ***Reliant on*, like *dependent on*, takes *on* — never ✗ *reliant of* or ✗ *reliant to*.*** The difference from *dependent on* is one of tone: *dependent* can suggest weakness or need, while *reliant* is neutral about the relationship and is the word chosen in policy and technical writing (*reliant on imported grain*). Note the verb *rely on* takes an infinitive after a person — *rely on him to lock up* — and that this frame belongs to the verb, not the adjective: ✗ *reliant on him to lock up* is odd.

Needing something in order to function.

1. The region is heavily *reliant on* a single employer.
2. The whole system is *reliant on* one aging server.
3. Rural clinics are *reliant on* volunteers for weekend coverage.
4. She's *reliant on* the bus until the car is fixed.
5. We've become *reliant upon* forecasts that were never that accurate.

**See also:** [dependent on](#dependent-on), [independent of](#independent-of), [based on](#based-on), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### reluctant to

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈlʌktənt tu/ &middot; *rih-LUCK-tunt too* &middot; weak /rɪˈlʌktənt tə/
**Русский:** неохотно делающий что-л., не желающий — после to инфинитив, а не герундий
**Type:** adjective + **infinitive marker** — the counterexample in this file &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** a **bare to-infinitive** (*reluctant to sign*) — and here the *to* is **not a preposition**, so ✗ *reluctant to signing* is the error. Apply the noun test: ✗ *reluctant to the signature* fails, which proves the *to* is an infinitive marker. The adjective takes *about* + gerund for the prepositional frame: *reluctant about signing*.
**Pattern:** *be reluctant to* [verb] &middot; *be reluctant about* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *reluctantly* [verb] — the adverb is commoner than the *about* frame
**Variants:** *unwilling to*, *hesitant to*, *loath to*, and *disinclined to* all take the infinitive the same way; *averse to* takes a **gerund** and is the odd one out of that set.
**Contrast:** **This entry exists to mark the boundary.** *Reluctant to*, *hesitant to*, *unwilling to*, *ready to*, *able to*, *likely to*, and *about to* all take a **bare verb** — the *to* is an infinitive marker. *Accustomed to*, *used to*, *opposed to*, *averse to*, *committed to*, *dedicated to*, *devoted to*, *subject to*, and *look forward to* all take a **gerund** — the *to* is a preposition. There is no way to tell from the spelling, and the noun test is the only reliable check: if *to the noise* is grammatical, write *-ing*. Note the near-pair *averse to doing* versus *reluctant to do*, which mean the same thing and take opposite complements.

Unwilling to do something, and showing it.

1. The bank was *reluctant to* extend the line of credit.
2. She's *reluctant to* commit before seeing the final numbers.
3. Witnesses were *reluctant to* say anything on the record.
4. He was *reluctant about* the whole arrangement from the start.
5. Nobody is *reluctant to* take the credit once it works.

**See also:** [opposed to](#opposed-to), [accustomed to](#accustomed-to), [used to](#used-to), [ready for](#ready-for), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### replete with

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈplit wɪθ/ &middot; *rih-PLEET with* &middot; weak /rəˈplit wəθ/
**Русский:** изобилующий чем-л., до краёв полный — книжн.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to literary; a reviewer's and a judge's word (*the record is replete with examples*), and it sounds ornate in speech
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming what the thing is full of, usually plural or mass — **never a gerund** and never a clause. The complement may be good or bad, which is what separates the word from *fraught with*.
**Pattern:** *be replete with* [examples / detail / errors] &middot; *the record is replete with* [noun] — the standing judicial phrase &middot; *replete* — the rare bare use, meaning full up
**Variants:** *full of* is the plain everyday word; *rife with* is negative and journalistic; *fraught with* is negative and about danger; *teeming with* is about living things; *abounding in* is the literary variant with a different preposition.
**Contrast:** Against [fraught with](#fraught-with), which is always negative, *replete with* is neutral and often admiring — a book replete with detail is a compliment, a process fraught with detail is not. Against [full of](#full-of), the difference is register alone: *replete with* is what *full of* wears to a hearing. Note the older sense of *replete* meaning "having eaten enough," which survives in *repletion* in clinical writing. ✗ *replete of* and ✗ *replete in* are errors.

Abundantly supplied with something — filled up with it.

1. The record is *replete with* warnings the agency chose to ignore.
2. Her first novel is *replete with* the kind of detail only a nurse would know.
3. The manual is *replete with* diagrams and short on plain instructions.
4. That period of the archive is *replete with* letters from the same three families.
5. The report is *replete with* qualifications, which is how you know it was lawyered.

**See also:** [fraught with](#fraught-with), [full of](#full-of), [crowded with](#crowded-with), [endowed with](#endowed-with), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### representative of

**Pronunciation:** /ˌrɛprɪˈzɛntətɪv ʌv/ &middot; *rep-rih-ZEN-tuh-tiv uv* &middot; weak /ˌrɛprəˈzɛnədəv əv/, with both *t*'s flapped in fast speech
**Русский:** показательный для, представляющий собой целое в миниатюре
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to technical; standard in survey research, sampling, and criticism
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the whole that the sample or example stands for — **never a gerund** and never a clause. What follows *of* is the population, not the trait.
**Pattern:** *be representative of* [population / body of work] &middot; *broadly* / *hardly representative of* &middot; *a representative sample of* [noun] &middot; *a representative of* [organization] — the **noun**, which looks identical and means a delegate
**Variants:** *typical of* is the everyday equivalent; *illustrative of* claims only that it makes the point; *emblematic of* is the loaded literary word; *characteristic of* looks at the trait rather than the sample.
**Contrast:** ***Representative of* claims the example resembles the whole in the ways that matter*** — which is a statistical claim when the whole is a population, and a critical judgment when it is a body of work. Against [typical of](#typical-of), which says something is what you would expect from a group, *representative of* says it can stand in for the group. Watch the identical-looking noun: *a representative of the union* is a person, and confusion between the two is easy in a sentence like *she is a representative of the district* versus *her district is representative of the state*. ✗ *representative for* is an error in this sense, though *a representative for the company* exists as a job title.

Standing in for a larger whole because it resembles it in the relevant ways.

1. The sample isn't *representative of* the district's actual age spread.
2. These three cases are *representative of* the hundred we reviewed.
3. That painting is not *representative of* his later work at all.
4. Weekend traffic is not *representative of* what the intersection sees at rush hour.
5. The panel was chosen to be *representative of* the counties, not of the parties.

**See also:** [typical of](#typical-of), [illustrative of](#illustrative-of), [characteristic of](#characteristic-of), [indicative of](#indicative-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### repugnant to

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈpʌɡnənt tu/ &middot; *rih-PUG-nunt too* &middot; weak /rəˈpʌɡnənt tə/ — the *g* is pronounced, unlike in *impugn*
**Русский:** противоречащий высшей норме (юр.); разг. — глубоко отвратительный кому-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** two registers, and both are formal: a legal one meaning "inconsistent with and therefore void" (*a statute repugnant to the Constitution*), and a moral one meaning "disgusting to"
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the higher authority or the offended party — **never a gerund** in the legal sense; the moral sense allows a gerund as subject rather than complement (*being asked to lie was repugnant to her*). No clause follows.
**Pattern:** [statute / clause] *be repugnant to* [constitution / statute] &middot; [conduct] *be repugnant to* [person / conscience] &middot; *morally repugnant to* &middot; *repugnant* — the bare adjective, meaning disgusting
**Variants:** *inconsistent with* and *contrary to* are the neutral legal equivalents; *offensive to* and *abhorrent to* cover the moral sense; *incompatible with* is the general word.
**Contrast:** ***In law the word means "cannot stand with," not "disgusting"*** — Marbury's famous formula is that a law repugnant to the Constitution is void, and no revulsion is implied. In ordinary use the moral sense has taken over so completely that the legal one now reads as a term of art. Against [consonant with](#consonant-with) and [compatible with](#compatible-with), *repugnant to* is the direct opposite in the legal sense. Note that the bare adjective needs no complement at all in the moral sense: *a repugnant argument*. ✗ *repugnant with* and ✗ *repugnant for* are errors.

Irreconcilable with a higher rule — or, in the everyday sense, deeply offensive to somebody.

1. A local ordinance *repugnant to* state law cannot be enforced.
2. The clause is *repugnant to* the granting language earlier in the deed.
3. The practice was *repugnant to* every doctor who testified.
4. Nothing in the settlement is *repugnant to* the court's earlier order.
5. He found the whole arrangement *repugnant to* his sense of the job.

**See also:** [consonant with](#consonant-with), [adverse to](#adverse-to), [compatible with](#compatible-with), [opposed to](#opposed-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### responsible for

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈspɑnsəbəl fɔr/ &middot; *rih-SPON-suh-bull for* &middot; weak /rɪˈspɑnsəbəl fər/
**Русский:** ответственный за; виновник чего-л.; responsible to — подотчётный кому-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *for* (*responsible for closing up*) — **never a to-infinitive after *for***. ✗ *responsible to close up* changes the meaning entirely, because *responsible to* takes a **person you answer to**.
**Pattern:** *be responsible for* [duty / outcome / verb-ing] &middot; *be responsible to* [person or body one answers to] &middot; *hold* [someone] *responsible for* [noun] &middot; *personally responsible for* [noun] &middot; *responsibility for* [noun] — the noun keeps *for*
**Variants:** *accountable for* a result and *accountable to* a person split exactly the same way; *in charge of* covers the duty sense; *to blame for* covers the fault sense.
**Contrast:** ***Responsible for* names the job or the fault; *responsible to* names the boss.*** *She's responsible for scheduling* is her duty; *She's responsible to the board* is her reporting line. Mixing them produces a real misunderstanding rather than a stylistic slip. Note the two senses of the *for* frame — duty (*responsible for the night shift*) and culpability (*responsible for the leak*) — which are distinguished only by context, and the bare adjective meaning trustworthy (*a responsible adult*), which takes no preposition at all.

Having a duty for something, having caused something, or answering to someone.

1. She's *responsible for* everything that leaves the loading dock.
2. Faulty wiring was *responsible for* the outage.
3. The director is *responsible to* a seven-member board.
4. Each department is *responsible for* keeping its own inventory.
5. Nobody wanted to be held *responsible for* the decision afterward.

**See also:** [guilty of](#guilty-of), [innocent of](#innocent-of), [subject to](#subject-to), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### rich in

**Pronunciation:** /rɪtʃ ɪn/ &middot; *RICH in* &middot; weak /rɪtʃ ən/
**Русский:** богатый чем-л., с высоким содержанием чего-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; common in nutrition, geology, and description
**Complement:** a **mass noun** naming what there is a lot of (*rich in iron*, *rich in detail*) — **never a gerund**, **never a to-infinitive**, never a clause.
**Pattern:** *be rich in* [substance / quality] &middot; [noun] *rich in* [noun] &middot; [noun]*-rich*: *protein-rich*, *nutrient-rich* &middot; *rich with* [something abundant and sensory] &middot; *rich* alone: *a rich family*
**Variants:** *high in* is the everyday nutrition version (*high in sodium*); *abundant in* and *replete with* are the formal ones; *loaded with* is the informal one.
**Contrast:** ***Rich in* is the standard; *rich with* is possible when the abundance is sensory or atmospheric*** — *air rich with woodsmoke* — and it is the more literary choice. ✗ *rich of* and ✗ *rich on* are errors. The exact opposite is *poor in* (*soil poor in nitrogen*), which is worth learning as a pair, since the antonym keeps the same preposition. Note that the plain sense of *rich* — having money — takes no preposition.

Containing a great deal of something.

1. Kale is *rich in* vitamin K and nobody is happy about it.
2. The valley is *rich in* iron ore and short of everything else.
3. Her account is *rich in* the kind of detail that can't be invented.
4. The soil along the creek is *rich in* organic matter.
5. The town's history is *rich in* feuds and thin on documents.

**See also:** [full of](#full-of), [poor at](#poor-at), [crowded with](#crowded-with), [lacking in](#lacking-in), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### rude to

**Pronunciation:** /rud tu/ &middot; *ROOD too* &middot; weak /rud tə/
**Русский:** грубый с кем-л., невежливый
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a **person** after *to* — **never a gerund** and never a to-infinitive. The judgment frame takes *of* plus a doer and an infinitive: *It was rude of her to interrupt.*
**Pattern:** *be rude to* [person] &middot; *it is rude to* [verb] &middot; *it was rude of* [someone] *to* [verb] &middot; *rude about* [something] — insulting concerning
**Variants:** *impolite to*, *discourteous to*, and *disrespectful toward* are the formal ladder; *nasty to* and *short with* are the informal ones — note that *short with* takes *with*.
**Contrast:** ***Rude to* the person, *rude about* the subject.*** *She was rude to the waiter* is how she treated him; *She was rude about the food* is what she said concerning it. The *of*-frame assigns blame to the doer: *It was rude of him to leave without saying goodbye*. ✗ *rude with* is an error, though *short with* and *sharp with*, which mean something close, both take *with*.

Behaving in a way that offends ordinary good manners.

1. He was *rude to* the receptionist and wondered why the appointment slipped.
2. It's *rude to* check your phone while someone is answering your question.
3. She was *rude about* the neighbors within earshot of the neighbors.
4. It was *rude of* them *to* leave before the toast.
5. Nobody there was *rude to* us; they were just impossible to reach.

**See also:** [polite to](#polite-to), [cruel to](#cruel-to), [nice to](#nice-to), [kind to](#kind-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### sad about

**Pronunciation:** /sæd əˈbaʊt/ &middot; *SAD uh-BOWT* &middot; weak /sæd əˈbaʊt/
**Русский:** огорчённый чем-л., которому грустно из-за
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *about* (*sad about leaving*) — **never a to-infinitive after *about***. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (*sad to see it go*) and a *that*-clause (*sad that nobody came*).
**Pattern:** *be sad about* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be sad to* [verb] &middot; *be sad* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *be sad for* [someone] &middot; *sad* alone, of a thing: *a sad story*
**Variants:** *upset about* is the more agitated version; *sorry about* the apologetic one; *heartbroken over* the intensified one, which takes *over*.
**Contrast:** ***Sad about* names the situation; *sad for* names the person you feel it on behalf of; *sad to* + verb reports the reaction as it happens.*** *Sad about the closing* is the fact; *sad for the staff* is on their behalf; *sad to hear it* is the moment of learning. ✗ *sad of* and ✗ *sad with* are errors. Note the useful distinction from *sorry about*, which carries either apology or sympathy — *I'm sorry about your father* is condolence, while *I'm sad about your father* would be an odd thing to say to a mourner, because it centers the speaker.

Unhappy about something.

1. She's *sad about* leaving the house more than about leaving the town.
2. Everyone was *sad about* the way the season ended.
3. I'm *sad for* him; he put four years into that program.
4. It was *sad to* see the place boarded up.
5. He's *sad about* not having asked more questions while he could.

**See also:** [upset about](#upset-about), [sorry for](#sorry-for), [disappointed with](#disappointed-with), [happy about](#happy-about), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about)

### safe from

**Pronunciation:** /seɪf frʌm/ &middot; *SAYF from* &middot; weak /seɪf frəm/
**Русский:** защищённый от, вне досягаемости опасности
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a noun naming the **danger escaped** after *from* — **a gerund is possible but rare.** The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive in a different frame (*safe to drink*, *safe to assume*), which describes the thing rather than the person. **Never a to-infinitive after *from*.**
