**Pattern:** *be safe from* [danger] &middot; *be safe to* [verb] &middot; *keep* [someone] *safe from* [danger] &middot; *safe for* [a use or a group]: *safe for kids* &middot; *safe with* [a person entrusted]: *the secret is safe with me*
**Variants:** *secure from* and *protected from* / *against* are the formal equivalents; *out of reach of* is the concrete one.
**Contrast:** **Four frames, four jobs.** ***Safe from*** the threat; ***safe for*** the user or purpose (*safe for pets*); ***safe with*** the person you trusted; ***safe to*** + verb about what may be done (*safe to eat*). Substituting one changes the sentence: *safe for children* means it will not hurt them, while *safe from children* means they cannot get at it — and both sentences occur in real life. ✗ *safe of* is an error.

Out of reach of some danger.

1. The files are *safe from* anything short of a fire.
2. Nothing on that block was *safe from* the reassessment.
3. Keep the seedlings *safe from* frost for another two weeks.
4. It's *safe to* assume the meeting will run long.
5. Your secret is *safe with* me, which people say and rarely mean.

**See also:** [free of](#free-of), [vulnerable to](#vulnerable-to), [independent of](#independent-of), [from](02-core-relational.md#from)

### satisfied with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsætɪsfaɪd wɪθ/ &middot; *SAT-iss-fyd with* &middot; weak /ˈsætɪsfaɪd wəθ/
**Русский:** удовлетворённый чем-л.; в офиц. смысле — убеждённый в чём-л.
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *with* (*satisfied with letting it stand*) — **never a to-infinitive after *with***. A *that*-clause attaches to the bare adjective in the "convinced" sense: *satisfied that the accounts were in order*.
**Pattern:** *be satisfied with* [thing assessed] &middot; *be satisfied* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *be satisfied by* [the thing that satisfies] &middot; *satisfy* [someone] — the verb, no preposition &middot; *satisfaction with* [noun] — the noun keeps *with*
**Variants:** *happy with* and *pleased with* are the everyday twins; *content with* is the quieter one; *okay with* is the conversational one.
**Contrast:** ***Satisfied with* means content; *satisfied that* means convinced.*** The second sense is the language of inspectors and judges: *satisfied that the requirements have been met* is a finding, not a mood. *Satisfied by* points at the cause, and it is also the frame used for requirements: *the condition was satisfied by the second payment*. ✗ *satisfied of* is an error in modern American English, though *satisfied of the truth of it* survives in legal prose.

Content with what was received or done — or, in the formal sense, convinced.

1. The customer was *satisfied with* the replacement and dropped the complaint.
2. She wasn't *satisfied with* the first three explanations.
3. The inspector was *satisfied that* the repairs met code.
4. He seems *satisfied with* the arrangement, or at least done arguing about it.
5. Nobody is ever entirely *satisfied with* a compromise, which is how you know it worked.

**See also:** [pleased with](#pleased-with), [content with](#content-with), [happy about](#happy-about), [disappointed with](#disappointed-with), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### scared of

**Pronunciation:** /skɛrd ʌv/ &middot; *SKAIRD uv* &middot; weak /skɛrd əv/
**Русский:** боящийся, испуганный чем-л.
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** informal to neutral; the everyday American word
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*scared of falling*) after *of*; a noun after *by* — **never a to-infinitive after a preposition.** The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (*scared to look*), a separate frame.
**Pattern:** *be scared of* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be scared by* [the event that caused it] &middot; *be scared to* [verb] &middot; *be scared for* [someone] &middot; *scared stiff* / *scared to death* &middot; *scare* [someone] (no preposition on the object)
**Variants:** *afraid of* is the neutral twin; *frightened of* the slightly formal one; *terrified of* and *petrified of* the intensified ones.
**Contrast:** ***Scared of* is the standing fear, *scared by* the single event, *scared to* the hesitation to act.*** *Scared of the dark* is general; *scared by the noise* was one moment; *scared to open it* is not doing something. All three are correct and they are not interchangeable. ✗ *scared from* is an error. Register is the only thing separating *scared of* from *afraid of*: the first is what Americans say, the second is what they write.

Feeling fear.

1. He's *scared of* heights and took the job on the ninth floor anyway.
2. She was *scared by* how quickly the water came up.
3. I was *scared to* ask what the estimate was.
4. They were *scared for* the crew still out on the water.
5. Everybody's a little *scared of* the first day.

**See also:** [afraid of](#afraid-of), [frightened of](#frightened-of), [nervous about](#nervous-about), [worried about](#worried-about), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### seized of, seised of

**Pronunciation:** /sizd ʌv/ &middot; *SEEZD uv* &middot; weak /sizd əv/ — both spellings are pronounced the same
**Русский:** юр. владеющий недвижимостью на законном праве; о суде — принявший дело к рассмотрению
**Type:** adjective (past participle) + preposition &middot; **Register:** legal and archaic-legal; the *seised* spelling is the traditional one in real-property law, and an American meets either only in a deed, a title opinion, or a court's statement that it has a matter in hand
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming land, an estate, or a matter before a tribunal — **never a gerund** and never a clause.
**Pattern:** *be seized of* [land / estate] — the property sense &middot; *be seised of* [an estate in fee simple] — the conveyancing spelling &middot; *the court is seized of* [the matter] — the jurisdiction sense, borrowed from international practice &middot; *seized with* [a matter] — a variant of the second sense
**Variants:** *seised of* is the same phrase in the older spelling kept by property lawyers to distinguish it from ordinary seizing; *possessed of* is the neighboring formal phrase; *in possession of* is the modern equivalent; *have jurisdiction over* replaces the tribunal sense in American courts, which more often say *the matter is before the court*.
**Contrast:** ***Seized of* means lawfully holding, which is nearly the opposite of what the everyday verb suggests*** — police seize property from you, but a landowner is seized of an estate in it. The tribunal sense (*the court is seized of the matter*) is common in international and European practice and is used in America chiefly by arbitrators and by writers with a foreign audience. Against [possessed of](#possessed-of), which covers qualities as well as property, *seized of* is confined to estates in land and to matters before a tribunal. ✗ *seized to* is not English, and *seized by* means the ordinary taking.

Lawfully holding an estate in land — or, of a tribunal, having a matter properly in hand.

1. The grantor was *seized of* an estate in fee simple at the time of the conveyance.
2. Title showed the decedent *seised of* the eastern half only.
3. The tribunal is *seized of* the dispute and has set a briefing schedule.
4. Anyone *seized of* land subject to the covenant takes it with the burden.
5. The abstract recites that she died *seized of* the property.

**See also:** [possessed of](#possessed-of), [vested with](#vested-with), [appurtenant to](#appurtenant-to), [entitled to](#entitled-to), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### sensitive to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsɛnsətɪv tu/ &middot; *SEN-suh-tiv too* &middot; weak /ˈsɛnsətɪv tə/
**Русский:** чувствительный к — физически, эмоционально или деликатный по отношению к
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or pronoun after *to*; noun phrase or **gerund** after *about* (*sensitive about being asked her age*) — **never a to-infinitive after either.**
**Pattern:** *be sensitive to* [stimulus / concern] &middot; *be sensitive about* [a personal sore point] &middot; *sensitive to* [substance] — the allergic sense &middot; *a sensitive* [noun] — attributive, meaning delicate or confidential
**Variants:** *responsive to* covers the neutral technical sense; *touchy about* is the informal version of *sensitive about*; *allergic to* is the medical one.
**Contrast:** ***Sensitive to* points outward; *sensitive about* points inward.*** *Sensitive to criticism* means it affects you; *sensitive about your accent* means you don't want it mentioned. A third sense is purely physical — *sensitive to light*, *sensitive to penicillin* — and a fourth is approving: *sensitive to the concerns of the neighborhood* means considerate, not fragile. ✗ *sensitive of* is an error, though *sensitive of you to notice* appears occasionally and is better rendered *thoughtful of you*.

Reacting readily to something — physically, emotionally, or considerately.

1. The film is *sensitive to* even a few seconds of daylight.
2. He's *sensitive about* how long it took him to finish school.
3. The proposal is *sensitive to* small changes in the discount rate.
4. She's *sensitive to* the fact that half the staff has been here longer.
5. Some patients are *sensitive to* the dye used in the scan.

**See also:** [vulnerable to](#vulnerable-to), [aware of](#aware-of), [harmful to](#harmful-to), [indifferent to](#indifferent-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### serious about

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsɪriəs əˈbaʊt/ &middot; *SEER-ee-uss uh-BOWT* &middot; weak /ˈsɪriəs əˈbaʊt/
**Русский:** всерьёз настроенный на что-л., не шутящий
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*serious about going back to school*) — **never a to-infinitive.** ✗ *serious to go back* is an error.
**Pattern:** *be serious about* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *get serious about* [noun] &middot; *be serious* (alone): *are you serious?* &middot; *dead serious about* [noun] &middot; *serious about* [someone] — in a relationship
**Variants:** *in earnest about* is the formal one; *committed to* takes *to* and a gerund; *not kidding about* is the conversational one.
**Contrast:** ***Serious about* + *-ing* versus *determined to* + verb** is the usual pairing: same idea, opposite complements. Note the relationship sense — *serious about her*, meaning committed romantically — which is one of the few cases where the object of *about* is a person. Do not confuse the adjective with *serious* meaning grave (*a serious injury*), which takes no preposition at all. ✗ *serious in* is an error, though *serious in tone* and *serious in intent* are correct phrases where *in* names the respect.

Genuinely intending something, and not joking about it.

1. She's *serious about* moving the whole operation to the coast.
2. If you're *serious about* the offer, put it in writing.
3. They finally got *serious about* fixing the drainage.
4. He's *serious about* her, which surprised everyone including him.
5. The county is *serious about* enforcing the ordinance this time.

**See also:** [committed to](#committed-to), [intent on](#intent-on), [enthusiastic about](#enthusiastic-about), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about)

### shocked at

**Pronunciation:** /ʃɑkt æt/ &middot; *SHOKT at* &middot; weak /ʃɑkt ət/
**Русский:** потрясённый, шокированный чем-л. неожиданным
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *at* and *by* (*shocked at finding the door open*) — **never a to-infinitive after a preposition.** The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (*shocked to learn*) and a *that*-clause (*shocked that nobody objected*).
**Pattern:** *be shocked at* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be shocked by* [the cause] &middot; *be shocked to* [verb] &middot; *be shocked* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *shocked into* [verb-ing] &middot; *shock* [someone] (no preposition on the object)
**Variants:** *appalled at* / *by* and *horrified at* / *by* are the stronger versions with the same split; *taken aback by* is the milder one.
**Contrast:** **Same *at* / *by* split as *surprised* and *astonished*: *at* frames it as your reaction, *by* frames it as the thing acting on you.** *Shocked at the price* is a judgment; *shocked by the news* is an effect. *Shocked to learn* reports the moment. The literal electrical sense takes *by* only (*shocked by a live wire*), which is a useful anchor for remembering which preposition means "acted on by." ✗ *shocked of* and ✗ *shocked with* are errors.

Startled and upset by something unexpected, usually something bad.

1. Neighbors were *shocked at* how fast the building came down.
2. She was *shocked by* the tone of the letter.
3. I was *shocked to* find out he'd been gone since March.
4. The board was *shocked at* the size of the shortfall.
5. He was *shocked into* silence, which for him was remarkable.

**See also:** [surprised at](#surprised-at), [astonished at](#astonished-at), [upset about](#upset-about), [at](01-core-in-on-at.md#at)

### short of

**Pronunciation:** /ʃɔrt ʌv/ &middot; *SHORT uv* &middot; weak /ʃɔrt əv/
**Русский:** испытывающий нехватку чего-л.; во втором значении — кроме, разве что не (+ герундий)
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase after *of* in the "lacking" sense; noun phrase or **gerund** in the "except" sense (*short of rebuilding the whole thing*) — **never a to-infinitive.** After *on*, a noun naming the supply that is low.
**Pattern:** *be short of* [something needed] &middot; *be short on* [supply] &middot; *short of* [verb-ing] — meaning "except by" &middot; *fall short of* [standard] &middot; *nothing short of* [noun] &middot; *stop short of* [verb-ing]
**Variants:** *low on* and *out of* are the everyday American versions of *short on* and *short of*; *wanting in* and *deficient in* are the formal ones and take *in*.
**Contrast:** ***Short of* has two distinct senses in the same shape.*** The first is lacking: *short of cash*. The second is "anything except": *Short of selling the building, I don't see a way out* — and this second sense takes a gerund and is genuinely useful. *Short on* is the near-twin used with supplies and qualities: *short on patience*. ✗ *short in* is an error for both senses, though *short in stature* is a fixed phrase about height. The idioms *fall short of*, *stop short of*, and *nothing short of* all keep *of*.

Not having enough of something — or, in the second sense, anything other than.

1. We're *short of* volunteers for the Saturday shift.
2. He's *short on* patience and long on opinions.
3. *Short of* rebuilding the retaining wall, there's no permanent fix.
4. The count came up two *short of* what the manifest said.
5. The performance fell *short of* what the reviews had promised.

**See also:** [lacking in](#lacking-in), [free of](#free-of), [full of](#full-of), [absent from](#absent-from), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### sick of

**Pronunciation:** /sɪk ʌv/ &middot; *SIK uv* &middot; weak /sɪk əv/
**Русский:** которому надоело что-л. — разг., сыт по горло
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** informal to neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*sick of hearing about it*) — **never a to-infinitive** and never a clause.
**Pattern:** *be sick of* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *get sick of* [noun] &middot; *sick and tired of* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be sick with* [an illness] &middot; *be sick to death of* [noun]
**Variants:** *tired of* is the calmer twin; *fed up with* the equivalent with a different preposition; *done with* the flat American one.
**Contrast:** ***Sick of* is impatience; *sick with* is illness; *sick from* is the cause of illness.*** *Sick of the flu* would mean weary of it; *sick with the flu* means having it; *sick from the shot* means it made you ill. Keep them apart. Note also that *fed up* takes *with*, so ✗ *sick with waiting* and ✗ *fed up of waiting* are mirror errors. The fixed *sick and tired of* is a set phrase and cannot be rearranged.

Thoroughly tired of something and out of patience with it.

1. I'm *sick of* explaining the same policy every August.
2. She got *sick of* waiting and drove herself.
3. They're *sick and tired of* being told the parts are on order.
4. He was *sick with* something all week and blamed the flight.
5. Everyone is *sick of* the construction noise by now.

**See also:** [tired of](#tired-of), [fed up with](#fed-up-with), [bored with](#bored-with), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### similar to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsɪmələr tu/ &middot; *SIM-uh-lur too* &middot; weak /ˈsɪmələr tə/
**Русский:** похожий на, сходный с — но не тождественный
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*similar to working from home, but with worse coffee*) — **never a to-infinitive** and never a *that*-clause. A free relative works: *similar to what we saw last year*.
**Pattern:** [X] *is similar to* [Y] &middot; *broadly* / *strikingly similar to* [noun] &middot; *similar in* [respect] *to* [noun] &middot; *similarly* [adjective] &middot; *similarity to* / *between* — the noun takes both
**Variants:** *like* is the everyday equivalent and takes the same kind of complement; *comparable to* is the measured one; *akin to* the literary one; *close to* the loose one.
**Contrast:** ***Similar takes *to*, never *with* and never *as*.*** ✗ *similar with the original* and ✗ *similar as the original* are both errors, the second borrowed from *the same as*. Note the noun's double life: *similarity to* compares one thing to another, while *similarity between* treats them as a pair — *the similarity between the two accounts*. See [similar to](09-comparison-opposition.md#similar-to) for the word treated as a comparison preposition.

Resembling something without being identical to it.

1. The new layout is *similar to* the old one with two extra exits.
2. Her symptoms are *similar to* what the first patient described.
3. The two contracts are *similar in* structure *to* the one we signed in June.
4. The taste is *similar to* buckwheat, if buckwheat had been left in the sun.
5. Their approach is *similar to* ours and their timeline is not.

**See also:** [identical to](#identical-to), [different from](#different-from), [equal to](#equal-to), [related to](#related-to), [similar to](09-comparison-opposition.md#similar-to)

### skilled at

**Pronunciation:** /skɪld æt/ &middot; *SKILD at* &middot; weak /skɪld ət/
**Русский:** умелый в чём-л., владеющий навыком
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *at* (*skilled at defusing arguments*) and after *in* (*skilled in negotiation*) — **never a to-infinitive.** ✗ *skilled to negotiate* is an error.
**Pattern:** *be skilled at* [practice / verb-ing] &middot; *be skilled in* [field or discipline] &middot; *highly skilled at* [verb-ing] &middot; *skilled labor* (no preposition)
**Variants:** *proficient in*, *adept at*, *practiced at*, *accomplished in*, and *versed in* fill out the same range; *good at* is the plain everyday version.
**Contrast:** ***At* takes the practice, *in* takes the field.*** *Skilled at soldering* is what you do with your hands; *skilled in electrical engineering* is a body of knowledge. The distinction is soft — both are correct in most sentences — but *in* sounds credentialed and *at* sounds practical, which is why résumés prefer *in* and recommendations prefer *at*. ✗ *skilled on* and ✗ *skilled of* are errors. The parallel adjectives *expert*, *good*, *bad*, *hopeless*, and *terrible* all take *at* for practices.

Having developed ability in some practice or field.

1. She's *skilled at* getting two departments to agree on a date.
2. He's *skilled in* both civil and criminal procedure.
3. The crew is *skilled at* working around weather delays.
4. We need someone *skilled in* structural inspection, not just licensed.
5. He's *skilled at* the part of the job nobody writes down.

**See also:** [expert at](#expert-at), [good at](#good-at), [capable of](#capable-of), [successful in](#successful-in), [at](01-core-in-on-at.md#at)

### slow at

**Pronunciation:** /sloʊ æt/ &middot; *SLOH at* &middot; weak /sloʊ ət/
**Русский:** медлительный в чём-л. (о навыке); slow to — не спешащий сделать что-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *at* (*slow at reading music*) and after *in* (*slow in responding*, the formal frame). The adjective also takes a **bare to-infinitive** meaning "not quick to" (*slow to anger*, *slow to respond*), which is the commoner frame in writing. **Never a to-infinitive after *at*.**
**Pattern:** *be slow at* [skill / verb-ing] &middot; *be slow to* [verb] &middot; *be slow in* [verb-ing] &middot; *slow on the uptake* &middot; *slow* alone, of business: *sales are slow*
**Variants:** *not quick to* is the plain paraphrase of *slow to*; *behind on* covers the schedule sense.
**Contrast:** ***Slow at* names a skill you perform slowly; *slow to* names a reaction you are late in having.*** *Slow at arithmetic* is about ability; *slow to complain* is about temperament, and it is one of the small set of adjectives — with *quick to*, *ready to*, *eager to* — where an infinitive follows directly. *Slow in* + *-ing* is the formal alternative to *slow to*: *slow in acknowledging receipt*. ✗ *slow of* is an error except in the fossil *slow of speech*.

Taking longer than others do — at a task, or in reacting.

1. He's *slow at* mental arithmetic and unbeatable on paper.
2. She's *slow to* take offense, which is why people tell her things.
3. The office was *slow in* processing the refund.
4. I'm *slow at* anything involving a keyboard shortcut.
5. The county has been *slow to* release the inspection reports.

**See also:** [bad at](#bad-at), [poor at](#poor-at), [late for](#late-for), [good at](#good-at), [at](01-core-in-on-at.md#at)

### sorry for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsɑri fɔr/ &middot; *SAH-ree for* &middot; weak /ˈsɑri fər/
**Русский:** виноватый, сожалеющий о своём поступке; sorry for someone — жалеющий кого-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a **person** after *for* in the pity sense; a noun phrase or **gerund** after *for* and *about* in the apology sense (*sorry for keeping you waiting*) — **never a to-infinitive after a preposition.** The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (*sorry to bother you*) and a *that*-clause (*sorry that it came to this*).
**Pattern:** *be sorry for* [person] — pity &middot; *be sorry for* [verb-ing] — apology &middot; *be sorry about* [thing] &middot; *be sorry to* [verb] &middot; *be sorry* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *feel sorry for* [person] &middot; *sorry* alone, as an apology
**Variants:** *apologize for* + gerund is the formal verb frame; *regret* takes a direct object or a gerund with no preposition at all (*regret saying it*).
**Contrast:** **The three frames say three different things, and mixing them is a genuine social error.** ***Sorry for you*** is pity — often unwelcome. ***Sorry about your father*** is condolence. ***Sorry for interrupting*** is an apology for something you did. And ***sorry to hear that*** is a reaction to news. So *I'm sorry for you* and *I'm sorry about that* are not interchangeable, and the first can insult where the second consoles. ✗ *sorry of* is an error; *sorry for* + gerund and *sorry about* + gerund are both fine for apologies, with *for* slightly the more contrite.

Regretful about something you did, or sympathetic about something that happened.

1. I'm *sorry for* keeping you waiting; the earlier meeting ran long.
2. She was *sorry about* the whole misunderstanding.
3. Don't feel *sorry for* him; he chose every part of this.
4. I'm *sorry to* hear about the diagnosis.
5. He was *sorry that* he'd ever mentioned it.

**See also:** [ashamed of](#ashamed-of), [guilty of](#guilty-of), [sad about](#sad-about), [grateful to](#grateful-to), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### specific to

**Pronunciation:** /spɪˈsɪfɪk tu/ &middot; *spuh-SIF-ik too* &middot; weak /spəˈsɪfɪk tə/
**Русский:** характерный только для данного случая, специфичный для
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to technical; the modern workhorse for "belonging only to," common in medicine, software, and policy writing
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the group, place, context, or case — or a **gerund** where the context is an activity (*specific to running the night shift*). **Never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *specific to run the night shift*.
**Pattern:** *be specific to* [group / context] &middot; [noun] *specific to* [noun] &middot; *not specific to* [noun] — the common denial &middot; *site-specific*, *species-specific*, *context-specific* — the hyphenated compounds, where the complement moves in front &middot; *be specific about* [noun] — the unrelated sense, "give details"
**Variants:** *peculiar to* is the formal equivalent; *unique to* is the strongest; *particular to* is a close synonym; *restricted to* and *confined to* say the same thing about range.
**Contrast:** **Two different adjectives share this spelling.** *Specific to* + noun means "belonging only to": *a symptom specific to the disease*. *Specific about* + noun means "giving detail": *be specific about the dates*. The prepositions keep them apart and are not interchangeable — ✗ *be specific to the dates* asks for something else entirely. Against [peculiar to](#peculiar-to), which is formal and slightly literary, *specific to* is the neutral modern choice. In medicine the pair *sensitive* and *specific* are terms of art about tests, and there *specific* takes no complement at all.

Belonging to one context, group, or case and not to others.

1. The error is *specific to* the Windows build and doesn't appear on a Mac.
2. That rule is *specific to* districts with fewer than five hundred students.
3. The training is *specific to* the machines on this floor.
4. Symptoms like these are not *specific to* any one condition.
5. The permit conditions are *specific to* the parcel and don't travel.

**See also:** [peculiar to](#peculiar-to), [native to](#native-to), [endemic to](#endemic-to), [common to](#common-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### stuck with, stuck in, stuck on

**Pronunciation:** /stʌk wɪθ, stʌk ɪn, stʌk ɑn/ &middot; *STUCK with, STUCK in, STUCK on* &middot; weak /stʌk wəθ/, /stʌk ən/, /stʌk ən/ — *in* and *on* both reduce to /ən/, so *stuck in traffic* and *stuck on a problem* sound alike in the unstressed syllable and are told apart by the noun
**Русский:** застрявший — with с обузой, in в месте, on на задаче
**Type:** adjective (past participle) + preposition &middot; **Register:** informal to neutral; thoroughly American conversational English, and common in writing that wants a spoken texture
**Complement:** after *with*, a noun phrase naming the burden you cannot get rid of — and a **gerund** (*stuck with paying for it*). After *in*, a noun naming the place or condition you cannot get out of, or a **gerund** (*stuck in waiting for approval* is possible but marginal). After *on*, a noun naming the problem you cannot get past or the person you are infatuated with. **No to-infinitive after any of the three**: ✗ *stuck with to pay*. The bare frame *stuck* + *-ing* with no preposition (*stuck waiting an hour*) is also standard.
**Pattern:** *be stuck with* [bill / responsibility / verb-ing] &middot; *be stuck in* [traffic / a job / a rut] &middot; *be stuck on* [a problem / question / person] &middot; *get stuck* [verb-ing] — no preposition &middot; *be stuck at* [a number] — a fourth, quantitative frame
**Variants:** *saddled with* and *left holding the bag* replace *stuck with*; *bogged down in* replaces *stuck in*; *hung up on* is the near twin of *stuck on* in both its senses.
**Contrast:** **The three prepositions carve up the idea cleanly.** *Stuck with* is about a burden you did not want (*stuck with the bill*); *stuck in* is about a place or state you cannot leave (*stuck in traffic*, *stuck in a bad contract*); *stuck on* is about a point you cannot get past (*stuck on question four*) or, in the older slang, a person you cannot stop thinking about (*he's been stuck on her since high school*). Note that *stuck with* takes people too, and then it is rude: *stuck with my brother-in-law for the weekend*. ✗ *stuck to* means physically adhering (*gum stuck to the seat*) and is a different pattern altogether.

Unable to get free of something — a burden, a place, or a problem, depending on the preposition.

1. We got *stuck with* the catering bill when the sponsor pulled out.
2. She's been *stuck in* traffic on the bridge for forty minutes.
3. I'm *stuck on* the third question and the rest went fine.
4. He's *stuck in* a job he outgrew four years ago.
5. They were *stuck with* explaining the whole thing to the board twice.

**See also:** [fed up with](#fed-up-with), [tired of](#tired-of), [involved in](#involved-in), [encumbered by, encumbered with](#encumbered-by-encumbered-with), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### subject to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsʌbdʒɪkt tu/ &middot; *SUB-jikt too* &middot; weak /ˈsʌbdʒɪkt tə/ — the adjective stresses the first syllable, unlike the verb *subject* /səbˈdʒɛkt/
**Русский:** при условии чего-л.; подверженный чему-л.; подчинённый чему-л.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; the standard idiom of contracts, notices, and regulation
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** (*subject to being called back*) — **never a to-infinitive.** ✗ *subject to be reviewed* is the error; write *subject to review* or *subject to being reviewed*.
**Pattern:** *be subject to* [condition / authority / liability] &middot; [noun], *subject to* [condition] &middot; *subject to change without notice* &middot; *subject* [someone] *to* [something] — the **verb**, stressed on the second syllable
**Variants:** *contingent on* and *conditional on* cover the "depending on" sense and take *on*; *liable to* covers the "likely to suffer" sense; *governed by* covers the authority sense.
**Contrast:** ***Subject to* carries three related senses, all with the same preposition.*** Conditional: *the offer is subject to financing*. Liable to undergo: *prices subject to change*, *subject to delays*. Under the authority of: *subject to state law*. Distinguish the verb, which is spelled the same and stressed differently: *they subjected him to three hours of questions*. ✗ *subject of* is a different word entirely — the noun, meaning topic.

Conditional on something, liable to undergo it, or under its authority.

1. All prices are *subject to* change without notice.
2. The sale is *subject to* a satisfactory inspection.
3. Flights out of that airport are *subject to* fog delays all spring.
4. Contractors are *subject to* the same background checks as employees.
5. The award is *subject to* being reviewed after the first year.

**See also:** [dependent on](#dependent-on), [based on](#based-on), [responsible for](#responsible-for), [vulnerable to](#vulnerable-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### subordinate to

**Pronunciation:** /səˈbɔrdənɪt tu/ &middot; *suh-BOR-duh-nit too*; the verb is *suh-BOR-duh-nayt* &middot; weak /səˈbɔrdənət tə/
**Русский:** подчинённый чему-л., стоящий ниже по власти или значимости
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; standard in organizational writing, in finance (*subordinated debt*), and in grammar (*a subordinate clause*)
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming what ranks higher — a person, a body, an interest, a lien. A **gerund** is possible where the higher thing is an activity (*subordinate to keeping the lights on*), and a **to-infinitive is impossible**: ✗ *subordinate to keep the lights on*.
**Pattern:** *be subordinate to* [person / interest] &middot; *make* [something] *subordinate to* [noun] &middot; *subordinate* [a lien] *to* [another] — the verb, in finance &middot; *subordinated debt* / *a subordination agreement* — the terms of art
**Variants:** *secondary to* is the neutral equivalent; *inferior to* ranks quality rather than authority; *junior to* is the finance and military word for priority; *beneath* and *under* are the plain prepositions.
**Contrast:** Against [inferior to](#inferior-to), which judges quality (*an inferior copy*), *subordinate to* describes rank and authority and implies no judgment about worth. In American lending the term is precise: a subordinated lien is paid after the senior one, and a *subordination agreement* is what makes it so — which is why a second mortgage is *subordinate to* the first regardless of when it was recorded. Note that in grammar a subordinate clause is one that cannot stand alone, another use with no preposition. ✗ *subordinate of* is an error, though *a subordinate of the director* exists as a noun.

Ranking below something else in authority, priority, or importance.

1. The regional offices are *subordinate to* the state director.
2. The second lien is *subordinate to* the bank's mortgage.
3. Everything else in the plan is *subordinate to* keeping the plant open.
4. She refuses to be *subordinate to* a committee that never meets.
5. In this design, style is *subordinate to* legibility.

**See also:** [inferior to](#inferior-to), [ancillary to](#ancillary-to), [incidental to](#incidental-to), [superior to](#superior-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### successful in

**Pronunciation:** /səkˈsɛsfəl ɪn/ &middot; *suk-SESS-full in* &middot; weak /səkˈsɛsfəl ən/
**Русский:** добившийся успеха в чём-л., достигший задуманного
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *in* (*successful in raising the money*) and after *at* (*successful at getting people to show up*) — **never a to-infinitive.** ✗ *successful to raise the money* is an error; use *succeeded in raising* or *managed to raise*.
**Pattern:** *be successful in* [field / verb-ing] &middot; *be successful at* [practice / verb-ing] &middot; *be successful with* [a particular case or group] &middot; *succeed in* [verb-ing] — the verb keeps *in* &middot; *success in* / *at* — the noun follows suit
**Variants:** *effective in* / *at*, *productive in*, and *does well at* cover the same ground; *manage to* + verb is the everyday paraphrase with an infinitive.
**Contrast:** ***In* leans toward the field or the endeavor; *at* leans toward the repeated practice.*** *Successful in business* names a domain; *successful at raising money* names something done more than once. Both are correct with a gerund. The verb takes only *in*: ✓ *succeeded in convincing them*, ✗ *succeeded to convince them* — a very common error, since *manage* and *try* both take infinitives. ✗ *successful of* is an error.

Achieving what was intended.

1. She was *successful in* getting the deadline moved twice.
2. The clinic has been *successful at* reaching families the old program missed.
3. He's been *successful in* every business he's touched except restaurants.
4. The campaign was *successful in* raising the money and useless at spending it.
5. They were *successful with* the first group and are repeating it in the fall.

**See also:** [skilled at](#skilled-at), [good at](#good-at), [expert at](#expert-at), [in](01-core-in-on-at.md#in)

### suggestive of

**Pronunciation:** /səˈdʒɛstɪv ʌv/ &middot; *sug-JESS-tiv uv* &middot; weak /səˈdʒɛstəv əv/
**Русский:** наводящий на мысль о — намекающий, но не доказывающий
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; the natural home is a radiology report or a clinical note (*findings suggestive of pneumonia*), and by extension any careful analytical writing
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming what is hinted at, or a **gerund** (*suggestive of having been repaired before*) — **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *suggestive of to be repaired*.
**Pattern:** [finding] *be suggestive of* [condition] &middot; *strongly* / *only mildly suggestive of* &middot; *suggestive* — used alone, where the modern meaning is risqué &middot; *suggest* [conclusion] — the verb, direct object, no preposition
**Variants:** *indicative of* is the stronger word; *consistent with* is the radiologist's hedge that claims even less; *symptomatic of* names the condition outright; *reminiscent of* is the aesthetic cousin (*a style reminiscent of the thirties*).
**Contrast:** **A ladder of confidence runs *consistent with* → *suggestive of* → *indicative of* → *diagnostic of***, and American clinicians choose among them deliberately: findings *consistent with* pneumonia would fit it, findings *suggestive of* pneumonia point toward it, findings *diagnostic of* it settle the matter. Watch the bare adjective: *suggestive* with no complement now means sexually suggestive, so *the photograph was suggestive* and *the photograph was suggestive of an earlier repair* are two different sentences and only the second is this pattern. ✗ *suggestive to* and ✗ *suggestive for* are errors.

Pointing toward a conclusion without establishing it.

1. The films show changes *suggestive of* an old fracture.
2. The pattern of withdrawals is *suggestive of* something other than payroll.
3. Her account is *suggestive of* a much longer delay than the log shows.
4. Wear on the threshold is *suggestive of* daily use, not occasional.
5. The findings are *suggestive of*, though not diagnostic of, the disorder.

**See also:** [indicative of](#indicative-of), [symptomatic of](#symptomatic-of), [illustrative of](#illustrative-of), [predictive of](#predictive-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### suitable for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsutəbəl fɔr/ &middot; *SOO-tuh-bull for* &middot; weak /ˈsutəbəl fər/
**Русский:** подходящий для — для цели, применения или аудитории
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** a noun naming a **purpose, use, or group** after *for*; a **gerund** is possible (*suitable for freezing*) — **never a to-infinitive after *for***. The adjective also takes a bare to-infinitive in the passive-ish frame *suitable to serve*, which is rarer.
**Pattern:** *be suitable for* [purpose / group] &middot; *be suitable to* [a standard or occasion] &middot; *suitable for framing* &middot; *not suitable for* [group]
**Variants:** *appropriate for* and *right for* are the everyday equivalents; *fit for* is the blunter one; *suited to* is the close twin and takes *to*.
**Contrast:** ***Suitable for* a purpose; *suited to* a role or a person.*** *A car suitable for city driving* and *a candidate well suited to the job* — the pair differs by preposition and by what fills the slot, and ✗ *suitable to city driving* and ✗ *suited for the job* both drift. Note the fixed phrases: *suitable for all ages*, *not suitable for children*, *suitable for framing*. ✗ *suitable of* is an error.

Right for a particular purpose, use, or audience.

1. The lot isn't *suitable for* anything heavier than a shed.
2. The material is *suitable for* outdoor use in freezing weather.
3. The film is not *suitable for* younger viewers.
4. That soil is *suitable for* almost nothing but blueberries.
5. She's better *suited to* the research side than the client side.

**See also:** [available to](#available-to), [useful for](#useful-for), [essential to](#essential-to), [ready for](#ready-for), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### superior to

**Pronunciation:** /səˈpɪriər tu/ &middot; *suh-PEER-ee-ur too* &middot; weak /səˈpɪriər tə/
**Русский:** превосходящий что-л. — выше по качеству или рангу
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or pronoun — **never a gerund**, **never a to-infinitive**, never a clause.
**Pattern:** [X] *is superior to* [Y] &middot; *superior in* [respect] *to* [noun] &middot; *vastly* / *demonstrably superior to* [noun] &middot; *a superior* [noun] — attributive, no preposition &middot; *feel superior to* [person]
**Variants:** *better than* is the everyday equivalent and takes *than*, which is exactly the source of the error this entry warns about.
**Contrast:** ***Superior takes *to*, never *than*.*** ✗ *superior than the original* is a persistent error, and it happens because the word means "better" while behaving like a Latin comparative. The same rule covers *inferior to*, *senior to*, *junior to*, *prior to*, *preferable to*, *anterior to*, and *posterior to* — a family worth learning together. Note also that *superior* takes no *more*: ✗ *more superior to* is redundant, though it is common in speech.

Higher in quality or rank than something else.

1. The older tooling is *superior to* anything available now at that price.
2. She was *superior to* every other applicant on paper and in person.
3. The second route is *superior in* every respect *to* the one we took.
4. He has a way of sounding *superior to* the people he's asking for help.
5. Nothing about the newer model is *superior to* the one it replaced.

**See also:** [inferior to](#inferior-to), [equal to](#equal-to), [similar to](#similar-to), [than](09-comparison-opposition.md#than), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### sure of

**Pronunciation:** /ʃʊr ʌv/ &middot; *SHOOR uv* &middot; weak /ʃʊr əv/, and in fast speech /ˈʃʊrə/
**Русский:** уверенный в чём-л.; в инфинитивных оборотах — непременно случится или ожидается
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral; the everyday twin of *certain*
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, **gerund** (*sure of getting the spot*), or a *wh*-clause after *about* (*sure about what she wants*) — **never a to-infinitive after the preposition.** A *that*-clause attaches to the bare adjective (*sure that he'd locked it*), and a bare to-infinitive attaches directly with a **different meaning**: *sure to happen* means bound to happen.
**Pattern:** *be sure of* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be sure about* [matter] &middot; *be sure* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *be sure to* [verb] — an instruction or a prediction &middot; *make sure of* [noun] &middot; *for sure*
**Variants:** *certain of* / *about* is the more formal twin and covers every frame; *positive about* is the emphatic conversational one.
**Contrast:** ***Sure of* is confidence; *sure to* is inevitability or instruction.*** *He's sure of a spot* means he feels confident; *He's sure to get a spot* means it will happen; *Be sure to lock up* is an order. Three different things from one adjective. *Of* takes a fact or an outcome, *about* takes a matter under consideration, and *about* is the one that takes *wh*-clauses. ✗ *sure for* is an error.

Free of doubt — or, in the infinitive frames, bound to happen or expected to be done.

1. I'm *sure of* the date; the receipt is right here.
2. She wasn't *sure about* moving the whole family in January.
3. Make *sure of* the measurement before you cut.
4. Be *sure to* turn off the water at the street.
5. That corner is *sure to* flood again in the spring.

**See also:** [certain of](#certain-of), [confident of](#confident-of), [aware of](#aware-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### surprised at

**Pronunciation:** /sərˈpraɪzd æt/ &middot; *sur-PRYZD at* &middot; weak /sərˈpraɪzd ət/
**Русский:** удивлённый чем-л. неожиданным
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *at* and *by* (*surprised at finding it unlocked*) — **never a to-infinitive after a preposition.** The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (*surprised to learn*) and a *that*-clause (*surprised that anyone remembered*).
**Pattern:** *be surprised at* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be surprised by* [the cause] &middot; *be surprised to* [verb] &middot; *be surprised* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *surprise* [someone] (no preposition on the object) &middot; *take* [someone] *by surprise*
**Variants:** *surprised at* and *surprised by* are both standard American English; *by* is the commoner of the two in writing and points harder at the cause.
**Contrast:** ***At* frames the reaction; *by* frames the cause.*** *Surprised at how little it cost* is your reaction to a figure; *surprised by the phone call* is the call acting on you — which is why an event that arrives takes *by* and a fact you consider takes *at*. The infinitive frame is different again: *surprised to see her there* reports the moment. Note the reproachful idiom *I'm surprised at you*, which is a mild rebuke and cannot be rebuilt with *by*. ✗ *surprised of* and ✗ *surprised from* are errors.

Reacting to something you did not expect.

1. I was *surprised at* how empty the place was on a Friday.
2. She was *surprised by* the second call, which came at midnight.
3. He was *surprised to* find the office already unlocked.
4. Nobody was *surprised that* the vote was postponed again.
5. I'm *surprised at* you — you know better than that.

**See also:** [astonished at](#astonished-at), [shocked at](#shocked-at), [impressed with](#impressed-with), [at](01-core-in-on-at.md#at)

### susceptible to, susceptible of

**Pronunciation:** /səˈsɛptəbəl tu, səˈsɛptəbəl ʌv/ &middot; *suh-SEP-tuh-bul too, suh-SEP-tuh-bul uv* &middot; weak /səˈsɛptəbəl tə/ and /səˈsɛptəbəl əv/
**Русский:** подверженный чему-л. (to); допускающий что-л., поддающийся (of); книжн.
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** *susceptible to* is neutral and everyday in medicine and engineering; *susceptible of* is formal and now largely legal and literary
**Complement:** after *to*, a noun phrase naming the harm or influence you are open to, or a **gerund** (*susceptible to being talked into things*). After *of*, a noun naming what the thing admits — *susceptible of proof*, *susceptible of two readings* — and a gerund is not used there. **Never a to-infinitive after either**: ✗ *susceptible to be persuaded*.
**Pattern:** *be susceptible to* [disease / influence / damage] &middot; *be susceptible of* [proof / interpretation / improvement] &middot; *particularly* / *especially susceptible to* &middot; *susceptibility to* [noun] — the noun frame keeps *to*
**Variants:** *vulnerable to* and *prone to* replace the *to* frame; *open to* and *capable of* replace the *of* frame; *liable to* covers both loosely; *at risk of* is the public-health phrase.
**Contrast:** ***To* for what can happen **to** it; *of* for what it will **admit**.*** Old pipe is susceptible to corrosion (it can be attacked); a clause is susceptible of two readings (it will bear them). The *of* frame is the older and rarer one, and it is nearly always followed by an abstract noun like *proof*, *interpretation*, *division*, or *improvement*. Against [vulnerable to](#vulnerable-to), which stresses harm and defenselessness, *susceptible to* is neutral enough for engineering and for personality (*susceptible to flattery*). ✗ *susceptible for* is an error.

Open to being affected by something, in the *to* frame; capable of admitting something, in the *of* frame.

1. Older patients are more *susceptible to* the complication than the trial suggested.
2. Galvanized fittings are *susceptible to* corrosion where they meet copper.
3. He's *susceptible to* flattery from anyone who mentions the boat.
4. The sentence is *susceptible of* at least two readings.
5. The claim is not *susceptible of* proof one way or the other.

**See also:** [vulnerable to](#vulnerable-to), [immune from, immune to](#immune-from-immune-to), [amenable to](#amenable-to), [sensitive to](#sensitive-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### suspicious of

**Pronunciation:** /səˈspɪʃəs ʌv/ &middot; *suh-SPISH-uss uv* &middot; weak /səˈspɪʃəs əv/
**Русский:** относящийся с подозрением к, не доверяющий
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*suspicious of being followed*) — **never a to-infinitive.** A *that*-clause attaches to the bare adjective only in the sense "suspecting": *suspicious that something had been moved*.
**Pattern:** *be suspicious of* [person / thing] &middot; *be suspicious about* [a matter] &middot; *grow* / *become suspicious of* [noun] &middot; *look suspicious* (no complement) &middot; *suspicion of* [noun] — the noun keeps *of*
**Variants:** *wary of* and *leery of* are the cautious versions; *skeptical of* / *about* is the intellectual one; *distrustful of* is the formal one.
**Contrast:** ***Suspicious of* is what you feel about someone; *suspicious about* is what you feel about a matter; and *suspicious* alone means looking guilty.*** The last is the ambiguity worth naming: *He was suspicious* can mean either that he suspected something or that he seemed suspect, and only context decides — the second sense usually appears as *He looked suspicious*. ✗ *suspicious to* and ✗ *suspicious from* are errors.

Not trusting someone or something, and having reasons.

1. The bank was *suspicious of* three transfers in one afternoon.
2. She's *suspicious of* anyone who volunteers to take the minutes.
3. Investigators grew *suspicious about* the timing of the sale.
4. He was *suspicious of* being sent on an errand at that hour.
5. Everyone was *suspicious of* the offer and nobody said so.

**See also:** [critical of](#critical-of), [jealous of](#jealous-of), [confident of](#confident-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### sympathetic to

**Pronunciation:** /ˌsɪmpəˈθɛtɪk tu/ &middot; *sim-puh-THET-ik too* &middot; weak /ˌsɪmpəˈθɛtɪk tə/
**Русский:** сочувствующий кому-л.; благосклонный к делу, идее
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** a noun naming a **person, group, or cause** after *to* and *toward* — **never a gerund** and **never a to-infinitive.**
**Pattern:** *be sympathetic to* [cause / person] &middot; *be sympathetic toward* [person] &middot; *be sympathetic with* [a position] — less common &middot; *sympathize with* [person] — the **verb takes *with***
**Variants:** ***toward*** is the American spelling and ***towards*** the British; both are read as ordinary in the United States, with *toward* the house style of most American publishers. *Sympathetic to* is the commoner of the two in American writing.
**Contrast:** ***The adjective takes *to* or *toward*; the verb takes *with*.*** ✓ *sympathetic to the strikers*, ✓ *sympathize with the strikers*, ✗ *sympathize to the strikers*. That split is the whole difficulty. A second sense of the adjective means favorably disposed rather than compassionate — *a judge sympathetic to the defense* — and it is the one used in political writing. The unrelated technical sense (*the sympathetic nervous system*) takes no preposition.

Feeling or showing understanding for someone, or favorably disposed toward a cause.

1. The committee was *sympathetic to* the tenants but bound by the ordinance.
2. She's *sympathetic toward* anyone trying to get a permit out of that office.
3. The paper has always been *sympathetic to* the union's position.
4. He was *sympathetic to* the argument without agreeing with the conclusion.
5. I *sympathize with* the family — the verb, with *with*.

**See also:** [sensitive to](#sensitive-to), [kind to](#kind-to), [sorry for](#sorry-for), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### symptomatic of

**Pronunciation:** /ˌsɪmptəˈmætɪk ʌv/ &middot; *simp-tuh-MAT-ik uv* &middot; weak /ˌsɪmptəˈmædək əv/, with the *t* flapped: "simp-tuh-MAD-ik-uv"
**Русский:** симптоматичный для — служащий признаком более глубокой причины
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal; medical in origin and now at least as common in commentary about institutions (*symptomatic of a deeper problem*)
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the underlying condition or problem, or a **gerund** (*symptomatic of having grown too fast*) — **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *symptomatic of to grow too fast*.
**Pattern:** [sign] *be symptomatic of* [condition] &middot; *symptomatic of a larger* / *deeper* / *wider* [noun] — the commentary cliché &middot; *symptomatic* — the clinical bare use, meaning showing symptoms (*symptomatic patients*) &middot; *asymptomatic* — the negative, a word every American learned in 2020
**Variants:** *indicative of* is the neutral evidential word; *a symptom of* is the noun phrase and takes *of* too; *emblematic of* is the loaded version; *a sign of* is the plain one.
**Contrast:** ***Symptomatic of* names the sign of a condition that lies underneath***, which is why the figurative use is so common and so lazy: *symptomatic of a deeper problem* is a way of not naming the problem. Against [indicative of](#indicative-of), which points at any conclusion, *symptomatic of* implies a whole disease behind the sign. Note the clinical bare use, where *symptomatic* means "having symptoms" and takes no complement at all — *symptomatic patients were tested first* — which is a different construction from this one. ✗ *symptomatic to* and ✗ *symptomatic for* are errors.

Serving as a sign of a larger condition lying underneath.

1. The rash is *symptomatic of* the reaction, not of the infection itself.
2. Three resignations in a month are *symptomatic of* something nobody wants to name.
3. The backlog is *symptomatic of* a system built for half the volume.
4. His answer was *symptomatic of* how the department handles complaints.
5. Late shipments were *symptomatic of* a supplier who had already given up.

**See also:** [indicative of](#indicative-of), [suggestive of](#suggestive-of), [illustrative of](#illustrative-of), [characteristic of](#characteristic-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### synonymous with

**Pronunciation:** /sɪˈnɑnəməs wɪθ/ &middot; *sih-NON-uh-mus with* &middot; weak /səˈnɑnəməs wəθ/ — note that the adjective stresses the second syllable while the noun *synonym* stresses the first
**Русский:** синонимичный чему-л.; об имени — ставший символом какого-л. качества
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; a linguistic term and, far more often, a journalistic idiom about reputations
**Complement:** a noun phrase, or a **gerund** (*synonymous with cutting corners*) — **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *synonymous with cut corners*.
**Pattern:** *be synonymous with* [word / thing] &middot; *practically* / *virtually* / *now synonymous with* &middot; *a synonym for* [word] — the **noun**, which switches to *for*
**Variants:** *interchangeable with* is the strict linguistic claim; *equivalent to* claims equal value; *identified with* is the reputational sense stated plainly; *a byword for* is the idiom for the journalistic use.
**Contrast:** **Adjective *with*, noun *for*.** ✓ *synonymous with*, ✓ *a synonym for*, ✗ *a synonym with*, ✗ *synonymous for*. In strict use the word claims two expressions mean the same thing; in the commoner journalistic use it claims that a name has come to stand for a quality — *a town synonymous with steel*. Against [equivalent to](#equivalent-to), which is quantitative, *synonymous with* is about meaning and reputation. ✗ *synonymous to* is an error and a frequent one.

Meaning the same thing as something else — or, of a name, so associated with a quality that it stands for it.

1. In everyday speech, *begin* is *synonymous with* *start*, though the registers differ.
2. The family name became *synonymous with* the mill and closed with it.
3. That neighborhood was for decades *synonymous with* cheap rent.
4. Speed is not *synonymous with* haste, whatever the schedule says.
5. His byline is *synonymous with* stories nobody else would take.

**See also:** [equivalent to](#equivalent-to), [identical to](#identical-to), [tantamount to](#tantamount-to), [similar to](#similar-to), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### tangent to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtændʒənt tu/ &middot; *TAN-junt too* &middot; weak /ˈtændʒənt tə/
**Русский:** касательный к — мат., касающийся кривой в одной точке
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** technical — geometry and calculus, where a line tangent to a curve touches it at exactly one point; the figurative work is done by the noun *tangent* and by *tangential to*
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the curve or surface touched — **never a gerund** and never a clause.
**Pattern:** [line] *be tangent to* [curve] &middot; *a plane tangent to* [surface] &middot; *the tangent line at* [point] &middot; *go off on a tangent* — the idiom, with no complement &middot; *tangential to* [noun] — the figurative adjective
**Variants:** *tangential to* is the form for the figurative sense, meaning only slightly connected (*a question tangential to the motion*); *touching* is the literal paraphrase; *osculating* is the specialist's word for a closer contact in differential geometry.
**Contrast:** ***Tangent to* stays mathematical; *tangential to* carries the figurative sense.*** A line is tangent to a circle; a remark is tangential to the discussion, and using *tangent to* for the second reads as an error to anyone who took calculus. Against [perpendicular to](#perpendicular-to) and [parallel to](#parallel-to), which describe fixed relations between straight lines, *tangent to* describes contact at a single point on a curve. The related American idiom *go off on a tangent* keeps the image: you leave the curve along the line that just touched it.

Touching a curve or surface at exactly one point without crossing it.

1. Draw the line *tangent to* the circle at the point where they meet.
2. The derivative gives the slope of the line *tangent to* the curve.
3. A plane *tangent to* the sphere touches it at a single point.
4. The road runs *tangent to* the reservoir for a quarter mile.
5. His question was *tangential to* the agenda, which is the figurative form.

**See also:** [perpendicular to](#perpendicular-to), [parallel to](#parallel-to), [orthogonal to](#orthogonal-to), [adjacent to](#adjacent-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### tantamount to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtæntəˌmaʊnt tu/ &middot; *TAN-tuh-mownt too* &middot; weak /ˈtænəˌmaʊnt tə/ — the first *t* after *n* often drops in speech: "TAN-uh-mownt"
**Русский:** равносильный чему-л. — книжн., по сути то же, но названное мягче
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal to journalistic; almost always used to characterize an act as something worse than it was called
**Complement:** a noun phrase or a **gerund** — *tantamount to admitting the whole thing* ✓ — and **never a to-infinitive**: ✗ *tantamount to admit the whole thing*. The gerund is the commoner complement, which makes this one of the highest-value entries in the file for the *-ing* rule.
**Pattern:** *be tantamount to* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *amount to* [noun / verb-ing] — the near-identical verb &middot; *tantamount to a* [confession / declaration / admission] — the standard collocations
**Variants:** *amounts to* is the plain verb and does the same work in ordinary prose; *equivalent to* is neutral; *in effect* is the adverbial paraphrase (*in effect a confession*); *no better than* is the blunt version.
**Contrast:** ***Tantamount to* is a judgment, not a measurement.*** Where [equivalent to](#equivalent-to) says two things are equal in value, *tantamount to* says an act has the force of something graver — which is why the complement is nearly always negative: *tantamount to fraud*, *tantamount to a threat*, *tantamount to giving up*. The word has no attributive use: ✗ *a tantamount admission* is not English. And the *to* is a preposition every time, so the *-ing* form is obligatory: ✗ *tantamount to say nothing*, ✓ *tantamount to saying nothing*.

Equal in effect to something graver than what it was called.

1. Saying nothing at that point was *tantamount to* agreeing.
2. Refusing the inspection is *tantamount to* an admission.
3. The transfer was *tantamount to* a gift, whatever the paperwork called it.
4. Cutting the route is *tantamount to* stranding half the riders.
5. Her memo was *tantamount to* accusing the vendor of padding the invoices.

**See also:** [equivalent to](#equivalent-to), [synonymous with](#synonymous-with), [equal to](#equal-to), [amenable to](#amenable-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### terrible at

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtɛrəbəl æt/ &middot; *TAIR-uh-bull at* &middot; weak /ˈtɛrəbəl ət/
**Русский:** ужасно плохой в чём-л. (at); terrible for вредный, terrible with не умеющий обращаться с
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** informal to neutral; emphatic
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *at* (*terrible at keeping secrets*) — **never a to-infinitive.** After *for*, a person or thing harmed (*terrible for morale*). After *with*, a category handled badly.
**Pattern:** *be terrible at* [skill / verb-ing] &middot; *be terrible for* [person / thing] &middot; *be terrible with* [category] &middot; *be terrible about* [verb-ing] — chronically negligent &middot; *terrible* alone: *a terrible mistake*
**Variants:** *awful at*, *horrible at*, *bad at*, and *hopeless at* are the same word at different volumes; *no good at* is the flat one.
**Contrast:** ***Terrible at* / *for* / *with* splits exactly as *good at* / *for* / *with* and *bad at* / *for* / *with* do*** — skill, harm, and category respectively — so learning one of the three learns all three. ✗ *terrible in math* is an error in American English. Note the *about* frame, which reports a chronic failure to do something rather than a lack of skill: *terrible about returning calls* means you can, but don't.

Very bad at something, or very bad for something.

1. I'm *terrible at* estimating how much paint a room takes.
2. That schedule is *terrible for* anyone with a second job.
3. He's *terrible with* names and excellent with faces.
4. She's *terrible about* checking the shared calendar.
5. We're both *terrible at* saying no to a weekend project.

**See also:** [bad at](#bad-at), [hopeless at](#hopeless-at), [poor at](#poor-at), [good at](#good-at), [at](01-core-in-on-at.md#at)

### thankful for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈθæŋkfəl fɔr/ &middot; *THANK-full for* &middot; weak /ˈθæŋkfəl fər/
**Русский:** благодарный за, с облегчением радующийся тому, что
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to warm
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *for* (*thankful for having somewhere to go*) — **never a to-infinitive after *for***. The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive directly (*thankful to be home*) and a *that*-clause (*thankful that nobody was hurt*).
**Pattern:** *be thankful for* [thing] &middot; *be thankful to* [verb] &middot; *be thankful* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *be thankful to* [a benefactor] — restricted &middot; *thankfully* as a sentence adverb
**Variants:** *grateful for* is the close twin and additionally takes a person with *to*; *appreciative of* is the formal one.
**Contrast:** ***Thankful* takes *for*, and, unlike *grateful*, it does not comfortably take a person.*** ✓ *grateful to the neighbors*, and ✗ *thankful to the neighbors* is odd — the exception is *thankful to God*, a fixed religious phrase. That is the whole difference between the two words grammatically; in tone, *thankful* is warmer and more personal, *grateful* more likely to acknowledge a specific debt. ✗ *thankful of* and ✗ *thankful about* are errors.

Feeling relief and gratitude that something is the case.

1. We're *thankful for* every dry day this month.
2. She's *thankful for* having had those last two summers with him.
3. I'm *thankful to* be back in a house with working heat.
4. They were *thankful that* the storm turned before the harbor.
5. Be *thankful for* small mercies and a short agenda.

**See also:** [grateful to](#grateful-to), [glad about](#glad-about), [happy about](#happy-about), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### tired of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtaɪərd ʌv/ &middot; *TY-urd uv* &middot; weak /ˈtaɪərd əv/
**Русский:** которому надоело что-л.; tired from — физически утомлённый чем-л.
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** after *of* (*tired of waiting*) and after *from* (*tired from standing all day*) — **never a to-infinitive after either.** ✗ *tired to wait* is an error; *too tired to wait* is a different, correct construction.
**Pattern:** *be tired of* [noun / verb-ing] — fed up &middot; *be tired from* [noun / verb-ing] — physically worn out &middot; *get tired of* [noun] &middot; *sick and tired of* [noun] &middot; *too tired to* [verb]
**Variants:** *sick of* and *fed up with* are the impatience versions; *worn out from* and *exhausted from* are the physical ones.
**Contrast:** ***Tired of* is impatience; *tired from* is exhaustion.*** *Tired of the drive* means you'd rather not do it again; *tired from the drive* means you need to sit down. The two are constantly confused and the difference is total. A third frame, *too tired to* + verb, uses an infinitive and belongs to *too … to*, not to this adjective. ✗ *tired with* is an error in American English, though it appears in older British prose.

Out of patience with something, or physically worn out by it.

1. She's *tired of* explaining why the office closes at four.
2. He was *tired from* three days of moving furniture.
3. I'm *tired of* being the only one who reads the agenda.
4. They were *tired from* the flight and useless until noon.
5. Voters are *tired of* hearing about a bridge that never gets built.

**See also:** [sick of](#sick-of), [fed up with](#fed-up-with), [bored with](#bored-with), [of](02-core-relational.md#of), [from](02-core-relational.md#from)

### typical of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtɪpɪkəl ʌv/ &middot; *TIP-ih-kull uv* &middot; weak /ˈtɪpɪkəl əv/
**Русский:** типичный для, характерный для — часто со вздохом: «как всегда»
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or pronoun; a **gerund** where a behavior is meant (*typical of showing up unannounced* is possible but heavy) — **never a to-infinitive** and never a clause. The *it*-frame takes an infinitive: *It's typical of him to volunteer.*
**Pattern:** *be typical of* [person / group / period] &middot; [noun] *typical of* [noun] &middot; *it is typical of* [someone] *to* [verb] &middot; *typically* [adjective / verb] &middot; *That's typical.*
**Variants:** *characteristic of* is the neutral formal twin; *representative of* is the statistical one; *par for the course* is the idiomatic complaint.
**Contrast:** ***Typical of* is very often a complaint; *characteristic of* almost never is.*** *That's typical of them* usually means they have let you down again, and the bare *That's typical* is a pure grumble. Note also the difference from *representative of*: a *typical* case is an ordinary one, while a *representative* sample is one chosen to stand for the whole. ✗ *typical for* is an error in this sense, though *typical for this time of year* is correct and means normal for that period — a genuinely different frame worth knowing.

Showing the usual qualities of a person, group, or kind — often said with a sigh.

1. The long lead-in is *typical of* his later work.
2. Rain like this is *typical of* early spring here.
3. It's *typical of* her *to* have read the whole file before the meeting.
4. That kind of answer is *typical of* the whole department.
5. Snow in October is *typical for* this elevation, if not for this state.

**See also:** [characteristic of](#characteristic-of), [similar to](#similar-to), [known for](#known-for), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### upset about

**Pronunciation:** /ʌpˈsɛt əˈbaʊt/ &middot; *up-SET uh-BOWT* &middot; weak /ʌpˈsɛt əˈbaʊt/ — the adjective stresses the second syllable, unlike the noun *upset* /ˈʌpsɛt/
**Русский:** расстроенный, огорчённый чем-л. — от лёгкой досады до настоящего горя
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *about*, *over*, and *by* (*upset about being left out*) — **never a to-infinitive after a preposition.** A person follows *with*. A *that*-clause attaches to the bare adjective: *upset that nobody called*.
**Pattern:** *be upset about* [situation / verb-ing] &middot; *be upset with* [person] &middot; *be upset by* [the cause] &middot; *be upset over* [situation] &middot; *be upset* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *get upset about* [noun]
**Variants:** *bothered by*, *rattled by*, and *thrown by* are the milder ones; *distraught over* is the intensified one and prefers *over*.
**Contrast:** **The familiar split, with one extra option.** ***About*** and ***over*** take the situation, ***with*** takes the person, ***by*** takes the cause acting on you. *Upset with her sister about the will* fills two slots at once. *Over* is slightly more emotional than *about* and is the newspaper's choice for grief and grievance. ✗ *upset of* is an error, and the medical *an upset stomach* is a different use with no preposition at all.

Distressed or annoyed — the general-purpose word covering everything from mild irritation to real grief.

1. Parents are *upset about* how late the schedule was announced.
2. She's *upset with* her brother and won't say why.
3. He was visibly *upset by* the question.
4. The whole neighborhood is *upset over* the tree removal.
5. Don't get *upset about* something you can't change before Friday.

**See also:** [angry with](#angry-with), [annoyed with](#annoyed-with), [sad about](#sad-about), [worried about](#worried-about), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about)

### used to

**Pronunciation:** /just tu/ &middot; *YOOST too* &middot; weak /just tə/, in ordinary speech /ˈjustə/ — note the adjective is /just/ with the *s* of *loose*, not /juzd/ with the *z* of *used a pencil*
**Русский:** привыкший к (прилагательное, дальше герундий); либо «раньше, бывало» — о прошлой привычке
**Type:** adjective + preposition — and, spelled identically, a semi-modal of the habitual past &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase, pronoun, or **gerund** (*used to working nights*) — **never a to-infinitive after the adjective.** ✗ *I'm used to work nights* is the error. The **semi-modal** *used to*, by contrast, takes a **bare infinitive** and no *be*: *I used to work nights*, meaning I no longer do.
**Pattern:** *be* / *get* / *become used to* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *used to* [bare verb] — habitual past &middot; *didn't use to* [verb] &middot; *never used to* [verb] &middot; *not used to* [noun / verb-ing]
**Variants:** *accustomed to* is the formal equivalent of the adjective and behaves identically. *Would* covers part of the habitual-past sense, but only for repeated actions, not for states: ✓ *We used to live there*, ✗ *We would live there*.
**Contrast:** **This is the most consequential ambiguity in the file.** Look at what precedes and what follows. ***Preceded by *be*, *get*, or *become*, and followed by a noun or *-ing*:*** the adjective, meaning accustomed — *I'm used to the noise*, *She got used to driving in it*. ***Preceded by a bare subject and followed by a bare verb:*** the semi-modal, meaning a discontinued habit — *I used to smoke*. The negative and interrogative of the semi-modal drop the *d* in American writing: *didn't use to*, *did he use to* — though *never used to* is commoner in speech. And there is no present tense of the semi-modal: ✗ *I use to smoke* for a current habit is an error; say *I usually smoke*.

Accustomed to something — or, in the identical-looking semi-modal, in the habit of doing something in the past.

1. She's *used to* the noise from the interstate by now.
2. I'm not *used to* getting up before the alarm.
3. He got *used to* driving on the left in about a week.
4. We *used to* eat there every Friday before it closed.
5. There *didn't use to* be a light at that intersection.

**See also:** [accustomed to](#accustomed-to), [familiar with](#familiar-with), [comfortable with](#comfortable-with), [look forward to](#look-forward-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### useful for

**Pronunciation:** /ˈjusfəl fɔr/ &middot; *YOOS-full for* &middot; weak /ˈjusfəl fər/
**Русский:** полезный для (цели); useful to — полезный кому-л., useful in — в какой-л. обстановке
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a noun naming a **purpose**, or a **gerund** (*useful for getting into tight corners*) after *for*; a **person** after *to* — **never a to-infinitive after a preposition.** The adjective takes a bare to-infinitive in the frame *useful to know*, which describes what may be done, not who benefits.
**Pattern:** *be useful for* [purpose / verb-ing] &middot; *be useful to* [person] &middot; *be useful in* [a situation or process] &middot; *it is useful to* [verb] &middot; *make oneself useful*
**Variants:** *helpful for* / *to* splits the same way; *handy for* is the informal one; *of use to* is the formal noun frame.
**Contrast:** ***For* names the purpose; *to* names the person who benefits; *in* names the setting.*** *Useful for removing rust*, *useful to anyone with an old car*, *useful in a pinch*. The infinitive frame is a fourth thing: *It's useful to know the code* means knowing it helps, and its subject is the impersonal *it*, not a person. ✗ *useful of* is an error.

Serving a purpose, or being of help to someone.

1. That attachment is *useful for* getting into corners.
2. Her notes were *useful to* everyone who came after her.
3. The old map turned out to be *useful in* ways nobody expected.
4. It's *useful to* know which door they actually unlock at seven.
5. A second set of hands is *useful for* about an hour of this job.

**See also:** [suitable for](#suitable-for), [essential to](#essential-to), [good at](#good-at), [available to](#available-to), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### vested with

**Pronunciation:** /ˈvɛstɪd wɪθ/ &middot; *VEST-id with* &middot; weak /ˈvɛstəd wəθ/
**Русский:** наделённый чем-л. — полномочием, властью, правом; юр./офиц.
**Type:** adjective (past participle) + preposition &middot; **Register:** formal and legal; the natural home is a statute, a charter, or a ceremony — *by the power vested in me*
**Complement:** a noun phrase naming the power, authority, or interest conferred — **never a gerund** and never a clause. The recipient goes in the subject, and the source, if named, in a *by*-phrase.
**Pattern:** *be vested with* [authority / power] &middot; *vest* [power] *in* [person] — the converse frame, where the power is the object and the person takes *in* &middot; *by the power vested in me by* [authority] — the wedding formula &middot; *a vested interest in* [noun] &middot; *fully vested* — the retirement-plan sense, with no complement
**Variants:** *endowed with* covers qualities and funds; *invested with* is the same word in its longer form and is used of dignity and symbolism (*invested with meaning*); *granted* and *conferred on* are the plain verbs.
**Contrast:** ***Vested with* takes the power; *vested in* takes the person.*** The board is vested with authority to set rates; authority to set rates is vested in the board — same fact, mirrored. Against [endowed with](#endowed-with), which suits qualities and gifts, *vested with* suits offices and legal powers. Note the American benefits sense, where an employee becomes *vested* after a period of service and takes no complement at all, and the idiom *a vested interest in*, which means a stake worth protecting.

Formally granted a power, an authority, or an interest.

1. The commission is *vested with* authority to subpoena records.
2. By the power *vested in* me by the state — the converse frame, with *in*.
3. The trustee is *vested with* discretion over distributions.
4. No single office is *vested with* the power to waive the requirement.
5. The council is *vested with* everything the charter took from the mayor.

**See also:** [endowed with](#endowed-with), [possessed of](#possessed-of), [entitled to](#entitled-to), [seized of, seised of](#seized-of-seised-of), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

### vulnerable to

**Pronunciation:** /ˈvʌlnərəbəl tu/ &middot; *VUL-nur-uh-bull too* &middot; weak /ˈvʌlnərəbəl tə/
**Русский:** уязвимый для, беззащитный перед
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** a noun naming the **threat** after *to*; a **gerund** where a process is meant (*vulnerable to being outvoted*) — **never a to-infinitive.** ✗ *vulnerable to be attacked* is an error; write *vulnerable to attack*.
**Pattern:** *be vulnerable to* [threat] &middot; *leave* [someone] *vulnerable to* [threat] &middot; *particularly* / *especially vulnerable to* [noun] &middot; *vulnerable* alone: *a vulnerable population*
**Variants:** *susceptible to*, *exposed to*, *prone to*, *open to*, and *at risk of* are the neighbors; all but the last take *to*.
**Contrast:** ***Vulnerable to* names what can hurt you; *at risk of* names what might happen to you.*** *Vulnerable to flooding* and *at risk of flooding* are close, but the first says the defenses are weak and the second says the odds are high. Note the related *prone to*, which takes both a noun and, unusually, sometimes a bare verb (*prone to overreact* alongside *prone to overreacting*) — a rare crossover in this family. ✗ *vulnerable for* and ✗ *vulnerable from* are errors.

Open to being harmed by something.

1. Older homes on that street are *vulnerable to* basement flooding.
2. The system was *vulnerable to* a fairly obvious attack for two years.
3. Seedlings are *vulnerable to* a late frost for another month.
4. The company is *vulnerable to* any real change in fuel prices.
5. The change left part-time staff *vulnerable to* being cut first.

**See also:** [sensitive to](#sensitive-to), [safe from](#safe-from), [subject to](#subject-to), [harmful to](#harmful-to), [to](02-core-relational.md#to)

### worried about

**Pronunciation:** /ˈwɜrid əˈbaʊt/ &middot; *WUR-eed uh-BOWT* &middot; weak /ˈwɜrid əˈbaʊt/, casually /ˈwɜrid ˈbaʊt/
**Русский:** беспокоящийся о том, что может пойти не так
**Type:** adjective (participial) + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** noun phrase or **gerund** after *about* (*worried about losing the deposit*) and after *by* — **never a to-infinitive after a preposition.** A *that*-clause attaches to the bare adjective: *worried that the check hadn't cleared*.
**Pattern:** *be worried about* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *be worried by* [the thing causing the worry] &middot; *be worried for* [someone's welfare] &middot; *be worried* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *get worried about* [noun] &middot; *worry* [someone] (no preposition on the object)
**Variants:** *concerned about* is the more formal twin; *anxious about* the more clinical one; *nervous about* the version reserved for a specific upcoming event.
**Contrast:** ***About* names the topic of the worry; *by* names what produced it; *for* names the person you fear for.*** *Worried about the results* is ordinary; *worried by the results* means the results themselves disturbed you and is what a doctor says; *worried for her* is on her behalf. The *by* frame is the one that reports an active cause, exactly as with *annoyed by* and *bored by*. ✗ *worried of* and ✗ *worried with* are errors.

Anxious about something that might go wrong.
