1. She's *worried about* the second mortgage more than the first.
2. Doctors were *worried by* the pattern in the second scan.
3. He's *worried for* his parents, who haven't left the house all winter.
4. I'm *worried about* signing anything before the survey comes back.
5. Nobody was *worried that* the vote would fail; they were worried about the margin.

**See also:** [concerned about](#concerned-about), [anxious about](#anxious-about), [nervous about](#nervous-about), [afraid of](#afraid-of), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about)

### worthy of

**Pronunciation:** /ˈwɜrði ʌv/ &middot; *WUR-thee uv* &middot; weak /ˈwɜrði əv/, and before a consonant often /ˈwɜrði ə/ — "WUR-thee-uh-note"
**Русский:** достойный чего-л., заслуживающий
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal; ordinary in reviews, recommendations, and argument
**Complement:** a noun phrase or a **gerund** (*worthy of being taken seriously*) — **never a to-infinitive after *of***: ✗ *worthy of to be considered*. A separate and much older frame takes a bare infinitive with no *of* at all (*worthy to be called a son*), which survives in scripture and in deliberate archaism.
**Pattern:** *be worthy of* [noun / verb-ing] &middot; *hardly* / *richly* / *well worthy of* &middot; *worthy of note* / *worthy of mention* / *worthy of consideration* — the fixed collocations &middot; *worth* [noun / verb-ing] — the different adjective, with no preposition
**Variants:** *deserving of* is the close twin and stresses what conduct has earned; *meriting* is the participial version; *worth* replaces the whole phrase in ordinary speech and takes no *of*.
**Contrast:** ***Worthy of* takes *of*; *worth* takes nothing.*** ✓ *worth a look*, ✓ *worthy of a look*, ✗ *worth of a look*, ✗ *worthy a look* except in the frozen literary *worthy a second glance*. That is the trap, and it catches native writers. Against [deserving of](#deserving-of), which is about desert earned by conduct, *worthy of* can simply weigh quality: a sequel worthy of the original has met a standard, not earned a reward. Note the common compound suffix *-worthy* — *newsworthy*, *trustworthy*, *seaworthy* — where the complement has moved in front and the *of* disappears.

Good enough or important enough to deserve something.

1. The proposal is *worthy of* a closer look than it got.
2. Two findings in the audit are *worthy of* mention in the cover letter.
3. That's a performance *worthy of* the room it was played in.
4. Her record is *worthy of* being read aloud at the ceremony.
5. Nothing in the complaint is *worthy of* the attention it has drawn.

**See also:** [deserving of](#deserving-of), [entitled to](#entitled-to), [suitable for](#suitable-for), [capable of](#capable-of), [of](02-core-relational.md#of)

### wrong with

**Pronunciation:** /rɔŋ wɪθ/ &middot; *RAWNG with* &middot; weak /rɔŋ wəθ/
**Русский:** не в порядке с — wrong with о неисправности или недомогании, wrong about об ошибке во мнении
**Type:** adjective + preposition &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a noun or pronoun naming **what has the fault** after *with*; a noun phrase or **gerund** after *about* (*wrong about assuming they'd call*) — **never a to-infinitive after either.** The *it*-frame takes an infinitive: *It's wrong to say that*.
**Pattern:** *there is something wrong with* [noun] &middot; *what's wrong with* [noun] &middot; *be wrong about* [matter / verb-ing] &middot; *it is wrong to* [verb] &middot; *go wrong with* [noun] &middot; *nothing wrong with* [noun / verb-ing]
**Variants:** *the matter with* is the exact equivalent of *wrong with* in the fault frame: *what's the matter with the car*. *Mistaken about* is the formal twin of *wrong about*.
**Contrast:** ***Wrong with* locates a fault; *wrong about* assigns a mistaken belief.*** *Something's wrong with him* means he is unwell or acting oddly; *He's wrong about the date* means he has the wrong information. These two do not overlap at all, and only the preposition separates them. Note the very common defensive frame *nothing wrong with* + *-ing*, which is a standard American way of defending a choice: *There's nothing wrong with taking the long way*. ✗ *wrong of* names the doer instead: *It was wrong of him to ask*, which is correct in that frame only.

Faulty, or mistaken — the preposition decides which.

1. Something is *wrong with* the compressor again.
2. What's *wrong with* leaving an hour earlier?
3. He was *wrong about* the closing date by two weeks.
4. There's nothing *wrong with* the plan except that nobody will fund it.
5. It was *wrong of* them *to* announce it before telling the staff.

**See also:** [bad at](#bad-at), [guilty of](#guilty-of), [responsible for](#responsible-for), [with](02-core-relational.md#with)

## Zero-preposition traps: the words that take no preposition at all

The mirror image of everything above. These verbs take a **direct object and nothing in between** — no *about*, no *to*, no *with* — and inserting one is the single most recognizable non-native error in English, because the equivalent verb in French, Spanish, Italian, Russian, Arabic, Hindi, and half a dozen other languages demands a preposition there. ✗ *We discussed about the budget*, ✗ *She married with a pilot*, ✗ *He entered into the room*, ✗ *The plan lacks of detail* — all four are ungrammatical in English, and all four are produced by fluent speakers every day.

Two things make the trap sticky. First, several of these verbs **do** take a preposition in a different sense, so the learner has heard *enter into an agreement* and *answer to the board* and reasonably generalizes. Second, the **related noun usually does take a preposition** — *a discussion about the budget*, *a lack of detail*, *a request for information* — so the pattern that is wrong for the verb is right for the noun standing next to it. Each entry below marks both boundaries.

One member of the set is not an error at all but a transatlantic split: Americans ***protest* a decision** with no preposition, while British English requires ***protest against*** it. That entry is included here because the bare transitive is the American form and because the same instinct that produces ✗ *discuss about* produces *protest against* — correctly in London, and only optionally in Chicago. In pronunciation, note what these entries have in common: with no preposition in the phrase, the only weak form left between verb and object is the article, /ðə/ or /ði/ or /ə/.

### answer

**Pronunciation:** /ˈænsər/ &middot; *AN-sur* &middot; the object attaches with no preposition and no pause: *answer the question* /ˈænsər ðə ˈkwɛstʃən/ — the only weak form in the phrase is the article /ðə/, because no preposition intervenes
**Русский:** отвечать на — без предлога: answer the question, не ✗ answer to the question
**Type:** verb + direct object (zero preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a **direct object** — a question, a letter, a phone, a door, a person. ✗ *answer to the question* and ✗ *answer at the phone* are errors. The verb does take *to* and *for* in other senses, described below.
**Pattern:** *answer* [question / letter / phone / door] &middot; *answer* [someone] &middot; *answer to* [a person or body you report to] &middot; *answer for* [something you are held accountable for] &middot; *answer back* &middot; *an answer to* [question] — the **noun takes *to***
**Variants:** *reply to* and *respond to* both **require** *to*, which is exactly where the confusion starts: ✓ *answer the letter*, ✓ *reply to the letter*, ✗ *answer to the letter*.
**Contrast:** **The verb takes no preposition; the noun takes *to*; and the two idiomatic frames take *to* and *for*.** ✓ *She answered the question.* ✓ *That's not an answer to my question.* ✓ *He answers to the regional director.* ✓ *Somebody has to answer for the delay.* Four different structures from one word, and only the first is the plain transitive use.

Give a reply to a question, a letter, a call, or a door — with the thing replied to as a direct object.

1. She *answered* every question without looking at her notes.
2. Nobody *answered* the door, so we left the package on the porch.
3. Would you *answer* the phone if it rings while I'm out?
4. He *answers to* a board of nine people, none of whom agree.
5. Somebody is going to have to *answer for* how this was budgeted.

**See also:** [discuss](#discuss), [contact](#contact), [request](#request), [responsible for](#responsible-for)

### approach

**Pronunciation:** /əˈproʊtʃ/ &middot; *uh-PROHCH* &middot; the object attaches directly: *approach the building* /əˈproʊtʃ ðə ˈbɪldɪŋ/ — weak /ðə/, and nothing else in the phrase to reduce
**Русский:** подходить к, обращаться к — без предлога: approach the problem, не ✗ approach to
**Type:** verb + direct object (zero preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** a **direct object** — a place, a person, a figure, a subject. ✗ *approach to the building* and ✗ *approach at the deadline* are errors. The verb takes *about* when a person is approached **regarding** something: *approach her about the job*, which is a second object slot, not a substitute for the first.
**Pattern:** *approach* [place / person / number] &middot; *approach* [someone] *about* [matter] &middot; *approach* [a problem] &middot; *an approach to* [problem] — the **noun takes *to***
**Variants:** *come up to* and *go up to* are the everyday physical equivalents and do take prepositions; *get in touch with* replaces the "approach someone about" sense in casual speech.
**Contrast:** **The verb takes a bare object; the noun takes *to*.** ✓ *We approached the problem differently.* ✓ *That's a new approach to the problem.* ✗ *We approached to the problem.* The *about* frame is fully correct and worth knowing, because it is what you actually want when raising a subject with someone: *I approached her about covering the shift.*

Come near to something, or raise a matter with someone.

1. The storm *approached* the coast faster than forecast.
2. Temperatures are *approaching* ninety by mid-morning.
3. She *approached* the whole problem from the accounting side.
4. Nobody had *approached* him *about* taking over the route.
5. Two hikers *approached* the ranger station just before dark.

**See also:** [reach](#reach), [contact](#contact), [enter](#enter), [close to](#close-to)

### attend

**Pronunciation:** /əˈtɛnd/ &middot; *uh-TEND* &middot; the object attaches directly: *attend the meeting* /əˈtɛnd ðə ˈmitɪŋ/ — weak /ðə/, with no preposition between verb and object
**Русский:** присутствовать на, посещать — без предлога: attend the meeting
**Type:** verb + direct object (zero preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** a **direct object** — a meeting, a school, a service, an event. ✗ *attend to the meeting* means something else entirely, and ✗ *attend at the conference* is an error. The separate phrasal verb *attend to* means to deal with or care for.
**Pattern:** *attend* [event / institution] &middot; *attend to* [a matter or a patient] — a **different verb** &middot; *attendance at* [event] — the **noun takes *at*** &middot; *in attendance*
**Variants:** *go to* is the everyday equivalent and requires *to*: ✓ *go to the meeting*, ✓ *attend the meeting*, ✗ *attend to the meeting* in that sense. *Be present at* is the formal one.
**Contrast:** ***Attend* an event; *attend to* a task.*** ✓ *She attended the hearing* (she was there). ✓ *She attended to the paperwork* (she handled it). ✓ *A nurse attended to him* (cared for him). The two are different verbs that happen to share a spelling, and choosing wrong changes the meaning rather than merely sounding foreign. Note that the **noun takes *at***: *attendance at the meeting was poor*.

Be present at something — with the event as a direct object.

1. About forty people *attended* the hearing.
2. He *attended* the same high school as both his parents.
3. She couldn't *attend* the funeral and sent her brother.
4. I'll *attend to* the invoices as soon as the phones stop — a different verb.
5. Everyone on the crew is expected to *attend* the safety briefing.

**See also:** [join](#join), [reach](#reach), [absent from](#absent-from), [late for](#late-for)

### contact

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑntækt/ &middot; *KON-takt* &middot; the object attaches directly: *contact the office* /ˈkɑntækt ði ˈɔfɪs/ — weak /ði/ before the vowel, and no preposition to reduce
**Русский:** связаться с кем-л. — без предлога: contact me, не ✗ contact with me
**Type:** verb + direct object (zero preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a **direct object** — a person or an organization. ✗ *contact with the office* and ✗ *contact to the office* are errors as verb frames. The **noun** *contact* does take *with*: *contact with the family*.
**Pattern:** *contact* [person / office] &middot; *contact* [someone] *about* [matter] &middot; *be in contact with* [someone] — the **noun frame** &middot; *make contact with* [someone]
**Variants:** *get in touch with* and *reach out to* are the everyday American equivalents and both take prepositions, which is where the pull toward ✗ *contact to* comes from.
**Contrast:** **The verb takes a bare object; every noun frame takes *with*.** ✓ *Contact the office before noon.* ✓ *We've had no contact with the office.* ✗ *Contact with the office before noon.* The verb was coined from the noun in the twentieth century and was once condemned as business jargon; it is now entirely standard, but the noun's *with* still leaks into the verb frame.

Get in touch with someone.

1. *Contact* the county clerk before you file anything.
2. She *contacted* three references and heard back from one.
3. Please *contact* us *about* any charge you don't recognize.
4. He hasn't been in *contact with* his sister since the spring.
5. We *contacted* every household on the affected block.

**See also:** [phone](#phone), [approach](#approach), [answer](#answer), [reach](#reach)

### discuss

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈskʌs/ &middot; *dih-SKUSS* &middot; the object attaches directly: *discuss the budget* /dɪˈskʌs ðə ˈbʌdʒɪt/ — weak /ðə/, with no weak *about* /əˈbaʊt/ in between
**Русский:** обсуждать что-л. — без предлога: discuss the plan, не ✗ discuss about
**Type:** verb + direct object (zero preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a **direct object** or a ***wh*-clause** (*discuss what to do next*) — ✗ ***discuss about* is an error**, and it is the most common of all zero-preposition mistakes. A *that*-clause is not possible either: ✗ *discuss that we should wait*.
**Pattern:** *discuss* [topic] &middot; *discuss* [topic] *with* [person] &middot; *discuss* [wh-clause] &middot; *a discussion about* / *of* [topic] — the **noun takes a preposition**
**Variants:** *talk about* and *speak about* both **require** *about*, which is exactly the source of the error: ✓ *talk about the budget*, ✓ *discuss the budget*, ✗ *discuss about the budget*.
**Contrast:** **The verb takes no preposition for the topic and *with* for the person.** ✓ *I discussed the schedule with her.* ✗ *I discussed about the schedule.* ✗ *I discussed with her the problem* is grammatical but awkward in American English; put the topic first. The **noun** takes *about* or *of* freely — *a discussion about pay*, *a discussion of the evidence* — and this asymmetry between verb and noun is what keeps the error alive.

Talk about something in a considered way, with the topic as a direct object.

1. The board *discussed* the budget for two hours and voted in five minutes.
2. We *discussed* the schedule *with* both crews before posting it.
3. She wants to *discuss* what happens if the grant isn't renewed.
4. Nobody had *discussed* the possibility of closing the second site.
5. There was a long *discussion about* pay — the noun, which does take *about*.

**See also:** [mention](#mention), [emphasize](#emphasize), [answer](#answer), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about)

### emphasize

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛmfəsaɪz/ &middot; *EM-fuh-syz* &middot; the object attaches directly: *emphasize the deadline* /ˈɛmfəsaɪz ðə ˈdɛdlaɪn/ — weak /ðə/, with no weak *on* /ən/ in between
**Русский:** подчёркивать, особо отмечать — без предлога: emphasize the risk
**Type:** verb + direct object (zero preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** a **direct object** or a ***that*-clause** (*emphasized that the deadline was firm*) — ✗ ***emphasize on* is an error**, though it is common in Indian and other South Asian English and is spreading. The related noun frames take *on*, which is where it comes from.
**Pattern:** *emphasize* [point] &middot; *emphasize* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *place* / *put emphasis on* [noun] — the **noun frame takes *on*** &middot; *lay stress on* [noun] &middot; *stress* [point] — also zero-preposition
**Variants:** *stress* and *underscore* behave identically and take no preposition; *focus on* and *dwell on* do take *on* and are not substitutes.
**Contrast:** **The verb takes a bare object; the noun takes *on*.** ✓ *She emphasized the deadline.* ✓ *She placed great emphasis on the deadline.* ✗ *She emphasized on the deadline.* The same asymmetry runs through the near-synonyms: ✓ *stress the point* / ✓ *lay stress on the point*. In American English the ✗ *emphasize on* form is corrected in every register, so it is worth eliminating deliberately.

Give special importance to something in speaking or writing.

1. The chief *emphasized* that no decision had been made.
2. She *emphasized* the deadline three separate times.
3. The report *emphasizes* cost and says almost nothing about safety.
4. He *stressed* the same point in every meeting — *stress*, also with no preposition.
5. The curriculum places too much *emphasis on* memorization — the noun, with *on*.

**See also:** [discuss](#discuss), [mention](#mention), [serious about](#serious-about), [on](01-core-in-on-at.md#on)

### enter

**Pronunciation:** /ˈɛntər/ &middot; *EN-tur* &middot; the object attaches directly: *enter the building* /ˈɛntər ðə ˈbɪldɪŋ/ — weak /ðə/, and in the literal sense no *into* /ˈɪntə/ at all
**Русский:** входить в, вступать в — без предлога: enter the room, не ✗ enter into the room
**Type:** verb + direct object (zero preposition) in the literal sense &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a **direct object** — a place, a competition, a profession, data. ✗ ***enter into the room* is an error** in the literal sense. *Enter into* is correct and idiomatic in the **abstract** sense: *enter into an agreement*, *enter into negotiations*, *enter into the spirit of the thing*.
**Pattern:** *enter* [place / contest / profession] &middot; *enter* [data] *into* [a system] &middot; *enter into* [an agreement / negotiations / a discussion] &middot; *enter on* / *upon* [a period or undertaking] — formal &middot; *entrance to* [place] — the **noun takes *to***
**Variants:** *go into* and *come into* are the everyday physical equivalents and require *into*: ✓ *go into the room*, ✓ *enter the room*, ✗ *enter into the room*.
**Contrast:** **Literal movement takes a bare object; abstract commitment takes *into*.** ✓ *He entered the room.* ✓ *They entered into a five-year lease.* ✓ *Enter the figures into the spreadsheet* — where *into* marks the destination of the data, not the thing entered. The formal *enter upon a new phase* survives in ceremonial writing. And the noun takes *to*: *the entrance to the building*.

Go into a place, or begin taking part in something.

1. She *entered* the building through the loading dock.
2. Three of our people *entered* the contest and one placed.
3. He *entered* the profession the year the rules changed.
4. The two firms *entered into* an agreement in March — the abstract sense.
5. *Enter* the reading *into* the log before you leave the site.

**See also:** [approach](#approach), [join](#join), [reach](#reach), [into](01-core-in-on-at.md#into)

### join

**Pronunciation:** /dʒɔɪn/ &middot; *JOYN* &middot; the object attaches directly: *join the union* /dʒɔɪn ðə ˈjunjən/ — weak /ðə/, and no preposition to reduce
**Русский:** вступать в, присоединяться к — без предлога: join the club
**Type:** verb + direct object (zero preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a **direct object** — an organization, a group, a person, a line. ✗ *join to the union* and ✗ *join with the line* are errors in this sense. *Join in* takes an activity (*join in the singing*) and *join with* takes a partner in a joint action (*joined with the county to fund it*), both of which are correct in their own frames.
**Pattern:** *join* [organization / group / person] &middot; *join* [someone] *for* [a meal or an activity] &middot; *join in* [an activity] &middot; *join with* [a partner] *to* [verb] &middot; *join* [X] *to* [Y] — physically connect
**Variants:** *sign up for* and *become a member of* are the everyday equivalents and take prepositions; *take part in* replaces *join in*.
**Contrast:** **Four frames.** ✓ *She joined the union* (became a member). ✓ *Join us for dinner* (accompany). ✓ *He joined in the discussion* (took part). ✓ *Join the two pipes to each other* (connect). Only the first is the bare transitive, and it is the one where an inserted preposition is wrong: ✗ *She joined to the union*.

Become a member of something, or come together with someone.

1. She *joined* the union her first week on the job.
2. Will you *join* us *for* dinner Thursday?
3. Two more agencies *joined* the search by Sunday morning.
4. He never *joined in* the complaining, which people noticed.
5. The city *joined with* the county *to* pay for the new intersection.

**See also:** [attend](#attend), [enter](#enter), [involved in](#involved-in), [engaged to](#engaged-to)

### lack

**Pronunciation:** /læk/ &middot; *LAK* &middot; the object attaches directly: *lack evidence* /læk ˈɛvɪdəns/ — no article and no preposition, so the phrase carries no weak form at all; compare the noun frame *a lack of evidence* /ə ˈlæk əv/, with weak /ə/ and /əv/
**Русский:** не иметь чего-л., испытывать нехватку — без предлога: lack money
**Type:** verb + direct object (zero preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** a **direct object** — usually an abstract or mass noun. ✗ ***lack of* as a verb is an error**: ✗ *The report lacks of detail*. The **noun** *lack* takes *of* (*a lack of detail*) and the **adjective** *lacking* takes *in* (*lacking in detail*), and those three shapes are the whole difficulty.
**Pattern:** *lack* [something] &middot; *be lacking in* [quality] — the **adjective frame** &middot; *a lack of* [noun] — the **noun frame** &middot; *for lack of* [noun] &middot; *lacking* [noun] — participle with a bare object
**Variants:** *be short of* and *be without* are the everyday equivalents and both take prepositions; *want for* is the literary one (*want for nothing*).
**Contrast:** **Three related forms, three structures, and this is the most instructive case in the section.** ✓ *The plan lacks detail* — verb, no preposition. ✓ *The plan is lacking in detail* — adjective, *in*. ✓ *There is a lack of detail* — noun, *of*. Learners who meet the noun first produce ✗ *lacks of detail*, which is the commonest error involving the word. Note also that *lack* has no progressive in careful writing: ✗ *is lacking confidence* is better as *lacks confidence*, though *lacking in confidence* is fine.

Be without something that is needed or expected.

1. The report *lacks* any discussion of what it would cost.
2. He doesn't *lack* confidence; he lacks a plan.
3. The building *lacks* a second exit on the top floor.
4. The proposal is *lacking in* specifics — the adjective, with *in*.
5. The case was dropped for *lack of* evidence — the noun, with *of*.

**See also:** [lacking in](#lacking-in), [short of](#short-of), [free of](#free-of), [full of](#full-of)

### marry

**Pronunciation:** /ˈmɛri/ &middot; *MAIR-ee* &middot; the object attaches directly: *marry her* /ˈmɛri hər/, in ordinary speech with weak /ər/ — and no preposition to reduce
**Русский:** жениться на, выходить замуж за — без предлога: marry her, не ✗ marry with
**Type:** verb + direct object (zero preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a **direct object** — the person married. ✗ ***marry with* and ✗ *marry to* are errors** as verb frames: ✗ *He married with her*, ✗ *He married to her*. The **adjective** *married* takes *to* (*married to a pilot*), and *get married to* is correct, which is exactly what makes the verb frame confusing.
**Pattern:** *marry* [person] &middot; *get married to* [person] &middot; *be married to* [person] &middot; *marry into* [a family] &middot; *marry* [two people] — what the officiant does &middot; *marriage to* [person] — the **noun takes *to***
**Variants:** *wed* behaves identically and takes a bare object; *get hitched* is the informal one.
**Contrast:** **The verb takes a bare object; every other form takes *to*.** ✓ *He married her in 1998.* ✓ *He got married to her in 1998.* ✓ *He's been married to her since 1998.* ✗ *He married to her.* ✗ *He married with her.* The *with* error is produced by speakers of Spanish (*casarse con*), Italian, Polish, and Russian, among others, and is worth drilling because it is instantly noticeable. The fixed phrase *married with two kids* is about children, not spouses.

Take someone as a husband or wife.

1. They *married* in a courthouse two weeks after they met.
2. She *married* a man she'd known since middle school.
3. He *got married to* his college roommate's sister — the *get* frame, with *to*.
4. A judge *married* them on a Tuesday afternoon.
5. She *married into* a family that has farmed the same section since 1890.

**See also:** [married to](#married-to), [engaged to](#engaged-to), [related to](#related-to)

### mention

**Pronunciation:** /ˈmɛnʃən/ &middot; *MEN-shun* &middot; the object attaches directly: *mention the delay* /ˈmɛnʃən ðə dɪˈleɪ/ — weak /ðə/; the only weak preposition arrives with the person, as in *mention it to her* /tə hər/
**Русский:** упоминать о чём-л. — без предлога: mention the delay, не ✗ mention about
**Type:** verb + direct object (zero preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a **direct object**, a ***that*-clause** (*mentioned that the road was closed*), or a **gerund** (*mentioned seeing him there*) — ✗ ***mention about* is an error**, as is ✗ *mention to the delay*. The person told is introduced with *to*: *mention it to her*.
**Pattern:** *mention* [thing] &middot; *mention* [thing] *to* [person] &middot; *mention* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *mention* [verb-ing] &middot; *not to mention* [noun] &middot; *make mention of* [noun] — the **noun frame takes *of***
**Variants:** *bring up* and *refer to* are the everyday equivalents, and *refer to* requires *to*, which is part of the pull toward the error.
**Contrast:** **The verb takes a bare object for the thing and *to* for the person.** ✓ *She mentioned the delay to the crew.* ✗ *She mentioned about the delay.* ✗ *She mentioned to the crew about the delay* is doubly wrong. The **noun** takes *of*: *no mention of the delay*. Note the useful frozen phrases: *not to mention*, meaning "and also", and *don't mention it*, the reply to thanks.

Refer to something briefly in speech or writing.

1. Nobody *mentioned* the delay until the trucks were already loaded.
2. She *mentioned* it *to* her supervisor the same afternoon.
3. He *mentioned* that the road would be closed through Friday.
4. The report makes no *mention of* the second inspection — the noun, with *of*.
5. The rent went up twice, *not to mention* the new parking fee.

**See also:** [discuss](#discuss), [emphasize](#emphasize), [request](#request), [about](10-topic-reference-role.md#about)

### phone

**Pronunciation:** /foʊn/ &middot; *FOHN* &middot; the object attaches directly: *phone the office* /foʊn ði ˈɔfɪs/ — weak /ði/ before the vowel, and no preposition to reduce
**Русский:** звонить кому-л. — без предлога: phone her, не ✗ phone to her
**Type:** verb + direct object (zero preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral; *call* is the American default and *phone* the slightly British-sounding alternative
**Complement:** a **direct object** — the person or place called. ✗ *phone to the office* and ✗ *call to her* are errors. American English also allows the **double object** *call me back* and, distinctively, *write me* without *to*.
**Pattern:** *phone* [person / place] &middot; *call* [person] &middot; *phone* [someone] *about* [matter] &middot; *give* [someone] *a call* &middot; *a call to* [person] — the **noun takes *to***
**Variants:** *call* is the ordinary American verb; *ring* is British; *reach* covers the "get hold of" sense. All three take a bare object.
**Contrast:** **The verb takes a bare object; the noun takes *to*.** ✓ *Phone the office.* ✓ *Make a call to the office.* ✗ *Phone to the office.* A related American-versus-British point belongs here: Americans write ***write me*** with no preposition (*write me when you land*), while British English requires *write to me*. Both varieties agree on *call me* and *phone me*.

Telephone someone.

1. *Phone* the office before you drive all the way out there.
2. She *called* him twice and left one message.
3. *Call* me when you get in, no matter how late.
4. He *phoned* the clinic *about* the results.
5. *Write me* when you get settled — American, with no *to*.

**See also:** [contact](#contact), [answer](#answer), [reach](#reach)

### protest

**Pronunciation:** /prəˈtɛst/ &middot; *pruh-TEST* &middot; the object attaches directly, with the article in its weak form and no preposition to reduce: *protest the ruling* /prəˈtɛst ðə ˈrulɪŋ/ — and note that the verb stresses the second syllable, unlike the noun *protest* /ˈproʊtɛst/
**Русский:** протестовать против — в амер. англ. без предлога: protest the ruling
**Type:** verb + direct object (zero preposition) in American English &middot; **Register:** neutral; the bare transitive is the default of American news writing
**Complement:** a **direct object** naming what is objected to, or a ***that*-clause** (*protested that no one had told him*). In American English **no preposition** comes between: *protest the verdict*. British English requires *protest against the verdict*, which American readers accept without blinking. *Protest to* names the person the complaint is taken to and is standard in both varieties.
**Pattern:** *protest* [decision / policy / verdict] — American &middot; *protest against* [noun] — British, and available in American writing &middot; *protest to* [official] &middot; *protest* (*that*) [clause] &middot; *protest* (intransitive): *they protested for three days* &middot; *under protest* &middot; *a protest against* [noun] — the **noun keeps *against***
**Variants:** *object to* and *complain about* are the everyday equivalents and both **require** their prepositions, which is part of what pulls *against* back into the verb frame. *Demonstrate against* covers the street sense only. *Contest* and *challenge* are the legal equivalents and are also bare transitives.
**Contrast:** **This is the sharpest American-versus-British split in the section, and both forms are defensible.** ✓ *Thousands protested the ruling* is standard in the United States and strikes many British ears as ungrammatical; ✓ *Thousands protested against the ruling* is the British requirement and passes everywhere, including American print. Keep both apart from *protest to*, which names the person you complain to — *she protested to the commissioner* — and from the *that*-frame, which introduces a denial (*he protested that he'd never seen the form*) and is where *protest too much* comes from. The **noun** keeps a preposition in either variety: ✗ *a protest the closure*, ✓ *a protest against the closure*.

Object publicly or formally to something.

1. Several hundred people *protested* the closure outside the county building.
2. Students *protested* the tuition increase through most of the spring.
3. She *protested* the charge in writing and got half of it reversed.
4. In London the same crowd would have *protested against* the bill.
5. He *protested to* the commissioner and was told to file a form.

**See also:** [opposed to](#opposed-to), [critical of](#critical-of), [answer](#answer), [request](#request), [against](09-comparison-opposition.md#against)

### reach

**Pronunciation:** /ritʃ/ &middot; *REECH* &middot; the object attaches directly: *reach the airport* /ritʃ ði ˈɛrpɔrt/ — weak /ði/ before the vowel, with nothing between verb and object
**Русский:** добираться до, дотягиваться до, дозваниваться кому-л. — без предлога: reach Tucson
**Type:** verb + direct object (zero preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral
**Complement:** a **direct object** — a place, a figure, a decision, or a person you get hold of. ✗ *reach to the airport* and ✗ *reach at the office* are errors in these senses. *Reach for* is correct and different: it means to extend a hand toward something, whether or not you get it.
**Pattern:** *reach* [place / figure / agreement / person] &middot; *reach for* [something] &middot; *reach out to* [someone] &middot; *reach into* [container] &middot; *within reach of* [noun] — the **noun frame takes *of***
**Variants:** *get to* and *arrive at* are the everyday equivalents for the travel sense and both require prepositions; *get hold of* replaces the contact sense.
**Contrast:** ***Reach* the destination; *reach for* the object; *reach out to* the person.*** ✓ *We reached Tucson by four.* ✓ *He reached for the light switch.* ✓ *She reached out to three former clients.* Only the first is the bare transitive, and inserting a preposition there is the error: ✗ *We reached to Tucson*. Note that *reach* meaning "get hold of by phone" is also bare: ✓ *I couldn't reach her all morning*.

Arrive at something, extend to it, or get hold of someone.

1. We *reached* the trailhead just before the rain started.
2. Temperatures *reached* ninety-four on Sunday.
3. The two sides *reached* an agreement at two in the morning.
4. I couldn't *reach* her all afternoon.
5. He *reached for* the railing and missed it — a different frame.

**See also:** [approach](#approach), [contact](#contact), [enter](#enter), [attend](#attend)

### request

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈkwɛst/ &middot; *rih-KWEST* &middot; the object attaches directly: *request a copy* /rɪˈkwɛst ə ˈkɑpi/ — weak /ə/, and no weak *for* /fər/ after the verb
**Русский:** просить о чём-л., запрашивать — офиц., без предлога: request a transcript
**Type:** verb + direct object (zero preposition) &middot; **Register:** formal; *ask for* is the everyday equivalent
**Complement:** a **direct object**, a ***that*-clause with a subjunctive** (*requested that she be present*), or an **infinitive** (*requested to see the file*) — ✗ ***request for* as a verb is an error**: ✗ *He requested for a copy*. The **noun** takes *for*: *a request for a copy*.
**Pattern:** *request* [thing] &middot; *request* [thing] *from* [source] &middot; *request that* [subjunctive clause] &middot; *request to* [verb] &middot; *a request for* [thing] — the **noun frame** &middot; *at the request of* [person]
**Variants:** *ask for* is the everyday equivalent and **requires** *for*, which is exactly the source of ✗ *request for*: ✓ *ask for a copy*, ✓ *request a copy*, ✗ *request for a copy*.
**Contrast:** **The verb takes a bare object; the noun takes *for*.** ✓ *She requested a transcript.* ✓ *She submitted a request for a transcript.* ✗ *She requested for a transcript.* The subjunctive frame is worth noting because American formal writing keeps it: *We request that he be notified*, not *is notified*. The same subjunctive follows *ask*, *demand*, *insist*, *recommend*, and *suggest*.

Ask for something, in a formal register.

1. She *requested* a full transcript of the hearing.
2. The office *requested* three references *from* each applicant.
3. They *requested that* the meeting be moved to the following week.
4. He *requested to* see the original file, not a copy.
5. The clerk denied the *request for* an extension — the noun, with *for*.

**See also:** [answer](#answer), [mention](#mention), [contact](#contact), [for](02-core-relational.md#for)

### resemble

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈzɛmbəl/ &middot; *rih-ZEM-bull* &middot; the object attaches directly: *resemble her mother* /rɪˈzɛmbəl hər ˈmʌðər/, in ordinary speech with weak /ər/ — and no preposition to reduce
**Русский:** быть похожим на — без предлога: resemble his father
**Type:** verb + direct object (zero preposition) &middot; **Register:** neutral to formal
**Complement:** a **direct object** — the thing resembled. ✗ ***resemble to* and ✗ *resemble with* are errors**: ✗ *She resembles to her mother*. The related adjective takes *to* (*similar to her mother*), which is where the pull comes from.
**Pattern:** *resemble* [someone / something] &middot; *closely* / *strongly resemble* [noun] &middot; *bear a resemblance to* [noun] — the **noun takes *to*** &middot; *look like* [noun] — the everyday equivalent, with *like*
**Variants:** *look like* and *be like* are the everyday versions and require *like*; *take after* is the family-specific one (*she takes after her father*).
**Contrast:** **The verb takes a bare object; the noun and every synonym take a preposition.** ✓ *She resembles her mother.* ✓ *She bears a strong resemblance to her mother.* ✓ *She looks like her mother.* ✗ *She resembles to her mother.* Note that *resemble* has no passive in careful usage — ✗ *her mother is resembled by her* is not English — and no progressive: ✗ *she is resembling her mother*.

Look or be like something else.

1. The second draft barely *resembles* the first.
2. He *resembles* his grandfather more every year.
3. The building *resembles* a filing cabinet with windows.
4. Their proposal closely *resembles* one we rejected in the spring.
5. There's a strong *resemblance to* the earlier design — the noun, with *to*.

**See also:** [similar to](#similar-to), [identical to](#identical-to), [related to](#related-to), [typical of](#typical-of)
